# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 31

## Page 15225

VOLUME THIRTY-ONE
FROM THE BOOK OF ENDOWMENTS TO THE BOOK OF MARRIAGE

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Especially Merciful.

All praise belongs to Allah alone, and may blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man leased a plot of orchard land from another man. The tenant then erected a structure resembling a mosque on the land and built a prayer niche in it. He told the landowner, “I have built this as a mosque, so do not charge me rent for it.” The landowner agreed. Subsequently, the landowner sold the orchard without excluding any part of it. Does this make that place a mosque or not?

If it does not thereby become a mosque, does the landowner’s refraining from collecting rent make it a mosque?

If it has not become [a mosque] and the entire orchard is sold, may the person who erected the mosque-like structure dismantle what he built?

He answered:

If it has not been dedicated to public use as mosques are, such that the five daily prayers held in mosques are performed there, it does not become a mosque merely through permission for the aforementioned construction. If there is no dedication to a pious purpose requiring its exclusion from the property sold, it is included in the sale. Merely beginning the process of making it a mosque does not make it a mosque.

---

## Page 15226

The same applies to the structure. However, the person who provided the money for it should not reclaim it as his own. This is like someone who sets aside some of his wealth to give in charity but cannot find the beggar: he should carry out his intention by giving it to another beggar rather than taking it back into his ownership, although this is not obligatory.

If a place such as this is devoted to serving the needs of another mosque, that is permissible. Indeed, even if it has become a mosque, but no one prays there, it may be transferred to another mosque where it can be put to use. Moreover, if it may be sold and its price spent on another mosque, then it is permissible to erect a structure that benefits another mosque.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man built a mosque and endowed a shop for a designated muezzin and muqīm, but none of the shop’s income was received during his lifetime. Is it permissible to receive it after his death?

He answered:

All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

If someone establishes an endowment but does not relinquish possession of it, the scholars hold two well-known views:

The first is that it is void. This is the position of Mālik and of Imam Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. It is also the view of Abū Hanīfah and his companion Muhammad.

---

## Page 15227

The second is that it is binding. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī and of Imam Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him. It is also the second view within Abū Hanīfah’s school and the view of Abū Yūsuf. Allah knows best.

He was asked:

An area belonging to a religious lodge lies outside it, and a prayer niche was installed there some years ago. The person responsible for overseeing the premises considers it beneficial to build an upper storey above that prayer niche, either to accommodate the imam or someone who serves the premises, although neither the premises nor its occupants have any pressing need for this. Is that permissible?

He answered:

If that area is not a mosque designated for the five daily prayers, but merely an appurtenance of the premises, it is permissible to construct there whatever serves the interests of the premises. Merely fashioning a prayer niche does not make it a mosque—especially since, if it is a mosque designated for the prayers, the scholars disagree over building above it.

---

## Page 15228

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling concerning someone who leases land and builds on it a house, a shop, or another structure that, when developed, would command twenty dirhams in monthly rent, while the monthly ground rent payable is one and a half dirhams?

He had previously withheld judgment:

May the tenant build a mosque for Allah alongside the property he has already built, and dedicate that property as an endowment for the mosque?

He answered:

He may dedicate the building he has erected on the leased land as an endowment, whether as a mosque or otherwise. This does not extinguish the rights of the landowners. Once the lease has expired and the building has collapsed, its status as an endowment ceases, whether it was a mosque or otherwise. The owners may then reclaim their land and make use of it. As long as the building remains standing on it, the fair rental value is payable.

If someone were to establish a mosque as an endowment above a residential property or a house, and the house or residential property subsequently collapsed, endowing the upper storey would not extinguish the rights of the owners of the lower storey. Likewise, endowing a building does not override the rights of the landowners.

---

## Page 15229

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

What is the ruling concerning someone who makes a bequest or establishes an endowment for his neighbours?

He answered:

If the intention of the endowment founder or the person making the bequest cannot be established through wording or customary usage, and he has no customary understanding of who counts as a neighbour, then reference should be made to the meaning established in Islamic law: forty houses in every direction. This is based on the report that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Neighbours extend forty houses in this direction and that. By Him in Whose Hand is my soul, no one whose neighbour is not safe from his harm will enter Paradise.”

And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man who oversees boats built a mosque and allocated to its imam, from his own resources, a month’s hire from all the boats. Is this lawful or unlawful? And is it permissible to pray in the mosque or not?

He answered:

If he pays these dirhams from the hire of boats that belong to him, it is permissible to accept them. But if he pays them from money he takes from people without right, it is not permissible. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15230

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Some people hold an endowment established by their forefather more than one hundred and fifty years ago for a shrine attributed to Seth, the founder’s descendants, and the poor, with its administration entrusted to them. The endowment has long been known on this basis. This has been established before the honourable court, and they possess royal decrees dating from the time of Nūr al-Dīn and Saladin that attest to this and order that the endowment be granted exemption and its inviolability respected. The current administrators now seek to spend half its revenue on maintaining the shrine, while taking for themselves the remaining half due to the founder’s descendants, neither giving it to them nor spending it on the endowment’s designated purposes.

He answered:

This is not permissible for the administrator. Nor is it permissible to allow them to spend the endowment’s funds on purposes other than those lawfully designated for it, or to deprive the founder’s heirs and the poor who are included in his stipulations. Rather, his descendants and the poor have a greater claim than the aforementioned shrine to receive what has been stipulated for them. How, then, can they be deprived under these circumstances? Indeed, even if the endowment had been established solely for the shrine, allocating its surplus to them when they are in need would take precedence over allocating it to others.

---

## Page 15231

Thus, anyone who spends part of the endowment’s revenue on the shrine, takes another part to spend on purposes not provided for in its stipulations, and deprives the descendants included in those stipulations has disobeyed Allah and His Messenger and transgressed His limits concerning the obligation to pay the endowment’s proceeds to the founder’s descendants. He is unjust by the consensus of the Muslim imams who recognize the validity of endowments. This practice dates back to the time of the Companions and their Successors.

As for building shrines over graves and establishing endowments for them, this is a religious innovation. It did not exist in the time of the Companions, their Successors, or the generation following them—not even in the time of the four imams. The imams agree that building these shrines over graves is not prescribed, nor is supporting their construction through an endowment or otherwise, making vows to them, or remaining at them in worship. Nor does prayer or supplication there have any superiority over prayer and supplication in mosques free of graves. He must be informed that this contradicts the religion of Islam as necessarily known and agreed upon by the imams. If he does not recant, he must be called upon to repent. Indeed, the recognized imams have explicitly stated that building mosques over graves, such as this shrine and others like it, is forbidden.

This is because it is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse the Jews and Christians: they took the graves of their prophets as places of worship.”

He was warning against what they had done. ‘Ā’ishah, may Allah be pleased with her, said: “Were it not for that, his grave would have been left in the open, but he disliked the prospect of its being taken as a place of worship.”

It is also reported in Sahīh Muslim that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said five days before his death:

“Those before you used to take graves as places of worship. Beware, then: do not

---

## Page 15232

take graves as places of worship, for I forbid you to do so.” It is also reported in the Sunan collections that he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse women who visit graves and those who establish places of worship and lamps over them.”

Thus, anyone who builds a mosque over a grave and lights a lamp there—whether a lantern, a candle, or the like—has been cursed. How, then, can he spend any of their money on what the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade, while withholding the amounts stipulated for them, despite their entitlement to them under Allah’s religion? Certainly, if this were a mosque devoted to Allah and free of any grave, both they and the mosque would be equally covered by the founder’s stipulations for them. But spending money on building the shrine is disobedience to Allah, whereas paying them is obligatory. If the mosque is separate from the grave, it has the same ruling as other Muslim mosques, but it has no special merit over them. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man established an endowment for a school and stipulated in its deed that no one should reside at that school unless he held neither a salaried appointment nor a regular allowance, and that its revenue should not be paid to anyone receiving a regular allowance from another institution. He also stipulated a specified stipend for each student. Is this condition valid under these circumstances? If it is valid, but the endowment’s revenue decreases so that each student does not receive his prescribed stipend, is it permissible

---

## Page 15233

for a student to receive a stipend elsewhere? If the endowment’s revenue decreases and each student does not receive his full entitlement, may the administrator annul the aforementioned condition or not? If a judge has ruled that the endowment is valid, does the condition become void under these circumstances?

He replied:

The principle underlying these questions is that if the founder’s stipulation constitutes an act of devotion and obedience to Allah and His Messenger, it is valid, even if it is not a binding condition. If it is permissible, the parallel is that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, allowed prizes only for camel racing, horse racing, or archery, although he permitted footraces and other competitions without prizes.

Moreover, Allah Most High said concerning property acquired without fighting:

“ So that it may not circulate only among the rich among you.”

Thus, we know that Allah dislikes wealth circulating only among the rich. Although being wealthy is permissible, establishing an endowment exclusively for the wealthy is not permissible; the same reasoning applies to other permissible characteristics.

Furthermore, if an activity is not an act of devotion, the founder receives no reward for spending money on it. He will therefore have spent his money on something that benefits him neither during his lifetime nor after his death. If the person performing that activity derives no worldly benefit from it either, it amounts to tormenting him without any benefit accruing to him or to the founder. This resembles the restrictions placed on property during the days of pre-Islamic ignorance, to which attention is drawn in Surahs al-An‘ām and al-Mā’idah.

If an activity stipulated in any kind of contract provides no religious or worldly benefit

---

## Page 15234

it is invalid by consensus, in accordance with numerous established principles, because it is a condition not sanctioned by the Book of Allah Most High. It is therefore invalid, even if there are a hundred such conditions. Examples include requiring someone to adhere to a particular kind of food, clothing, or accommodation that Islamic law does not recommend, or to refrain from certain actions that Islamic law recommends, and similar stipulations.

What remains is to determine how this criterion applies to individual cases. This may be agreed upon or disputed, because scholarly judgments differ concerning certain actions. A condition that has been left unfulfilled is therefore examined from another perspective: if it does not serve a purpose sanctioned by Islamic law, either exclusively or predominantly, it is invalid.

Even if the condition is valid, if the revenue then falls short of what the founder stipulated, the beneficiary may obtain enough from another source to meet the remainder of his needs. Providing students of knowledge with sufficient support is an obligation under Islamic law. Indeed, it is among the overarching interests without which society cannot function. No one may therefore stipulate anything that conflicts with it. How much more so when it is not known that the founder intended such a restriction?

In these circumstances, the administrator may deliver the allotted support to those maintained for their pursuit of knowledge without preventing them from obtaining the remainder of what they need from another institution where they hold appointments. This does not annul the condition; rather, it suspends its application when compliance is impossible. The same ruling applies to conditions laid down by Allah. A judge’s ruling does not preclude what has been stated.

---

## Page 15235

These stipends received for religious services are provisions that support religious work, like the support that fighters and scholars receive from fayʾ (revenues acquired without fighting). Obligations under Islamic law are waived when there is a valid excuse. These payments are not rewards offered for completing worldly work, nor are they payments under contracts of hire for such services. This is the true nature of these funds. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man endowed a school and stipulated that anyone holding an appointment there must not hold another appointment elsewhere. He assigned each appointee a specified stipend.

He stated in the endowment deed:

“If this school’s revenue decreases because of drought or any other cause, the remaining revenue of this endowment shall be distributed proportionately among those holding appointments there, according to their specified stipends.”

He also stated in the endowment deed:

“If prices rise, the administrator may increase their stipends sufficiently to meet their needs at that time.”

Now, suppose the endowment’s revenue decreases because its endowed assets have diminished, such that annulling this condition prohibiting appointments both there and elsewhere would lead to the school ceasing to function. In these circumstances, may someone appointed there also hold an appointment elsewhere in order to obtain enough to meet his needs, given that the founder took into account adequate support for those appointed there, or as stated above in the clause concerning rising prices—or is this not permissible?

---

## Page 15236

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

These conditions imposed on those appointed at the school, such as the prohibition on holding another appointment elsewhere, must be fulfilled only when doing so does not compromise the objective prescribed by Islamic law, which is—your name—either obligatory or recommended. It is not permissible to uphold certain conditions while defeating the purpose those conditions were intended to serve.

Thus, when part of the endowment’s principal has been lost and the person appointed at the school no longer receives sufficient support, the condition prohibiting simultaneous appointments is invalid. He is not obliged to observe it, nor may he be compelled to do so, for two reasons:

First: This condition was imposed on them while the endowed property continued to yield revenue for them, whether that revenue was full or reduced. If part of the endowment’s principal is lost, the conditions were not stipulated for that situation. There is a difference between a reduction in the endowment’s revenue while its principal remains intact and the loss of part of its principal.

Second: Adequate support for the person appointed at the school is indispensable. Even if the founder were assumed to have expressly stipulated otherwise, that condition would be invalid. For example, he might say: “Anyone appointed at the school shall not obtain support from elsewhere, even if he does not receive enough to meet his needs.” Even if he expressly stipulated this, it would not be valid, because such a condition contradicts the Book of Allah. Adequate support is indispensable, and securing it for a Muslim is an obligation either upon that Muslim himself or upon the Muslims. No condition contrary to this is valid.

It is clear, moreover, that the founder did not intend this, since he stipulated adequate support for them. Rather, the loss of some of the endowment’s assets is comparable to the destruction of the endowed property itself and similar circumstances.

---

## Page 15237

Whether an endowment is likened to a reward for completing a task, wages, or a stipend, the worker is obliged to perform the work only when he receives what was stipulated for him. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man established an endowment for a mosque and for shrouding the dead. He stipulated that its administration should pass to the most capable of his heirs, then to the next most capable, and thereafter to the judge. He stipulated six dirhams for the mosque’s imam, and six dirhams for the muezzin and the caretaker of the burial ground. He also stipulated two houses for the two men to live in.

The endowment’s revenue subsequently increased fivefold, with no need to increase expenditure on shrouds. The judge therefore allocated thirty dirhams a month to the two men. Afterwards, however, he learned of the founder’s stipulations and hesitated to pay them anything beyond the respective amounts stipulated by the founder. Is he permitted to do so, and are the two men permitted to accept it?

Furthermore, the aforementioned house collapsed, and the endowment’s administrator leased it out for two dirhams a year. The tenant rebuilt it and rented it out for fifty dirhams a year. Is this lease arrangement valid?

He replied:

Yes, the imam and the muezzin may be paid, from an endowment with a surplus such as this, the customary stipend for people in comparable positions—[hal]—even if it exceeds thirty dirhams. Indeed, if they are poor and there is no designated use for the surplus, enough of it may be paid to them to meet their needs in full.

This is for two reasons:

---

## Page 15238

First:

When the founder of an endowment specifies a fixed number of dirhams from an endowment yielding a certain revenue, he may intend a proportion: for example, he may stipulate ten for someone when the revenue is one hundred, intending one tenth. If contextual evidence indicates that this was his intention, it should be acted upon. It is customary that, if an endowment yields one hundred dirhams and the founder stipulates six for someone, then its revenue rises to five hundred, the amount stipulated in such circumstances would increase proportionately—for example, fivefold. It is not customary for someone who stipulates six out of one hundred to stipulate only six out of five hundred. People’s words should therefore be understood according to their customary manner of expression.

Second:

Even if the founder had not stipulated this, the endowment’s surplus should be spent on purposes comparable or similar to its own, such as other mosques, poor neighbours, and the like. The alternatives are either to spend it on such purposes or to hold it in reserve for future repairs and similar needs.

Keeping it in reserve indefinitely while the revenue continues to increase serves no purpose. Rather, it is harmful, since it holds the money for unjust officials and administrators who assume authority over them and take it without right.

It is reported that ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib urged people to raise money for a slave who had contracted to purchase his freedom. When some money remained, he ordered that it be spent on other slaves with such contracts. The reason is that, when funds cannot be spent on their specifically designated purpose, they are directed to another of the same kind. This is therefore the sound view concerning endowments, and any surplus from their coverings should be given in charity, just as ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb used to give the covering of the Kaaba in charity each year, distributing it among the pilgrims.

---

## Page 15239

That being so, it is clear that allocating the surplus to the mosque’s imam and muezzin, provided they are entitled to it, takes precedence over allocating it to others. The amount fixed by the founder does not preclude their entitlement to an additional sum on other grounds, just as it does not preclude entitlement on the part of another mosque. Accordingly, if the founder specified the shrouds to be paid for from part of the revenue, the surplus is to be allocated to the imam and muezzin as mentioned. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man established an endowment and stipulated that the shaykh should have authority to admit residents to it. He also stipulated that no troublemaker or person relying on influence should reside there. Someone gained admission through influence, but subsequently displayed conduct indicating that he was a troublemaker. Relying on the founder’s stipulation, the shaykh considered it appropriate to remove him and admitted another person in accordance with the founder’s stipulation and its prescribed terms.

Is it permissible, without any basis in Islamic law, to remove the person admitted in accordance with the founder’s stipulation and reinstate the troublemaker who relied on influence, contrary to that stipulation? Is doing so forbidden for the administrator and the shaykh, and does it compromise their fitness for office? Is the person who works to bring this about and assists in it likewise acting unlawfully?

He answered:

When the founder’s stipulation is known, it must be set aside in favour of Allah’s stipulation if it conflicts with it, for Allah’s stipulation takes precedence over that of the founder.

---

## Page 15240

For it is not permissible for a person guilty of sinful misconduct to reside in religious institutions such as lodges, schools and the like, whether his misconduct consists in wronging people and transgressing against them in word and deed, or in violating Allah’s rights in matters between himself and Allah. Both types of offender must be reproved and punished. How, then, can such a person be granted a place in religious institutions and the like? How much more so when the founder has stipulated this, making the obligation all the more emphatic!

Anyone entitled to residence who has been lawfully admitted may not be removed for this unjust purpose or for any other. How, then, can a just person be replaced by a wrongdoer, or a righteous person by a wicked one? Whoever assists in this has assisted in sin and transgression, whether or not he knows the founder’s stipulation.

---

## Page 15241

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man established an endowment for a school, stipulating that its income be used for maintenance, and that two thirds go to the jurists, the school and its officeholders. He also stipulated that the administrator act in accordance with the best interests of the endowment. This arrangement has continued for thirty years. The school’s mats and the filling of its cistern have been paid for out of the jurists’ stipends, because they have periods of absence and places elsewhere. The imam’s monthly allowance is twenty dirhams, and the muezzin receives the same.

After this matter arose, the jurists asked the officeholders to share in the deductions made from their stipends for the mats and the filling of the cistern. These officeholders are performing their duties. Even if they have no other post, is the administrator obliged to agree to the jurists’ request and reduce these poor men’s already modest allowances, or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If the administrator considers it appropriate to give priority to officeholders who are paid for specified work—such as the imam and the muezzin—he is right to do so, provided that what they receive does not exceed the customary remuneration for people in comparable positions. Likewise, he must give priority to the revenue collector, the porter, the craftsman, the builder and others who are paid for work they perform to collect the revenue or maintain the premises: they are to receive their wages first.

---

## Page 15242

Leading the prayer and giving the call to prayer are religious observances that may neither be discontinued nor curtailed under any circumstances. The allowance assigned to those who perform them therefore takes precedence over what the jurists receive. This differs from the teacher, the instructor and the jurists, who all belong to the same category.

If the cost of the mats and filling the cistern can be paid from the third allocated to maintenance, or from another source, this should be done, leaving the two thirds intact for those entitled to them. For if it is stipulated that one third is for maintenance and two thirds for the officeholders, there is no greater reason to charge the cost of the mats and similar expenses to one share rather than the other—unless the endowment has a stipulation valid under Islamic law that provides otherwise.

He was asked:

Someone endowed a burial place and stipulated that its Quran reciter must be unmarried. Is it permissible for a married man to take up this post?

He answered:

This stipulation is invalid. A married man has a stronger claim to such a position than an unmarried man if they are equal in all other respects, since remaining unmarried serves no purpose recognized by Islamic law here.

---

## Page 15243

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man established an endowment for a specified number of women, widows and orphans. He stipulated that he himself would administer it during his lifetime, and that after his death its administration would pass to a righteous member of his offspring, whether male or female. Some of the founder’s relatives among his grandchildren are in need, and the administrator wishes to give them preference over others in distributions. Is he permitted to do so?

He answered:

If they and the others are equally in need, the founder’s relatives take precedence over unrelated people in the same circumstances, just as they take precedence in receiving his generosity during his lifetime. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Your charity to Muslims is charity, while your charity to relatives is both charity and maintaining ties of kinship.”

For this reason, a person is instructed to make a bequest to relatives who do not inherit from him. Some scholars hold this instruction to be obligatory, while the majority hold it to be recommended. Both positions are reported from Ahmad. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15244

He was asked—may Allah sanctify his soul:

A man established an endowment for a specified purpose and laid down certain conditions. The founder died before the endowment had been authenticated before a judge, and the deed went missing before that could be done. A separate record was then drawn up that conflicted with the conditions and provisions stated in the endowment deed. This record was authenticated before a judge two years after the date of the aforementioned endowment. The endowment deed subsequently came to light, containing conditions none of which appeared in the record, and came up for authentication. Is it permissible to prevent its authentication and implementation?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Under no circumstances is it permissible to prevent its authentication. Rather, if it can be authenticated, it must be authenticated and implemented, even if the record authenticated subsequently conflicts with it. Even if a judge has ruled on the basis of that record, he is excused because nothing contradicting it had been established before him.

However, if a document said to be the endowment deed comes to light, an opportunity must be afforded to authenticate it through the legally prescribed procedure. If it is authenticated, it must be implemented. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15245

He was asked:

A man established an endowment and stipulated among its conditions that they should recite whatever they could of the Quran, glorify Allah, proclaim His oneness and greatness, and invoke blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, after the dawn prayer until sunrise. Is it preferable to do this quietly or aloud? And what if the founder makes a stipulation concerning this?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Rather, performing remembrance and supplication quietly—including invoking blessings upon the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and other supplications—is preferable. Nor is it absolutely preferable except where there is an overriding consideration; it is especially preferable at this time. For Allah says:

“Remember your Lord within yourself, humbly and with fear.”

An authentic report relates:

“When the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, saw the Companions—may Allah be pleased with them—raising their voices in remembrance, he said: ‘O people, be gentle with yourselves! You are not calling upon one who is deaf or absent. Rather, you are calling upon One who hears and is near. Indeed, the One upon whom you call is nearer to each of you than the neck of his mount.’”

Another hadith states:

“The best remembrance is that performed quietly, and the best provision is that which suffices.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15246

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man established an endowment and imposed conditions upon a group of Quran reciters: they were to attend every day after the dawn prayer and recite whatever they could of the Quran until sunrise, then take turns among themselves in pairs during the daytime, a day at a time. They were also to assemble after the afternoon prayer, each reciting two portions of the Quran, and to assemble every night preceding Friday. Their gatherings in this manner at his grave within the burial site total seventy-seven each month. He further stipulated that they spend every night at the aforementioned burial site, provided each of them with suitable accommodation, and assigned them regular stipends from the endowment’s proceeds, payable daily and monthly.

Are they obliged to attend as he stipulated? Or must they fulfil those requirements wherever they can perform their assigned recitation duties? Or is neither the place nor the time binding? Are they also obliged to spend the night at the aforementioned place, or not?

If these conditions are deemed binding, and one of them appoints someone to perform his assigned recitation duty under the endowment and at the specified place, while the founder stipulated in the endowment deed that they may appoint substitutes in cases of necessity, what constitutes the necessity that permits such substitution?

---

## Page 15247

Furthermore, if the administrator reduces the stipends specified for them in the endowment deed, may their stipulated duties be reduced accordingly? Does this apply whether the reduction is due to necessity, the administrator’s judgment, or a decision made without such judgment? May our master provide a satisfying and comprehensive answer supported by evidence, clearing every difficulty from our hearts. May he be rewarded for doing so.

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The fundamental principle governing this matter—endowments for those who perform deeds intended to draw one closer to Allah Most High, bequests to them, and vows in their favour—is that those deeds must be acts of obedience loved by Allah and His Messenger. If they are prohibited deeds, it is impermissible to establish an endowment for them or to stipulate their performance as a condition of an endowment, by the consensus of the Muslims. The same applies to vows and similar matters. This principle concerning endowments, vows, and the like is agreed upon among Muslims; there is no disagreement whatsoever among the scholars about it.

Among its foundations is the report recorded by al-Bukhārī in his Sahīh from ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

Another of its foundations is the report recorded by al-Bukhārī and Muslim in their two Sahīh collections, also from ‘Ā’ishah:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed the people from the pulpit when Barīrah’s owners wished to

---

## Page 15248

stipulate that the rights of patronage should belong to someone other than the person who emancipates the slave. He said:

‘What is the matter with people who stipulate conditions that are not in Allah’s Book? Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in Allah’s Book, that condition is invalid, even if there are a hundred such conditions. Allah’s Book has greater right to be followed, and Allah’s condition is more binding.’”

This noble, widely transmitted hadith, which the scholars have unanimously accepted, is agreed by them to apply generally to conditions in all contracts. None of them confines it to conditions in sales. Rather, if anyone stipulates conditions in an endowment, emancipation, gift, sale, marriage, lease, vow, or any other transaction that contradict what Allah has prescribed for His servants—such that those conditions require what Allah has forbidden, prohibit what He has commanded, permit what He has prohibited, or prohibit what He has permitted—then those conditions are invalid, by the consensus of the Muslims, in all contracts, whether endowments or otherwise.

Abū Dāwūd and other compilers of the Sunan collections narrated that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Settlement is permissible between Muslims, except for a settlement that permits what is forbidden or forbids what is permissible. Muslims are bound by their conditions, except for a condition that permits what is forbidden or forbids what is permissible.”

The hadith of ‘Ā’ishah is an instance of a general statement made on a particular occasion. Although most scholars hold that, in such cases, the general wording is to be applied rather than confining its application to the occasion that prompted it, there is no disagreement among them that most general statements made on particular occasions are not restricted to those occasions. This is the case with verses revealed on a specific occasion, such as the verses concerning inheritance, jihad, and zihār,

---

## Page 15249

and mutual oaths of imprecation, accusations of unchastity, armed hostility, adjudication, gains obtained without fighting, usury, almsgiving, and other matters. Most of these verses were revealed on particular occasions well known from the books of hadith, Quranic commentary, jurisprudence, and military campaigns. Yet the Muslim community agrees that their rulings apply generally to people other than those particular individuals, and to other cases corresponding to theirs in every respect.

The same applies to hadiths. The hadith of ‘Ā’ishah is among those whose general application they agree upon. It is one of the concise, comprehensive sayings that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was granted and sent with. He said:

“Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in Allah’s Book, that condition is invalid, even if there are a hundred such conditions. Allah’s Book has greater right to be followed, and Allah’s condition is more binding.”

However, they disagree regarding permissible contracts, such as sales, leases, and marriage: does the hadith refer to someone who stipulates a condition that has not been established to contravene any rule of the revealed Law, or to someone who stipulates a condition known to contradict what Allah has prescribed? This is a matter of disagreement, because his words at the end of the hadith:

“Allah’s Book has greater right to be followed, and Allah’s condition is more binding,”

indicate that an invalid condition is one that contradicts this. His words:

“Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in Allah’s Book, that condition is invalid,”

may, however, be understood as referring to anything not sanctioned by the revealed Law.

The proponent of the first view says: whatever permissible matters Allah has not forbidden are among those He has authorized, and are therefore sanctioned by Allah’s Book. As for conditions in contracts intended for

---

## Page 15250

acts of obedience, such as vows, the thing vowed must itself be an act of obedience. If it is merely permissible, fulfilling the vow is not obligatory. However, there is a well-known disagreement among scholars over whether expiation is obligatory for such a vow, just as they disagree over expiation for a vow to commit a sin. A vow to commit a sin must not be fulfilled, whereas someone who vows to do something permissible may choose whether or not to fulfil it. The same applies to endowments.

The ruling on conditions attached to an endowment can be understood by considering two principles:

The founder establishes endowments for after his death solely to benefit from their reward and recompense with Allah, not to derive worldly benefit from them. After death, the deceased benefits only from reward and recompense. Hence, a distinction is drawn between arrangements that may be intended to secure worldly benefit and those intended solely to earn reward and recompense.

The former include sales, leases, and marriage. A person may spend his wealth on these to achieve permissible worldly aims, as well as commendable and religious aims, but not forbidden ones. As for an endowment, he may devote his property only to something that benefits him in his religion. For if he devotes it to something that brings him no religious benefit—and he derives no worldly benefit from the endowment after his death—he will have given away wealth without receiving any benefit in either his religious or worldly affairs. This is not permissible.

For this reason, scholars distinguish between an endowment for a particular individual and one devoted to a cause. If someone establishes an endowment or makes a bequest for a particular individual, it is permissible even if the recipient is a protected non-Muslim subject, because showing kindness to him is sanctioned by the revealed Law. This is indicated by the Quran and Sunnah, as in Allah’s words:

“Keep their company in this world with kindness,”

and in the hadith of

---

## Page 15251

Asmāʾ bint Abī Bakr, who, when her polytheist mother visited her, said: “O Messenger of Allah, my mother has come seeking my kindness. Should I maintain ties with her?” He replied: “Maintain ties with your mother.” This hadith is recorded in both Sahīh collections. Concerning this, Allah Most High revealed:

“Allah does not forbid you from being kind and just towards those who have neither fought you because of your religion nor driven you from your homes. Indeed, Allah loves those who act justly.”

And He Most High said:

“You are not responsible for their guidance, but Allah guides whom He wills. Whatever good you spend is for your own benefit, and you spend only seeking Allah’s Face. Whatever good you spend will be repaid to you in full, and you will not be wronged.”

He thus made clear that gifts to such people are given only for Allah’s sake. It is also authentically reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“There is a reward for kindness to every living creature.”

Thus, if someone makes a bequest or establishes an endowment for a particular individual who is an unbeliever or a sinner, that person’s unbelief or sinfulness is neither the basis nor a condition of his entitlement. Rather, he would still be entitled to what was given to him even if he were an upright Muslim. His sinful status therefore has no bearing on the matter. This differs from making that status a condition of entitlement, by establishing the endowment for unbelievers and sinners as a class, or for a particular group on condition that they be unbelievers or sinners. Scholars have no doubt that such an arrangement is invalid.

However, scholars hold two well-known views concerning an endowment devoted to a merely permissible cause, such as an endowment for the wealthy. The correct view, supported by the Quran, the Sunnah, and the fundamental principles of the revealed Law, is that this too is invalid, for Allah, glorified is He, said concerning fayʾ (wealth acquired without fighting):

“so that it may not circulate only among

---

## Page 15252

the wealthy among you.”

He, glorified is He, thus explained that He prescribed what He mentioned so that wealth acquired without fighting would not circulate among the wealthy to the exclusion of the poor. It is therefore clear that He, glorified is He, dislikes this, forbids it, and condemns it. Whoever establishes an endowment exclusively for the wealthy has made its wealth circulate among them, generation after generation, to the exclusion of the poor. This opposes Allah’s command and His religion, and is therefore impermissible.

It is reported in the Sunan collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“No prize may be offered except for camel racing, horse racing, or archery.”

If he prohibited offering prize money except for what aids obedience to Allah and jihad—even though this is a gift made during one’s lifetime that comes to an end and is not perpetual—what, then, of an endowment?

This is clear from the fundamental principles of the revealed Law in two respects:

First: wealth may only be spent for some religious or worldly benefit. This is a principle upon which the scholars agree. Whoever departs from it is financially irresponsible and, according to the majority of scholars, who impose legal restrictions on the financial dealings of such people, would be placed under those restrictions. He would also be squandering his wealth, and Allah has forbidden squandering wealth in His Book:

“and do not squander your wealth.”

This means spending it on what serves no beneficial purpose. He would also be wasting his wealth, and the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, prohibited wasting wealth in the hadith recorded by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim, narrated by al-Mughīra

---

## Page 15253

ibn Shuʿba, who reported that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, “used to forbid gossip, excessive questioning, and wasting wealth.”

Allah Most High has said in His Book:

“Do not give the weak-minded your property, which Allah has made a means of sustenance for you.”

Many of the Companions and Successors, may Allah be pleased with them, said that this refers to such cases as appointing a financially irresponsible person as one’s agent: a man hands his wealth over to his financially irresponsible son or wife, so that they provide for him and he becomes subject to their authority.

Others said that it refers to handing a financially irresponsible person his own wealth, for Allah has forbidden handing his wealth over to him unless sound judgment has been observed in him.

The verse encompasses both cases: Allah has forbidden allowing a financially irresponsible person to manage wealth, whether for himself or for someone else as an agent or guardian. Spending wealth on what brings no religious or worldly benefit is among the gravest forms of financial irresponsibility and is therefore forbidden by the revealed Law.

Once this is understood, it is clear that the founder of an endowment does not derive worldly benefit from it as he does from what he pays in a sale, a lease, or marriage. Nor does it benefit him religiously unless he spends it in Allah’s cause. Allah’s cause consists in obedience to Him and His Messenger. Allah rewards His servants only for what they spend on things He loves; as for what He does not love, there is no reward for spending on it. And the expenditure of

---

## Page 15254

a person on himself, his children, and his wife is obligatory; hence the reward for it is greater than that for voluntary spending on those outside his family. Accordingly, permissible activities for which the Lawgiver grants no reward likewise carry no reward for spending on them or establishing endowments for them. Establishing such endowments brings neither religious benefit and reward nor worldly benefit. Thus, they afford their founder no benefit in either religious or worldly terms and are therefore invalid.

This is clear in the case of the wealthy. Although giving to them may sometimes be recommended or even obligatory, that is only when they are given something for a reason other than wealth, such as kinship, jihad, religious commitment, or the like. But if wealth itself is made the basis of entitlement, and the wealthy person is singled out to receive while the poor person shares all his other grounds of entitlement—and has a greater claim besides—then every religious community necessarily recognizes that Allah neither loves nor approves of this. It is therefore impermissible to stipulate such a condition in an endowment.

The second argument:

Endowments include those settled in perpetuity upon disbelievers and the like, and those that prevent property from passing by inheritance. This would be justified if there were an overriding benefit; otherwise, the founder would be prevented from doing so, because it withholds wealth from the heirs and those to whom it would pass.

This is the reasoning behind the view that “No property may be tied up so as to withhold the shares prescribed by Allah.” However, this view disregards the position of Umar and others, as well as the overriding benefit involved. But if there is no overriding benefit, and instead he has tied up the wealth

---

## Page 15255

and thereby prevented the heir and everyone else from benefiting from it, while deriving no benefit from it himself, then there is no doubt that such an arrangement must not be given effect.

The disputed issue here concerns endowments whose beneficiaries are designated by permissible worldly attributes, such as material wealth.

As for endowments supporting religious activities—such as the Quran, hadith, jurisprudence, prayer, the call to prayer, leading prayer, jihad, and the like—these fall under the second principle. There can be no disagreement among scholars that endowments may support only those activities which Allah has prescribed and loves.

If someone introduces a practice that Allah has not prescribed and makes it part of religion, he is forbidden to engage in that practice. How, then, could it be lawful for him to endow wealth to support it? Rather, this is akin to establishing endowments for practices that Jews and Christians regard as acts of worship, belonging to religion that has been altered or abrogated. This is why we have made this one of the two foundational principles governing endowments.

Worship, religious observances, and acts by which one draws near to Allah must be based on what is received from Allah and His Messenger. No one may therefore deem anything an act of worship or a means of drawing near to Allah without evidence from the Sharia.

Allah Most High says:

“Or do they have partners who have prescribed for them as religion what Allah has not permitted?”

Allah Most High also says:

“And this is My straight path, so follow it, and do not follow other paths, lest they lead you away from His path.”

And Allah Most High says:

“Alif Lām Mīm Sād.”

“A Book sent down to you—so let there be no distress in your heart because of it—that you may warn by it, and as a reminder to the believers.”

“Follow what has been sent down to you from your Lord, and do not follow any protectors besides Him. Little do you take heed!”

---

## Page 15256

And there are many similar passages in the Book in which Allah commands obedience to His Messenger and adherence to His Book, and forbids following anything outside them. All innovations are of this kind, for an innovation in the technical religious sense—that is, one condemned by the Sharia—is something Allah has not prescribed in religion: something that does not fall within the commands of Allah and His Messenger or obedience to them. Whatever does fall within this is part of the prescribed religion, not an innovation in the religious sense, even if it was carried out after the Prophet’s death, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, on the basis of what was known of his commands. Examples include expelling the Jews and Christians after his death, compiling the Quran into a single volume, and gathering people behind one reciter for the Ramadan night prayers.

Although Umar ibn al-Khattāb, who ordered this, called it an innovation, he did so only in the linguistic sense: anything done without a prior precedent is called an innovation in ordinary language. It is not, however, something the Sharia calls an innovation and prohibits. Thus, it does not fall within the statement narrated by Muslim in his Sahīh from Jābir, who said that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, used to say in his sermons:

“The truest speech is the speech of Allah, the best guidance is the guidance of Muhammad, the worst matters are those newly introduced, and every innovation is misguidance.”

His statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “Every innovation is misguidance,” is true. Nothing that the evidence of the Sharia establishes as recommended is an innovation, as he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith narrated by the compilers of the Sunan and classed as authentic by al-Tirmidhī, from al-Irbād ibn Sāriyah, who narrated from

---

## Page 15257

the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Al-Irbād said:

“The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, gave us an eloquent exhortation that brought tears to our eyes and made our hearts tremble. We said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, this seems like a farewell exhortation. What counsel do you leave us?’

“He said, ‘I counsel you to fear Allah, and to listen and obey, even if the one over you is an Abyssinian slave. Those of you who live after me will see much disagreement. So adhere to my Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs after me. Hold fast to it and cling to it with your very teeth. Beware of newly introduced matters, for every innovation is misguidance.’”

Another version reads: “For every newly introduced matter is an innovation, and every innovation is misguidance.” Another version adds: “And every misguidance is in the Fire.”

In this hadith, he commanded Muslims to follow his Sunnah and the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs, and explained that the newly introduced matters—the innovations he prohibited—are those that contradict this. Thus, even if it were not known that his statements and actions supported the Ramadan night prayers and similar practices, they would, at the very least, be part of the Sunnah of the rightly guided caliphs. They would therefore not be among the innovations in the religious sense that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, called innovations and prohibited.

In short, the scholars do not disagree that an endowment established to support prayer, fasting, recitation, jihad or similar activities that are not prescribed by the Sharia is invalid. Indeed, the person is forbidden both to engage in such an activity and to spend on it. Their disagreement concerning endowments for permissible activities does not extend to cases such as these. For treating something as an act of worship, believing it to be an act of worship, and performing it because it is an act of worship must fall under either what is commanded or what is forbidden. If it is commanded—whether obligatory

---

## Page 15258

or recommended in the Sharia—then regarding it as worship, desiring it for that reason, loving it and performing it are all prescribed. But if Allah neither loves nor approves of it, so that it is neither obligatory nor recommended, no one may believe it to be recommended or an act of obedience that draws one near to Allah, adopt it as part of religion, or encourage it on the grounds that it is worship. This is a fundamental principle of religion: distinguishing between a permissible act performed simply because it is permissible and something adopted as religion, worship, obedience and a means of drawing near to Allah—in belief, desire and practice.

Whoever takes something that is not prescribed, nor part of religion, nor an act of obedience or a means of drawing near to Allah, and treats it as such, commits something forbidden by the consensus of the Muslims.

Scholars may, however, disagree over whether certain matters constitute acts of devotion and worship, whether they concern professed beliefs or practical intentions. Indeed, one scholar may consider obligatory what another considers forbidden. For example, some consider killing an apostate obligatory, while another considers it forbidden. One considers it obligatory to separate an intoxicated man from his wife if he divorces her while intoxicated, while another considers separating them forbidden. Likewise, one considers reciting al-Fātihah obligatory for someone praying behind an imam, while another considers his recitation disliked—either in all cases or when he hears the imam reciting aloud. There are other such points of disagreement.

Just as beliefs concerning these matters and their practice are subject to disagreement, so too is spending money on them. These are matters open to independent scholarly judgment.

---

## Page 15259

As for any practice that a Muslim knows to be a prohibited innovation, someone who knows this may not permit an endowment to support it, by agreement of the Muslims. Some people may nevertheless stipulate such practices as conditions of an endowment because they do not know the Sharia, or because they follow someone whose authority they may not follow in that matter. Such conditions are invalid, as ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb said:

“Bring practices born of ignorance back into line with the Sunnah.”

This is also established by the report in the Sahīh collection from ʿĀ’ishah, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, who said:

“Whoever performs an act that does not conform to our guidance will have it rejected.”

For this reason, scholars agree that a just judge’s ruling must be overturned if it contradicts an explicit scriptural text or a scholarly consensus of which he was unaware. What, then, of the actions of someone unfamiliar with this subject—an endowment founder who does not know the ruling of the Sharia, or the agents who act on his behalf?

Even supposing that a judge ruled that such an arrangement was valid and binding, he could at best be learned and just; by agreement of the Muslims, he may not enforce anything that contradicts an explicit scriptural text or scholarly consensus. Conditions that require what Allah has forbidden, or prohibit what Allah has commanded, contradict both explicit scripture and scholarly consensus. In everything Allah has commanded or forbidden, obedience to Him is required to the extent possible. As the Most High says:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

And as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said in the hadith reported by both al-Bukhārī and Muslim:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

---

## Page 15260

These principles, then, are the comprehensive maxims and general foundations upon which these and similar questions rest. We have outlined some concise, comprehensive points, as space permits, which those trained in understanding the religion will recognize. The next step is to examine, through considered judgment, whether the grounds for the ruling are present in the case under question and in other cases. Any condition shown to be invalid must be disregarded; any condition shown to accord with the Book of Allah must be followed; and any condition whose status is unclear or disputed is subject to the ruling governing comparable cases.

Changing some of these invalid conditions requires firm resolve and effective authority, which Allah strengthens through knowledge and religious commitment. Otherwise, if a person acts merely out of personal desire, seeking worldly gain or warding off worldly harm, while presenting this as enjoining what is right and forbidding what is wrong, his efforts are unlikely to succeed. Even if he is seeking redress and looking for someone to help him, he will attain his aim if Allah assists him through someone endowed with those qualities, or through whatever source of assistance He decrees for him. There is no power or strength except through Allah.

What the questioners have described assumes that all these circumstances are present. May Allah facilitate for them, and for all other Muslims, someone who will help them attain what is good in their religious and worldly affairs. Indeed, He has power over all things.

One matter on which scholars agree, without dispute, is that regularly spending the night in a particular place is not, in itself, an act of drawing near to Allah or of obedience to Him. It is so only at certain times, when specifying that place serves a purpose recognized by the Sharia: such as spending the nights in Minā, or

---

## Page 15261

spending the night at a frontier post to defend it, keeping watch overnight in the cause of Allah, or staying with a scholar or righteous man from whom one benefits, and the like. But requiring a Muslim to remain stationed every night at a particular location without any religious benefit is not part of the religion. Indeed, even if the overnight stay were occasional and this practice were prescribed there, it would still not be part of the religion. If someone imposes this condition upon him and establishes an endowment to support him for that purpose, there is no doubt that such a condition is invalid and ceases to be binding.

Moreover, designating a particular place for the five daily prayers, Quran recitation, or dedicating the reward of that recitation—other than a place designated by the Lawgiver—is likewise not prescribed, by scholarly agreement. Even if someone vows to pray, recite the Quran, or observe a devotional retreat in a particular mosque other than the three mosques, that particular location does not become binding upon him. He may perform these acts elsewhere. Scholars do, however, hold two opinions on whether expiation is required for not adhering to the specified location.

Scholars also hold two opinions on whether the reward of physical acts of worship, such as Quran recitation, prayer, and fasting, reaches the deceased. The sounder view is that it does. However, no scholar has said that one location is superior to another in this respect.

Nor has any of the leading scholars of the Muslim community ever said that prayer or Quran recitation beside a grave is better than elsewhere. Rather, they have differed over whether reciting the Quran beside a grave is disliked. Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, Imam Ahmad—according to one of the two reports from him—and various groups among the early generations considered it disliked, while another group permitted it

---

## Page 15262

among the followers of Abū Hanīfa, Imam Ahmad, and others. This is also one of the two reports from Ahmad. We know of no explicit statement from al-Shāfiʿī concerning any of this.

No scholar has said that reciting the Quran beside a grave is better. Whoever claims that the deceased benefits from hearing it when it is recited beside his grave, but not when the reciter is farther away, advances an invalid innovation contrary to scholarly consensus.

By the agreement of the Muslims, the deceased does not benefit after death from acts he himself performs thereafter—whether listening, reciting, or anything else; such acts are of no avail. Rather, he benefits from the continuing effects of what he did during his lifetime, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When the son of Adam dies, his deeds cease except for three: ongoing charity, knowledge from which benefit is derived, or a righteous child who prays for him.”

He also benefits from the reward dedicated to him from financial acts of worship, such as charity and gifts, by agreement of the jurists. The same applies to physical acts of worship, according to the sounder of their two opinions. Requiring Muslims to perform deeds or give charity only in a designated place, such as Christian churches, is invalid.

In any event, appointing a substitute to perform such stipulated duties is permissible. As for their being performed on behalf of the endowment’s founder, if the substitute is comparable to the person appointing him, there may be harm in this that outweighs the religious benefit, as with duties stipulated in a contract for work owed as an obligation, because

---

## Page 15263

specifying a particular person serves a legitimate religious interest. Thus, it is an invalid condition. Whenever they receive less than what was stipulated for them, they may reduce their stipulated duties proportionately. And Allah knows best.

He also said—may Allah have mercy on him:

A Principle Concerning the Conditions People Stipulate in Endowments:

(*)
Some of these involve recompense in this world and the Hereafter, while others do not; some impose hardship on the beneficiary.

We therefore say:

The duties stipulated in endowments devoted to religious purposes fall into three categories. Such endowments include those supporting prayer leaders, callers to prayer, and those engaged in studying the Quran, hadith, jurisprudence, and similar branches of knowledge, or in acts of worship or jihad in Allah’s cause.

The first category comprises acts through which one draws closer to Allah Most High: obligatory and recommended deeds that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, encouraged and urged people to perform. Such conditions must be fulfilled, and entitlement to the endowment’s benefits generally depends on their fulfillment.

The second category comprises acts that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited or discouraged. Stipulating such an act is invalid by scholarly agreement, for it is widely reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed the people from his pulpit and said:

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hāmid al-Fahd said (p. 227):
> There are two points here:
> First, this principle was drawn from a fatwa included in the same volume (pp. 75–64).
> Second, this principle concludes with his statement (31:46): “Such a person’s sole aim in establishing the endowment is to draw closer to Allah . . And Allah knows best.” It appears that the compiler—may Allah have mercy on him—inserted the “two dots” to indicate omitted text or something similar. The remainder of the text appears in the other passage (31:60–64). And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 15264

“What is the matter with people who stipulate conditions that are not in Allah’s Book? Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in Allah’s Book, it is invalid, even if there are a hundred such conditions. Allah’s Book has greater authority, and Allah’s condition is more binding.”

Although this hadith arose over a condition assigning the right of patronage to someone other than the emancipator, the majority of scholars hold that what counts is the general wording, not the particular occasion that prompted it. Its application to this hadith is unanimously agreed upon.

Likewise, any condition that necessarily entails something prohibited by the Lawgiver is treated in the same way as the prohibited act itself. Whatever is known to be prohibited on the basis of evidence from the Sharia is treated in the same way as something known to have been explicitly prohibited. Scholars may, however, differ in their judgments as to whether a particular act falls within the category of prohibited acts. Consequently, their judgments concerning the condition in question will differ accordingly. Such differences are inevitable within the Muslim community.

This category also includes cases in which the stipulated act is not prohibited in itself, but conflicts with attaining the objective that has been commanded. Examples of such conditions include requiring the residents of a ribāt to remain there constantly. This is among the practices people have introduced that are disapproved of in the Sharia. Other examples include requiring jurists to embrace certain religious innovations contrary to the Quran and Sunnah, or certain prohibited views; or requiring the prayer leader or caller to prayer to omit some of the prescribed sunnah practices of prayer or the call to prayer, or to introduce innovations into either.

Examples include requiring the prayer leader to recite the shorter surahs of the Mufassal at the dawn prayer; requiring the call to prayer to be joined to a formula of remembrance that is not prescribed; or requiring the Eid prayer to be held in the school or mosque while the Muslims are holding it in accordance with the Sunnah of their Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.

---

## Page 15265

Another example in this category is to stipulate that they pray individually.

Also belonging to this category are conditions that necessarily entail abandoning something recommended by the Lawgiver. An example is stipulating that the residents of a ribāt or school beside the main mosque must perform their obligatory prayers in their own premises. This calls upon them to forgo performing the obligatory prayer in the manner most beloved to Allah and His Messenger. No heed should be paid to such a condition; rather, praying in the main mosque is preferable. Indeed, mosques established to cause harm must be demolished, though this is not the place to discuss that in detail.

Another example in this category is stipulating that lights be kept burning over graves, whether candles, oil lamps, or the like. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has cursed women who visit graves and those who establish mosques and lamps over them.”

I know of no disagreement that building mosques and lighting lamps over graves constitutes disobedience to Allah and His Messenger. A detailed discussion of these conditions would be very lengthy; here we mention only their general categories.

(The third category: An act that is neither disapproved of nor recommended in the Sharia, but is simply permissible, with neither its performance nor its omission preferred. Some scholars have held that such a condition must be fulfilled.

The majority of scholars, both those belonging to the well-known schools of jurisprudence and others, hold that such a condition is invalid. In their view, a condition is valid only if it stipulates an act that draws one closer to Allah Most High. This is because a person may spend his wealth only on something that benefits him in his religious or worldly affairs. Thus, as long as

---

## Page 15266

a person is alive, he may spend his wealth in pursuit of permissible aims, since he benefits from doing so. After death, however, he no longer benefits from the deeds of the living except through righteous deeds that he instructed others to perform, helped bring about, or whose reward is gifted to him, and the like. As for deeds that do not constitute obedience to Allah and His Messenger, the deceased does not benefit from them at all. Thus, if a testator or the founder of an endowment stipulates an act or a manner of performing it for which there is no reward, striving to fulfil that stipulation means striving for something that benefits him neither in this world nor in the Hereafter. Such a thing is impermissible, whereas his sole purpose in establishing the endowment is to draw closer to Allah. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man endowed a ribāt (residential lodge), housed a group of people devoted to the Quran there, and allocated sufficient daily provision for them. He imposed conditions upon them that are not prescribed in the Sharia. Among these was that they gather at two specified times during the day to recite a specified portion of the Quran in the premises he had endowed, and nowhere else, assembled together rather than separately. He also stipulated that they gift him the reward of their recitation, and that anyone who did not fulfil his stipulations in the endowed premises would not receive the provision allotted to him.

Are all these conditions binding upon anyone who receives the stipend, or only some of them, or do none of them have any legal effect? If recitation is binding, must they fulfil every condition he stipulated concerning it? Or may they recite whatever they can manage without gifting any reward?

---

## Page 15267

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The governing principle here is that every act stipulated under an endowment established to support particular activities must be an act of devotion—either obligatory or recommended. A stipulation requiring a prohibited act is invalid by agreement of the Muslim scholars. The same applies to a disliked act and, according to the correct view, to a merely permissible act as well. Muslims agree that an endowment founder’s stipulations, like conditions in other contracts, may be either valid or invalid.

When some jurists say that an endowment founder’s stipulations are texts comparable to the wording of the Lawgiver, they mean that they resemble scriptural texts in indicating the founder’s intent, not in being obligatory to implement. That is, the founder’s intent is understood from the wording of his stipulations, just as the Lawgiver’s intent is understood from His wording. Thus, just as generality and specificity, unrestricted and qualified meanings, joint inclusion and ordering are understood in the Sharia from the Lawgiver’s wording, so too are they understood in an endowment from its founder’s wording.

The precise position, however, is that the wording of an endowment founder, a person swearing an oath, an intercessor, a testator, or any contracting party is to be understood according to that person’s customary manner of expression and the language they speak—whether pure Arabic, later-developed Arabic, grammatically incorrect Arabic, or a language other than Arabic, and whether or not it accords with the Lawgiver’s usage. The purpose of words is to convey the intent of those who utter them. We therefore need to understand the Lawgiver’s speech, because knowledge of His language, customary usage, and manner of expression enables us to understand His intent. The same applies to

---

## Page 15268

the speech of every nation and every people. When they address one another concerning sales, leases, endowments, bequests, vows, or other matters, their words must be understood by reference to their intent, what their customary manner of expression indicates about that intent, and the accompanying circumstances.

As for treating the stipulations of an endowment founder, or those of any other contracting party, as equivalent to the Lawgiver’s texts in being obligatory to implement, this is disbelief by agreement of the Muslims. No human being other than the Messenger of Allah—peace and blessings be upon him—is to be obeyed in everything he commands. Conditions that accord with the Book of Allah are valid; those that contradict it are invalid.

As established in the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet—peace and blessings be upon him—delivered a sermon from his pulpit and said:

“What is the matter with people who stipulate conditions that are not in the Book of Allah? Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in the Book of Allah, it is invalid, even if there are a hundred conditions. The Book of Allah has greater right to be followed, and Allah’s condition is more binding.”

The imams agree that the ruling expressed in these words applies to sales, leases, endowments, and other transactions, whether or not the Lawgiver’s wording encompasses them. Either the general scope of the wording, rather than the particular occasion that prompted it, is what counts, or its application to conditions outside sales follows through analogical reasoning, on the basis of its general meaning.

---

## Page 15269

Since the stipulations of an endowment founder are, by agreement, either valid or invalid, if he stipulates a forbidden act, the stipulation is plainly invalid: no created being may be obeyed in disobedience to the Creator. If he stipulates a permissible act that does not draw one nearer to Allah, that too is invalid, because he has imposed a condition that benefits neither himself nor the beneficiary. He himself benefits only by helping to further righteousness and piety.

As for spending wealth on something permissible, this is allowed when a person spends it during his lifetime, as in purchasing or hiring, because he benefits from enjoying permissible things during his life.

An endowment founder and a testator, however, derive no worldly benefit from the permissible acts performed by the beneficiaries of their endowment or bequest, nor are they rewarded in the Hereafter for spending wealth on such things. Even if someone were to spend wealth on this frivolously and foolishly, that would not justify accepting it. How much more so if he requires a permissible act that serves no purpose for him, from which he benefits neither in this world nor in the Hereafter? Rather, this leaves wealth being spent on falsehood, with the recipient subjected to toil and suffering and consuming wealth wrongfully.

Given that the Lawgiver has said:

“No prize may be awarded except for camel racing, horse racing, or archery,”

and has not permitted prizes for activities that do not assist in jihad—even if they are permissible and may have some benefit, such as wrestling and footracing—how can someone provide perpetual remuneration for work that has no benefit, especially when an endowment is set aside in perpetuity? How, then, can he tie up wealth

---

## Page 15270

permanently and in perpetuity for work that benefits neither him nor the person performing it? This harms the heirs and other people by withholding wealth from them without anyone deriving any benefit. It also harms the recipients by putting them to work in which they are bound to serve, preventing them from attending to their religious and worldly interests. Thus, neither he nor they derive any benefit. We have discussed this principle at length elsewhere.

Once this is understood, it is better for each person to recite the Quran individually than for a group to recite together in unison. The latter is called “qirā’at al-irāda.” A number of scholars have disapproved of it, including Mālik, some followers of Imam Ahmad, and others. Those who permitted it—such as some followers of Imam Ahmad—did not say that it was better than individual recitation, in which each person recites the entire Quran. As for the recitation under discussion, no individual recites the entire Quran; rather, one continues from where another left off, and another continues from where he left off. Anyone who has not memorized the Quran omits the portions he has not memorized.

Recitation after Maghrib has no particular recommended merit that would give it precedence over recitation in the middle of the night, after Fajr, or at other such times. Thus, singling out that time for such an act does not in itself draw one nearer to Allah. If someone were to vow to pray, fast, recite the Quran, or undertake a devotional retreat in a particular place

---

## Page 15271

then, if specifying that place carries a particular merit under Islamic law—as with prayer and devotional retreat in the three mosques—he must fulfil his vow accordingly. If it carries no particular merit—as with prayer and devotional retreat in the mosques of other towns—the vow does not make that particular location binding. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah, let him obey Him; and whoever vows to disobey Allah, let him not disobey Him.”

The imams agree that a vow must be fulfilled only insofar as it constitutes obedience to Allah. Thus, one is not obliged to fulfil a vow to do something merely permissible, just as one is not obliged to fulfil a vow to do something forbidden; the scholars agree on both cases. Their disagreement concerns only whether expiation is required, as in the schools of Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and al-Shāfiʿī. How much more, then, does this apply to agreements other than vows, whose binding force is not supported by the same scriptural evidence as vows?

As for stipulating that the reward of recitation be dedicated to another person, this depends on the question of dedicating the reward of physical acts of worship, such as prayer, fasting, and recitation. There is no disagreement that the reward of acts of worship involving wealth may be dedicated to others. Regarding physical acts of worship, however, there are two well-known positions.
