For those whose school holds that their reward may not be dedicated to others—such as most followers of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī—this condition is invalid. It is like stipulating that someone take upon himself the endowment founder’s debts, for no bearer of burdens shall bear another’s burden.

For those whose school holds that the reward of physical acts of worship may be dedicated to others, such as Ahmad

---

## Page 15272

and the followers of Abū Hanīfah and a group of Mālik’s followers, another consideration must be taken into account: such deeds constitute acts of worship only when performed seeking Allah’s countenance. Whatever becomes due under a contract of hire or an agreement for a stipulated reward is not an act of devotion, even if receiving wages or a stipulated reward for it is permissible. For it is permissible to hire someone to lead prayers, give the call to prayer, and teach the Quran. We say … (1).

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Someone endowed a school in Jerusalem and stipulated that its residents perform the five daily prayers there. Is this condition valid? May the residents perform the five daily prayers in al-Aqsā Mosque instead and still receive their allotted stipends? Or is receiving them lawful only if they fulfil this condition?

He answered:

This is not a valid condition upon which entitlement depends. Shaykh ʿIzz al-Dīn ibn ʿAbd al-Salām and other scholars issued this very ruling in precisely this situation, on the basis of several proofs. We have discussed these at length elsewhere, together with the scholars’ statements on the matter.

The residents may perform the five daily prayers in al-Aqsā Mosque rather than in the school. They remain entitled to their allotted stipends, and this is better for them

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space in the original.

---

## Page 15273

than praying in the school. Refraining from performing the obligatory prayers in al-Aqsā Mosque to ensure that one’s stipend is lawful is misguided scrupulousness that deprives its practitioner of the immense reward for praying in the mosque. And Allah knows best.

What do the esteemed scholars say concerning a founder who endowed a lodge for Sufis? This lodge had long followed the customary Sufi practice in such lodges regarding meals and gathering only after the afternoon prayer. A person then assumed its administration and sought to abolish its established practice, imposing conditions upon its residents that had never existed there before.

The residents perform the five daily prayers in this lodge and recite about one and a half of the Quran’s thirty parts after the dawn prayer, and about three parts after the afternoon prayer. If any resident misses a prayer or recitation, an absence is recorded against him, even though no endowment deed or stipulations are known for this lodge.

Is it permissible to introduce these conditions upon them or not? Does the person who introduces them incur sin or not? Is the current administrator permitted to record absences against them or not? Must these conditions be abolished or not? Will the ruler be rewarded for abolishing them or not?

If these conditions were stipulated by the founder, must they be fulfilled or not? Under what circumstances is a person entitled to this support? If someone among the residents is engaged in studying the Islamic religious sciences, does he have a stronger claim than someone who merely adopts an outward guise but possesses no knowledge? And someone who does not conduct himself

---

## Page 15274

in accordance with the manners prescribed by Islamic law, is he permitted to receive any of this support or not? If one of them is engaged in studying sacred knowledge, but his worldly means do not cover even part of his needs, does he have a stronger claim than someone who neither observes Islamic manners nor possesses any knowledge? Give us your ruling—may you be rewarded—and explain this fully, with evidence from the Quran and Sunnah. May Allah be pleased with you.

He replied—may Allah have mercy on him:

The administrator is not permitted to introduce these conditions or any others, for his role is merely to implement the founder’s stipulations. He has no authority to introduce conditions that neither the founder nor the Lawgiver has made obligatory. Whoever introduces them incurs sin, for he withholds from those entitled to support their rightful due until they perform deeds that are not obligatory. Nor is it lawful to record an absence against anyone who fails to comply with these conditions. Rather, these conditions must be abolished. Whoever strives to abolish them, seeking thereby the Face of Allah Most High, will be rewarded.

As for a Sufi who qualifies as a beneficiary of an endowment for Sufis, he must meet three conditions.

The first:

He must be upright in his religion, fulfilling the obligatory duties and avoiding what is forbidden.

The second:

He must observe most of the manners prescribed by Islamic law most of the time, even when they are not obligatory, such as the manners of eating, drinking, dressing and sleeping

---

## Page 15275

and travelling, riding, companionship, social relations and dealings with others, along with other noble manners in speech and action. No account should be taken of manners introduced by some Sufis that have no basis in religion, such as adhering to a particular style of dress or similar practices that Islamic law does not recommend. Proper manners are founded upon following the Sunnah. Nor should account be taken of the dismissal by some students of jurisprudence of prescribed manners which, owing to their limited knowledge, they believe are not part of Islamic manners merely because they are absent from the knowledge that has reached them or the books they have read. Rather, the standard for manners is what Islamic law has prescribed in speech, action and abstention, just as this is also the standard for obligatory duties and prohibitions.

The third condition for a Sufi is contentment with sufficient provision, such that he retains no worldly possessions beyond his needs. Anyone who accumulates surplus wealth is not among the Sufis for whom these stipends are intended, although such people may be permitted merely to reside in the lodges and similar places. Whoever possesses these three qualities is among the Sufis for whom the lodges and the endowments supporting them are intended.

Those above them—people of lofty spiritual stations and pure spiritual states, endowed with the realities of religion and divine gifts—are included within this general category. However, the endowment is not restricted to them, because they are few and because most people find it difficult to discern inward states. Entitlement to worldly support therefore cannot be made dependent on such states. Moreover, people of this kind may only rarely stay in the lodges.

Those who fall short of these qualities, confining themselves to mere outward forms in dress, gait and the like, are not entitled to the endowment and do not fall within the designation of Sufis, especially

---

## Page 15276

if those practices are innovations with no basis in the Sunnah. Spending money on such outward forms involves a kind of mockery of religion, wrongful consumption of people’s wealth, and turning away from Allah’s path.

Any of the aforementioned Sufis entitled to support who possesses some additional merit—such as diligence in voluntary worship, striving to rectify the states of the heart, seeking something of the a‘yān, or acquiring knowledge that constitutes a communal obligation—has greater entitlement than others. Anyone who does not observe the manners prescribed by Islamic law is entitled to nothing whatsoever. A student of knowledge whose needs are not fully met has greater entitlement than someone who neither observes those manners nor possesses knowledge. Indeed, the latter is entitled to nothing.

---

## Page 15277

What do the esteemed scholars say concerning the conditions customarily imposed by founders of endowments upon their beneficiaries? Some have an evident benefit and serve a desirable purpose, while others serve no substantial purpose for the founder and may impose hardship upon the beneficiary: fulfilling them causes him difficulty, while neglecting them leaves him fearing that he is sinning and receiving what is unlawful.

Examples include the founder of a lodge or school requiring beneficiaries to spend the night there, remain unmarried, perform the obligatory prayers in the lodge, or perform a specified recitation at a particular location; or requiring them to come from a particular city or tribe, or to follow a particular school of jurisprudence. Similar conditions are imposed upon those leading prayers in mosques, giving the call to prayer, and attending hadith readings in study circles at Sufi lodges.

Are these and similar conditions—which are generally permissible and serve some slight purpose for the founder—binding, such that no one may neglect any of them? Or are some binding while others are not? Which are binding, and which are not? What is the criterion for distinguishing between them?

He replied—may Allah sanctify his soul:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Religious activities stipulated in endowments—such as endowments for imams, muezzins, and those engaged in the pursuit of knowledge

---

## Page 15278

and the Quran, hadith, jurisprudence, and the like, or in worship or jihad in Allah’s cause—fall into three categories:

The first:
An act through which one draws near to Allah Most High: namely, obligatory and recommended deeds which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, encouraged and urged people to perform. Such a condition must be fulfilled, and entitlement to benefit from the endowment depends upon its fulfilment in general.

The second:
An act which the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbade, either as unlawful or as discouraged without being unlawful. Stipulating such an act is invalid by agreement of the scholars, for it is widely reported that the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed the people from his pulpit, saying:

“What is the matter with people who stipulate conditions that are not in Allah’s Book? Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in Allah’s Book, it is invalid, even if there are a hundred conditions. Allah’s Book has greater authority, and Allah’s condition is more binding.”

Although this hadith was occasioned by a stipulation assigning the rights of patronage arising from emancipation to someone other than the emancipator, what counts, according to the general body of scholars, is the general wording, not the particular occasion. There is scholarly consensus on applying this principle to this hadith. Any condition that necessarily entails something the Lawgiver has forbidden is treated like the forbidden act itself. Likewise, whatever is known from evidence in Islamic law to have been forbidden is treated like that which he is known to have expressly forbidden.

Scholars may, however, differ in their independent judgments concerning certain acts: do they fall within the category of what is forbidden? Their judgments concerning the condition will consequently differ on this basis. Such differences are inevitable within the Muslim community.

---

## Page 15279

This category also includes cases where what is stipulated is not unlawful in itself, but conflicts with achieving the prescribed purpose. Examples include requiring the residents of a ribāt to remain there permanently—a practice people have introduced which is discouraged in Islamic law; requiring jurists to subscribe to certain innovations contrary to the Quran and Sunnah, or to certain unlawful views; or requiring the imam and muezzin to omit some prescribed sunnah practices of prayer and the call to prayer, or to introduce innovations into them. For example, the imam might be required to recite the short surahs of the Mufassal in the dawn prayer, to append an unprescribed invocation to the call to prayer, or to hold the Eid prayer in the school and mosque while the Muslims are holding it in accordance with the Sunnah of their Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Requiring them to pray individually also falls into this category.

Also included in this category are conditions that necessarily encourage abandoning what the Lawgiver has recommended. An example is requiring the residents of a ribāt or school beside the main mosque to perform their obligatory prayers on their own premises. This encourages them to forgo performing the obligatory prayer in the manner most beloved to Allah and His Messenger. Such a condition should therefore be disregarded; rather, praying in the main mosque is better. Indeed, mosques established to cause harm must be demolished, though this is not the place to discuss that in detail.

This category also includes stipulating that lights be lit over graves, with candles, oil, and the like. For the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“May Allah curse women who frequently visit graves and those who set up

---

## Page 15280

mosques and lamps over them.” I know of no disagreement that building a mosque and lighting lamps over graves constitutes disobedience to Allah and His Messenger. Discussing these conditions in detail would take a very long time; here we mention only their general principles.

The third category:

An act that is neither discouraged nor recommended in Islamic law, but is merely permissible, with neither performing it nor refraining from it being preferable. Some scholars have held that fulfilling such a condition is obligatory. However, the majority of scholars, both those belonging to the well-known schools of law and others, hold that it is an invalid condition. In their view, a condition is valid only if it involves an act that brings one closer to Allah Most High.

This is because a person may spend his wealth only on something beneficial in religious or worldly affairs. As long as a person is alive, he may spend his wealth to attain permissible objectives, since he benefits from doing so. After death, however, he no longer benefits from the deeds of the living except through a righteous deed that he instructed others to perform or helped bring about, or whose reward has been gifted to him, and similar things. As for deeds that do not constitute obedience to Allah and His Messenger, the deceased derives no benefit whatsoever from them.

Thus, if a testator or the founder of an endowment stipulates an act or an attribute that carries no reward, striving to fulfil it amounts to striving for something that benefits him neither in this world nor in the Hereafter. This is not permissible. His purpose in establishing the endowment is only to draw closer to Allah Most High, and the Lawgiver knows better than the founders of endowments what brings one closer to Allah Most High. Their conditions must therefore be implemented in accordance with what Allah stipulates and approves concerning them.

---

## Page 15281

If the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“No prize may be offered except for archery, camel racing, or horse racing,”

and the jurists versed in hadith and their followers acted upon this hadith—thus he prohibited offering money for competitions except those that assist in jihad, which is an act of obedience to Allah Most High—then how can it be permissible to provide remuneration in perpetuity to someone who continually performs an act that is not obedience to Allah Most High?

This principle is well known among scholars, although people’s opinions and inclinations may differ concerning some of its applications. It is not possible here to elaborate on this general principle. Yet someone guided by Allah Most High will rarely fail to recognize what is intended in most cases. Scholars call principles of this kind “ascertaining the grounds for applying a ruling.” For example, they all require moral integrity in witnesses and oblige the provision of reasonable maintenance, and so forth.

Their juristic judgments may nevertheless differ concerning certain requirements: is a particular requirement necessary for moral integrity? They also differ over what constitutes reasonable maintenance. We shall mention an example that illustrates this.

As for a stipulation that the residents of a religious lodge or school perform the five obligatory prayers there, if this serves a purpose recognized by Islamic law, it is comparable to someone vowing to pray in a particular place. If specifying that place is a means of drawing closer to Allah, the vow must be fulfilled by praying there, unless one performs a prayer equivalent to that prayer or better; otherwise,

---

## Page 15282

he must fulfill the vow by praying, without being bound to the specified place. Generally, specifying the place serves no purpose recognized by Islamic law. If he has stipulated that they perform the five obligatory prayers there in congregation, the congregational requirement must be observed, since it serves a purpose recognized by Islamic law. Thus, anyone who does not pray in congregation has not fulfilled the valid condition. As for specifying the place, the preceding explanation applies.

As for stipulating celibacy and monasticism, the view more consistent with the Quran and Sunnah is that such a stipulation is never valid, whether imposed on scholars, worshippers, or those engaged in jihad. Most people experience sexual desire, and marriage, when they have the means, is either obligatory or recommended for them. If celibacy is stipulated for such people, this contradicts Islamic law.

If it is said that the condition of celibacy is intended only for someone for whom marriage is not recommended according to some scholars, then most young people fall outside this condition, even though they are generally the ones whose education is expected to yield benefit. It would therefore be as though he had said:

“I have dedicated this endowment to students from whom benefit is generally not expected.”

Yet:

“Whenever wealth came to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), he allotted two shares to a married man and one share to an unmarried man.”

How, then, can a married man be deprived of support?

He also said to his Companions who devoted themselves to learning and worship:

“Young men, whoever among you is able to marry should marry, for it is more effective in lowering the gaze and safeguarding chastity.”

---

## Page 15283

How, then, can those devoted to learning and worship be told, “Do not marry,” when the Lawgiver has recommended marriage and urged it? He also said:

“There is no monasticism in Islam.”

How, then, can a condition requiring monasticism be valid?

The notion that remaining unmarried is more helpful in countering Satan’s schemes, learning, and worship is mistaken and contrary to both Islamic law and reality. Rather, being married is more helpful in countering Satan’s schemes. Supporting those devoted to worship and learning is more beloved to Allah and His Messenger than supporting those among them who embrace monasticism. This is not the place to discuss the matter exhaustively.

Likewise, stipulating that imams and muezzins must come from a particular town or tribe is invalid. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) said:

“The people should be led in prayer by the one most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah. If they are equal in recitation, then by the one most knowledgeable about the Sunnah. If they are equal in knowledge of the Sunnah, then by the one who emigrated earliest. If they are equal in the time of their emigration, then by the oldest.”

Narrated by Muslim.

Mosques belong to Allah; they are built for His sake in the manner He has prescribed. If the founder of an endowment restricts the position of mosque imam to someone from a particular town, there may be someone from elsewhere who has a stronger claim to lead the prayer according to the conditions laid down by Allah and His Messenger. Observing the founder’s condition in this case would necessarily mean abandoning what Allah and His Messenger have commanded. Allah’s condition takes precedence and is more binding.

---

## Page 15284

As for the remaining conditions asked about, each requires a separate discussion for which this paper has insufficient space. We have, however, stated the governing principle. The believer in Allah must constantly consider whatever Allah and His Messenger love to see in people and strive to bring it about through endowments and other means. Whatever Allah and His Messenger dislike, he must strive to eliminate. As for what Allah neither dislikes nor loves, he should leave it aside and make neither entitlement to an endowment’s benefits nor exclusion from them—nor anything else—conditional upon it.

Allah, glorified and exalted, knows best what is correct. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bless our master Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace until the Day of Judgment.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“In a religious lodge, ten poor men reside. Within that lodge is a dwelling occupied by an unmarried woman, an ordinary woman. The founder of the endowment did not stipulate that she should have accommodation in that lodge, and she is not related to him. No one other than this woman lives in the dwelling, and its entrance lies behind the lodge’s outer door, which is closed upon it. Is she permitted to live among these resident poor men or not? Please give us a ruling.”

He answered:

“If the founder stipulated that only men should reside there, whether unmarried or married, she must be prevented from residing there in accordance with that condition. Likewise, a woman residing among men, or men residing among women, is prohibited as a matter of Allah’s right. Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 15285

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“An endowment has a trustee with lawful authority over it. Someone at the endowment acts without the trustee’s authorization, relying instead on authorization from one of the judges on the grounds that the judge has general supervisory authority. The trustee dismissed this administrator, but he continued to perform his duties after his dismissal. The judge asked the trustee to prevent this man from continuing his duties, then brought a claim against the trustee, which the latter denied. May the judge make an appointment without the lawful trustee’s authorization? May he himself adjudicate between himself and this trustee, who is his adversary, rather than another judge? If he wrongs the trustee, what punishment does he deserve for doing so?”

He answered:

“The judge may neither make appointments nor administer the endowment without authorization from the lawful trustee specifically responsible for it, unless that trustee has exceeded the proper bounds in his actions. The judge may intervene if the trustee departs from his obligations.

“If a dispute arises between the trustee and the judge, someone other than either of them must adjudicate between them according to the judgment of Allah and His Messenger. Whoever wrongs another must be held accountable for his wrongdoing: he is to be punished in kind if an equivalent punishment is possible; otherwise, he is to be punished in whatever manner is possible under Islamic law. Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 15286

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about two trustees:

“May they divide the property under their supervision so that each supervises only his own half?”

He answered:

“They may act only jointly in administering all the property entrusted to their supervision. If either were to act independently, that would be impermissible; how much less permissible, then, to divide what is a single whole! Islamic law provides for bringing together what is dispersed through partition and pre-emption. How, then, can what is united be divided?”

He was asked:

“Someone established an endowment and stipulated an allowance and a stipend for its trustee, just as he stipulated for the designated person and the jurists. Should the trustee be given priority in receiving his allotted remuneration or not?”

He answered:

“The wording mentioned contains nothing that entails giving him priority in receiving any remuneration. Rather, he is mentioned using the conjunction ‘and,’ which signifies shared inclusion and combination without specifying any order. If, however, there is independent evidence, apart from the aforementioned stipulation, establishing that preferential allocation and priority are permissible:

---

## Page 15287

such as his receiving remuneration for his work while being poor, as in the case of an orphan’s guardian—then that independent evidence from Islamic law is to be acted upon. Otherwise, the founder’s stipulation does not entail giving him priority. There is no difference between the stipend and the allowance. It is comparable to maintenance, funded from the endowment’s property, not from the trustee’s remuneration. And Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked:

“Someone established an endowment for a specified group of people, including some for whose duties the founder assigned a fixed remuneration. He authorized the trustee of this endowment to dismiss whomever he wished from among them, removing them without payment; to remove and replace whomever he wished; and to increase or reduce anyone’s allowance according to his judgment and choice and what he considered beneficial. The trustee then dismissed one of the designated individuals and replaced him with someone qualified to perform the duties for part of the remuneration assigned to that position, applying the remainder for the benefit of the endowment. May the trustee do this?

“If he dismisses one of the designated individuals for the endowment’s benefit, but that individual continues to receive the remuneration after learning of his dismissal, does he thereby become a transgressor, and must he return what he has taken? Must the trustee explain the benefit served by his decision?”

He answered:

“Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The trustee may do nothing concerning the endowment except in accordance with its best interests as determined by Islamic law. He must always pursue the most beneficial course available. If the founder authorizes the trustee to dismiss whomever he wishes and to increase or reduce anyone’s allowance,

---

## Page 15288

then this stipulation does not entitle him to act as he pleases or to follow conjecture and personal desires. Rather, it entitles him to take the best course for pleasing Allah and His Messenger.

This applies to everyone who acts on behalf of others by virtue of his authority, such as the ruler, the judge, the founder of an endowment, its trustee, and others. When it is said that he may choose between one course and another, or do whatever he wishes and considers appropriate, this is a choice governed by benefit, not by personal desire. What is meant is that he is not bound to take one particular course; rather, he may depart from it for one that is more beneficial and more pleasing to Allah and His Messenger. The founder himself stipulated: “According to his judgment and choice and what he considers beneficial.” All of this requires him to exercise his judgment and choice in accordance with Islamic law, pursuing the benefit recognized by it.

He may consider something beneficial while Allah and His Messenger command otherwise; in that case, it is not beneficial as he supposes. He may also choose what he desires rather than what pleases Allah, in which case his choice is to be disregarded. Even if the founder explicitly stipulated that the trustee could act entirely according to his desires and judgment, this stipulation would not be valid; rather, it would be void, because it contradicts the Book of Allah:

“Whoever stipulates a condition that is not in the Book of Allah, it is void, even if there are a hundred conditions. The Book of Allah takes precedence, and Allah’s condition is more binding.”

That being so, if the trustee’s dismissal of someone and appointment of a replacement accord with the command of Allah and His Messenger, neither the dismissed person nor anyone else may reject that decision, nor may the dismissed person take anything from the endowment

---

## Page 15289

in these circumstances. If, however, it does not accord with the command of Allah and His Messenger, it must be reversed as far as possible, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever does a deed that is not in accordance with our command, it is rejected.”

He also said:

“No created being may be obeyed in disobedience to the Creator.”

If they dispute whether what he has done is what was commanded, they must refer their dispute to Allah and His Messenger. If the trustee’s action is more pleasing to Allah and His Messenger, it shall stand. If the former arrangement is more pleasing, the trustee must maintain it. If a third course is more pleasing, it must be followed. The trustee must explain the benefit involved. If that benefit becomes evident, it must be pursued; if the course proves harmful, it must be rejected. If the matter remains unclear and the trustee is knowledgeable and upright, he may act according to his considered judgment. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man has a farm containing trees endowed for the poor, with the annual proceeds of their sale disbursed for their designated purposes. The trustee then leased the endowment to someone who damages it. There was a public drinking-water basin and an ablution facility for Muslims, but this tenant demolished the facility and the walls. Is that permissible or not?

He answered:

It is not permissible to lease endowed property to someone who damages it, by the consensus of the Muslims. Indeed, the trees may not be leased under any circumstances. Even if an agreement to water and tend them in return for a share of their yield is used as a legal device, this is not permissible with endowed property

---

## Page 15290

by scholarly consensus. Nor is it permissible to remove what Muslims have used for drinking water and purification. Rather, this unjust tenant who did so must receive a discretionary punishment and be required to compensate for the structures he destroyed. As for the monetary value and the trees, they are to be used to generate income in the customary manner, and the proceeds disbursed for their lawful purposes.

He was asked:

There are mosques and congregational mosques with endowments, caretakers, imams and muezzins. May the local judge allocate any of these funds to himself?

He answered:

Rather, these funds must be disbursed for their lawful purposes. The imams, muezzins and caretakers of congregational and other mosques must be paid from these funds what their counterparts are entitled to receive. Likewise, sufficient funds must be spent, within reasonable bounds, on furnishing and lighting the mosques. Any surplus may be spent on the needs of other mosques. It may also be spent on public interests, such as judges’ stipends, according to one of the two scholarly opinions. However, allocating these funds to judges while withholding provision for the mosques’ needs is not permissible. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15291

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man built a school and established an endowment to support its jurists and staff. The sultanate subsequently took most of the endowment, although the founder had stipulated proportional distribution among the beneficiaries. May the administrator pay the staff their full allocations and give the jurists whatever remains?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If the proportional shares received by those employed to perform services—such as the doorkeeper, caretaker, driver and others—equal the customary wages for their work, they are to be paid an additional amount. If their shares fall below the customary wages, but someone can be found to work for that amount, no increase is needed. If, however, their shares fall below the customary wages and no one can be found to work for less, their pay must be brought up to the customary level, provided that the institution cannot function properly without them.

If one person can serve as both caretaker and doorkeeper, or caretaker and muezzin, or can combine and fulfil all those duties, this should be done. The number of staff should not be increased unnecessarily, even though the endowment has returned to its former yield. Indeed, if one person can fill four positions, this should be done. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15292

He was asked about a hadith school whose founder stipulated in its endowment deed the following, which I have transcribed verbatim. He stated:

“Authority over the affairs of those belonging to the institution, in all their categories—including appointment and dismissal, granting and withholding payments, increasing and reducing them, and similar matters—rests with the institution’s shaykh. Likewise, oversight of its library and all similar or related matters rests with him. In any matter in which the endowment is under his charge, he may delegate responsibility to someone who will undertake it.”

He then stated:

“Oversight of the endowments and their financial affairs rests with the founder—may Allah multiply his reward—who may delegate it to whomever he wishes. Whoever receives this delegated authority assumes it by virtue of the stipulation made when the endowment was established. Thereafter, it passes to the Muslim judge in Damascus. He may also make payments from the endowment’s revenue to others, whether employees or otherwise, as circumstances require.”

If the endowment deed contains no further stipulations concerning oversight, does the wording of the aforementioned stipulation restrict the oversight assigned to the judge to a judge of one particular school of law? Or is it not restricted to a particular judge, but instead vested in whoever serves as a judge in Damascus, whichever of the four schools of law he follows? And if it is not

---

## Page 15293

restricted in this way, and one of the chief judges—may Allah strengthen them—in the protected city of Damascus delegates the aforementioned oversight to a qualified person, on the understanding that it is not restricted, may another judge prevent him from doing so, or obstruct some of what he has done, without grounds for objection?

He replied:

Nothing in the wording of the founder’s stipulation entails that oversight is exclusively reserved, without qualification, to a particular school of law. Such a restriction would mean that, if the town had only one judge, whose school differed from that followed by the town’s judge in the founder’s time, he would have no authority to oversee the endowment. This is invalid by the consensus of the Muslims.

Muslims have always established endowments, sometimes stipulating that oversight belongs to the judge and sometimes making no such stipulation in the endowment deed. That interpretation would nullify the operation of Islamic law concerning public endowments for which the ruler has appointed no specific administrator. As for private endowments, there is a well-known disagreement.

Moreover, the judge at the time an endowment is established may follow one school, while a later judge follows another. This has occurred in Iraq and elsewhere in the Muslim lands: chief judges have been appointed sometimes from the Hanafī school, sometimes from the Mālikī school, sometimes from the Shāfiʿī school, and sometimes from the Hanbalī school. Such a judge, in turn, sometimes appoints judges in outlying districts who follow his own school, and sometimes appoints those who follow a different school.

If the ruler stipulates that a judge must rule according to a particular school, or a judge imposes that stipulation upon his deputy, the stipulation is invalid. There are two juristic views on whether the appointment itself is thereby invalidated.

---

## Page 15294

There is no doubt that this applies where judges can and do rule with knowledge and justice without this condition. If, however, departing from that restriction would produce greater harm through ignorance and injustice than imposing it would, then this falls under averting the greater of two evils by accepting the lesser. Yet this does not entitle a founder to reserve oversight of the endowment permanently to a follower of one particular school, even where possible—unless someone from that school assumes office. How much less so when no such condition was stipulated!

For this reason, in some Muslim lands judges were required to rule only according to a particular school. In some lands, independent judges also came to be appointed, with general judicial authority throughout the territory. There is, however, well-known disagreement concerning each of these arrangements, and concerning which judge is to be designated when the two litigants disagree: should the nearest judge be designated, or should the choice be made by drawing lots? There is well-known disagreement on this. When the ruler adopts measures in these matters that admit of juristic reasoning, those measures take effect.

Accordingly, whatever school a judge follows, if his authority includes oversight of this endowment, his delegation of that oversight stands, and another judge may not overturn such a delegation—especially where entrusting it to that person serves the interests of the property and its beneficiaries in a way that entrusting it to someone else would not.

If two judges were each to appoint a different person, the ruler would be obliged to give precedence to the one more entitled to the office. For, by the consensus of the Muslims, someone whose competence and trustworthiness are known is to be preferred over someone who is not so qualified.

---

## Page 15295

He was asked:

When does the administrator of an endowment become entitled to his remuneration: from the time he is appointed, from the time the sultan enables him to take up his duties, or from the time he actually begins performing them?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The remuneration stipulated for the administrator is due in return for the work required of him. Whoever performs his required duties is entitled to his remuneration. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man leased endowed land for thirty years at the customary rent from its lawfully authorized administrator and had the lease authenticated before a judge. Over four years, he erected buildings and planted on the property. He then travelled while the property remained under his lease and was absent for eleven years. Upon returning, he found that someone else had taken possession of the land and claimed to have leased it through legally valid means. Is the original lessee entitled to remove this second occupant and demand from him the difference in rent?

---

## Page 15296

He replied:

If the second occupant leased the premises from someone who had no authority to lease them, while a valid lease remained in force, his lease is invalid. His possession is unlawful, and he is liable to eviction and removal.

If the second occupant leased the property and took possession while the first lessee’s lease remained in force, the first lessee has two options. He may rescind the lease, whereupon his liability for rent ceases from the time of rescission, and pursue a claim against the owners concerning their leasing the premises to this second occupant who has taken possession. They are to demand the customary rent from him if his lease is invalid; if it is valid, they are to demand that he rescind it. Alternatively, the first lessee may maintain his lease, pay the owners their rent, and demand the customary rent from the usurper from the time he took possession of the leased property.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A share in some shops was endowed for the benefit of a group of people, while another portion was endowed for another cause and belonged to others. The aforementioned founder stipulated that administration should belong to the eldest; if several were equal in age, they would share its administration.

The endowed property began to fall into ruin, so they leased it to the owners of the remaining share for thirty years, with part of the rent payable immediately and part deferred. They specified for the witnesses to the lease everything contained in the shops: timber, reeds, palm branches and walls, their length and breadth, and other particulars.

The witnesses recorded in the lease:

“So-and-so and so-and-so—the other two previously mentioned—acknowledged receiving the rent payable immediately in full; and those of the same rank.”

The lessee died, and there passed

---

## Page 15297

to others whatever part of the property he had owned. The lease expired, and the aforementioned endowment passed to the second generation of beneficiaries.

May the second generation take possession of the aforementioned shops in their present condition, given that the other two acknowledged receiving the rent payable immediately in order to spend it on repairing the endowment and restoring it to its former condition? Or must they produce evidence that the aforementioned two, upon receiving the rent, spent it on repairs? Or may the lessees, or those to whom the lessee’s property has passed, refuse, in these circumstances, to hand over the aforementioned share of the shops except in its original condition?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah. Rather, the buildings on the jointly owned plot are likewise in the joint possession of the plot’s owners by virtue of their co-ownership, unless one of them produces legally valid proof that the buildings belong exclusively to him. A mere claim by one of the co-owners of the plot that the buildings belong exclusively to him is not accepted, whether the plot comprises an endowed share and a share held outright, two shares held outright, or two endowed shares.

The lessee’s possession extends only to the usufruct. The lease alone does not establish his claim to ownership of the buildings; he must produce proof of that. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15298

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man acknowledged, ten days before his death, that the entire shop and the assets within it were an endowment devoted to charitable purposes and acts of devotion, with the rent and reward allocated accordingly from a date twenty years before this acknowledgment. He acted in accordance with the stipulation in his acknowledgment. After his death, the imam appointed an administrator, then appointed another without dismissing the first. One of the two administrators spent some of the endowment’s income on legally establishing the endowment, in the amount customarily spent on establishing such an endowment, without distributing anything to those entitled to its income.

Must this fee be paid from the income or from the estate of the deceased who acknowledged the aforementioned endowment? If the endowed property cannot be leased because it is occupied by the heirs’ belongings, must the heirs pay rent for that period? Does the earlier rent owed by the deceased on the basis of his acknowledgment concerning the earlier period lapse, and may it be recovered from his estate? If an administrator is appointed and then another is appointed, does this dismiss the first without an express declaration of dismissal, or do they share the administration? If the witnesses know that the sum is owed by the deceased’s estate, is it permissible for them to conceal this or not?

He replied:

The cost of legally establishing the endowment and attending to its interests is not payable from the estate of

---

## Page 15299

the deceased, since everything beyond what he acknowledged belongs to the heirs. They are required only to relinquish possession of that property and give the administrator access to it. There is no obligation to undertake those efforts or pay their cost.

As for the property acknowledged as an endowment, if the heirs use it or retain possession of it in a manner that prevents its rightful use, they must pay rent for that use, according to the positions of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others who hold that liability for usurped property extends to its usufruct. The disagreement on this issue is well known. The deceased’s acknowledgment that it had been an endowment since the earlier date does not explicitly establish that his possession of it constituted usurpation, and liability cannot be imposed on the basis of mere possibility.

As for appointing one administrator after another, reference must be made to the customary practice governing endowments of this kind and comparable cases. If this customarily constitutes revocation of the earlier appointment, then it is a revocation. The same applies if the wording of the appointment entails that the second administrator alone is to exercise authority. Otherwise, you are already familiar with the issue: if someone bequeaths a particular property to one person and then bequeaths it to another, does this constitute revocation of the first bequest or not?

Whatever rightful claim the witnesses know of, if their testimony will secure it for its rightful claimant, they must not conceal that testimony. If, however, there is someone who is not entitled to it, and it will not reach the person entitled to it, they are not obliged to designate either of the two. If the person who took it did so on the basis of an interpretation and independent legal judgment, they are likewise not obliged to remove it from his possession. Rather, one acting on an interpretation and independent legal judgment is to be supported against one who has neither.

---

## Page 15300

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about the wording of an endowment deed, which reads:

“This is what Āmir ibn Yūsuf ibn Āmir has endowed for his children, Alī, Tarīfah, and Zubaydah, to be divided among them according to the shares prescribed by Islamic law; then for their children after them; then for their children’s children; then for their children’s children’s children; then for their descendants and posterity after them, however far down the line of descent, all according to the shares prescribed by Islamic law.

“This is subject to the condition that, if any of their aforementioned children, their children’s children, or their descendants and posterity after them dies leaving a child, a grandchild, descendants, or posterity, however far down the line of descent, the share endowed for that person shall pass to his child, grandchild, descendants, and posterity after him, however far down the line of descent, all according to the shares prescribed by Islamic law. If any of them dies leaving no child, grandchild, descendants, or posterity, however remote, the share endowed for that person shall pass to those beneficiaries of the endowment who belong to the same generation and degree of descent as him, according to the shares prescribed by Islamic law; then to purposes he specified in the endowment deed.”

The learned scholars are requested to examine the aforementioned founder’s stipulation. He then died leaving two daughters, who received what passed to them from him. Then one of them died leaving a son and a granddaughter through a son.

---

## Page 15301

Do they, then, share her portion, or is the son alone entitled to it, to the exclusion of the son’s daughter?

The aforementioned son then died leaving a son. Is he alone entitled to what his father had been receiving, to the exclusion of the son’s daughter? Does the founder’s aforementioned stipulation require one entire generation to succeed another, or individuals to succeed their respective predecessors?

He replied:

Where the wording is unqualified, there are two well-known views on this question among the jurists of Imam Ahmad’s school and other schools. The stronger view, however, is that it establishes individual succession: a grandchild takes the place his father would have occupied had he been alive and entitled. This applies whether the son survived the grandfather and became entitled; survived but did not become entitled because of an impediment affecting him, because he did not accept the endowment, or for some other reason; or did not survive the grandfather, but died during his lifetime.

On this interpretation, one plural expression is set against another, which requires each individual in one group to correspond to the respective individual in the other. Thus, Allah says:

“You shall have half of what your wives leave.”

That is, each man receives half of what his own wife leaves. Likewise, His words:

“You are forbidden to marry your mothers.”

That is, each man is forbidden to marry his own mother. Other expressions follow the same pattern.

Similarly, the founder’s words, “for their children, then for their children’s children,” mean that each succeeds after his own father’s death. In the present case, moreover, the founder explicitly stipulated that, when someone dies leaving a child, his share passes to his child. This expressly establishes individual succession, leaving no room for disagreement on this point in the present case.

The only uncertainty concerns a child who dies during his father’s lifetime, leaving a child of his own. The father then dies leaving another child and the child of his first child: do these two share, or is the former alone entitled to the portion?

---

## Page 15302

The stronger view in this case is that the two share. For if the intention is that each child becomes entitled after his father’s death—whether his paternal uncle is alive or dead—then such wording makes entitlement conditional upon the father’s lack of entitlement. This is comparable to what the jurists say regarding the order of agnatic heirs: the son, then his son, then the father, then his father, then the paternal uncle, then the paternal uncle’s sons, and so forth.

The only condition for the second rank is that the first should have no entitlement. Whenever members of the second rank exist and those of the first have no entitlement, the second becomes entitled, whether or not the first had previously been entitled. Entitlement at the second rank does not require prior entitlement at the first. This is because the second generation receives its entitlement to the endowment from the founder, not from that second generation itself. It is not like property inherited by a son and then passed on to his son. Rather, it resembles the bond of manumission, by virtue of which one inherits: if the emancipator’s son dies during the emancipator’s lifetime, his son’s son inherits the entitlement arising from that bond.

Those who err in cases of this kind do so because they suppose that the second generation receives its entitlement from the preceding one, so that if the first was not entitled to anything, neither is the second. They therefore suppose that if a father dies before becoming entitled, his son has no entitlement. This is not so; rather, they receive their entitlement from the founder.

This holds even if the first generation is excluded by an impediment. For example, the founder may stipulate that the beneficiaries must be poor, scholars, upright people, or meet some other condition. If the father does not meet the stipulated condition but his son does, the son becomes entitled even though his father did not. Likewise, if he dies

---

## Page 15303

before becoming entitled, his son becomes entitled. The same applies to every order of priority in child custody, guardianship over marriage and property, inheritance through agnatic kinship or the bond of manumission, and all other matters in which those entitled are arranged in successive ranks and degrees: the principle is as explained above.

This is what founders of endowments intend when asked about their meaning. Those who state their intention explicitly explain that the grandchild is to receive what the founder’s child would have received had he been alive. This is especially so because people feel compassion for someone whose father has died and who has inherited nothing; a grandfather may even make a bequest to his grandchild. It is well known that this grandchild and the other son’s child stand in the same relationship to the grandfather. How, then, could he deprive his orphaned grandchild while giving to his grandchild who is not an orphan? No reasonable person would intend this.

If we do not hold that they share, the endowment remains confined to this son and his offspring, excluding the descendants of the son who died during his father’s lifetime. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15304

He was asked:

A woman established an endowment to support her tomb after her death. She allocated a specified amount to Quran reciters, with any surplus going to the poor or charitable causes. She has a relative—her maternal uncle—who has become impoverished and needy and is no longer in service. The administrator has not provided him with enough to sustain him. Must the administrator be compelled to provide for this relative’s subsistence and meet his needs in preference to others?

He answered:

If the woman who established the endowment has a needy relative, such as a maternal uncle or the like, he has a greater claim than an unrelated poor person whose need is equal to his, and he should be given priority. If the endowment has sufficient resources to meet his needs, they should be met from it.

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Concerning endowments in a town dedicated to various institutions and purposes: schools, mosques, Sufi lodges, congregational mosques, hospitals, hospices, charitable assistance, and ransoming captives from the hands of the unbelievers. Some have their own administrator, while others have an administrator appointed by

---

## Page 15305

the ruler. The ruler has established an office for each of these categories to safeguard its endowments and distribute their proceeds to their designated purposes. The administrator considers it appropriate to appoint an auditor specifically for these transactions—meaning all the endowments—to examine their accounts, review the actions of the administrators and managing officials, verify the sums spent and remaining for which they must account, and maintain records of this. This would safeguard the endowments’ funds as they pass through different hands and managing officials are replaced. His oversight evidently encourages some officials to exercise care, producing a benefit. May the ruler do this if he considers it beneficial, or not?

If he now proceeds with this because he considers it beneficial, appoints the aforementioned auditor, and assigns him a small fixed stipend charged to each of these endowments—far below the regular income of any of their managing officials—because of the benefit he sees in it, is that permissible? Is the aforementioned auditor entitled to receive his stipulated remuneration if he performs his duties?

If his duty is to obtain each year’s accounts in accordance with the procedures followed by the scribes, and he finds accounts submitted for several years or more, which he then processes in fulfilment of his duties, is he entitled to remuneration for the period whose accounts he obtained and examined in the performance of those duties?

He answered:

Yes. The ruler may establish an office to audit the accounts of endowed funds when this serves a beneficial purpose, just as he may establish offices to audit state revenues, such as fay’ revenues and the like. He may also assign the auditor remuneration for his work commensurate with what someone in his position deserves:

---

## Page 15306

from each fund he administers, in proportion to the size of that fund. Auditing accounts and keeping accurate records of receipts and expenditure are duties with an established basis in the words of the Most High:

“and those employed to administer it”

And in an authentic hadith:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, appointed a man to collect zakat, and when he returned, he examined his accounts.”

This establishes a basis for auditing officials working in different places. The central auditor acts as the ruler’s deputy in examining their accounts. Where funds are numerous and their officials must be audited, a central office is indispensable. Thus, when revenues increased during the time of the Commander of the Faithful, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, he established administrative offices: the land-tax office, which was the office for personnel employed on stipends, and placed ʿUthmān ibn Hunayf in charge of it; and the expenditure office, which handled disbursements to fighting men and their descendants and resembled the office of al-habs and al-thubūtāt and similar offices in our own time. He placed Zayd ibn Thābit in charge of it.

Likewise, those in authority—the ruler, the judge, and others like them—must administer endowed funds according to valid conditions consistent with the Book of Allah, and appoint officials for work to which the endowment administrator has appointed none. In the terminology of Islamic law, an official includes the person called an administrator, as well as others charged with collecting money from those who owe it and disbursing and delivering it to those entitled to it, in accordance with His words:

“Indeed, Allah commands you to render trusts to whom they are due.”

The appointment of a central auditor to oversee officials working in different places depends on need and benefit. It may be obligatory if the proper collection and disbursement of funds cannot be achieved without it, for that without which

---

## Page 15307

an obligation cannot be fulfilled is itself obligatory. Such an official may be unnecessary when the workload is light and the ruler personally audits the accounts. The same applies to the ruler’s appointment of a judge: he must appoint one when need and public benefit require it, if rights cannot otherwise reach those entitled to them, or obligations cannot be fulfilled and prohibited acts avoided. The ruler may dispense with a judge if he can adjudicate personally.

{For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, personally adjudicated disputes and examined accounts.}

In places distant from him, he appointed someone to undertake these duties. When the population under Muslim rule increased during the time of Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, and the other caliphs, they appointed judges and established administrative offices in their garrison cities and elsewhere. Thus, ʿUmar appointed Zayd ibn Thābit as his deputy in Medina to oversee judicial affairs and the administrative office.

In Kufa, he appointed ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir to lead the prayers and command the army—combining the roles of the ruler’s deputy and the preacher—for the established sunnah was that the military commander would lead the people in prayer. He appointed ʿAbd Allah ibn Masʿūd to oversee judicial affairs and the public treasury, and ʿUthmān ibn Hunayf to oversee the land-tax office.

When the central auditor performs his assigned duties, he is entitled to the stipend allocated to him and any remuneration he was lawfully permitted to stipulate. If he performs this work but is not paid his remuneration, he may seek payment for the particular work, for whatever becomes due through a transaction must be paid.

---

## Page 15308

He was asked:

A man leased plots of endowed land and planted trees on them, which bore fruit. Once the lease term had elapsed, the trustees of the endowment wished to uproot the trees. Are they entitled to do so, or are they entitled to the fair rental value? Will the ruler be rewarded for assisting him?

He replied:

Those responsible for the land are not entitled to uproot the trees. Rather, they may demand the fair rental value, acquire the trees at their assessed value, or compensate for their loss in value if they are uprooted. As long as the trees remain, their owner must pay the fair rental value. The ruler must prevent the wrongdoer from committing injustice. Allah knows best.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man has served for several years as the imam and preacher of a mosque and as the administrator of its endowment, by decree of the ruler. He is entitled to remuneration by virtue of his lawful appointment. May the trustees of another endowment take control of this endowment or administer it without this administrator, spend the mosque’s funds on purposes other than those designated for them, or withhold the remuneration allocated to him for these duties? Even supposing that this endowment had been registered with their administrative office for some time, and then

---

## Page 15309

the ruler removed it from their administration and entrusted it to the imam and preacher, are they entitled, in these circumstances, to administer it and prevent him from doing so while his appointment remains in force? If someone unlawfully administers it and diverts some of its funds to someone else, while the imam is in need and the mosque’s services are being maintained, and persists in doing so under these circumstances, does this impugn his religious commitment and uprightness or not?

He replied:

No administrator other than the one entrusted with this endowment may take control of it or administer it without his permission—neither the trustees of another endowment nor anyone else—whether or not they previously administered it. Nor may they, in the circumstances described, spend the mosque’s funds on purposes other than those for which they were endowed. Rather, the imam and others must be paid their entitlements in full. Their entitlements must not be reduced in order to divert the resulting surplus to another endowment, for there is no disagreement that this is impermissible.

The scholars differed only over whether surplus funds may be transferred. Even those who permitted this did not allow anyone other than the appointed administrator to act independently in doing so. Whoever persists in disbursing funds to someone not entitled to them while withholding them from the rightful recipient thereby impugns his religious commitment and uprightness.

---

## Page 15310

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A person established an endowment for poor Muslims. May its administrator, in these circumstances, distribute all its income to three people or not? If he may distribute it to three people, and the founder has a poor relative whose poverty and entitlement to receive from this endowment have been established, may that relative receive a share in place of one of the three beneficiaries unrelated to the founder?

If he may receive a share, should he be given priority over the two unrelated beneficiaries? If he should be given priority, may the administrator, in these circumstances, give the founder’s relative enough from the endowment to meet his needs? If that is permissible, is doing so preferable and better than giving him less than he needs and distributing the difference to an unrelated person in these circumstances?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The administrator of an endowment must exercise his best judgment in distributing its proceeds, giving priority to those most entitled, in descending order. If a benefit recognized by Islamic law requires distributing it to three people—for example, because any less would not suffice for them—he should not include other poor people alongside them. If it suffices for them and for other poor people, he should include those others and treat them equally in distributing the income it yields. They have a stronger claim than he does when claims compete, and in similar circumstances. And the relatives of

---

## Page 15311

the founder, when poor, have priority over unrelated poor people whose needs are equal to theirs. The administrator may give such a relative enough to meet his needs if no one has a stronger claim. If a poor person should be in dire need, relieving that need is obligatory. If this can only be done by reducing what those others receive from this endowment to meet their needs, without thereby leaving them in dire need, then that must be done. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man appointed a person of influence to oversee an endowment comprising mosques, lodges, and other institutions, relying on his religious commitment and knowing that he intended to serve their best interests. Upon taking office, he found that these endowments were not being administered properly and were subject to interference. Direct involvement in their administration was considered undesirable, lest their funds become an object of greed. Those involved pay no attention to disbursing the funds to their rightful recipients. They include the judge, the preacher, the imam of the congregational mosque, and others, who draw upon the endowment as a whole. Despite his diligent and strenuous efforts, he is unable to prevent interference with these endowments.

Is it permissible for the questioner to withdraw from their administration and cease attending to such of their interests as he can, knowing that, where remuneration is involved, interference with them and covetousness for their funds increase? Is it permissible for him to receive payment from them for his work, given that he has a family to support and cannot obtain their sustenance from any other source? And is it permissible for the administrator, if he finds a place in ruins, to spend

---

## Page 15312

its funds on another institution’s needs when he is certain that restoring it cannot conceivably serve its intended purpose? If any of its property is surplus to the needs of the institution under his charge, may he devote it to another important purpose and to necessary repairs that might preserve it, given the frequent encroachments upon it, or not?

He replied:

The underlying principle here is that whatever Allah has required of obedience to Him and fear of Him is conditional upon ability. As He, Most High, says:

“So fear Allah as much as you are able.”

And as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“When I command you to do something, do as much of it as you can.”

Accordingly, when benefits and harms conflict, Islamic law requires securing the greater of two benefits by forgoing the lesser, and tolerating the lesser of two harms to avert the greater. Thus, if serious harm to these endowed funds and their lawful disbursement can only be prevented through what has been mentioned—tolerating a lesser harm—then that is what Islamic law requires. If this becomes this man’s individual duty, he may not abandon it unless undertaking it involves harm or conflicts with a more pressing obligation.

By the consensus of the Muslims, he may receive remuneration for his work in administering them when he is in need. Indeed, some have permitted this even when he is well-off, just as Allah, Most High, has permitted those employed to administer zakat to receive payment even when they have no need of it.

If an endowed property falls into ruin and ceases to provide any benefit, it is to be sold and its price spent on a comparable property, or it may be transferred to a comparable one. Likewise, if any of the places supported by an endowment fall into ruin

---

## Page 15313

—such as a mosque or the like—to the point that restoration is impossible, the income from the endowment supporting it is to be directed elsewhere. Any endowment income exceeding its intended needs is to be spent on a comparable purpose or for the benefit of Muslims in its locality; the money must not be retained indefinitely without benefit. Every year, Umar ibn al-Khattāb used to distribute the Kaaba’s covering among the pilgrims. The same applies to mosque mats and similar furnishings that are no longer needed. He also ordered the mosque of Kufa to be moved from one location to another, until its original site became a marketplace.

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him—about an endowment established for the ashrāf (members of the Prophet’s noble family), and the statement that certain people are relatives:

“Are these relatives among the ashrāf or not? May they receive anything from the endowment or not?”

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If the endowment is for the household of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, or for some members of his household—such as the descendants of Alī or Fātimah, or the descendants of Abū Tālib, who include the descendants of Jaʿfar and Aqīl—or for the descendants of al-Abbās and the like, then only those whose lineage is genuine and established are entitled to receive from it.

As for someone who claims to belong to them but whose membership has not been established, or who is known not to belong to them, he is not entitled to receive from this endowment, even if he claims otherwise. Such are the descendants of Abd Allāh ibn Maymūn al-Qaddāh, for the people of

---

## Page 15314

learning who are versed in genealogy, as well as others, know that their claimed descent is not genuine. Groups of scholars specializing in jurisprudence, hadith, speculative theology, and genealogy have testified to this, and formal judicial records have established it. It is mentioned in major Muslim works; indeed, it is established among scholars through mass transmission.

The same applies when someone establishes an endowment for the “ashrāf,” since customary usage includes under this designation only those whose descent from the household of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is genuine. If, however, someone establishes an endowment for the descendants or relatives of a particular person, or the like, and nothing in its terms indicates that it is intended for the Prophet’s household, and the endowed property belongs to the founder and may validly be endowed for that particular person’s descendants, then the descendants of Hāshim are not included in this endowment.

He was also asked—may Allah sanctify his soul—about a man who holds a mosque appointment under written authorizations to revive a practice prescribed by Islamic law, following the previous holder’s relinquishment of the appointment through a written instrument validated by the judges. The son of the man who originally held the appointment then challenged its present holder and demanded to share it with him. He had no basis in Islamic law other than the fact that his father had held it. May the present holder be compelled to share the appointment without his consent?

---

## Page 15315

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. In these circumstances, it is not permissible to compel the imam of a mosque to share his appointment, to appoint the two men jointly, or to dismiss him merely on the grounds mentioned—that the other man’s father was the imam. Mosque appointments must be given to those most entitled to them under Islamic law: those most proficient in reciting the Book of Allah, most knowledgeable about the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and having precedence in righteous deeds—for example, by having emigrated earlier or being older. How much more so when the person most entitled to the appointment already holds it! The scholars agree that he may not be dismissed. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a school endowed for jurists and students of jurisprudence of a specified group, to provide them with accommodation and a place to pursue their studies: is accommodation restricted to those receiving stipends? Is it permissible to evict a resident who belongs to the category for which the endowment was established?

He replied:

Accommodation and receipt of a stipend are not restricted to a single individual. It is permissible to reside there without receiving a stipend from the funds, just as it is permissible to receive a stipend without residing there. No member of either category may be deprived of his entitlement without a valid reason under Islamic law—provided the resident is engaged in study—whether or not he attends the lesson.

---

## Page 15316

He—may Allah have mercy upon him—was asked:

About a man who transferred to another person ownership of property standing on land endowed for the benefit of the aforementioned person and others during his lifetime, and, after his death, for his children and any further children Allah might grant him, male and female, in equal shares, on the following conditions:

If any of them dies leaving a child, his share of the endowment shall pass to his child, or his grandchild or more remote descendants, whether one or more, male or female, descended through sons or daughters. Males and females shall receive equal shares. If he dies leaving no child, grandchild or more remote descendant, his share shall pass to those of the same degree of kinship, in addition to their entitlement to the income of this endowment. If he has neither a brother nor a sister, nor anyone of the same degree of kinship, his share shall pass to his nearest relatives, in order of proximity, through both male and female lines. The higher generation shall exclude the lower generation, with descendants through male and female lines treated equally, until they have all died out.

If no one remains whose lineage goes back to the beneficiary, whether through the father or through the daughter, the income of the endowment shall be allocated to the poor and needy in the protected frontier port of Damietta, those arriving there and those who frequent it. The administrator shall distribute it as he sees fit. Thereafter, it shall be allocated to Muslim captives.

---

## Page 15317

One of the daughters among the beneficiaries of the original endowment died without leaving any children, and her siblings received her share. Then the second daughter died, leaving two daughters, who received her share. After that, the third daughter died without leaving any children, and her sister received her share. Then the fourth sister died, and they took the two-thirds share on her behalf. Are his maternal aunt’s children entitled to a share alongside him, or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The first daughter’s share passed to her three siblings, as stipulated by the founder of the endowment. The children of one sister do not share with the children of the other in the original share that belonged to the mother of those other children. As for the reallocated share—the share that belonged to the third sister and passed to the fourth—the children of both sisters share in it, just as their mother shares in it. This is the stronger of the two opinions on this question.

Another opinion holds that everything the fourth sister received—her own share and the third sister’s share—passes exclusively to her children, because the founder stipulated:

“If he dies leaving no child, grandchild or more remote descendant, his share shall pass to those of the same degree of kinship, in addition to their entitlement to the income of the endowment.”

Those who hold this opinion argue that the added share has the same status as the share to which it is added. Since the latter passes to his children, so does the former, because the founder’s stipulation, “If any of them dies leaving a child, his share of the endowment shall pass to his child,” encompasses both the original and the reallocated shares.

The first opinion, however, is stronger, for his words, “his share shall be,” encompass the share

---

## Page 15318

previously mentioned. Whether it also encompasses what came afterward is doubtful, and mere doubt does not justify including it. Indeed, it may be argued:

This was originally the share of the deceased from whom it passed, as the founder stated. The founder’s wording and customary usage evidently indicate that he treated members of the same generation equally with regard to the share of someone who had a child. The other beneficiary received it because he belonged to that same generation, and his children belong to the same generation as the children of the first deceased. Just as the two deceased beneficiaries would have shared in this reallocated share had they both been alive, so their children share in it after them, for both sets of children bear the same relationship to its original holder. This is what people intend by such stipulations, as their customary usage and practice attest.

The aim is to administer the endowment according to the stipulations intended by its founder. Hence the jurists said: “His provisions are like the texts of the Lawgiver”—meaning in how they are understood and what they signify. Their intended meaning may thus be understood in various ways, just as the Lawgiver’s intent is understood. Anyone who examines the circumstances of endowment founders knows that this is what they intend, for it is more consistent with justice. The grandchildren all bear the same relationship to the founder, so he has no purpose in giving one beneficiary’s son two or three shares merely because his father died later, while the others receive only one share. This is especially so since the beneficiary who died later had already received income from the endowment and may have left his children some of that income, whereas the one who died first received only a little of it, so his children are closer to

---

## Page 15319

need, although both groups stand in the same relationship to the founder. How, then, can those in greater need be given precedence over those in lesser need, when—after that generation has died out—both groups are equally closely related to the founder and to the deceased holder of the share? This is just as if the holder of that share had died last: had he died last, all the children would have shared in it.

Indeed, this case is covered by the founder’s stipulation:

“If he dies leaving neither a child nor a grandchild, his share shall be allocated to those of his own degree of kinship.”

If he has neither a brother nor a sister, nor anyone of the same degree of kinship, his share is to be allocated to his nearest relatives—and they are all equally closely related to him. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15320

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A founder established an endowment for his children, then his children’s children, then his children’s children’s children, and his descendants and posterity, in perpetuity for as long as they continue to have descendants, stipulating:

“If any of them dies leaving neither a child nor a grandchild, nor descendants nor posterity, whatever he had been receiving from this endowment shall pass to those of his degree of kinship and generation.”

If one of these beneficiaries dies leaving a child, a grandchild, descendants or posterity, to whom does his share belong? Does it belong to his child, or to those of his degree of kinship, such as his brothers, paternal cousins and the like?

He replied:

His share passes to his child, not to his brothers or paternal cousins, for several reasons, three of which we shall mention.
