The first is that the founder’s statement, “For his children, then his children’s children,” is restricted by the qualification that follows it, namely:

“On condition that, if any of them dies leaving no child, his share shall pass to those of his generation.”

Whenever a statement is joined to something that restricts it, that restriction must be taken into account, rather than treating the opening words as unrestricted.

To explain the first premise: this clause—“On condition that, if any of them dies”—functions syntactically as a circumstantial clause in the accusative position. A circumstantial clause conveys a qualifying description, and a qualifying description restricts what it describes.

---

## Page 15321

And, if you wish, you may say: because it is a prepositional phrase attached to the verb, and a prepositional phrase functions as an object in negation, thereby restricting the verb.

Or, if you wish, you may say: because it is a statement that does not stand independently and must therefore be joined to what precedes it.

Or, if you wish, you may say: because the speaker did not stop at the initial statement, but joined further words to it, and that continuation restricts it. All these principles are self-evident in every language.

To explain the second premise: whenever a statement is joined to a qualifying description, a condition, or any other wording that changes what it would entail if left unrestricted, that wording must be given effect. It is impermissible to detach the statement from the qualifications joined to it. This, too, is undisputed among jurists—indeed, among all rational people.

Upon all this rest the rulings relating to the statements of legally accountable persons in matters of worship and transactions, such as endowments, bequests, acknowledgments, sales, gifts, pledges, leases, partnerships, and other matters.

Hence the jurists say: the founder’s wording determines whether an endowment is unrestricted or restricted.

Thus, if the opening words are unrestricted or general, and the speaker joins to them something that limits their scope or qualifies them, effect must be given to that qualification or limitation. If, for example, he says, “I establish an endowment for my children,” this is general. But if he adds “the poor,” “the upright,” or “the males,” the endowment is confined to them, even though his opening words were general.

No one may say: “The word ‘children’ is general, and specifically mentioning one of the two categories does not exclude the other from the ruling.” Rather, all rational people unanimously agree that he

---

## Page 15322

has confined the ruling to those specifically designated at the end of his statement. Both those who affirm implication and those who deny it call this “connected specification.” They say: when the general wording is joined to a particular description, the ruling becomes attached exclusively to that description, and those who do not meet it are excluded from the ruling.

For those who deny implication, this is because such people would have no entitlement unless they were included in the wording. When the general wording was joined to the particular description, it excluded them from its scope. They therefore were not included and have no entitlement. Thus, they deny their entitlement because there is no basis for it.

Those who affirm implication exclude them on this ground and on another: specifically mentioning one of the two categories indicates an intention to confine the ruling to it, and intending to confine the ruling to them necessarily entails denying it to others. Thus, they deny their entitlement both because its basis is absent and because an impediment to it exists.

The same applies if he qualifies unrestricted wording, as when he says: “I establish an endowment for my children, on condition that they receive distributions if they are poor”; or “on condition that they are entitled if they are poor”; or “I establish an endowment for my children, on condition that distributions from the endowment are made to those of them who are living if they are poor”; or “I establish an endowment on condition that whoever is poor shall be among its beneficiaries.”

This is equivalent to saying: “I establish an endowment for my children—for the poor among them”; or “on condition that they are poor”; or “if he is poor.”

Likewise, if he says: “I establish an endowment for my daughters, on condition that any daughter who is unmarried shall receive a distribution, and any daughter who marries and is then divorced by her husband shall receive a distribution,” this is equivalent to saying: “I establish an endowment for

---

## Page 15323

my daughters—for the unmarried among them.” For the preposition ‘alā (“on condition that”) is one of the expressions used to stipulate a condition, as Allah says:

“I wish to marry you to one of these two daughters of mine, on condition that you serve me for eight years.”

The jurists agree that if someone says, “I give you my daughter in marriage for a thousand,” or “on condition that you give her a thousand,” or “on condition that you owe her a thousand,” this constitutes a binding stipulation, and the specified dower is valid. There is no disagreement about this. Whoever believes that there is disagreement on this point in the school of Imam Ahmad or any other imam is mistaken. This misconception arises from their disagreement concerning someone who says to his wife, “You are divorced in return for a thousand,” or to his slave, “You are free in return for a thousand,” when the wife or slave does not accept. According to one of the two reports from Ahmad, the emancipation and divorce take effect. The basis for this is not that this expression does not convey a condition.

For there is no disagreement within his school that if someone says, “I release you from the marriage in return for a thousand,” “I enter into an emancipation contract with you for a thousand,” “I contract your marriage for a thousand,” “I sell you this slave on condition that you pledge such-and-such to me as security for its price,” “on condition that Zayd guarantees its payment,” or “I give you my daughter in marriage on condition that you are a free man,” these are valid stipulations. There is no disagreement about this among any of the jurists.

Rather, the reasoning is that emancipation and divorce do not require compensation, and that he has not made the divorce contingent upon a condition, but has merely attached a stipulation to it. A distinction is drawn between making an act contingent upon a condition and attaching a stipulation to a declaration that takes immediate effect. Thus, many legal transactions are invalid when made contingent upon a condition, although attaching stipulations to them is valid. This qualifying condition cannot be fulfilled, and the divorce

---

## Page 15324

thus qualified—if its qualifying condition is absent, does it fail altogether, or does it take effect without that qualification? There is disagreement on this.

Once it is clear that his statement, “on condition that whoever among them dies,” constitutes a legally operative condition and a substantive qualification of the aforementioned endowment, and that it must be taken into account and acted upon, then the following is evident: taking this qualification into account makes the transfer of the deceased’s share to those of his generation conditional upon his leaving no child, and the founder did not assign the deceased’s share to them in this circumstance. It is then evident that the deceased’s share may not be allocated to them in the opposite circumstance, namely, when he leaves a child. This is precisely the point to be established.

It is also clear that this is agreed upon by the jurists—indeed, by rational people, whether they accept or reject arguments from implication. Allocating an endowment to anyone other than those to whom its founder assigned it is forbidden, and he did not assign it to them. This prohibition, based on the absence of grounds for entitlement, is unanimously accepted. Another ground is that he has prohibited its allocation to them; this prohibition, based on the existence of a preventive factor, is disputed.

The wording thus amounts to:

“I establish this endowment for my children, then for their children, on condition that the share of any deceased beneficiary passes to those of his generation if he dies leaving no child.”

No jurist disputes the restrictive effect of all the stated qualifications in this area. The only person who errs here is one who has not given due weight to the linguistic meanings of words, has not distinguished between the different categories of textually grounded principles of jurisprudence, and has not acquired experience in the rulings of Islamic law attached to the statements of legally accountable persons

---

## Page 15325

and who, in understanding this discourse, has not followed the natural instincts of an Arabic speaker and a sound, uncorrupted disposition. He has risen above the level of ordinary people, no longer sharing their understanding of customary speech, yet fallen below the heights of the learned. Thus he has not learned to distinguish between deceptively similar expressions, so that innate dispositions may remain as they were fashioned by Him Who perfected everything He created; and folly has led him to escape from the refuse heap of Batrā.

Whoever masters the sciences and comprehends their furthest reaches is thereby brought back to affirming innate dispositions in their original state. The messengers were sent only to perfect the innate disposition, not to change it:

“The natural disposition Allah has instilled in mankind. There is no altering Allah’s creation.”

It is well known that all Arabic speakers who hear this statement, learned and ordinary alike, understand it only as assigning the share to those of the deceased’s generation on condition that he leaves no child. They recognize that this is a single, connected statement whose parts belong together. The mistaken interpreter’s error arose only because he imagined that the first clause was general in scope, while the second singled out one of the two categories for mention, thus making this a case of “conflict between general wording and implication.”

He may also have read the works of certain speculative theologians or jurists who do not accept arguments from implication, or who, if they accept them, consider the indication of general wording stronger. This is because opposition to the former is weaker than opposition to the latter: only an insignificant handful, whose views carry no weight, have disputed the validity of general wording, whereas the validity of implication has been disputed by a group of jurists

---

## Page 15326

and groups of speculative theologians. Someone influenced by this may even imagine that he should not abandon the explicit wording of the condition, or its general scope, for the weaker implication of a qualifying attribute. We seek refuge in Allah from blindness of insight, or from a squint that makes one see a single thing as two. For a blind person’s perception is safer than that of someone with a squint if he follows a sighted person whose perception is sound. Were it not for the fear that someone might suppose this view to be tenable, or valid according to the principles of certain jurists, it would have been better to refrain from explaining it.

The answer is as follows:

The debate over whether implication constitutes authoritative evidence, and, if so, whether it can restrict the scope of general wording, concerns only two separate statements made by a single speaker, or treated as coming from a single speaker. It does not concern a single, connected statement whose parts belong together, nor statements made by two speakers whose intentions need not coincide. There are therefore three cases.

The first is two statements made by a single speaker, or treated as coming from a single speaker. We have included this latter qualification to cover cases in which one statement is Allah’s speech and the other is His Messenger’s. Such a case is treated just as though both statements were Allah’s speech or both were His Messenger’s speech.

An example is the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Water is purifying; nothing renders it impure,”

together with his statement:

“When water reaches two qullahs (large jars), it does not become impure.”

Both were uttered by the same speaker, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, but they are two separate statements.

Thus, whoever holds that implication constitutes authoritative evidence by which general wording may be restricted restricts the general scope of his statement:

“Water is purifying; nothing renders it impure,”

by the implication of:

“When water reaches two qullahs, it does not become impure,”

even though the implication of a numerical specification

---

## Page 15327

is weaker than the implication of a qualifying attribute. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad—according to the well-known report from him—and others.

Those who reject this approach argue that his statement, “Water is purifying,” is general, while his statement, “When water reaches two qullahs, it does not become impure,” concerns a particular instance covered by that general wording and accords with its ruling. The general wording’s indication should therefore not be set aside on this account.

The same applies to his statement in the document on zakat issued by Abū Bakr:

“For camels, one sheep is due for every five,”

and the remainder of that passage, alongside his statement in another hadith:

“For grazing camels, a two-year-old she-camel is due for every forty, and a three-year-old she-camel for every fifty.”

There are many similar examples. In some, the scholars have agreed that the implication takes precedence, such as his statement:

“The earth has been made for me a place of prayer and a means of purification,”

alongside his statement:

“Every part of the earth that is pure has been made for me a place of prayer and a means of purification.”

There is no disagreement that impure earth is not a means of purification.

In other cases, they have differed, as with his statement in this hadith:

“And its soil has been made for me a means of purification.”

Al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others held that the implication of this hadith restricts the general scope of his statement:

“Every part of the earth that is pure has been made for me a means of purification.”

In still other cases, they have agreed that the general wording takes precedence, such as His statement:

“Do not approach the orphan’s property except in the way that is best,”

alongside His statement:

“Do not consume it wastefully and in haste lest they grow up.”

Consuming it is forbidden whether or not one intends to use it up before the orphan comes of age.

Scholars have differed over these two kinds of indication when they conflict. Thus, the proponents of juristic reasoning, the literalists, many speculative theologians, and a group of Mālikī and Shāfiʿī scholars

---

## Page 15328

and Hanbalī scholars favored giving precedence to the general wording.

The majority of Mālikī, Shāfiʿī, and Hanbalī scholars, together with a group of speculative theologians, favored giving precedence to the implication. This is explicitly reported from al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others. The issue is open to debate, and this is not the place to discuss it in detail, for it has many ramifications. It is connected with the question of “unrestricted and qualified wording,” a deep sea within legal theory whose various forms have perplexed many who swim in its waters.

The point, however, is that the issue before us does not fall into this category. Even if it did, anyone issuing fatwas according to the schools of al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad would be obliged to base this issue on their principles and those of their followers, rather than on principles formulated by certain speculative theologians. These theologians have not thoroughly examined Allah’s signs and the proofs He has explained in His Book and through His Messenger. Nor have they comprehensively understood the aspects and subtleties of the evidence Allah has placed in the revelation He sent down, or mastered the ways in which meaning is conveyed by the clearest of languages—the language in which Allah revealed the noblest of books.

Rather, this issue belongs to the second category: a single, continuous statement whose parts are connected, with its ending qualifying its beginning. An example would be to say:

“Water is purifying; nothing renders it impure when it reaches two qullahs.”

Or to say:

“Water is purifying when it reaches two qullahs

---

## Page 15329

; nothing renders it impure.” Or he might say:

“One sheep is due for every five camels, and two sheep for ten. This zakat is obligatory on grazing camels.”

Likewise, Allah said:

“And whoever among you cannot afford to marry free believing women…”

He initially left the wording unrestricted and general, then said at the end:

“That is for those of you who fear falling into sin.”

There is no disagreement that such a statement is not understood according to the general scope of its opening words. Rather, it establishes only the purity of water amounting to two qullahs or more, and the obligation of zakat on grazing camels.

However, those who reject the authority of implication say: “The statement neither affirms nor denies the ruling in other cases. We therefore deny it in those cases on the basis of the default presumption, unless evidence establishes a departure from that presumption.” The majority say: “Rather, we deny it on the evidence furnished by this statement, which accords with the default presumption.”

The distinction between connected and separate statements can be illustrated by a man saying: “I bequeath this wealth to the scholars; they are to receive from it if they are poor.” Alternatively, he might say on one occasion, “I bequeath it to the scholars,” and then say, “Give from my wealth to the scholars if they are poor.” In the latter case, one may speak of a conflict between the general wording and the implication.

Such a situation, however, does not arise with an endowment. Once a person establishes an endowment for beneficiaries described in general or specific terms, he cannot change those terms, unlike a bequest. Moreover, if a testator explains his wording in a way that differs from its apparent meaning, his explanation is accepted, unlike that of the founder of an endowment.

This is why we have said that restricting this statement by the qualification appearing at its end is obligatory according to everyone, both those who recognize the authority of implication and those who reject it. This does not belong to that category at all; rather, it is a statement restricted by a qualification at its end.

---

## Page 15330

The third category:

This concerns statements made by different speakers whose intentions need not coincide. For example, two witnesses testify that an entire house belongs to Zayd, while two others testify that a particular part of it belongs to ‘Amr. These two sets of testimony conflict concerning that part. No one says that the general testimony should here be construed in light of the specific testimony. Someone once erred in a case of this kind, thinking that the two sets of testimony could be reconciled because this was a matter of general and specific wording, just as others erred concerning the second category by treating it as belonging to the first.

Those whose hearts Allah has illuminated distinguish between these three categories and recognize that the distinction between them is firmly rooted in everyone’s natural understanding. The distinctive role of scholars is simply to make explicit what is implicit. Even if we concede that the initial wording is general or unrestricted, it has been directly joined to wording that qualifies it and limits its scope. All rational people agree that wording of this kind is qualified and limited in scope, neither general nor unrestricted.

There is thus a difference—may Allah set you right—between completing a general, unrestricted statement and then falling silent, after which an implication with a specific or qualified scope conflicts with it, and directly joining that statement to wording that qualifies it and limits its scope. Do you not know that all rulings rest on this distinction? If someone swears an oath, remains silent for a long time, and then appends an exception, a coordinated phrase, a qualification, or something else of that kind, it has no effect.

For example, suppose he says, “By Allah, I will not travel,” then remains silent for a long time before saying, “If Allah wills”; or says, “To such-and-such a place”; or says, “Nor will I marry”; or says, “I will not travel on foot.” The oath is not qualified by any of this.

Likewise, if he swears on one occasion, “I will not travel,” and then swears on a second occasion that he will not travel on foot, no restriction would thereby be imposed

---

## Page 15331

on the first oath by the qualification in the second.

But if he said, “I will not travel on foot,” his oath would, by agreement, be restricted accordingly.

Thus, when the founder says here, “I have established an endowment for my children, then their children, then their children’s children, on condition that, if any of them dies without leaving a child, his share shall pass to those of his generation,” the meaning becomes: “I have established an endowment subject to this qualification,” which provides that the deceased’s share passes to those of his generation on condition that he leaves no child.

This is comparable to saying, “I have established an endowment for my children, my children’s children, my progeny, and my descendants, on condition that the children become entitled to this endowment after their fathers’ deaths.” Would not every jurist hold that the children’s entitlement is conditional upon their fathers’ deaths? And that, had he confined himself to saying, “For my children and my children’s children,” this would have entailed their sharing in the entitlement?

This is further clarified by a case that might be thought analogous to the present one: if someone establishes an endowment for Zayd, ‘Amr, and Bakr, and then for the poor, nothing passes to the poor until all three have died. This is the well-known position.

Suppose, however, that in this case he says, “I have established an endowment for Zayd, ‘Amr, and Bakr, and then for the poor, on condition that, when any of the three dies, his share shall pass to the other two if there is no poor person in the town where the endowment is located.”

Or he says, “On condition that, if any of the three dies and no poor person is to be found, his share shall pass to the other two.”

Or he says, “On condition that, when any of the three dies, his share shall pass to the other two if they are poor,” or “if they reside in the town where the endowment is located,” and similar

---

## Page 15332

conditions. Would not every jurist—indeed, every rational person—judge that the remaining beneficiaries’ entitlement to the deceased’s share is subject to this condition, and that this condition contained in the statement must be taken into account? A statement is complete only when its end is reached, and its qualified wording may not be considered apart from its unqualified wording. Allah has made this something rational people cannot fail to recognize, unless an impediment stands between their insight and their understanding. Allah does whatever He wills. Our case is clearer still than these examples.

Another example is to say:

“An endowment for my children, then for their children, on condition that, if any of them dies while upright, his share shall pass to his child.”

May the endowment then pass to the child regardless of whether the deceased was a Muslim or a disbeliever, upright or sinful? Anyone who imagines that it passes to the child because he falls within the general wording should be told:

“The general wording was not brought to an end and left there so that it could be acted upon independently. Rather, it is connected to wording that qualifies it and restricts its scope. One part of a single statement may not be considered apart from another.”

This is clearer than daybreak. Yet whoever Allah has not granted light has no light.

Anyone wishing to overwhelm his interlocutor on this point should multiply examples in which general or unrestricted wording is connected to wording that restricts its scope and qualifies it. For instance, you might say:

“I have established an endowment for jurists, on condition that whoever attends the lesson each morning shall be entitled to benefit.”

Or: “I have established an endowment for the poor, on condition that those among them who take up residence in the two Holy Sanctuaries shall be entitled to benefit.”

Or you may say:

“On condition that

---

## Page 15333

residence be taken up in one of the two Holy Sanctuaries.” Or: “On condition that the jurists attend the lesson every morning.” There are countless comparable examples.

One mistake some minds make in such cases is to suppose that the beginning of a statement contradicts or conflicts with its ending. This is one of the specious objections raised by some of the linguistically inept among those who deny that wording can have general application. They say:

“If these forms of expression were general, an exception would constitute a retraction or a contradiction.”

This is ignorance. Numerical expressions are unambiguous, yet they admit exceptions, as Allah Most High says:

“So he remained among them a thousand years, less fifty.”

Likewise, an indefinite noun in an affirmative statement is unrestricted, yet it admits qualification, as in His words:

“Then the freeing of a slave.”

Their error arises from imagining that to describe these forms of expression as general is to describe them as unconditionally general. If they are said to be unconditionally general, and an exception then removes part of what they entail, the excluded case would be both included by the general expression and excluded by the exception. That, they argue, would constitute a contradiction or a retraction.

The answer is:

“When these expressions are described as general, their general application is conditional upon their not being connected to a clause that restricts their scope. Thus, they are general when left unqualified, not general without qualification. The same expression conveys different meanings depending on whether it is left unqualified or qualified.”

For this reason, the jurists unanimously agree that if a man were to say, “I owe him a thousand dirhams in such-and-such coinage,” or “in broken coins

---

## Page 15334

or black coins, or underweight ones, or coins of Tiberias,” or “a thousand less fifty,” or the like, his acknowledgement would be subject to that specific qualification. If an exception constituted a retraction, it would not be accepted in an acknowledgement, since a person may not retract an acknowledgement concerning the rights of others.

Some marginal jurists frequently err in such matters. When asked about a condition stipulated by the founder of an endowment, an oath someone has sworn, or something similar, a jurist sees that the opening words are unrestricted or general, while their ending qualifies them. Sometimes he treats this as a conflict between two pieces of evidence, applying the familiar rules governing conflicting evidence, such as judging them equally strong or giving one precedence. Sometimes he regards the statement as contradictory because its ending differs from its beginning. At other times he flounders in bewilderment, attributing to a capable person the conduct of someone negligent. He may even say, “This is a mistake by the scribe.” All this stems from failing to distinguish between connected speech and separate statements.

Anyone who understands that a speaker’s opening words must not be judged until he has made a pause that definitively ends his statement, and that a writer’s text must not be judged until he has definitively finished writing, will be free of confusion in this matter and recognize the soundness of what scholars say about how discourse conveys meaning.

It is a grave failing to attribute an error to a speaker when his words admit a sound interpretation and follow the finest patterns of ordinary speech, and then to deem one of the two supposedly conflicting passages mistaken rather than the other. For if it were permissible to say, “His words, ‘provided that whoever among them dies without leaving a child,’ are an error,” that would be no more justified than to

---

## Page 15335

say, “His word ‘then’ is the error.” For a scribe is more likely to substitute one particle for another by mistake than to insert several words in error: the phrase “without leaving a child, a grandchild, any offspring, or any descendants” contains more than ten Arabic words.

It is also astonishing to suppose that this is merely emphasis, when emphasis serves to dispel doubt. If the ruling were the same in both cases and emphasis were intended, “whoever among them dies leaving a child” would have been more appropriate than “whoever among them dies without leaving a child.” For transferring the deceased’s share to his brothers even when he leaves a child already indicates that it should pass to them when he leaves none. To provide emphasis by explaining the obvious ruling rather than the less obvious one is to overstep the bounds of reason and language.

Moreover, emphasis is not expressed through adjectives that restrict the scope of the nouns they qualify. If someone said, “Honor Muslim men,” then explained, “I intended all men to be honored; I singled out Muslims for emphasis, and mentioning them does not exclude others once they have been included under the first term,” this claim would be entirely untenable and unacceptable. For “Muslim” qualifies “men,” and an adjective restricts the scope of the noun it qualifies, leaving it no longer general.

But if he said, “Honor men and Muslims”—using a conjunction, with the same instruction applying to both groups and Muslims being a subset of men—this would constitute emphasis. For a conjoined term does not necessarily restrict or narrow the term to which it is joined, owing to the distinction created between them by the conjunction, unlike adjectives

---

## Page 15336

and similar qualifiers, which do restrict meaning; so do certain kinds of conjunction. This, however, is not the place to discuss that distinction in detail. Hence, in their discussions of acknowledgments, divorce, and contracts, jurists distinguish between conjunctions that alter the preceding meaning and those that do not.

Anyone who seeks to assign a valid meaning to an utterance before it is complete must regard the beginning of the declaration of Allah’s oneness as disbelief and its ending as faith, and hold that the person uttering it has disbelieved and then believed. We seek refuge in Allah from such folly.

It has, indeed, been related that someone asked, “What declaration begins with disbelief and ends with faith?” When asked, “What is it?” he replied, “The declaration of pure faith.” I say: He meant that its beginning would constitute disbelief if the speaker stopped there. He did not mean that it constitutes disbelief when joined to the exception; had he meant that, he would have committed disbelief.

Hence, scholars of precise understanding have said, “Making an exception means speaking of what remains after the excepted portion is excluded.”

Some have mistakenly supposed that, when someone says, “A thousand except fifty,” the word “thousand” is used figuratively, because it is being used for something other than its assigned meaning: it denotes the number in its entirety, whereas the speaker did not intend that.

The reply is: That is its assigned meaning when it stands alone, without an attached qualification; that condition no longer holds here.

Furthermore, the meaning here is understood only from the complete expression, “A thousand except fifty,” not from “thousand” alone. Thus, these three terms together signify nine hundred and fifty. This is the misconception underlying the view that a general expression restricted by an attached qualification is figurative, just as many people regard a general expression restricted by a separate qualification as figurative.

---

## Page 15337

And the implications of this view are that every noun or verb accompanied by an adjective, an explanatory apposition, a substitute, any qualifying complement, a circumstantial qualifier, an accusative of specification, or the like would be used figuratively. This is self-evidently false. The distinction between an attached verbal indicator that signifies by linguistic convention, other indicators—whether circumstantial or verbal—that do not convey the intended meaning by linguistic convention, as in “I saw a lion writing and an ocean sailing on the sea,” and separate expressions is known with certainty in the languages of Arabs and non-Arabs alike.

Nevertheless, no rational person doubts that an utterance is complete only when it reaches its end, and that its meaning can be understood only after it is complete in its entirety. Its beginning cannot convey its meaning independently of its ending, whether that beginning is called “literal or figurative.” Nor may it be said that its beginning contradicts its ending. Contradiction can arise only between two independent proofs, whereas a connected utterance in its entirety constitutes a single proof. To posit a contradiction between its parts is like positing a contradiction between the parts of compound names.

This is clear, particularly in “the subject of endowments.” The founder of an endowment may wish to stipulate numerous conditions concerning the endowed property and its beneficiaries—joint or successive entitlement, equal or preferential allocation, and unrestricted or restricted provisions—enough to fill a lengthy document. Yet no Muslim has disputed that it is impermissible to treat the beginning of his statement as unrestricted and general while disregarding its ending, or to regard the restrictions, details, and specifications at the end of his statement as contradicting or conflicting with what came at the outset

---

## Page 15338

in the form of unrestricted or general terms. This is like a man examining an endowment whose founder had said, “I endow this for my children, then for their children,” and subsequently added, “One condition for the beneficiaries is that they be poor, upright in character, or the like.” The man then says, “This statement is self-contradictory, because at its beginning he endowed it for everyone, which contradicts restricting it to some.” He then treats this as a matter of “the specific and the general” and “conflicting proofs.”

It is clear that all this is confused reasoning. For a conflict presupposes that each proof independently conveys its meaning, and such independence presupposes that the statement has ended and stands separately. When, however, it remains connected to wording that changes its effect, one part cannot be treated as proof contradicting another. May Allah, glorified be He, guide us and all Muslims to what He loves and is pleased with.

If it is said: His wording, “on condition that whoever among them dies,” may be a condition stipulated by the founder to clarify that the endowment passes to those who remain and that it does not suffer an interruption midway. For some jurists have derived three opinions concerning what happens when one beneficiary dies under the stipulation, “For my children, then for my children’s children,” corresponding to the three opinions concerning the stipulation, “For my three children, then for the poor.” The first—and it is the well-known opinion—is that it belongs to the remaining members of the first tier. The second is that it passes to the second tier, as though all members of the first tier had died.

---

## Page 15339

And the third is that its disposition is left unspecified, resulting in an interruption midway through the endowment, as if he had said: “I endow it for Zayd, and, ten years after his death, for the poor.” Since an interruption midway upon the death of one beneficiary is a possible interpretation, the founder included this condition to exclude that possibility, even though, according to the first opinion, this condition merely expresses what the endowment already entails. Some conditions accord with what the wording already implies and thus serve to reinforce its effect.

We reply: Glory be to Allah, the Magnificent! These are the words of someone who has strayed far from the point of our argument. We did not base our argument on the bare formulation of a condition, but on the restriction contained within it. This objection would indeed be sound had the founder said: “On condition that whoever among them dies, his share shall pass to the members of his tier.” Had he said this, there would have been no doubt that the deceased’s share passes to the members of his tier, whether or not he leaves a child, for several reasons.

First, this is what the initial wording entails when left unrestricted, and nothing modifying its effect has been appended to it.

Second, he has appended wording that reinforces what his unrestricted statement entails.

Third, he has expressly stipulated this in a condition that excludes both transfer to the second tier and any interruption, whether or not the deceased leaves a child.

The actual case before us, however, is that he said: “On condition that whoever among them dies leaving no child

---

## Page 15340

or grandchild, or any progeny or descendants, his share shall pass to the members of his tier.” Thus, he made its transfer to them conditional upon the deceased leaving no child. Just as this condition excludes an interruption, it also restricts transfer to the members of that tier. The wording explicitly conveys both meanings; its conveying one does not prevent it from conveying the other.

This is like saying: “I endow it for my three children, then for the poor, on condition that whoever among them dies during the founder’s lifetime, his share shall be assigned to those of his own tier.” Would it then be permissible to assign his share to the members of his tier if he dies after the founder’s death? No one has held this in such a case. It might, however, be said that his dying after the founder’s death is left unaddressed, so that the endowment has an interruption midway.

The correct view, held in earlier times and today, is that his share passes to the poor. The wording assigns it to the three, then to the poor; whenever it is not assigned to the three, it must therefore be assigned to the poor, since the founder confined the endowment to these beneficiaries. Moreover, the argument in our case is clearer still, as explained above.

Indeed, even supposing someone had said that if he dies leaving no child there is an interruption, whereas if he dies leaving a child there is none, one could say that this condition serves to exclude the possibility of interruption. Nevertheless, it also conveys a restriction, as we have explained: it indicates that the share of someone who dies leaving no child is to be assigned to his tier, and that it is not to be assigned to them when he leaves a child. The first implication excludes interruption, while the second requires shared entitlement. There is no incompatibility between the two.

---

## Page 15341

Rather, the first implication follows from the conventional meaning of this wording, while the second follows from the condition taken as a whole or from the opening statement. How much more so when matters are not as supposed!

If it is said, “This excludes that possibility in this case, even though it does not exclude it in another,” we reply: This would hold only if the case mentioned did not restrict the unrestricted wording. His statement, “Whoever among them dies,” is unrestricted, but he has restricted it by saying, “leaving no child.” In such a construction, one cannot say, “He mentioned one case and left another unaddressed,” unless the statement stands independently, unconnected to anything else. When it is connected to another statement, however, it becomes a restriction upon that preceding statement.

The phrase “leaving no child” is also construed as a circumstantial qualifier in the accusative. A circumstantial qualifier is descriptive, and a description restricts the scope. It is therefore as though he said, “On condition that whoever dies in this state, his share shall pass to the members of his tier”; or, “The deceased’s share shall pass to the members of his tier on condition that he leaves no child.” It is self-evident that a transfer made conditional upon a particular qualification cannot be affirmed in the absence of that qualification.

Thus, “leaving no child” describes the circumstances of the deceased’s death. The transfer, when the deceased dies in this state, is a qualification of the endowment. When an endowment has a qualification, and that qualification is itself further qualified, the endowment cannot be affirmed except where both the qualification and its further qualification are present. Thus, it cannot be an endowment for the children, then their children, except on condition that the deceased’s share passes to the members of his tier.

---

## Page 15342

and the deceased’s share may not pass to the members of his tier unless he dies leaving no child, grandchild, offspring, or posterity. Thus, if he has a descendant—even a remote one—that descendant’s existence prevents the share from passing to the members of his tier and requires it to pass instead to that descendant, by virtue of the founder’s words: “For his children, then his children’s children, then his offspring and posterity, perpetually, for as long as they continue to have descendants.”

Know that, beyond what we have already mentioned, the flaws in this objection are almost too numerous to enumerate. Among them is the following: If his intention were merely to exclude the possibility of a lapse in the endowment, he would have had to use general wording, saying, “Whoever among them dies, his share shall pass on”; or draw attention to the implication by saying, “Whoever among them dies leaving a child, his share shall pass to the members of his tier.” For if his share passes to the members of his tier despite his having a child, then it should all the more do so when he has none.

But suppose he expressly states that the share passes to the members of the tier when there is no child, thereby excluding the possibility of a lapse, and then expects us to understand that it also passes to them when there is a child, merely because he said, “For my children, then my children’s children.” Here, however, the possibility of a lapse remains, alongside another possibility peculiar to this case: that the share passes to the deceased’s child. Indeed, the possibility of its passing to the founder’s grandchild here is more evident than the possibility of a lapse. Moreover, a restrictive reading, given the qualification, is more evident than the inference of a lapse. Furthermore, the words “For my children’s children” indicate more clearly that the share passes to the members of the tier when there is no child than that it passes to them when there is a child. He would thus be asking us to understand his words backwards and to step beyond the bounds of reason and clear expression. For

---

## Page 15343

his failure to dispel the possibility of a lapse and other possibilities where these are more apparent, and his departure from wording that would unequivocally exclude a lapse in every case, in favour of wording that more clearly indicates a restriction than merely excludes a lapse in certain cases, are conclusive evidence that this was not what he intended.

A parallel would be a man saying to his slave: “Honour Zayd. If he is righteous, honour him.” Zayd is not righteous, so the servant does not honour him. His master then says to him: “You have disobeyed my command. Did I not order you to honour him?”

The servant replies: “You told me: ‘If he is righteous, honour him.’”

The master says: “I only said this so that you would not imagine that I hate righteous people and therefore refrain from honouring him despite his righteousness. I was excluding the possibility of an exception in this particular case.”

Would such an explanation be accepted from a rational person? Would the servant be accused of negligence? Or would he say to his master: “This wording indicates a restriction. Had you intended the command to apply generally, you should have said: ‘Honour him even if he is not righteous,’ since honouring a righteous person would then follow all the more clearly; or: ‘Honour him even if he is righteous,’ if he is truly dear to you.”

Likewise, here the disputant says: “The founder transferred the share to the members of the tier, whether or not the deceased had a child.”

When asked, “Why, then, did he qualify the transfer by saying: ‘Provided that, if any of them dies leaving no child, his share shall pass to the members of the tier’?” he replies: “To exclude the possibility of a lapse in this case, but not in the case in which there is even greater reason to exclude it.”

He may then be told: “The wording would have been

---

## Page 15344

expressed in Arabic in such a case as follows: ‘Provided that, if any of them dies, even if he has a child, his share shall pass to the members of his tier.’ Or he could say: ‘Provided that, if any of them dies, even if he has no child,’ thus including the conjunction.”

But if he says, “Provided that, if any of them dies leaving no child,” this explicitly establishes a restriction and admits no other interpretation. Whoever imagines otherwise, considers it even remotely possible, or considers it possible for a rational person to accept such an interpretation, has undoubtedly forfeited the blessing Allah bestowed upon mankind when He taught them expression. I do not think that, left to his innate disposition, he would imagine such a thing. Yet innate dispositions may suffer corrupting influences that impair their soundness, as the hadiths declare.

Another argument is that a rational person does not exclude a remote possibility by introducing a more apparent one. Clearly, had the founder omitted this condition, the possibility of a lapse would have been exceedingly remote: it either contradicts scholarly consensus or ranks among discredited juristic opinions. Once the condition is mentioned, however, restriction and an ordered distribution become a strong possibility—an apparent meaning even according to the disputant, and a conclusive one according to others. How, then, can the founder’s words be interpreted as following a blameworthy course rather than a commendable one, when the latter interpretation is possible?

Another argument is that only some jurists would notice this possibility; perhaps it has only recently arisen in Islamic history. The possibility of restriction, however, is a feature of language that existed before Islam. How, then, can the words of an earlier founder be understood as guarding against a possibility that does not

---

## Page 15345

occur except to the occasional individual, and which perhaps never occurred to the founder, rather than as guarding against a possibility present in the mind of every speaker, or most speakers, ever since Allah taught Adam expression?

Another argument is that, if the founder intended by the words “leaving no child” to exclude the possibility of a lapse in this case, those words can also serve to exclude the possibility of a lapse in the other cases. In those cases, this possibility would be excluded by having the share of anyone who dies leaving a child pass to his child. This preserves the qualification from being disregarded while preventing a lapse in both cases. Clearly, interpreting the founder’s words in this way is better than rendering them ineffectual and incomplete.

Another argument is that this purpose would have been fully achieved had he said, “Provided that, if any of them dies.” Adding words while diminishing the meaning is an error that must not be attributed to a speaker’s words when they admit a sound interpretation, as they do here.

Another argument is that this reasoning rests on the assumption that his words, “For my children, then for their children,” entail the succession of one group as a whole to another group as a whole. This implication, however, depends on the wording not being joined to anything that qualifies it. If it is joined to wording that qualifies it and entails succession on an individual basis—such as, “Provided that, if any of them dies leaving a child, his share shall pass to his child,” or similar expressions—this implication would no longer hold

---

## Page 15346

by common agreement.

The wording, “Provided that, if any of them dies leaving no child,” plainly makes the transfer conditional upon the absence of a child. Those who divert it from this apparent meaning do so only because the earlier wording conflicts with it. Yet the earlier wording constitutes evidence only if no clause modifying its meaning is attached to it. The reasoning thus becomes circular, and its evidentiary force collapses. For the earlier wording can only be established as requiring succession of one group as a whole to another if it is unconnected to anything modifying that meaning. Here, however, the absence of such a modifying clause cannot be established until it is established that the later wording does not indicate a modification, but conveys another meaning. That other meaning, in turn, cannot be established until the earlier wording is established as evidence for succession of one group as a whole to another. This is circular reasoning and begs the question: he has made the conclusion a premise in proving itself.

Another argument is to ask: Does his phrase “leaving no child” qualify the transfer or not?

If he says, “It does not,” this is an obvious denial of the plain meaning of the language.

If he says, “It does,” he is asked: “May the qualified ruling, then, be affirmed without its qualification?”

If he says, “Yes, on the strength of the earlier evidence,” he is asked: “May the beginning of a statement, then, be used as evidence independently of the qualification placed upon it at its end?”

If he says, “Yes,” his obstinacy becomes evident. If he says, “No,” the point at issue is established. These are indubitable premises, which are not undermined by the fact that the wording serves other purposes as well. Anyone who has examined these premises can only persist in obstinacy or submit to the truth.

Another argument is that, if it is maintained that the endowment suffers an intervening lapse when a beneficiary dies leaving no child, but does not lapse when he dies leaving a child, then this objection would have

---

## Page 15347

some basis; yet it would count as evidence against the disputant. For if the endowment continues when he dies leaving a child, then, if his share passes to the child, that is precisely what we seek to establish. If, however, it passes to the generation as a whole, no jurist could possibly say that it passes to that generation when there is a child, but lapses when there is no child. Thus, interpreting this wording as excluding the possibility of a lapse plainly demonstrates that the share of someone who dies leaving a child passes to his child. The proofs of this are as abundant as rain. And Allah guides whom He wills to the straight path.

The second argument concerning the fundamental issue is that his wording, “For his children, then for their children,” entails an order of succession: the children’s children become entitled after the children.

Here we have two groups, one succeeding the other. Rulings attached to general terms are of two kinds.

The first applies to each individual covered by the general term, whether or not another individual exists.

The second applies to those individuals collectively, so that the existence of each is a condition for the ruling to apply to the others. An example of the first is the saying of the Most High:

“O mankind, worship your Lord, who created you and those before you.”

“O you who believe, when you rise for prayer.”

And an example of

---

## Page 15348

the second is His saying, the Most High:

“You are the best community brought forth for mankind.”

“Thus We have made you a just community.”

For every individual human being has been created, and each is commanded to worship and observe ritual purification; yet no individual member of the community is himself a just community or the best community.

Furthermore, when one general term is paired with another, each individual covered by the first may be paired with every individual covered by the second, as in His saying:

“The Messenger has believed in what was revealed to him from his Lord, and so have the believers. All have believed in Allah, His angels, His Books, and His messengers.”

For every believer believes in every angel, Book, and messenger. Alternatively, the two groups may be paired collectively, with both required to be considered as a whole, as in His saying:

“There was a sign for you in the two armies that met.”

For the encounter applies to each army when the two come together.

The pairing may also require only one of the two groups to be considered as a whole, as in His saying:

“You are the best community brought forth for mankind.”

For the community as a whole benefits people, whether they are together or apart.

Alternatively, the two groups may be paired by matching their respective individuals, so that each individual covered by either general term corresponds to one individual covered by the other. Thus one says, “The people put on their clothes,” or “The people mounted their animals.” Each person mounted his own animal and put on his own garment.

Likewise, when one says, “People love their children,” this means that each person loves his own child. Another example is His saying, glorified is He:

“Mothers shall breastfeed their children.”

That is, each mother breastfeeds her own child. This differs from saying, “People venerate the prophets,” for each person venerates every prophet.

Thus, the endower’s wording, “For his children, then for their children,” entails the ordering of

---

## Page 15349

one of the two general terms after the other. He may intend that the second group as a whole is to succeed the first group as a whole and every individual within it, so that no one covered by the second general term becomes a beneficiary of the endowment until all those covered by the first have passed away. Alternatively, he may intend an order of succession that matches individuals with individuals, so that each grandchild becomes a beneficiary upon the death of his own parent, not upon the death of someone else’s parent. This is like His saying:

“Mothers shall breastfeed their children.”

And the expression, “People love their children.” The wording readily admits both meanings. One, however, may be preferred over the other on additional grounds, just as the majority of scholars favoured succession of one group as a whole to another in the statement: “I have made an endowment for Zayd, ʿAmr, and Bakr, then for the poor.” The poor and those three are not equal in number, such that each individual in one group could be made to succeed an individual in the other. Nor is there any relationship requiring that this poor person be assigned to Zayd, that one to ʿAmr, and another to Bakr.

This differs from our saying, “People love their children,” for what is meant here is people who have children. Thus, the two general terms correspond to one another. Moreover, the possessive construction in “their children” requires that each person be associated with his own child, rather than someone else’s child.

Likewise, the second meaning is preferable in His saying, glorified is He:

“Forbidden to you are your mothers, your daughters, and your sisters,”

and so forth. For He did not forbid every person addressed from marrying all the mothers and daughters of those addressed; rather, He forbade each person from marrying his own mother and daughter.

---

## Page 15350

Likewise, He says:

“You shall have half of what your wives leave.”

This does not mean that all husbands collectively receive half of what all women leave. Rather, each husband receives half of what his own wife leaves, and no more. Likewise, He says:

“Those who believe and whose descendants follow them in faith—We shall join their descendants with them.”

This refers only to each person being followed by his own descendants; it does not mean that every descendant followed every forefather.

This is common in speech, as when one says: “People are in their homes, with their spouses, managing their wealth and providing for their children,” and similar expressions. Three considerations demonstrate that this interpretation has considerable force in the case of endowments.

First: Most founders of endowments transfer each parent’s share to that parent’s child, without postponing the transfer until the entire generation has passed away. This prevalence is evidence that the interpretation is strong—indeed, that it is the more likely one.

Second: An endowment for one’s children is generally intended to function like inherited property that cannot be sold. Its principal purpose is for the descendants to benefit from it without being able to dispose of the underlying property itself. Moreover, endowments and inheritance resemble one another here in that, in both cases, transfer to a grandchild is conditional upon the absence of the child.

Now, if wording of this kind were used without qualification in relation to inheritance, as Allah Most High uses it in His saying:

“You shall have half of what your wives leave,”

---

## Page 15351

“And they shall have a quarter of what you leave,”

it would convey only a distributive correspondence between the respective individuals—not a correspondence between the two groups collectively, between every individual and every other individual, or between each individual and the entire group. Likewise, if a jurist tells a man, “Your property passes to your heirs, then to their heirs,” this is understood to mean that each person’s property passes to his own heir. The wording, “for their children, then for their children’s children,” should therefore be understood similarly, either as an admissible interpretation or as the apparent meaning.

Third: In the wording “for their children,” the possessive relationship cannot possibly connect every individual with every other individual, since each child is not the child of every parent. Rather, the meaning is: “then for each person’s own children.” Thus, if someone says, “I establish an endowment for Zayd, ʿAmr, and Bakr, then for their children,” the pronoun refers to Zayd, ʿAmr, and Bakr, and the correspondence is distributive.

The statement involves two meanings: a possessive relationship and an order of succession. If the possessive relationship is distributive, the order of succession can likewise be distributive. Thus, His saying:

“They shall nurse their children,”

involves both nursing and a possessive relationship. Since the possessive relationship is distributive, the nursing is distributive as well. Likewise, in His saying:

“You shall have half of what your wives leave,”

the possessive relationship is distributive, so the entitlement is also distributive. This makes clear that the distributive interpretation has considerable force in expressions of this kind, whether it is stronger than the alternative, weaker, or equally plausible.

---

## Page 15352

Scholars differ over what this wording entails when used without qualification in an endowment, although many—or most—of them favour succession from one group as a whole to another, rather than distributive succession, as when we say:

“For these people, then for the poor.”

The jurists of our school hold two views on what this entails when left unqualified, although they mention no disagreement concerning the statement:

“I establish an endowment for these two, then for the poor.”

The distinction between the two formulations, according to one of these views, is what we explained earlier. The well-known position among the jurists of al-Shāfiʿī’s school is that the wording establishes succession from one group as a whole to another.

They also hold a view that, whenever a beneficiary dies, whether leaving children or not, his share is subject to a gap in the succession of beneficiaries. Some of them have derived a further view: that the deceased’s share passes to the entire second generation of beneficiaries.

Our purpose here is not to discuss what this wording entails when left unqualified. Rather, we are proceeding on the assumption that it does entail succession from one group as a whole to another, since the argument under the alternative interpretation is clear. As for the wording’s capacity to bear both meanings, no one who understands what we have said will dispute it. Once that capacity is established, it is well known that, when wording is joined to something that identifies one of its two possible meanings, that indication must be followed.

No reasonable person can doubt that the second statement makes clear that the founder of the endowment intended each parent’s share to pass to that parent’s children. Otherwise, there would be no difference between a beneficiary dying with children and dying without children. Indeed, there would have been no need to mention the condition at all.

The most that could be said is:

“It serves merely as emphasis, if it carries no implication to the contrary.”

The response is:

“Understanding it to establish a new provision is preferable to understanding it as mere emphasis.”

---

## Page 15353

And know that this indication rests on several considerations:

First: the initial wording can express distributive succession.

Second: the implied meaning suggests that the provision applies exclusively to the specified case. No reasonable person disputes this, even if he disputes its validity as evidence.

Third: understanding the wording to establish a new provision is preferable to understanding it as mere emphasis. This is neither a case of conflicting evidence nor one of qualifying an unrestricted statement. Rather, it is a matter of explaining wording that admits two meanings.

If you object:

“If the initial wording apparently denotes succession from one group as a whole to another, then this entails departing from its apparent meaning. If you say, ‘It admits either meaning, or apparently denotes distributive succession,’ we reject your claim. If you say, ‘Wording cannot be described as having an apparent meaning or as complete until it has been fully stated, and the initial wording has not yet been fully stated,’ then this is the first argument. What, then, is the difference between them?”

We reply:

“The first argument explains that, even if the initial wording, when unqualified, were explicit and admitted no alternative interpretation, attaching a qualification to it would negate that implication. This is like someone saying, ‘I establish an endowment for Zayd,’ then adding, ‘if he is poor.’ This is not considered an explanation of wording that admits more than one meaning; rather, it is a qualification.

“This argument, however, explains that the initial wording admits two meanings, and it cannot be described as apparently conveying either one unless it is detached from what follows it. When it is connected to what follows, that connection identifies which of the two meanings is intended.”

---

## Page 15354

As for your statement, “The initial wording must either apparently convey one of the two meanings or admit either,” we reply:

Before it is complete, it cannot be described in any of the three ways; it can only be described as capable of bearing the three meanings. Nor can this be called a departure from the apparent meaning at all, for speech that is not yet complete has no apparent meaning. Its apparent meaning is what emerges once the speaker has finished.

This reveals the source of the error concerning the generality of the initial wording. His statement, “For my children, then for their children,” unquestionably includes all his grandchildren, so none of them may be excluded. It also entails an order of succession, placing the children before the grandchildren. But what kind of generality is this? Is it explanatory and distributive generality, pairing each individual with one individual? Or is it collective generality, pairing each individual with every individual?

Anyone who claims that the wording explicitly conveys this latter meaning, in the sense of being unequivocal, is ignorant of revealed evidence and the rulings of Islamic law, and departs from the natural paths of reason. If someone acknowledges that the wording admits both meanings but maintains that one is more likely when the statement ends there, we do not dispute with him, for that is not the question under discussion. But if he disputes the greater likelihood of the first meaning after that appended clause, he too is certainly mistaken.

Like the first argument, this argument holds for both those who accept implied meaning as evidence and those who reject it. For the latter say:

“The unmentioned case is not included in the second statement. But if it is included in the first, I apply that statement to it. We concede that when it is considered most likely, or is known, that there is no reason for singling out the specified case other than to restrict the ruling to it, the implied meaning constitutes evidence.”

Thus, when he considers the statement:

---

## Page 15355

“On condition that, if any of them dies without leaving a child, his share shall pass to those of his generation,” he says:

“If the endower intended collective generality, this wording would qualify and clarify his intent. Whenever the choice is between treating this clause as an explanation of the initial wording and treating it as meaningless, interpreting it as meaningful and explanatory is preferable, for two reasons.

“First, I give it effect; giving effect to the endower’s words is preferable to disregarding them.

“Second, I treat it as clarifying wording that is then open to more than one interpretation. Through it, I exclude an interpretation upon which I would otherwise have acted. When speech admits two meanings, there are grounds for determining one of them, whether those grounds exclude the alternative or not. By interpreting this wording as explanatory, I give those grounds their due force and safeguard words that distinguish the lawful from the unlawful against being disregarded and rendered meaningless. What comparison is there between this and the approach of someone who adopts a possible meaning of the opening words, disregards their conclusion, and attributes inarticulacy and pointless speech to the speaker?”

What clarifies this is that the expression “on condition that” is a formula introducing a stipulation and a qualification. A stipulation concerns something that the contract can accommodate, although its unrestricted wording does not entail it.

To explain: saying “I have sold” and “I have purchased” does not entail a deferred payment, a pledge, a guarantor, a currency other than the local currency, or an additional quality in the item sold. Yet the wording admits these possibilities, in the sense that it is compatible with either arrangement. Left unrestricted, however, it excludes these things: the wording does not require them, and the default is their absence.

Thus, whenever he says, “On condition that you give me as security for it

---

## Page 15356

such-and-such an item,” this explains his words, “I have sold it to you for a thousand,” and is equivalent to saying, “For a thousand secured by a pledge.”

The third argument:

His statement, “On condition that, if any of them dies without leaving a child, his share shall pass to those of his generation,” indicates that if any of them dies leaving a child, his share does not pass to those of his generation. This is an inference from the implied meaning of the words. This is not the place to establish the validity of such inference, but we shall mention a few points sufficient for the present purpose.

First:

Accepting this form of inference is the position of the majority of jurists, both earlier and later, among the Mālikīs, Shāfiʿīs, and Hanbalīs. Indeed, these imams explicitly affirmed it. Only groups of speculative theologians, together with some jurists, disagreed. The positions attributed to the jurists’ schools must therefore accord with their foundational principles. Anyone who attributes the contrary position to these schools is mistaken. If, however, he is exercising independent juristic judgment in what he says, he must possess the requisite qualifications for doing so.

Particularly astonishing is some people’s belief that implied meaning is authoritative in the Lawgiver’s speech but not in human speech, as is the case with analogical reasoning. This contradicts the consensus: people either hold that implied meaning is one of the ways in which words convey meaning, or hold that it is not. This distinction, however, is newly introduced. Moreover, those who regard it as authoritative hold that it is authoritative in speech generally. To establish its authority, they have cited human speech, the statements of language scholars, and rational arguments

---

## Page 15357

which demonstrate to anyone who reflects that implied meaning, like generality, unrestricted wording, and qualification, is one of the ways in which words convey meaning. This is evident in the statements of the scholars. Analogical reasoning, however, is not among the ways words convey meaning as understood through language; it acquires evidentiary force only through the Lawgiver’s textual authorization. Implied meaning, by contrast, has evidentiary force in the language itself, and the Lawgiver explained the rulings in the language of the Arabs.

Second:

This implication arises from a specific qualifying attribute mentioned after a general noun. Many who rejected implication from an independently stated attribute accepted it in this case. Indeed, this form of implication is authoritative even in the case of a non-derived noun, as al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad argued on the basis of the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“The earth has been made a place of prayer for me,” and, “Its soil has been made a means of purification.”

For if someone says, “People are of two kinds: Muslim and unbeliever. As for the Muslim, you must treat him kindly,” it is necessarily understood that the speaker intended to single out the Muslim for this ruling. This differs from saying at the outset, “You must treat the Muslim kindly.” Here, one might suppose that he mentioned the Muslim merely in keeping with customary usage, because it is usually the ruling concerning him that needs clarification, as in his words:

“A Muslim is inviolable to another Muslim in every respect: his blood, his property, and his honour.”

Likewise, “Zakah is due on grazing camels” conveys a stronger implication than “Zakah is due on grazing livestock.” For when someone says, “On grazing camels,” if the ruling concerning them were the same whether or not they grazed, he would have lengthened the wording while diminishing the meaning conveyed. By contrast,

---

## Page 15358

if he says, “On grazing livestock,” one might suppose that he singled them out for mention because they were the most prevalent form of wealth, or because the need to clarify their ruling was more pressing. This is clear. The same applies here: if his intention were that the deceased’s share should pass to those of his tier, whether or not he left children, then saying, “Whoever among them dies, his share shall belong to those of his tier,” would have expressed the intended ruling comprehensively and concisely.

If, instead, he says, “Whoever among them dies leaving no child, offspring, or descendants, whatever he had been receiving from this shall belong to those of his rank and tier,” he would have lengthened the wording while diminishing the meaning conveyed. This is not the case if the wording is understood as restricting the ruling to the stated case: the statement then remains sound and meaningful. The author’s words must be given effect wherever possible; they must never be disregarded when they can be given effect.

Third:

Those who reject implied meaning do so because singling something out for mention may have a reason other than restricting the ruling to it. The speaker may be unaware of the unmentioned case, or may not intend to explain its ruling. The unmentioned case may be more deserving of the ruling than the stated case, or may appear at first sight to be equivalent to it. The speaker may have been asked about the explicitly stated case; circumstances may have arisen that required its ruling to be explained; there may be a particular need to explain it; or the explicitly stated case may represent the predominant instances of that type.

Thus, when it is known, or considered most likely, that none of these reasons or others like them accounts for singling it out for mention, it is understood that he mentioned it specifically only because the ruling applies exclusively to it.

---

## Page 15359

For this reason, those who reject implied meaning nevertheless appeal to implied meanings in many instances, since they do not deny that an intention to restrict the ruling may be apparent in some such cases. This is one of them. His statement, “Whoever among them dies leaving no child,” may suggest both possibilities, and he has a purpose in stating the condition. This is not an implicit indication that the ruling extends to the other case: if he stipulates that the deceased’s share passes to his siblings when he leaves no child, it does not follow that he would transfer it to them when the deceased does leave a child. Moreover, there is a need to clarify both cases. Indeed, if he regarded the two cases as equivalent—yet specifically mentioned the case of the father’s absence—he would be creating confusion and obscurity, since his wording would suggest the opposite of his intention. This difficulty does not arise if the wording is understood as restricting the ruling.

Fourth:

When an attribute is relevant to a ruling, this indicates that it is the ruling’s operative cause. The deceased’s having left no child is relevant to transferring his entitlement to those of his tier. This therefore indicates that the operative cause of the transfer to those of his tier is his dying without leaving a child. Accordingly, this transfer ceases to apply when its operative cause is absent—that is, when there is a child.

Fifth:

Everyone who hears this statement understands it as restricting the ruling. This establishes that such restriction is its literal meaning according to customary usage, whether that meaning is original to the language or subsequently acquired through a shift in usage. In either case, the jurists agree that the words of those making legal dispositions must be understood accordingly. Know that establishing this in this particular case does not require demonstrating that implied meaning constitutes evidence, since those who dispute implied meaning merely deny that implied meanings are universally authoritative

---

## Page 15360

, not that none of them are authoritative. Some implied meanings may constitute evidence because the intention behind them is clear. This implied meaning is of that kind, as shown by people’s understanding of it. Anyone who disputes that understanding is either unsound in reason or obstinately denying it.

Once it is established that this statement precludes transferring the entitlement to those of the deceased’s tier when he leaves children, either it passes neither to them nor to the children—which contradicts his statement, “To their children, then to their children’s children, in perpetuity for as long as they have descendants”—or it passes to the children, which is precisely what we seek to establish.

If it is said:

“It may be conceded that implied meaning constitutes evidence, but it conflicts with the explicit wording at the outset, or with the general wording. That evidence should not be set aside on account of the implied meaning.”

The reply is that there are several answers.

First:

The initial wording, on its own, establishes nothing whatsoever, because wording only constitutes evidence once it is complete and stands apart from what follows. When it is connected to what follows, it is part of the evidence, not evidence in itself; and part of the evidence is not the evidence itself. Anyone who thinks that, within a continuous statement, its unrestricted opening conflicts with its qualified ending does not understand what a conflict between two pieces of evidence is.

Second:

Even supposing that the initial wording were complete, it is not explicit, as explained earlier. Rather, it admits two meanings. As for its being general, that is conceded. However, we do not restrict its scope; rather, we preserve its

---

## Page 15361

general scope; the question concerns only the nature of that scope. Indeed, our interpretation gives it a broader scope, because each grandchild receives a share both during the lifetime of his paternal uncles and after their death. On the other interpretation, he receives a share only during their lifetime. Wording that includes them in both circumstances is more general than wording that includes them in only one.

Third:

Even supposing that this falls under “conflict between general wording and implied meaning,” the correct position is that an implied meaning of this kind takes precedence over general wording. This is the view of most Mālikī, Shāfiʿī and Hanbalī scholars. Some have even reported a consensus on this among those who recognize implied meaning as evidence, because implied meaning constitutes specific evidence, and specific evidence takes precedence over general evidence. Disagreement over the authority of implied meaning is immaterial here: clear analogy takes precedence over implied meaning, even though those who reject analogy are nearly as numerous as those who reject implied meaning. Likewise, a solitary report can restrict the general scope of the Quran, even though those who reject solitary reports are more numerous than those who reject the general wording of the Quran.

If it is said:

“What you have stated rests on the assumption that the pronoun in his statement, ‘provided that whoever dies,’ refers to everyone previously mentioned. This is not conceded, for some recognized jurists have said: ‘When an exception in the endower’s stipulations follows a series of coordinated clauses …’ (1) If the pronoun refers to the final clause, do the preceding clauses not retain their original ordering?”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 15362

The reply is:
