This is invalid on several grounds.

First:

This position entails that if someone said, “For my children, then their children, then their children’s children, then their children’s children’s children, and their offspring and posterity; provided that whoever among them dies leaving a child, his share shall pass to his child, and whoever among them dies without leaving a child, his share shall pass to those of his generation,” this stipulation would apply only to the last generation. If someone in the first, second or third generation died, his share would not pass to his child, but rather to the members of his own generation, in accordance with the unqualified provision for succession by generation.

If the disputant accepts this consequence and says, “That is indeed my position,” his position would contradict the practice of Muslims, past and present, in every age and every land. Indeed, Allah Most High alone knows the number of endowments governed by such stipulations. Muslims—their judges and muftis, their elite and ordinary people—have always treated such a stipulation as applying to every generation, without objection or dispute. Anyone who maintains otherwise is thus known to have introduced a position contrary to the consensus of past generations. This is self-evidently known.

Even if disagreement on this matter were assumed to exist, it would be an anomalous disagreement, counted among scholarly errors. It is sufficient evidence of a position’s weakness that it rests on something like this.

Another consequence of this position is that if someone said, “An endowment for my children, then their children, then their children’s children, provided that whoever

---

## Page 15363

among them is poor shall receive a distribution, and whoever among them is wealthy shall receive none.” Distributions would then be made to the first and second generations, whether wealthy or poor, or the distinction would apply exclusively to the third generation.

Likewise, if he said:

“Provided that whoever among them marries shall receive a distribution, and whoever does not marry shall receive none.”

Likewise, if he said:

“And whoever stipulates that the endowment be disbursed to the poor among them, but not the wealthy,” or, “on condition that it be disbursed to their poor, but not their wealthy.”

There are countless similar cases. Anyone who consistently applied the reasoning of this position to them would arrive at a monstrous absurdity. If, however, he said, “Rather, the condition applies to all generations, as everyone understands,” then it is self-evident that our case is one of this kind. There is no difference between it and these cases that could escape a discerning person.

The second ground:

People understand this wording only as imposing the condition on all generations. The evidence is that endowments carrying such stipulations are too numerous to count, yet people have understood them only in this way. Indeed, restricting the condition to the last generation may never have occurred to any endowment founder, scribe, witness, listener, judge or beneficiary.

Since this is how the wording is understood in customary usage, people’s statements must be interpreted according to their customary manner of speaking, whether that usage agrees with the established linguistic meaning or differs from it. Thus, if the linguistic meaning entails

---

## Page 15364

the condition applying to all generations, customary usage confirms that meaning. Even if we suppose that the linguistic meaning restricts it to the last generation, customary usage would have altered that established meaning.

The statements of endowment founders, those taking oaths, testators and others in similar positions are interpreted according to their established customary meanings, rather than their linguistic meanings. Moreover, we maintain the following:

This is how such wording is understood in customary usage, and the presumption is that customary usage preserves the linguistic meaning rather than changes it. This therefore serves as evidence that this is also the wording’s linguistic meaning, since the presumption is that no shift in meaning has occurred. Anyone who disputes that people, both learned and ordinary, understand this wording, when unqualified, as making the condition apply to all generations is plainly denying the obvious. If he concedes this but disputes that the words of someone making a legal disposition should be interpreted according to the meaning people understand, he is plainly departing from the principles of the Sharia. These, then, are two certain premises; knowing them necessarily entails that the condition applies to all generations.

The third ground:

If the wording is interpreted as making the condition apply only to the final clause, then, according to the opposing view, its effect would be as follows: without this condition, the descendants would jointly share in the entire endowment that had passed to them from the generation above them, as well as whatever had passed to them from those among them who had died, whether leaving children or not.

Thus, if he says:

“Whoever among them dies without leaving children, his share shall go to those of his own generation,”

this means that those of the deceased’s own generation alone receive his share if he leaves no children, to the exclusion of those above and below them. This was not implied by the wording. If he does leave children, all the beneficiaries of the endowment share in the deceased’s portion, his children and others alike. If, however, the wording is interpreted

---

## Page 15365

as making the condition apply to all generations, it means that the deceased’s share passes to those of his own generation if he leaves no children, and to his children if he does.

It is known with certainty from people’s conduct that someone who makes all generations joint beneficiaries would not transfer the deceased’s share solely to those of his own generation, excluding those above him, yet withhold it from his children when he leaves children, treating them merely as other beneficiaries. On this interpretation, he would have given those of the deceased’s generation priority over the deceased’s children, despite not observing the order of generations. Clearly, no reasonable person would intend this. A reasonable person either observes the order of generations, without allowing them to share concurrently, or transfers the deceased’s share to his children, as in inheritance.

But to make them joint beneficiaries while assigning the deceased’s share exclusively to his siblings rather than his children runs contrary to what is known of people’s conduct. Even if the pronoun were assumed to admit either reference—to all generations or to the final generation—this indication supplied by circumstances and customary practice would determine which of the two possibilities is intended.

If it is objected:

The same consequence follows from your interpretation if you take the pronoun to refer to all generations. The wording requires succession among four generations and concurrent sharing among the remaining generations. Your position concerning the remaining generations is therefore the same as ours.

We reply:

This is disputed. Should the remaining generations share concurrently, in accordance with the unrestricted sharing implied by the conjunction “and,” or should they succeed one another, by inference from the order in which

---

## Page 15366

he listed them, extending that same order to the remaining generations, as people generally understand such wording? For just as the conjunction “and” does not require an order of succession, neither does it exclude one. If the wording contains an indication of such an order, it must be observed. This is a matter of disagreement. If we adopt the second view, there is nothing further to discuss.

If we adopt the first view, we also say:

It requires the deceased’s share to pass to his children in every generation. Transferring the deceased’s share to those of his own generation, rather than to the other beneficiaries of the endowment, when he leaves no children implies that it passes to his children when he does leave children. Such an a fortiori implication constitutes stronger evidence than an explicit statement, even in the conditions stipulated by founders of endowments.

Thus, if someone says:

“I have established an endowment for my children, on condition that anyone among them who is openly sinful shall not receive a single dirham,”

there is undoubtedly no permission to give such a person two dirhams. By denying him a small amount, the founder has indicated that he is also to be denied a larger amount. Likewise, by transferring the deceased’s share to his siblings when he leaves no children, the founder has indicated that it passes to the children when both they and the siblings are present. Thus, the siblings’ exclusion when there are children follows from the stated restriction, while the children’s entitlement follows from the implication and evident purport of the wording.

To clarify: assigning the deceased’s share to those of his own generation, rather than to the other beneficiaries of the endowment, and without singling out those closest to the deceased, shows that the founder made closeness to the deceased the basis for this exclusive entitlement—not closeness to the founder or merely being entitled to benefit from the endowment. And it is well known

---

## Page 15367

that the children, when present, are closer to the deceased, so their basis for entitlement is stronger. This indicates that the founder intended them to receive the share. We shall mention, Allah willing, what may be said in response to this.

The fourth argument:

A pronoun must refer to everything previously mentioned. If this is impossible, it is taken to refer to the nearest antecedent or to whatever the evidence identifies. Restricting its reference to some of what has been mentioned without justification departs from the default rule, and the wording may not be construed in this way without evidence. This is because third-person pronouns are generally used to refer to what has already been mentioned, without themselves indicating any particular kind or quantity.

Thus, if someone were to say:

“I visit Banū Hāshim, then Banū al-Muttalib, then the rest of Quraysh, and honor them and have them seated,”

or something similar, the pronoun would refer to all those previously mentioned.

This is not the same as the disagreement over an exception following several clauses: does it apply to all of them or only to the nearest one?

That disagreement arises because an exception excludes some of what the wording encompasses. Those who restrict it to the final clause say:

“The basis for inclusion in the preceding clauses remains in place, while the ground for exclusion is uncertain. What that basis requires cannot therefore be set aside on account of doubt.”

This consideration does not apply to a pronoun. A pronoun is a word used to refer to what has previously been mentioned, and it can encompass all those referents collectively. It must therefore be understood as general in scope unless there is evidence restricting it. Accordingly, understanding the pronoun as general accords with its literal meaning, while restricting its reference is like restricting the scope of a general expression.

---

## Page 15368

The fifth argument:

If he says, “I establish an endowment for my children, then for their children, then for their children’s children, subject to the provision that whoever among them dies leaving a child or leaving no child,” referring the pronoun to the third generation alone would favor one interpretation without any grounds for doing so. It is apparent—indeed, certain—that a rational person would not do this, for a rational person does not distinguish between equivalent cases without a reason.

He may intend to give priority to those closest to him, followed by the next closest, throughout all generations, if the deceased’s share is transferred to his son in every generation. But that he should favor those closest to him in some generations, while in others transferring the share to the deceased’s child or to those of the deceased’s generation, is something scarcely any rational person would intend.

When wording admits either an interpretation that was apparently intended or one that was apparently not intended, it must be understood according to the apparent intention. Wording is acted upon only because it indicates what is intended. Thus, when it is inherently open to more than one interpretation and one of the two becomes more likely, it must be understood accordingly. If, in addition, the restrictive interpretation limits a general expression to certain individuals whose relationship to the founder’s purpose is no different from that of the others, this is virtually conclusive evidence of its general scope.

The sixth argument:

This qualification has the force of a condition, and a condition following several clauses applies to all of them by agreement of the jurists. No weight need be given here to the disagreement of certain later scholars.

The jurists have expressly stated that if a man says, “By Allah, I shall do such-and-such, and I shall do such-and-such—if Allah wills,” both acts would be contingent

---

## Page 15369

on Allah’s will.

The same applies if he says, “I shall certainly strike Zayd, then ‘Amr, then Bakr—if Allah wills.”

Likewise, if he says, “He pledges to do such-and-such on pain of divorce, and to do such-and-such on pain of his slave’s freedom, or to do such-and-such on pain of his wife being to him as his mother’s back—if Allah wills.” Their disagreement concerns only restrictive exceptions, not conditional exceptions.

The provision under discussion is a conditional exception, like a stipulation, for several reasons:

First: An exception introduced by illā (“except”) or similar particles applies to nouns, not to the statement as a whole. An exception introduced by conditional particles, however, applies to the statement as a whole. The expression “provided that” and similar expressions apply to the statement as a whole, so they resemble conditional particles more closely than the particles of exception, illā and its counterparts.

Thus, in the statement, “I establish an endowment for my children, except Zayd,” the exception applies to “my children.” But in the statement, “I establish an endowment for my children if they are poor,” the condition applies to the words “I establish an endowment”—that is, to the statement and its composite meaning. The same applies to “provided that they are poor”: the preposition ‘alā (“upon”), which expresses elevation, makes the meaning of the statement—“I establish an endowment”—dependent on the condition. This is conclusive for anyone who reflects upon it.

Second: This explains the conditions of the endowment upon which entitlement depends. Its purpose is not to exclude some of those encompassed by the wording. These are therefore conditions in substance.

Third: His statement, “Whoever among them dies leaving no child, his share shall pass to those of his generation,” is a conditional sentence expressing a condition and its consequence, serving as the predicate of anna (“that”), pronounced with an initial a; and the subject of anna is a pronoun

---

## Page 15370

anticipating the statement that follows; anna and its predicate are construed as a verbal noun. The resulting sense is thus: “I establish an endowment on this basis.”

Fourth: The preposition ‘alā (“upon”) denotes elevation. Thus, if a man says, “I establish an endowment on condition that such-and-such be the case,” or “I sell this to you on condition that you provide me with collateral,” the meaning is that he establishes an endowment resting upon this condition. The condition therefore becomes the foundation and basis of what rests above it, and a foundation precedes what is built upon it. This is characteristic of a condition. For this reason, those who distinguish between a condition and an exception do so on the grounds that a condition logically precedes that which is made conditional upon it. Even when placed later in the wording, it is treated as though it stood at the beginning. Were it placed at the beginning, all the clauses would be subject to it; the same therefore applies when it comes last.

Thus, if he says, “I establish an endowment for my children, then for their children, then for their children’s children, if they are poor,” this is equivalent to saying, “provided that they are poor.” Since one of these two formulations requires the pronoun to refer to all the generations, the other does likewise. Know that these proofs establish that the pronoun refers to all the generations in this case, both according to those who hold that an exception following several clauses applies to them all and according to those who hold that it applies only to the last, just as they agree on the corresponding rule concerning a condition.

Seventh argument: This objection is invalid according to al-Shāfi‘ī’s school in particular, and according to the schools of others as well. For if a man were to say to his wife:

---

## Page 15371

“You are divorced, then divorced, then divorced if you enter the house,” no divorce would take effect until she entered the house. She would then be divorced three times if the marriage had been consummated, or once if it had not. This is the position of Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad. Another position attributed to Abū Yūsuf and Muhammad is that a woman whose marriage has not been consummated is divorced three times, just as they hold when wāw (“and”) is used. This is also al-Shāfi‘ī’s position and the stronger of the two opinions within Ahmad’s school.

Abū Hanīfa, Judge Abū Ya‘lā of Ahmad’s school, and a group who agreed with him said: Rather, only the final clause is subject to the condition. If the marriage has been consummated, two divorces take immediate effect, while one remains subject to the condition. If the marriage has not been consummated, one divorce takes immediate effect, ending the marriage without a right of revocation. The other two therefore cannot validly be effected, either immediately or conditionally.

They argued: This is because thumma (“then”) indicates sequence with an interval. It is therefore as though he said, “You are divorced,” then paused, then said, “You are divorced if you enter the house.”

Those holding the first position, however, said: Thumma is a conjunction which, like wāw, gives the conjoined expression the same ruling as the expression to which it is conjoined. Yet wāw indicates only conjunction and a shared ruling, without indicating precedence, succession, or simultaneity, whereas thumma indicates a shared ruling together with succession. Their difference in meaning does not entail a difference in the shared ruling itself.

As for the claim that thumma indicates an interval, there are two answers. The first is that it signifies sequence as such; it can therefore connect both what follows immediately and what follows after an interval

---

## Page 15372

but since immediate succession has a conjunction specifically assigned to it—fa—thumma came to indicate the meaning distinctive to it: an interval. Otherwise, if a man said to a wife with whom he had consummated the marriage, “You are divorced, then divorced,” or, “You are divorced and immediately divorced again,” there would be no difference between these two statements in this case.

The second answer is that the interval indicated by thumma concerns the meaning, not the wording. If someone says, “Zayd came, then ʿAmr,” this is a single, connected statement. It cannot be treated as though he had paused and then said, “ʿAmr.” Thus, anyone who maintains that saying, “You are divorced, then divorced,” is equivalent to pausing and then saying, “Divorced,” is mistaken. At most, it is equivalent to saying, “You are divorced by a divorce that will be followed, after an interval, by another divorce.” This does not prevent all the divorces from being subject to the condition, whether the condition precedes or follows them.

If, then, al-Shāfiʿī and these scholars hold that saying, “You are divorced, then divorced, then divorced if you enter the house,” is equivalent, in the case of a wife whose marriage has been consummated, to saying, “You are divorced and immediately divorced again and immediately divorced again if you enter the house,” or, “You are divorced, and divorced, and divorced if you enter the house”; and likewise hold this concerning the statement, “You are all divorced, then you are all divorced if you enter the house,” with the condition applying to all the clauses—how can his school be credited with the view that conjunction by a word indicating sequence requires restricting the condition to those it immediately follows, to the exclusion of those mentioned earlier? Why was it not said here: “If divorce has explicitly taken effect through the first two pronouncements, and nothing has been established that changes this, then it is necessary to

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space sufficient for two words occurs in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (pp. 227–228):
> Perhaps the blank should read [Imam Mālik], for he, may Allah have mercy on him, was the strictest of the imams regarding the applicability of istithnāʾ (the qualification “if Allah wills”) to divorce and manumission, whether pronounced to effect them or invoked in oaths. The shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, mentioned the disagreement concerning istithnāʾ in divorce. Discussing its applicability to divorce, he said, may Allah have mercy on him (35/284): “Some held that it does [not] (1) apply to divorce and manumission, whether pronouncing them to effect them or invoking them in oaths, whether in a conditional formula or an oath formula. This is the better-known of the two positions in Mālik’s school and one of the two reported positions of Ahmad.”
> In al-Fatāwā, the reading is “does apply,” which plainly contradicts the context.

---

## Page 15373

uphold the divorce that has taken effect?” Indeed, there is greater reason in the case of divorce to restrict the condition to the final clause, because the occurrence of either divorce does not depend on the other; both can be effected simultaneously. This is unlike grandchildren, who can only come into existence in generational succession. Here, therefore, there is a need to specify sequence that does not arise in divorce.

Moreover, it is agreed that conditions may validly be appended to a sale, an endowment, and similar transactions. Divorce is different: Shurayh and a group who share his view hold—and this is also a less-preponderant report from Ahmad—that divorce cannot validly be made contingent upon a condition stated after it. Similarly, some jurists among the followers of … (1) and others held that an exception cannot validly be made to a pronouncement of divorce. If, then, they have applied the condition to all the clauses joined in sequence by thumma (“then”), there is all the more reason to do so in other cases. For anyone who reflects upon it, this argument uproots the very foundation of the position of those who attribute a contrary view to al-Shāfiʿī’s school.

If it is said:
“But some Hanafī and Hanbalī jurists have held that view; do they also hold it here?”

We reply:
We have already explained that, according to the legal principles of all these scholars, the pronoun refers back to all the preceding referents, since evidence establishes that reference on the grounds that the pronoun is used in its literal sense

---

## Page 15374

with reference to all the preceding referents, and that this is what the wording conveys. Moreover, those who hold this view distinguish this case from divorce in several respects.

First: In divorce, the condition is attached to the verb, which is … (1), and all those nouns joined by conjunctions fall within the scope of this verb. In meaning, they are its objects. This is comparable to a condition attached to an oath. If someone says:

“By Allah, I shall certainly do such-and-such and such-and-such, then such-and-such, if Allah wills,”

the condition is attached to the verb in the clause expressing what is sworn to, and the objects fall within the scope of its exception. The verb applies equally to all its objects. Thus, if its application to them is restricted by a qualification, its application to all of them is subject to that qualification.

This differs from saying:

“You are divorced, then divorced, if Zayd wills.”

Here, what is attached to the condition is the active participle, not the subject itself. The second predicate does not fall within the predication of the other predicate; rather, both are predicated of the subject. This is why disagreement arises here. The distinction is clear to anyone who reflects upon it.

Second: The condition in divorce—namely, the words “if you enter the house”—contains nothing requiring it to apply to all the clauses. This differs from the words “on condition that whoever among them dies,” since the pronoun requires reference back to all those mentioned.

Third: In divorce, neither of the two clauses is dependent upon the other, for

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank space approximately two words long in the original.

---

## Page 15375

the second pronouncement of divorce takes effect whether or not the first does. Thus, making it conditional does not entail making the first conditional, since the two are independent. They regard “then” as equivalent to an interval between the utterances, thereby removing the connection both in wording and in meaning. On their view, the first clause therefore remains unrelated to the condition.

As for the words “then for their children,” this clause is connected to the first through its pronoun, through existence, and through entitlement. It cannot validly be uttered without the first clause preceding it; its meaning cannot be realized without the first; and its beneficiaries have no entitlement until after those mentioned in the first. This holds whether or not an interval between the utterances is assumed. The first clause therefore cannot be treated as unrelated to the second, such that the condition would attach to the second alone.

What is established is that the disagreement concerns divorce alone. For if someone says, “By Allah, I shall certainly strike Zayd, then ʿAmr, then Bakr—if Allah wills,” the exception applies to them all.

Thus, the statement “I have endowed this for my children, then for their children, then for their children’s children, if they are poor” provides an even stronger connection than the words “if Allah wills,” because here the pronoun establishes that connection.

The eighth point: The distinction drawn by some jurists between a conjunction indicating sequence and one that merely conjoins was made specifically in relation to exceptions. The jurist then said, “The same applies to an attribute.” When the term “attribute” is used without qualification, it often means an adjective in the technical grammatical sense: a nominal modifier that agrees with the preceding word in grammatical inflection, as when you say:

---

## Page 15376

“I have endowed this for my children, then for the upright poor.” Here, it is quite plausible that the final adjective qualifies only the last clause. Our case, however, concerns conditions governing legal effect. These are closer to explicit conditional clauses than to exceptions, even if they may be called attributes in terms of their meaning.

The evidence that this is what he intended is his statement:

“If the conjunction is ‘and,’ with no intervening separation, al-Shāfiʿī’s position is that the exception applies to all the clauses. The same applies to an attribute.”

It is thus clear that he intended to identify this as al-Shāfiʿī’s position, alluding to Abū Hanīfa’s disagreement, since the latter applies it only to the final clause. Yet Abū Hanīfa says this only regarding exceptions and adjectival modifiers; he does not say it regarding conditions or attributes that function as conditions. There are therefore four categories here:

First: An exception introduced by the particle “except” following several clauses. The disagreement concerning this is well known.

Second: An exception introduced by conditional particles. Here, the exception applies to all the clauses.

Third: Adjectival modifiers of the noun they describe, similar constructions (1), and explanatory apposition. These are modifiers that restrict the scope of the preceding nouns, so they are equivalent to exceptions.

Fourth: Conditions conveyed in meaning through a preposition, as in the expression “on condition that he…”; or “you stipulate that he do so”; or through conjunctions, as in the expression “and among its conditions is such-and-such”

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank approximately one word long in the original.
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 228):
> Perhaps the blank should read [ka-l-badal (such as apposition)]. Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 15377

and similar expressions. These are equivalent to exceptions introduced by conditional particles. The governing principle is that anything completing a noun phrase belongs to the same category as an exception introduced by “except”; anything relating to the statement itself—that is, the predicative relationship between subject and predicate, or between a verb and its subject—is equivalent in meaning to an exception introduced by a conditional particle.

It is well known that subsequent prepositions and conditional particles relate to the preceding verb itself, namely, “I have endowed,” which constitutes the statement, or clause. Exceptions, substitutive apposition, adjectival modifiers, and explanatory apposition, by contrast, relate to the nouns that are the objects of this verb.

The statement of someone who makes this distinction may also be understood as referring to unrelated clauses, as when you say:

“I have endowed this for my children, then for so-and-so’s children, then for the poor, on condition that only those among them with dependants receive anything.”

In such a case, there may be strong grounds for restricting the condition to the final clause, since it is unrelated to the first clause and does not concern the same category. This differs from children and grandchildren, who belong to a single category. His statement must be understood in one of these two senses, or something similar, given the evidence we have cited that this was his intention. Moreover, even if this entails restricting the scope of his statement, that restriction is required for the reasons we have given.

For if he has taken the statement of the imams who said, “When an exception or an attribute follows several clauses joined by conjunctions, it applies to all the clauses,” and restricted it to certain conjunctions on the basis of what he considered evidence, then our restricting his statement on the basis of the explicit statements of theirs that we have cited

---

## Page 15378

—which require treating wāw (“and”) and thumma (“then”) alike—is all the more justified.

If he concedes that his words bear the interpretation we have given, well and good. Otherwise, we shall address him with the ninth argument:

This alleged distinction between a particle that simply joins items and one that places them in sequence has no basis in the language, customary usage, the statements of jurists or legal theorists, or the rulings of the Sharia. Moreover, the evidence cited to establish its validity is unsound. The distinction must therefore be unsound.

As for the first point, the scholars of language have said:

“Coordinating conjunctions are those that give what precedes them and what follows them the same grammatical inflection.”

They are of two kinds. One kind also joins the two in meaning: wāw, fāʾ, and thumma. Wāw indicates simple coordination and combination, except according to those who hold that it indicates sequence. Thumma indicates sequence in general, though it is also said to indicate an intervening delay. Fāʾ indicates a particular kind of sequence: immediate succession. These particles therefore do not differ in whether the coordinated element and the element to which it is joined share the same meaning; they differ only in the timing of that shared participation.

Thus, if it were said that coordination with wāw entails that the coordinated element and the element to which it is joined both fall within the scope of subsequent exceptions, adjectival modifiers, and similar qualifications attached to the clauses, whereas coordination with thumma does not entail their sharing in these subsequent qualifications, it would follow that thumma is not

---

## Page 15379

a particle conveying shared meaning where wāw does. Yet this plainly contradicts the position of the scholars of language; indeed, it contradicts what is known of the Arabic language itself.

Linguistic rules—that is, the meanings conveyed by expressions—are established through the usage of native speakers and what is transmitted from them. Since both transmitted evidence and usage establish that these two particles convey shared participation in meaning, claiming that one alone does so departs from the Arabic language and from what has been transmitted from its speakers.

As for customary usage, we have already explained that people understand such statements only as making the condition apply to all the clauses. This is self-evident from ordinary usage.

As for the statements of scholars among the jurists and legal theorists, they discussed an exception following several clauses. Some said that it applies to all of them. Others said that it applies to the last clause alone. Others said that if the two clauses are related, the exception applies to both, whereas if they are unrelated, it applies to the last. They then distinguished related clauses from unrelated ones, setting out several kinds of distinction.

Others held that coordination is open to all these interpretations. Still others advocated suspending judgment regarding all these views.

Yet none of them distinguished between coordination with wāw, fā, or thumma. Rather, their expression “clauses coordinated with one another” encompasses them all. Likewise, the jurists discussed this under the “Chapter on Oaths” and the “Chapter on Endowments.” They then based their conclusions on their respective principles, saying that when an exception or a descriptive qualification follows several clauses, it applies

---

## Page 15380

to all of them or to some of them. The person who drew this distinction has acknowledged that the imams stated this without qualification and that he himself introduced the distinction. Thus, nothing may be attributed to the imams except what they actually said.

As for the legal rulings, if someone says, “By Allah, I shall certainly strike Zayd, then Amr, then Bakr—if Allah wills,” the exception applies to them all.

Likewise, if he says, “On pain of divorce, I shall certainly strike this one, then this one, then this one.” Or he says, “I shall certainly accept the blood money; I shall certainly slaughter the sheep; I shall certainly cook it”—and other such cases.

As for the evidence he cited, he said:

“When the coordination uses a particle that indicates sequence, extending the exception to all those mentioned earlier is somewhat questionable and unclear. The exception certainly applies to those immediately preceding it, whereas its extension to all those mentioned earlier, when coordination uses a particle indicating sequence, is possible but not certain.

“When entitlement has been explicitly established by the wording of the endowment’s founder, and nothing has been established that would alter it, that entitlement must be upheld. It may not be altered on the basis of an uncertain possibility.”

We reply that this may be answered in several ways.

First, precisely the same holds for coordination with wāw. The exception’s extension to all those mentioned earlier is possible but not certain, whether the coordination uses a particle indicating sequence or one conveying shared participation without sequence. This is precisely the argument of those who maintain that the exception must be confined to the final clause.

---

## Page 15381

If he says, “The general applicability of the preceding clauses has been established, so it may not be restricted on the basis of an uncertain possibility”—our purpose here is not to refute this argument. Rather, we say that its necessary implication is that qualifying expressions apply exclusively to the final clause in all cases. Nothing in this argument warrants any distinction whatsoever between one coordinating particle and another.

What distinction would reasonable people draw when someone says, “I establish an endowment for my children and for the poor, unless they are sinful”?

Indeed, the proponent of this view may have come to consider it more likely that the exception applies exclusively to the final clause, yet feared contradicting al-Shāfiʿī, so that his own preference troubled him. We have, however, already explained that our case does not fall within the disputed area. The disagreement concerns only exceptions or grammatical adjectival qualifications. As for conditions and conditional qualifications, there is no disagreement among the jurists concerning them.

In short, if someone concedes that an exception applies to all the clauses coordinated by wāw, his invocation of this argument invalidates his own concession and therefore cannot be accepted. Conceding a ruling necessarily entails conceding the invalidity of whatever establishes its opposite. Thus, no argument from him establishing that the exception does not apply to all the clauses can be accepted.

Second: His assertion, “The exception certainly applies to those immediately preceding it,” is not conceded. Rather, the exception may apply exclusively to the first clause when evidence indicates this. The speaker may intend and mean precisely that, even if

---

## Page 15382

he is swearing an oath as the wronged party. For if someone says, “Fight the People of the Book, show enmity towards them, and hate them, unless they pay the jizyah (poll tax),” the exception applies only to the first clause. Allah, Glorified be He, has said:

“Let not the believers take the disbelievers as allies instead of the believers. Whoever does so has no connection with Allah, unless you protect yourselves against them as a precaution.”

This exception apparently applies to the first clause. He, Glorified be He, also said:

“A declaration of release from obligations, from Allah and His Messenger, to those polytheists with whom you have made treaties. So travel freely throughout the land for four months”—up to His words—“except those polytheists with whom you have made treaties and who have not failed you in anything or supported anyone against you. Fulfil your treaty with them until its term has ended.”

This is not an exception to what immediately precedes it, but rather to the opening statement.

A number of scholars have said, concerning His words, “You would have followed Satan, except a few,” that “a few” refers back to His words, “When news of security or fear comes to them, they broadcast it”—“except a few.” Thus, this exception applies to a clause separated from it by several other clauses.

“Earlier and later placement in the Quran” is a field of study on which scholars, including Imam Ahmad and others, have written, and it encompasses this case. A similar case is when an exception comes later in the wording but belongs earlier in the intended meaning.

Moreover, only someone unfamiliar with Arabic would deny that its word order permits earlier and later placement, or that a parenthetical clause may separate a coordinated element from the element to which it is joined, or separate other elements. He, Glorified be He, has said: “A group of the People of the Book said, ‘Believe in what has been sent down to those who

---

## Page 15383

believe, at the beginning of the day, and reject it at its end, so that they may turn back.’”

“‘And believe no one except those who follow your religion’—say, ‘True guidance is Allah’s guidance’—‘lest anyone be given the like of what you have been given.’”

His words, “lest anyone be given,” complete the statement of the People of the Book. That is, “out of aversion to His bestowing it”; this is the object of “believe.” Yet the two are separated by His words, “Say, ‘True guidance is Allah’s guidance,’” which constitute an intervening sentence, not part of the speech of the People of the Book. Which, then, is the greater separation: between a verb and its object, or between an exception and the statement to which it applies? If the exception’s application to the final clause is not certain, it need not apply to that clause. Indeed, the context may provide stronger grounds for applying it to the first. Our case falls into this category, as explained above.

Third is his statement: “If entitlement is established unequivocally by the endower’s wording, and nothing is established that modifies it, that entitlement must be upheld.”

Our first response is that our case does not fall into this category. The wording, “for his children, then for their children,” does not unequivocally establish that one generation succeeds another as a whole, for it can also mean that individuals succeed individuals. This response, however, is required in our particular case when addressing someone who wishes to bring it within the general scope of that statement.

There are also those who say from the outset: “We do not concede that entitlement is established unequivocally by the endower’s wording in any instance where it is followed by an exception or a condition. Wording is unequivocal only when it is not connected to something that modifies it. Since modification is possible, the condition for its being unequivocal is in doubt. And whenever the condition for

---

## Page 15384

a ruling is in doubt, that ruling is not established. There can be no unequivocal wording while modification remains possible, especially when that possibility is so strong that most scholars regard it as the more likely interpretation.”

If he says, “The ground for including them is present, while the impediment to excluding them is uncertain,” I reply: According to those who deny that an effective cause can admit exceptions, I do not concede that the ground for including them is present. That ground consists of wording unaccompanied by anything that excludes them. I cannot know that this wording is unaccompanied by anything that excludes them until I know that this exception does not exclude them; and this condition remains uncertain.

According to those who allow such exceptions, however, I concede that the ground is present, but its producing an effect depends on the absence of a counteracting impediment. Here, something capable of constituting such an impediment exists. Until evidence establishes otherwise, it remains capable of counteracting; without such evidence, the ground does not produce its effect. Mere capacity to counteract confers no advantage. This is precisely the debate between those who hold that an exception applies to all the clauses and those who restrict it to the final clause.

He then begins afresh: If, for example, someone says, “I dedicate this endowment to my children, then to the poor, except the sinful,” the disputant says: “‘My offspring’ unequivocally denotes his children, whereas ‘the sinful’ may refer exclusively to the poor.”

We reply: This is countered by an equivalent argument. “The sinful” unequivocally denotes all sinful people, since it is a collective noun with the definite article. Since it is general, it must encompass every sinful person. The claim that it refers exclusively to the sinful among the poor, rather than among the children, therefore requires evidence restricting its scope.

---

## Page 15385

Thus, preserving the general scope of “children” because no restriction is known is no more justified than preserving the general scope of “the sinful” because no evidence restricting it is known. Rather, excluding them is the stronger position, for several reasons.

First: The basic presumption is that they are not included in the endowment. Two general expressions conflict over their inclusion and exclusion, so the argument against their inclusion remains unopposed by any stronger counterargument.

Second: We know with certainty that they are excluded from one of the two clauses. One of the two conjoined general expressions is therefore restricted. Extending that restriction to its coordinate counterpart is more appropriate than introducing a restriction into an expression that is not conjoined with it.

Third: A conjoined clause and the clause to which it is joined are equivalent to a single clause. Once a restriction applies to this combined clause, its general force is weakened. By contrast, the general scope of the expression stating the exception has undergone no restriction.

Fourth: Sinfulness being an impediment implies that, in the endower’s estimation, it outweighs the ground for granting a share. Once we know with certainty that it takes precedence in one instance, giving it precedence in another is more appropriate than giving precedence to something whose superiority has not been established in any instance.

Fifth: As for his expression, “the endower’s explicit wording,” if he means the apparent meaning of the wording, then, according to this view, applying the exception to all the clauses is likewise its apparent meaning. There is therefore no distinction between the two. But if he means unequivocal wording that admits only one meaning, then it is well known that

---

## Page 15386

every expression that admits an exception must either specify a number or have general scope, and general expressions convey apparent meanings, not unequivocal ones.

Sixth: His statement, “It may not be altered on the basis of an uncertain possibility.” We accept this principle, for, in our view, the exception’s application to all the clauses is not an uncertain possibility. Rather, it too constitutes explicit wording in the first sense mentioned above.

The evidence for this is its prevalence in actual usage. Allah Most High says:

“And those who do not invoke another god besides Allah”—up to His words—“And whoever does that will meet a penalty. His punishment will be multiplied on the Day of Resurrection, and he will remain therein in humiliation—except those who repent.”

The exception applies to His words “will meet,” “will multiply,” and “will remain.”

He, Glorified be He, also says:

“Those are cursed by Allah and by those who curse—except those who repent, reform, and make the truth clear.”

Allah Most High says:

“Their recompense is that upon them is the curse of Allah, the angels, and all mankind. They will remain therein forever; their punishment will not be lightened, nor will they be reprieved—except those who repent thereafter and reform. Indeed, Allah is All-Forgiving, Most Merciful.”

Allah Most High also says:

“The recompense of those who wage war against Allah and His Messenger and strive to spread corruption in the land is only that they be killed or crucified”—up to His words—“That is a disgrace for them in this world, and in the Hereafter they will have a tremendous punishment—except those who repent before you gain power over them.”

Here, then, is an exception following several clauses. For in this discussion, a “clause” means an expression from whose scope some instances can validly be excluded: a general noun or a numeral. It does not mean a sentence in the sense of an utterance

---

## Page 15387

composed of two nouns, a noun and a verb, or a noun and a particle.

Reports from the Companions establish that His words, “Except those who repent,” in the verse concerning accusations of unchastity apply to both clauses. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No man should lead another in prayer within the latter’s domain of authority, nor sit in his place of honor, except with his permission.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said:

“An Arab has no superiority over a non-Arab, nor a non-Arab over an Arab, nor a black person over a white person, nor a white person over a black person, except through piety.”

Such instances are numerous in the Quran and Sunnah. Indeed, anyone who examines the exceptions in the Quran and Sunnah that follow several clauses will find that most apply to all of them. This concerns exceptions. As for conditional and adjectival qualifications, their instances are so numerous that only Allah could count them.

Since the predominant usage in the Quran, the Sunnah, and Arab speech is for an exception to apply to all the clauses, the presumption is that an individual instance follows the predominant pattern. For either the exception has been conventionally assigned both senses literally—in which case the presumption is against multiple literal meanings—or it has been assigned only the less common sense, which would require its remaining uses to be figurative. Figurative usage runs contrary to the default presumption, so its frequent occurrence likewise runs contrary to that presumption.

Thus, if we regard the exception as literal in its predominant usage and figurative in its less common usage, we will have upheld both the presumption against multiple literal meanings and the presumption against figurative usage where frequencies of usage differ. This is preferable to abandoning the presumption altogether.

---

## Page 15388

Once it is established that the exception’s application to all the clauses is textually indicated—in the sense that this is the wording’s apparent meaning—the desired point is proved. The purpose here is not to establish this issue, but only to identify the points open to challenge. The argument he mentioned applies to every restriction introduced by an attached qualification: preserving the general scope of the qualified wording is no more deserving than preserving the general scope of the qualifying wording. Indeed, the latter is more deserving, since it is a general expression whose general scope remains intact. Moreover, repeating the qualification after every clause is stylistically objectionable.

Thus, if someone were to say:

“I have established an endowment for my children, on condition that, if any of them dies leaving offspring or leaving none, his share shall pass to his offspring or to those of his own generation; then for my grandchildren on this condition; then for my great-grandchildren on this condition,”

this would be regarded as wording for which more eloquent and better alternatives exist.

We then say to anyone who disputes our position:

It is known with certainty that founders of endowments generally intend the condition to apply to every generation. Yet they do not use this unusual formulation; rather, they confine themselves to the wording mentioned at the outset. Were that insufficient to convey their intentions, they would not have confined themselves to it. Allah bears witness—and Allah suffices as a witness—that we know with certainty that the reasoning in the matter before us is conclusive, not a matter of conjectural reasoning. Yet Allah, glorified is He, has the power to make what is certainty to some ignorance to others. Discussing this is regarded as needless elaboration. Had the need not arisen because some imagine that those who dispute this matter have grounds to rely upon, or that it is a matter open to independent legal judgment, we would not have discussed it at such length.

---

## Page 15389

If it is said:

“What favors referring the pronoun to the final clause here is that this clause is joined to the preceding one by wāw (‘and’), and another clause is joined to it by wāw. This entails that it differs from the first clause regarding generation-by-generation succession. Here, the endowment is shared among the generations, so the only provision common to this clause and the first is that the endowment applies to them all; the manner of entitlement differs. This entails that the clause stands independently and that what follows it applies exclusively to it.

“For when clauses are separated by a condition attached to each, the final condition necessarily applies exclusively to the final clause. This is due solely to the differences in their provisions in that case, and such differences exist here.”

This may be answered in several ways.

First, your statement, “It is joined by wāw, and another clause is joined to it by wāw,” implies that the clause in question is the word “progeny.” If “progeny” and “posterity” have the same meaning, then nothing distinct in meaning has been conjoined to it. If they have two different meanings, the pronoun must refer to the clause being conjoined, not the clause to which it is conjoined.

Second, regarding your statement, “This entails that it differs from the first clause regarding succession,” we have already disputed this. We have explained that some jurists regard this endowment as passing from generation to generation until the Day of Resurrection. For the wording:

“Then for his grandchildren and his progeny and his posterity”

neither affirms nor negates succession. However, since the default is the absence of succession, we excluded it when the wording was unrestricted.

---

## Page 15390

Thus, since he stipulated succession here in his initial wording—bearing in mind that a rational person would not draw distinctions in such a case, but would rely on what he mentioned at the outset—repeating the condition was merely a matter of latitude in expression. Our purpose here, however, is to establish this point.

Third:

Even if we concede that it entails shared entitlement among the conjoined terms, this does not entail that the two clauses differ regarding the provision they share by virtue of the conjunction. At most, this means that he treated the fourth generation and all subsequent generations as a single class, just as the offspring of the elder and the younger were grouped together within the first generation. The elder and younger children form a single class; he did not make some succeed others on the basis of age.

His statement, “This entails that it differs from the first clause regarding succession,” is therefore ambiguous.

If he means that this clause differs from those clauses in how it treats its individual members, that is not so. Rather, it is a clause encompassing its members on the basis of shared entitlement, not succession.

If he means that no other clause was made to follow this one in succession, then the first clause was not made to follow any other. This is simply a necessary consequence of its being the final clause, not a material distinction—just as the first clause’s not being made to follow another was not a material distinction.

If he means that this clause encompasses generations at different levels, unlike the earlier clauses, that distinction concerns neither the meaning conveyed by the wording nor the provision indicated by it. Moreover, even greater disparities may arise within the earlier clauses. There may, for example, be twenty grandchildren, with seventy years between the eldest and the youngest, and the eldest may have

---

## Page 15391

children before his siblings are born, so that his children, grandchildren, and great-grandchildren die before his siblings have all died out. Perhaps only a few of his descendants and posterity remain, and they too die out. These differences concern actual circumstances, not what the wording signifies.

Fourth: His statement:

“Thus, the only provision it shares with the first clause is its designation as an endowment.”

The answer is:

There is no difference between them at all. The first clause encompasses its individual members just as the second encompasses its own, although the second generally includes more individuals. This has no bearing on the matter.

His statement, “The manner is different,” is disputed. An endowment for one’s children operates in the same manner as an endowment for one’s descendants and posterity: the members of each group share in it.

Fifth:

Even if we concede that there is a difference between them beyond what the wording signifies, this does not undermine their sharing in the provision conveyed by the conjunction. Were this difference in manner valid, it would be comparable to His saying:

“Every soul shall taste death.”

The experience of tasting death varies greatly, but the wording does not indicate these differences. They therefore do not preclude the shared applicability conveyed by the general wording.

Sixth:

The difference in manner is indicated by the conjunction, but this does not make the latter clause independent or mean that what follows applies exclusively to it. This is like saying: “I have established an endowment for my male and female children, my sons’ children, and my great-grandchildren, on condition that whoever among them dies

---

## Page 15392

Rather, the separation that severs the second clause from the first consists in inserting a condition between the two clauses. For example, someone might say: “I have established an endowment for my children, on condition that they are poor, then for my grandchildren, on condition that they are upright.” The second condition does not apply to what precedes it, because the first clause has already been followed by its own condition. Inserting a condition between two coordinated elements prevents them from sharing all the same provisions. This differs from a case where the difference involves no intervening wording.

Seventh: His statement:

“This is only because the provisions differ.”

We reply: We do not concede this. Rather, it is because of the intervening wording, which prevents the provisions conveyed by the wording from applying jointly to both.

If, however, the difference between the two coordinated elements arises from the meaning of the latter element’s wording, this is true of any two coordinated elements belonging to different categories. There is a difference between separating two clauses by an explicitly stated condition and the wording of one clause having a different meaning from that of the other. This is clear to anyone who reflects on it.

If it is said:

“There is a second consideration favouring this interpretation: making the condition apply exclusively to the final clause conveys something not otherwise indicated by the wording, namely, that the descendants are prevented from sharing in the portion of someone who dies without leaving children. Were it not for this condition, they would share in their entire entitlement, received from those above them and from those who die leaving children or leaving none. This is unlike the case where it applies to all

---

## Page 15393

the clauses; in that case, it would merely confirm what was already understood, since we would in any event assign the share of someone who dies without children to those of his own generation.”

This may be answered in several ways:

First: We have already explained that this supposed benefit is invalid. No reasonable person would say:

“All these people, of both earlier and later generations, share in the endowment. If someone dies without children, his siblings alone receive his share, to the exclusion of his parents and paternal uncles. But if someone dies leaving children, no one has an exclusive entitlement to his share, neither his children nor anyone else.”

No one has done this, nor would anyone who fully considers its implications. It is equivalent to saying:

“Give to those distantly related to me and to the deceased, and deprive those closely related to me and to the deceased.”

As for the assertion that people customarily intend such an arrangement, we know of no one who has intended it.

Second: We have already disputed that this wording entails joint entitlement, so the supposed benefit falls away.

Third: Applying the condition to all the clauses has several benefits.

The first is that its express wording indicates that the share of someone who dies without children passes to those of his own generation. Its implication, which is stronger than its express wording, indicates that the share of someone who dies leaving children passes to his children, as previously explained.

---

## Page 15394

The second benefit:

His statement, “For his children, then his children’s children”—through to his words, “in perpetuity, as long as they have descendants, and forever, as long as one generation succeeds another”—establishes his descendants’ entitlement to the endowment. If someone dies leaving only people of his own generation and his grandchildren, the condition excludes those of his own generation, while the children remain included under the first wording when read together with the second.

Thus, his statement, “For my children, then my children’s children,” taken together with his statement, “On condition that the share of anyone who dies without children passes to his siblings,” shows us that the share of someone who dies leaving children passes to his children. This is because they fall within the general scope of his words, “My children’s children.” Those of the deceased’s own generation were also included within that general scope; when the condition excluded them, the child’s offspring remained included. The same applies whenever a general expression encompasses two categories and one is excluded: the other necessarily remains. This is a second indication that the share of someone who dies leaving children passes to his children. It arises from the general wording, within whose scope only they remain, and is distinct from the indication conveyed by implication.

If you wish, you may express this by saying: the deceased’s share belongs either to the children or to the children’s children, since his words, “For my children, then for their children,” indicate that entitlement to the endowment is confined to these two categories. Since he has barred the children from receiving the share of someone who dies leaving children, it must belong to the other category.

It remains possible to object: “There may be someone who belongs neither to the deceased’s own generation nor to his offspring.”

We reply: If the benefit is evident in some cases, the intended point is established. The case under discussion is one such case, for we have addressed only the deceased’s share: is it to be given to his siblings or to his children?

---

## Page 15395

If, however, the deceased had a paternal uncle, for example, we would say:

Excluding those of the deceased’s own generation indicates that those more distantly related to him are also excluded. Those of his own generation were not excluded because of their more distant position in the endowment, since his children occupy a still more distant position. We have already explained that this entails giving the share to his children in most cases. It is therefore clear that those of his own generation were excluded because of their more distant relationship to the deceased. This consideration applies even more strongly to his paternal uncles, who are therefore all the more deserving of exclusion when he leaves children.

The third benefit:

It provides evidence that he intended succession from individuals to their respective descendants, rather than from one generation as a whole to the next as a whole. It is as though he had said:

“On condition that if any of them dies leaving children, his share shall pass to his children.”

If it is objected:

“This assigns two different meanings to a single expression. In the first instance, its function is to establish succession from individuals to their respective descendants; in the second, it establishes that the deceased’s generation alone is entitled to his share. Anyone who disallows a single expression from bearing two literal meanings, two figurative meanings, or both a literal and a figurative meaning must disallow this. As for anyone who permits it, we say: ‘On that assumption, when something is explicitly established by the founder’s wording, it cannot be altered on the strength of ambiguous wording open to competing interpretations.’”

The reply is that this objection is extremely weak for several reasons:

First: The person raising it presented it as establishing a second argument for the pronoun’s referring to the final clause, distinct from his initial argument concerning the exception’s applying to the final clause. Yet ultimately—when addressing the view of those who permit a single expression to bear both its meanings—he relied on that same answer. It therefore does not constitute a separate argument.

---

## Page 15396

Second:

We say: This rests on the assumption that, in the final generation, the condition specifies the amount of the share passing from someone who dies without children to those of his own generation, while the share of someone who dies leaving children is shared by the entire generation. This is rejected on the two grounds already explained.

Third:

Even if we conceded this, it would not constitute using an expression in two different meanings. Rather, it would constitute using a single expression in a single meaning, and thus belong to the category of univocal expressions. The wording explicitly provides for transferring the share of someone who dies without children to his own generation. This provision applies afresh in every clause. Moreover, making transfer to that generation conditional upon the existence of children indicates that he intended succession from individuals to their respective descendants. This is an indication by necessary implication.

When an expression directly signifies one meaning and necessarily implies another, this does not fall within the disputed category, as is the case with most expressions. Its indication of succession from individuals to their respective descendants arises solely from making the generation’s entitlement to the deceased’s share conditional upon his leaving no children. Reason then establishes that, had he intended succession from one generation as a whole to the next as a whole, he would not have imposed this condition, since succession between generations as wholes and the imposition of this condition are incompatible. The incompatibility of these two meanings is a rational proposition understood after conceiving each meaning, because one of the two expressions signifies both by linguistic convention.

This resembles their understanding, from His words:

“His gestation and weaning take thirty months,”

together with His words, exalted is He:

“They shall breastfeed their children for two complete years,”

that the minimum duration of pregnancy is six months. There are many similar examples.

---

## Page 15397

Fourth:

Even supposing that this falls under “using a single expression in both its meanings,” we do not concede that disallowing this is correct. Indeed, this is not the position of any of the recognized imams; it is merely the view of a group of speculative theologians. The statements of the general body of the Companions, the Successors, the jurists and the linguists, as well as most speculative theologians, indicate that this is permissible. Why, then, should his words not be understood in accordance with what he himself considers valid and argues in support of?

Fifth:

Their claim that an explicit provision cannot be set aside on the basis of a possible interpretation has already been answered. We have explained that there is no explicit provision here; rather, it is the possible interpretation that is set aside by the explicit provision. We have also mentioned that this argument is expressly stated by the leading imams.

The fourth benefit:

By this condition, he intended to prevent an interruption in the endowment and to prevent all its beneficiaries from sharing in the portion of someone who dies without children. He thereby indicated that, by his words “leaving his children,” he meant succession from individuals to their respective descendants.

If it is objected:

Applying the condition to all the clauses would entail an interruption in the endowment at an intermediate stage. It is therefore preferable to interpret the wording in a way that avoids such an interruption. For the share of someone who dies leaving children would pass neither to his generation, in accordance with the condition, nor to his children, in accordance with the unqualified order of succession.

If you say, “If we construe it as based on succession from individuals to their respective descendants, it would not entail an interruption,”

---

## Page 15398

then we answer this with the earlier argument: the generation’s entitlement is established by the apparent wording and cannot therefore be set aside on the basis of an uncertain possibility.

The reply is:

First, this line of reasoning is incomplete without that argument. Yet it was advanced only to corroborate that argument, and corroborating evidence must be distinct from, and independent of, what it supports. If this line of reasoning is incomplete without that argument, its validity depends upon the validity of that argument. A derivative argument cannot be stronger than its foundation or lend it strength. Rather, using it to strengthen that argument amounts to supporting something with itself. This is a form of begging the question.

Since this depends upon that line of reasoning, which we have already answered, this too has been answered.

We further say:

Benefit is precluded on three grounds. First, the endowment is restricted to the children and then their children. Thus, if someone dies leaving a child, his share must go either to his siblings, their children, his own children, or his paternal uncles, since the condition restricts the endowment to the children and then the children’s children.

These beneficiaries belong either to his own generation; to a generation above him—his paternal uncles and others like them, since he is entitled to nothing while his father is alive; or to a generation below him—his children, his siblings’ children, and others of their generation.

As for his own generation, they are excluded by the aforementioned restriction governing their entitlement. As for their children, they are excluded for three

---

## Page 15399

reasons. First, by a fortiori reasoning: their father is closer both to the deceased and to the founder of the endowment. If the share is not transferred to the nearer relative, there is all the more reason not to transfer it to the more distant one.

Second, whether the stipulated order of succession applies to the beneficiaries collectively or individually, they are not entitled in these circumstances: the preceding generation has not died out, and their fathers have not died.

Third, in these circumstances they are not among the beneficiaries of the endowment. What they would receive in their own right has not yet passed to them, so what they would receive derivatively cannot pass to them either.

As for the paternal uncles, the deceased could not conceivably have been entitled to anything while his paternal uncles were alive except according to our position. Thus, the very scenario is impossible on the opposing party’s view.

Since the existence of paternal uncles entails the validity of this position, it cannot also entail something that invalidates it. One and the same thing cannot entail both the validity and the invalidity of a position.

It may, however, be said: “The first deceased left only siblings and a child. Then his child died, leaving a child of his own and paternal uncles.”

We reply: excluding the siblings when there is a child implies that the paternal uncles are likewise excluded.

This is the substance of the answer: excluding his siblings implies the exclusion of his paternal uncles, as explained above.

The second ground—ruling out an interruption in entitlement—is that assigning the deceased’s share to his siblings rather than to the other beneficiaries of the endowment implies that it should be assigned to his child, as explained above.

---

## Page 15400

Third: this shows that the order of succession mentioned above is one in which individuals succeed individuals. We have already explained this. May Allah, Glorified is He, guide us to what He loves and is pleased with. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of the worlds. May Allah bless our master Muhammad, his family, and his Companions, and grant them abundant peace.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About an endowment for four persons: ‘Amr, Yāqūtah, Jahmah, and ‘Ā’ishah, its proceeds being distributed among them with each male receiving the share of two females. If any of them dies leaving a child, a grandchild, or descendants and posterity, however remote, whatever he had been receiving from it shall pass to his children, then to his grandchildren, then to his descendants and posterity, and thereafter to those succeeding him, however remote, with each male receiving the share of two females. If any of them dies leaving no child, grandchild, descendants, or posterity, his share shall pass as an endowment to his surviving siblings, then to their descendants and posterity, with each male receiving the share of two females, according to the condition and order of succession stated above. If these siblings, the beneficiaries of the endowment, have no descendants or posterity remaining, or if they all die without any one of them leaving descendants, the endowment shall pass to captives, then to the poor.

Then ‘Umar died, leaving Fātimah, and Fātimah died, leaving ‘Aynāshī, daughter of Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Ya‘lā. Then ‘Aynāshī died without descendants or posterity, and none remained of

---

## Page 15401

the descendants of these four except the daughter of Ismā‘īl ibn Abī Ya‘lā, both women being descendants of Jahmah. As for these two interests held by ‘Aynāshī after her father’s death, do they pass to her sister Ruqayyah? Or to her, or to her paternal cousin Safiyyah?

He replied:

This share which ‘Aynāshī received through her mother passes to the two aforementioned cousins. Her paternal half-sister may not receive it exclusively, because the founder of the endowment stipulated:

“If any of these siblings, the beneficiaries of the endowment, dies leaving no child, grandchild, descendants, or posterity, his share shall pass as an endowment to his siblings, then to their descendants and posterity, according to the condition and order of succession stated above.”

This wording covers both those who leave no descendants from the outset and those whose descendants subsequently die out. Thus, whenever the line of any of these siblings dies out, his share passes to his siblings, then to their children. Had the founder not intended this, he would have left unstated the ruling concerning someone who initially left descendants whose line later died out, without specifying who should receive his share. Such an interpretation is inadmissible, because he provided for the endowment to pass to captives and the poor only when neither he nor the beneficiaries had any descendants or posterity remaining. Thus, whenever they leave descendants—even if only one of them does—nothing passes to captives or the poor. This requires the share of any beneficiary whose line has died out to pass to the surviving siblings, which is the conclusion sought.

Furthermore, he divided the circumstances of a deceased beneficiary among the four into two cases: either he had a child, or descendants and posterity, or he did not. If he did,

---

## Page 15402

his share would pass to his child, then to his grandchild, then to his descendants and posterity; if he did not, it would pass to his siblings, then to their children. The latter category should therefore encompass everything not included in the first, so that the provisions cover all circumstances. This is the apparent intention of the speaker; otherwise, cases would be left unprovided for, provisions rendered inoperative, and—according to one view—the endowment invalidated. The circumstances rule out this possibility. If the latter category encompasses everything excluded from the first, it includes anyone who has no child, anyone whose child has no child, and anyone who has no posterity. Accordingly, whichever of these four has no posterity, his share passes to his siblings, then to his posterity.

Furthermore, the founder expressly stated that if any of them died without posterity, his share would pass to his siblings, then to their children. This intention does not differ between someone who leaves no child and someone who leaves a child who, in turn, leaves a child. A reasonable person does not intend to distinguish between these two circumstances. Consistent experience establishes that a reasonable person does not intend to treat equivalent cases differently, so his words must not be interpreted in that way. Rather, they should be understood according to what the circumstances and established custom indicate, unless the wording supports a more compelling interpretation. Since the absence of descendants from the outset and their subsequent extinction are equivalent with respect to the share passing to the siblings, the wording must be understood accordingly.

Know that anyone who examines the matter closely will recognize with certainty, from the circumstances, that this alone is what the founder intended

---

## Page 15403

and the wording admits of this interpretation, with no other possible reading being more compelling. It must therefore unquestionably be understood in this way.

Furthermore, an endowment is intended to provide support, so the provision governing a deceased beneficiary must be made clear for every generation. Thus, his statement:

“Any of them who dies leaving neither a child, nor a grandchild, nor descendants, nor posterity”

is equivalent to saying: “Any of them who is dead and has no posterity.” For the extinction of his descendants after his death is equivalent to their not existing at the time of his death. There is therefore no difference between this statement and his saying, “Any of them who dies and has no child,” or, “Any of them who dies and did not have a child.”

Although this expression may sometimes be understood as referring only to the absence of descendants at the time of death, its wording also admits of their absence without such a restriction. Thus, if the speaker were to say, “This is what I intended,” his words would still bear that meaning intelligibly.

Since the wording admits of this meaning, and no other wording covers the case at issue, the case must be included under it. For when a choice must be made, ruling in a case according to what the founder’s wording, the circumstances, and customary usage support is unquestionably the course that must be followed.

Once this is established, the paternal uncle of Aynāshī’s grandfather is now deceased and has neither a child, nor a grandchild, nor descendants, nor posterity. His share therefore belongs to his three siblings, passing to their descendants and posterity.
