At the time when his line died out, these two women were the only descendants of his, so they must share equally in Aynāshī’s portion. The same applies to everyone whose line has died out: his share passes to the descendants of his siblings, unless

---

## Page 15404

any descendants remain of their father, from whom the endowment passed to him, or of his mother, from whom it passed to him. In that case, the surviving descendants are entitled to their mother’s or father’s share, since they are covered by his words: “Any of them who dies leaving a child or a grandchild.”

Know that unless the wording is understood in this way, this person’s share would constitute an endowment whose ultimate beneficiaries are unspecified, for he said:

“Any of them who dies leaving a child, his share shall belong to his child, then to his grandchild, then to his descendants and posterity.”

He did not specify to whom it would pass after that line of descendants died out. However, at the end of the stipulation, he made clear that it would not pass to the captives and the poor until the descendants of all four had died out. These words therefore imply that it is to be allocated to the descendants. These two women are among those descendants, stand in the same degree of kinship, and are the only ones remaining. They must therefore share in it.

Beyond these two interpretations, the only remaining possibility is that his words, “Any of them who dies,” refer to the four and their descendants. It would then be said: Aynāshī died leaving a paternal half-sister and a paternal uncle’s daughter, so the share would belong to her sister. This interpretation is unquestionably invalid; if a judge were to rule on that basis, his judgment would not be enforceable.

For the pronoun in the first occurrence, “Any of them who dies,” refers to the four. Thus, the pronoun in the second occurrence, “Any of them who dies,” likewise refers to those four. For if someone says:

“Of these four, whoever does such and such, do such and such for him; and whoever does such and such, do such and such for his child,”

it is necessarily understood that the second pronoun has the same referent as the first.

Furthermore, he said:

“Any of them who dies without a child, his share shall revert

---

## Page 15405

to his surviving siblings.” This can only be said of someone who has siblings who will survive him. We know this to be the case with these four, whereas an individual among their descendants might have no surviving siblings. Had that meaning been intended, he would have said: “To his siblings, if he has any,” or: “Any of them who dies leaving siblings,” just as he said regarding children: “Any of them who dies leaving a child.” This is perfectly clear.

Furthermore, even supposing that the share of any beneficiary who died leaving siblings were to pass to those siblings, this would apply only to siblings who shared with him in the share received from his father or mother—not to siblings unconnected with the share he left behind—as established in the relevant sections of the books of jurisprudence according to the well-known schools. Aynāshī received this share only from her mother. Her sister Ruqayya is unrelated to her mother, since she is her sister through their father alone. Thus, her paternal half-sister and her paternal uncle’s daughter stand in the same relation to her mother’s share. This is clear to anyone who reflects upon it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About a founder who established an endowment for his two sons, Umar and Abd Allāh, to be divided equally between them in two halves throughout their lives, for as long as they live and remain alive; then, after them, for all their children, their children’s children, and their descendants and posterity, in perpetuity for as long as they continue to have descendants

---

## Page 15406

generation after generation. The aforementioned Abd Allāh died, leaving children. Umar brought Abd Allāh’s children before a judge who held that entitlement should pass in succession, asking him to remove them from possession of the endowment and hand it over to him. The judge removed Abd Allāh’s children from possession and handed it over to Umar, ruling that he belonged to the first generation. Does that ruling apply to all generations or not?

Then Umar died, leaving children, who took possession of the endowment without a judicial ruling. Abd Allāh’s children asked a judge who favoured concurrent entitlement to rule that they should share in the endowment. Their grounds were that the founder had joined the children, descendants and posterity together in entitlement after Abd Allāh and Umar using the conjunction “and,” which indicates shared rather than successive entitlement, and that his words “generation after generation” do not require successive entitlement. Is a ruling granting them a share valid or not? Does the first judge’s ruling giving Umar precedence over Abd Allāh’s children contradict a ruling that Umar’s children and Abd Allāh’s children should share in the endowment? May a third judge annul this ruling and its enforcement?

He answered:

Merely ruling that one of the two original brothers is entitled to the entire endowment after his brother’s death does not make that ruling applicable to all generations, nor does it constitute a ruling granting his children what was awarded to him.

Concerning the founder’s words, “then for their children”: do they prescribe succession by one group as a whole to another, or succession by individuals to individuals, whereby each deceased beneficiary’s share passes to his children? The jurists hold two views on this.

Likewise, concerning his words, “and their children after them, generation after generation”: do they prescribe successive or concurrent entitlement? There are two views. Thus, if the judge ruled that Umar was entitled to the whole endowment after Abd Allāh’s death, this was based on his understanding

---

## Page 15407

that one group as a whole succeeds another. Thus, when Umar dies, that judge may hold that successive entitlement applies only to the first generation, as the apparent wording may suggest. He may instead hold that it applies to all generations, but with each group as a whole succeeding another: each of the two generations shares in the endowment to the exclusion of those below it. Another judge may take a different view, holding that thereafter individuals succeed individuals. If a second judge rules on a matter on which the first did not rule, without contradicting his ruling, this does not constitute an annulment of the first ruling. The second ruling may therefore be annulled only if it contravenes a scriptural text or scholarly consensus. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked:

A man established an endowment for his named grandson, then for his children, whether one or more, then for his children’s children, then for his descendants and posterity. Whoever among them died leaving a child, a grandchild, or descendants and posterity, the share he had been receiving would revert to those of the same generational level as him. The first beneficiary died leaving children, one of whom had died during his lifetime leaving children. Then the first beneficiary died, leaving his daughter and his son’s two children. Does the daughter receive everything, or does the share to which the son would have been entitled had he been alive pass to his two children?

He answered:

Rather, the share to which Muhammad—who died during his father’s lifetime—would have been entitled had he lived passes to his two children, not to his sister. For the founder has explained that his words, “for

---

## Page 15408

his children, then for his children’s children,” were intended to mean that individuals succeed individuals, not that one group as a whole succeeds another, as his explanation makes clear—even though, according to one of the two views, the latter is what the wording signifies when unqualified.

Where the beneficiaries of rights are ranked in order, whether by Islamic law or by stipulation, those rights pass to the second rank only when the first is absent or lacks entitlement, because the first rank has priority. Thus the jurists say concerning agnatic relatives in inheritance or marriage: “The son, then his son, then the father, then his father.”

Thus, the son’s son’s entitlement is conditional upon his father’s absence, on account of his lack of entitlement—owing to an impediment affecting him, such as disbelief or the like. It is not required that his father should have been entitled to something that had not passed to him. Likewise with regard to the mother: marriage, custody, and authority over washing the deceased and offering the funeral prayer over him.

Those who imagine that the first rank must have acquired entitlement do so only because they imagine that the endowment passes from the first rank to the second, and that the second receives it from the first, as with inheritance. That is not so. Rather, the second receives the endowment from its founder, just as the first did, and just as relatives receive their rights from the Lawgiver. Entitlement, however, is determined by the order stipulated by the Lawgiver or the founder.

---

## Page 15409

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A person established an endowment for Zayd, then for Zayd’s eight children. One of the eight designated children died during Zayd’s lifetime, leaving a child. Zayd then died. Does Zayd’s grandchild receive the share to which his father would have been entitled had he been alive, or does the entire endowment belong exclusively to Zayd’s children?

He replied:

Yes, the grandchild is entitled to what his father would have been entitled to. That share does not pass to those in the same rank as the deceased so long as any of his children or grandchildren remains alive.

This is because, when the founder’s statement, “For Zayd, then for his children, then his children’s children,” is left unqualified, the jurists of Imam Ahmad’s school and others hold two views concerning it.

The first is that it provides for one group as a whole to succeed another, as in the well-known interpretation of the statement, “For Zayd and ʿAmr, then for the poor.”

The second is that it provides for individuals to succeed individuals, as in the words of Allah Most High:

“And you shall have half of what your wives leave.”

That is, each man receives half of what his own wife leaves. Likewise, His words:

“Forbidden to you are your mothers.”

That is, each man is forbidden to marry his own mother; for

---

## Page 15410

matching one plural with another implies that their individual members correspond to one another, as in the expressions: “People put on their clothes” and “People mounted their riding animals.”

This is certainly the intended meaning in the case under discussion, since the founder expressly stipulated that whenever one of these beneficiaries died leaving a child, his share would pass to his child. Thus, in this expressly qualified case, individuals are intended to succeed individuals, without any disagreement; the disagreement concerns only an unqualified provision.

Accordingly, where entitlement follows an order of succession—whether established by Islamic law or by a stipulation in a bequest, an endowment, or otherwise—the only condition for its passing to the second is that the first should not be entitled. This applies whether the first existed and was entitled, existed but was not entitled, or never existed at all. This is exemplified by what the jurists state concerning the order of precedence among agnatic relatives, marriage guardians, those entitled to custody, and others: the son has the entitlement, then his son, however far down the line of descent, then the father, then his father, however far up the line of ascent. If the nearer relative is absent or is disqualified by unbelief or slavery, the right passes to the next in order. For the right to pass to the next, it is not a condition that the first should previously have been entitled to it.

The same applies if someone says: “Oversight of this is entrusted to so-and-so, then to so-and-so or to his son.” Whenever the first lacks entitlement to oversight owing to his absence, insanity, or unbelief, it passes to the second, whether the latter is his son or someone else. The same applies to the order of precedence among agnatic relatives in inheritance, inheritance through patronage, custody, and other matters.

---

## Page 15411

The same applies to an endowment. If someone establishes an endowment for his children, generation after generation along their agnatic line, stipulating that they must be upright, poor, or meet some other condition, and one or all of the first generation fail to meet the condition of entitlement, the right passes, when the first beneficiary is not entitled, to the second generation, provided they qualify.

The underlying reason is that the second generation receives its entitlement to the endowment from the founder, not from the first generation. Their receiving it, however, is conditional upon the absence of the first generation. Similarly, more distant agnatic relatives receive their inheritance from the deceased, not from the nearer agnatic relative, although their entitlement is conditional upon the absence of the nearer relative. Likewise, according to the well-known position among the imams, inheritance through patronage goes to the deceased’s nearest agnatic relative at the time of the freedman’s death, for it is inherited just as property is inherited.

Some people misunderstand such matters because they suppose that the child receives this right as an inheritance from his father, or as something comparable to an inheritance. They therefore imagine that its passing to the second generation is conditional upon the first generation having been entitled to it, as some jurists have supposed. Such a person says: “If the father left nothing, the son inherits nothing from him.”

This is mistaken. The son does not, under any circumstances, receive what the father receives, nor does he receive anything from the father. Had the father been alive, he would have received the income throughout his lifetime. What then passes to his son is the income arising after the father’s death, not the income to which the father was entitled.

The endowed property itself, however, remains unchanged. The later beneficiary’s right to it during his time is analogous to the earlier beneficiary’s right during his time; it has not passed to them by inheritance.

---

## Page 15412

For this reason, Muslims agree that, among the successive generations of endowment beneficiaries, if the conditions are not met by the first generation, or by some of its members, this does not necessarily deprive the second generation of entitlement, provided they meet the conditions. Some have disputed only the case in which members of the first generation die before their entitlement arises. Yet there is no difference between the two cases.

This is further clarified by the following: to say that the deceased’s share passes to his siblings because he belongs to their generation would necessarily entail succession by one entire generation to another, or would mean that some or all members of the second generation become entitled only once the entire first generation has died. The founder’s wording, however, makes clear that he intended individuals to succeed individuals.

Nevertheless, we recognize two opinions where the wording is unqualified. The stronger is that individuals succeed individuals in all cases, since this is what such wording is intended to convey. They themselves favour giving the deceased’s child precedence over his brother with respect to what his father inherits, for the child is given precedence over the brother. If it is argued that the endowment’s succession is interrupted in this case, this founder has expressly used wording indicating continuity. His share must therefore pass to his child. In short, this is certain: it admits of no valid juristic dispute, but only disagreement arising from error.

As for the founder’s statement:

“If any of Zayd’s children or grandchildren dies, leaving a child or grandchild, however far down the line of descent, his share shall pass to his grandchild or great-grandchild.”

The following may be said about it: either his expression “his share” encompasses the share to which he would be entitled if he possessed the qualifying characteristic, whether or not he actually became entitled to it, or it covers only what he actually became entitled to. If the former is intended, there is nothing further to dispute. This is the more compelling interpretation, because after his death he is no longer entitled to that share; and because, if the father were excluded owing to the absence of

---

## Page 15413

a stipulated qualifying characteristic—for example, if the beneficiaries were required to be Muslim, morally upright, or poor—his share would pass to his child despite the impediment, just as it would in its absence.

Moreover, something may be attributed to someone on the strength of even the slightest connection, so it is valid to call it “his share” in this sense. Interpreting the wording this way also means that the founder’s statement encompasses every situation that may arise; this is preferable to interpreting it as leaving some situations unmentioned. It also accords with the sequence expressed in the wording. Yet that is not what ordinary people who make such stipulations understand from it.

This is also what reasoned consideration and theoretical analogy dictate in people’s stipulations concerning the entitlement of an orphaned grandchild, neither he nor his father having inherited anything from the grandfather. The founder therefore considers compensating him by granting him entitlement in that case; for he would then be included in what his father inherited from the estate and what passed to him by inheritance. This intention of theirs also accords with the Lawgiver’s purpose. Hence, they often make bequests for a child in this situation.

If it is said that this wording encompasses only what the deceased actually became entitled to, this would be an inference from an express statement framed in terms of the usual case, and no contrary implication may therefore be drawn from it. Since it carries no contrary implication, the case in question is left unaddressed in this passage.

It may, however, be encompassed by the founder’s statement:

“For Zayd, then for his children, then for their children’s children.”

We have explained that this wording, together with his subsequent statement that the deceased’s share passes to his child, explicitly indicates that individuals are intended to succeed individuals. The meaning is therefore: “For Zayd, then for his children, then for the child of each one

---

## Page 15414

after his father.” This wording requires that each person be entitled to what his father would have been entitled to had he met the qualifying condition, just as others of his generation are entitled to it. Muslim scholars agree on this in comparable cases, whether the entitlement arises under Islamic law or from a stipulation. If this establishes the child’s entitlement, while the detailed provision either also establishes his entitlement—which is the more evident interpretation—or does not require his exclusion, then the evidence remains operative, being free of any countervailing evidence of equal weight. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man established an endowment for his children, so-and-so, so-and-so, and so-and-so, and for his grandson through his son, so-and-so. He stipulated that if any of them died leaving a son, his share would pass to his son; and if anyone died leaving a daughter, his share would pass to her, then to her paternal uncles, then to the sons of her paternal uncles, the nearest taking precedence over the next nearest. A grandson through a son then died without children, leaving his full sister and his paternal uncles. Which of them has the stronger claim?

He answered:

His share passes to his full sister. It is clear that the founder intended to earmark what his ascendant would have been entitled to, and to assign the share of anyone dying without children to his closest relative. He also placed Mūsā, the grandson, in the position of his son, because his father was already deceased when the endowment was established. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15415

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a village that Sultan Saladin had made an endowment, assigning its revenue to a named individual, then to his children after him, and assigning one-half and one-quarter to the poor. This arrangement continued while the village remained prosperous. In the year of Ghāzān’s incursion, however, the village was devastated and remained derelict for eight years. Then one of the shaykhs obtained a sultanic decree authorizing him to restore the village, and he restored it. He subsequently passed away to Allah’s mercy, leaving young, impoverished orphans who had no wealth.

A woman descended from the beneficiary entitled to the revenue then established her lineage and took possession of the village’s revenue. The half and the quarter continued to go to the poor, in accordance with the founder’s stipulation, while the children of the man who had restored the village remained poor. May they receive enough to meet their needs as part of the general body of poor beneficiaries? Or are they entitled to reimbursement for what their father spent restoring it, insofar as he had not been reimbursed before his death?

He answered:

If they fall within the founder’s stipulated conditions, they are entitled to whatever those conditions provide. If it were impossible to disburse the funds to those possessing the specified qualifications because some of those qualifications could not be met, and these children shared in the entitlement of those to whom the funds are disbursed, then it would be appropriate

---

## Page 15416

that they also be paid, from the village’s revenue, what their father reasonably spent from his own funds, as reimbursement for his expenditure. They are to recover this from the endowment’s income.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About the partition of an endowment and the division of its benefits.

He answered:

Where an endowment is dedicated to a single beneficiary or cause, its corpus may not be partitioned. Only its benefits may be divided through an agreed allocation of use. If the beneficiaries make such an arrangement and subsequently wish to revoke it, they may do so. If the arrangement was not made by the entitled beneficiary or his agent, it is invalid. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About an endowment dedicated to a single beneficiary or cause, which a Hanbalī partitioner divided, believing this permissible because the abridged legal manuals state: “If we hold that partition is a confirmation of existing shares, an endowment may be partitioned.” The two co-beneficiaries then exchanged some of the individual properties, after which one of them demanded his original share from the partition.

He answered:

If the endowment is dedicated to a single beneficiary or cause, its corpus may not be subjected to a partition

---

## Page 15417

that is binding, either in Ahmad’s school or in any other. The abridged manuals merely intended to explain the implications of classifying partition as either a confirmation of existing shares or a sale. If we regard it as a sale, it is impermissible, because an endowment cannot be sold. If we regard it as a confirmation of existing shares, partition is permissible in principle. They did not mention the conditions governing partition, as is customary in such discussions.

Some of them mentioned two views concerning the partition of an endowment. The school’s jurists explicitly stated that an endowment may be partitioned only when it is dedicated to two distinct beneficiaries or causes. As for an endowment dedicated to a single beneficiary or cause, they agree that its corpus may not be partitioned, since the rights of the second and third generations remain attached to it. An agreed allocation of its use is, however, permissible. “An agreed allocation of use” means a division of benefits. In this respect, there is no difference between exchanging rights of use and retaining the agreed allocation without an exchange. If they mutually agree to this, the property is restored to its former undivided state, with respect to both its corpus and its benefits. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

About an endowment dedicated to a group: some of the co-beneficiaries had paid a sum for the fruit, while another had refused either to give or to accept a guarantee and had requested to receive his proportionate share of the fruit from whoever purchased it. Should the judge compel him to join the other co-beneficiaries in selling it or not?

He answered:

If it cannot be divided before the sale without causing harm, he must sell along with his co-beneficiaries and share the proceeds with them.

---

## Page 15418

He was asked:

About an endowment dedicated to the needs and upkeep of the Sanctuary, with what remains thereafter to be spent on charitable causes, alms, and the poor and needy residing in the Sanctuary: may any of its funds be spent on the caretakers and attendants who perform the necessary duties?

He answered:

Yes. Those who perform duties needed by the mosque—cleaning, guarding, laying out furnishings, providing lighting, opening and closing the doors, and similar tasks—serve its needs and are entitled to payment from the endowment dedicated to those needs.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a man who purchased a house whose purchase documents specified only three boundaries, while the fourth boundary adjoined a house held as an endowment. The purchaser then demolished and rebuilt his house. He subsequently opened a window in the endowment property to admit light into a storeroom, and installed a water-supply facility beside the wall, adjoining the endowment property. This new installation damages the endowment’s wall. He also constructed a projection over the perimeter of the endowment’s courtyard. If, then, he builds along the perimeter of the courtyard and rests roof timbers on the wall which

---

## Page 15419

belongs to the endowment, and does so without the permission of the authority in charge, claiming that he has rented it for three dirhams a year, although neither the authority in charge nor the administrators have leased it to him to date. Then another man swore by Allah that he would rent this wall, which stands between the houses. The projection and water-supply facility installed by the first man were removed, and any construction undertaken there was solely in hope of reward from Allah Most High. The second man rented it for twenty dirhams a year for twenty years, so that the houses in the endowment’s courtyard would remain well lit and the neighbours would not suffer harm from construction above them. May it be leased to the man who encroached, or to the man who sought divine reward and increased rental income for the endowment, if the authority in charge leases him its use at the higher rent and thereby removes the harm to the endowment?

He answered:

He has no right to build anything on the endowment’s wall that would damage it; this is unanimously agreed upon. Indeed, according to the majority of scholars, he has no right to do so even if it would cause no damage. His claim to have rented it is not admissible without proof. Even if a lease had been granted that harmed the endowment, it would not be a valid lease under Islamic law.

If someone subsequently seeks to rent it, and doing so serves the endowment’s interests, leasing it is permissible—indeed, obligatory. If his intention is good, he will receive reward and recompense accordingly. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15420

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man lives in an endowed inn with an official responsible for its maintenance and repair. The resident informed this official that his dwelling was in danger of collapse, but the official kept putting him off. Then the official went up to the dwelling, inspected it himself, and stamped on it with his foot, saying, “This is not going to collapse, and you will suffer no harm from it.” He left it and went downstairs. After he had gone down, the dwelling collapsed upon the resident’s wife and children. Three died, and all his belongings were destroyed. Is the official liable for those who died, and must he compensate him for the belongings that were destroyed?

He answered:

This official, who had been notified but delayed having the structure dismantled, is liable for the losses caused by its collapse. Indeed, he would be liable even if he owned the premises, provided that there was reason to fear their collapse and he had been informed of this—even if the person informing him was not his tenant. This is the position of the great majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfah, Mālik, Ahmad according to the well-known view attributed to him, a group of al-Shāfiʿī’s followers, and others. Some, however, require that the notification be made in the presence of witnesses, whereas most do not.

He was negligent in failing to dismantle and repair it, even if he thought it would not collapse. He should have had it inspected by people with expertise in construction. By failing to do so, he was negligent and liable for the losses caused by his negligence. This is all the more so given his statement to the tenant, “Live here if you wish, or do not live here if you wish,” for this constitutes wrongdoing on his part.

---

## Page 15421

For the tenant is entitled to demand that the landlord carry out the repairs the premises require and which the contract obliges him to undertake.

These repairs are obligatory on two grounds: the rights of the endowment’s beneficiaries and the rights of the tenant. Scholars agree that the administrator of an endowment may not neglect the repairs to which the tenant is entitled. Neglecting these two duties makes him liable for whatever is lost through his negligence. He must compensate the endowment for its lost property, including the benefits of use to which the tenant was entitled. This differs from the case in which the property itself remains intact: the tenant may then hold him liable for those benefits, and he may terminate the lease. As for the loss of life and the destruction of the tenant’s property through negligence, he is liable on these three grounds. He is also liable for losses suffered by the neighbours on the first ground, as held by the great majority of scholars.

He was asked:

Concerning funds endowed for ransoming captives: if money is borrowed to secure their release, leaving the captives personally liable for debts they cannot repay, may those debts be paid from the endowment? Likewise, what if the person responsible for securing their release borrows the money at the direction of the endowment’s administrator or someone else?

He answered:

Yes, that is permissible. Indeed, this is a better way to secure the captives’ release than giving money in advance to someone to ransom those particular captives, for that money is at risk and may be spent

---

## Page 15422

on purposes other than ransoming them. In this case, however, the money is certainly spent on their ransom. There is no difference between spending the endowment’s funds directly on their designated purpose and repaying money borrowed for that purpose. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would sometimes distribute zakat funds to those entitled to its allotted shares, and sometimes borrow on their behalf and then use the zakat to repay the creditors. Thus, spending in repayment is equivalent to spending directly. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who had charge of a share in a bathhouse endowed for the poor and needy: part of the bathhouse fell into disrepair during hostilities with the enemy. He leased that share to someone for eight years for eight hundred dirhams, authorizing him to spend the rent on essential repairs to the bathhouse. The tenant carried out the repairs, spending on them until his expenditure amounted to the rent for the aforementioned share. He then claimed that the endowment owed him an additional sum that he had spent beyond the rent, without the lessor’s permission. Is he entitled to that or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If he carried out additional improvements beyond the obligatory repairs authorized, those responsible for the bathhouse are not obliged to pay for the remaining additional improvements or their value. Rather, he may remove them, provided their removal does not harm the endowment. If the improvements increase the bathhouse’s rental income, and they agree that the improvements should remain his property, without paying him their value, but instead

---

## Page 15423

the additional rental income should serve as compensation for those improvements, that is permissible. If those in charge of the endowment wish to remove the additional improvements, they may do so, provided this does not diminish the benefit to which the tenant is entitled under the lease. If they agree to pay him the outstanding cost of the improvements, and he agrees to increase the rent in proportion to the additional benefit, that is permissible. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning an endowment for shrouding the dead whose annual income exceeds what is needed to fulfil its stipulated purpose: may the surplus be given in charity? May some of it be given to the founder’s poor relatives?

He answered:

If the endowment’s income exceeds what is needed for shrouds, the surplus should be spent on purposes benefiting the Muslims. If the founder’s relatives are needy, they have greater entitlement than others. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a jurist appointed to a madrasa who was absent during the recess: is it permissible to withhold his stipend?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If he was absent only during the month of recess, he is entitled to the same payment as someone who was present. During the months of recess, it makes no difference whether someone on recess is present or absent. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15424

He was asked:

Concerning a Quran teacher holding a paid post who travelled and appointed someone to act in his place without stipulating any terms with him. When he returned, he collected the entire stipend and did not vacate the post. Is the deputy entitled to the stipulated stipend or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, the deputy is entitled to the entire stipulated stipend. However, when the person who appointed him returns, he has the greater right to his post. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning someone who established an income-producing endowment and then died, after which a debt he owed came to light: may the endowment be sold to repay his debt?

He answered:

If the debt can be repaid from the endowment’s income, it is not permissible to sell it. If the debt cannot be repaid without selling some of the endowment—and he established it during his final illness—then it is to be sold, by agreement of the scholars.

If, however, he established the endowment while in good health, may it be sold to repay the debt? Scholars within Ahmad’s school and others differ on this. The view that it may not be sold is a strong one.

---

## Page 15425

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a man who said during his illness, “When I die, my house shall be an endowment for such-and-such a mosque.” He then recovered and subsequently incurred debts. Is this endowment valid and binding or not?

He answered:

He may sell the house to repay his debt, even if making the endowment conditional upon his death is valid, as scholars hold in one of their two views. This is no more binding than stipulating that a slave be emancipated upon his owner’s death. It is established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, sold a slave whose emancipation had been stipulated in this way to repay a debt. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning someone who designated an endowment to provide candles or oil for the grave of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him, to take effect after his own death. He then wished to change the endowment and dedicate it to the poor and needy in Cairo.

If that is not permissible, may he dedicate it to the poor residing in Medina—the city of the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings be upon him—or not?

---

## Page 15426

He answered:

As for a bequest concerning what is to be done upon his death, he may revoke or amend it by agreement of the Muslims, even if he has had it witnessed and formally recorded, whether it concerns an endowment, emancipation, or anything else. There are two well-known disputes concerning endowments and emancipation made conditional upon death. Establishing an endowment to provide oil or candles to be lit at a grave is not an act of righteousness, by agreement of the scholars. Indeed, it is established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Allah has cursed women who visit graves and those who build mosques over them and place lamps upon them.”

As for lighting the Prophet’s Mosque for those praying there, or lighting other mosques, illuminating the houses of Allah is good. However, if the mosque already has enough for its lighting, additional lighting that serves no purpose has no legitimate benefit. Such surplus should not be spent on its lighting, but elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

What should be done when an endowment yields surplus revenue that is no longer needed?

He answered:

It should be spent on a comparable purpose. For example, if a mosque has funds beyond its needs, the surplus should be spent on another mosque. The founder’s intention concerns that general category of purpose, and the category remains the same. Thus, if the first mosque were to fall into ruin and no one benefited from it, its endowment revenue would be spent on another mosque. The same applies when something remains beyond its needs, for this

---

## Page 15427

surplus can neither be spent on it nor left idle. Spending it on the same general kind of purpose is therefore preferable and comes closest to fulfilling the founder’s intention. Ahmad narrated from ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, that he urged people to give to a slave under a contract of manumission. When the contributions exceeded that slave’s needs, he allocated the surplus to others under such contracts.

He was asked:

A judge has assigned a righteous man an allowance from charitable funds for himself and the poor who come to him. Is anyone permitted to compete with him for it or forcibly take it from him?

He answered:

It is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“A Muslim is the brother of a Muslim. It is not lawful for a Muslim to undercut his brother’s sale or outbid him in purchase negotiations. Nor may a woman ask for her sister’s divorce so as to deprive her of her portion, for she will have what has been decreed for her.”

If, in contracts involving an exchange, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, forbade a man to outbid his brother in purchase negotiations or to propose marriage to a woman to whom his brother has already proposed—even before the person has acquired what he seeks—how can it be lawful for a man to approach someone whom the authority has assigned an allowance from charitable or other funds, to which he is entitled and which he needs, and then compete with him for it, seeking to take it away from him? This is even more strictly forbidden than those acts. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15428

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A plot of land endowed for a mosque contains trees that bear no fruit and prevent the land from being cultivated. Is it permissible to uproot the trees and spend the proceeds from their sale on the mosque’s needs, so that the land may be cultivated and put to use?

He answered:

Yes. If uprooting the trees benefits the land by allowing greater use to be made of it, they should be uprooted. The endowment’s administrator should uproot them and do whatever best serves the endowment. He should spend the proceeds from their sale on whatever is most beneficial to the endowment, whether maintaining the endowed property or a mosque, if such work is needed. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A burial chamber was built in a mosque’s summer prayer area, then demolished pursuant to a ruling of Islamic law. The mosque has a privy, but there is no room in it for ablution. Is it permissible to provide a place for ablution in the summer prayer area while leaving undisturbed whatever is inside the chamber that had been built as a grave?

---

## Page 15429

He answered—may Allah have mercy on him:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, if this benefits the mosque and its congregation, and the only concern is that ablution would be performed inside the mosque, then it is permissible to do so. Performing ablution in a mosque is permissible; indeed, the majority of scholars do not even regard it as disliked. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

An old, closed mosque collapsed and was demolished, then rebuilt to its former length and width. The builder raised it above its former level and extended it forward. There had been a private chamber beneath it, and he built a dwelling underneath for the benefit of the mosque. Is it permissible to renovate the dwelling and live in it?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. Yes, any work that benefits the mosque and its congregation may be carried out, including renovating the building, altering its layout, and similar work. And Allah, Glorified is He, knows best.

---

## Page 15430

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Some mosques and a congregational mosque require repairs. They have fixed stipends payable by the collector, but the revenue is insufficient to cover everything. Is it permissible for him to pay anyone before carrying out the essential repairs? To whom may payments be made? What should he do with any surplus revenue: save it or purchase real estate with it?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah.

If both interests can be served by making the payments necessary to meet the essential needs of those entitled to them and enable them to perform their obligatory duties, while using the remainder for repairs, then this is the prescribed course—even if some repairs are delayed, provided that the delay causes no harm. Repairs are obligatory; duties that cannot be performed without stipends are obligatory; and relieving need is obligatory. Fulfilling these obligations is preferable to leaving some of them unfulfilled.

As for those whose services are indispensable to the repairs, such as workers and those handling the accounts, their costs form part of the repair expenses.

Any revenue remaining after the stipulated expenditures and the expenses of the mosques should be spent on purposes of the same kind, such as repairing another mosque and meeting the needs of mosques, and on similar beneficial purposes. The money must not be withheld indefinitely without a specific reason, especially in the case of mosques whose revenue is known consistently to exceed their needs. Withholding such money is a form of corruption:

“And Allah does not like corruption.”

---

## Page 15431

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A judge who also serves as a preacher was assigned a stipend from a mosque’s surplus revenue. For two years he received nothing because there was no surplus. Then the revenue increased in the third year. May he take three years’ worth of stipends from that revenue?

He answered:

If the third year’s revenue has lawful uses designated by a valid stipulation, it must be spent on those purposes, and the judge may not take it. But if it has no designated use at all, and the ruler deems it appropriate to allocate it to the judge as compensation for what he missed in the past, that is permissible. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15432

The shaykh and imam, the learned and eminent scholar Abū al-‘Abbās Ahmad ibn ‘Abd al-Halīm ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section: Replacing Endowed Property

Endowed property—even mosques—may be replaced with its equivalent or something better on grounds of need or benefit. The same applies to replacing sacrificial animals designated for pilgrimage, animals designated for the Eid sacrifice, and things pledged by a vow. Likewise, a comparable beneficiary may be substituted for the designated beneficiary when the benefit cannot be delivered to the latter.

Replacement may take place through a direct exchange for a substitute, or through a sale followed by the purchase of a replacement with the proceeds. According to Ahmad’s school, endowed property other than a mosque may be sold when there is a need.

A mosque, too, may be sold when there is a need, according to the better-known of the two reports from him. According to the other report, its land may not be sold; rather, its building materials are moved to another location.

A “copy of the Quran” is analogous: selling it is regarded as either prohibited or merely discouraged. Exchanging it, however, is permissible without any disapproval according to one of the two reports from him. Yet the apparent position of his school is that, if it is sold and another is purchased with the proceeds, this falls within the category of

---

## Page 15433

replacement, since it fulfills the intended purpose; this involves directing its benefit to a comparable beneficiary when it cannot be directed to the designated beneficiary.

For if a mosque is endowed in a town or neighborhood, and the people of that area can no longer benefit from it, its benefit is directed to an equivalent purpose: a mosque is built with it elsewhere. He likewise holds that a mosque’s oil and mats, when no longer needed, are transferred to another mosque. In his view, they may also be given to poor neighbors. As evidence, he cited the fact that ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—used to distribute the covering of the Kaaba among the Muslims. The same therefore applies to the coverings of other mosques, since Muslims are entitled to benefit from mosques.

As evidence for directing such property to an equivalent purpose, he cited the fact that ‘Alī ibn Abī Tālib—may Allah be pleased with him—collected money for a slave under a contract of emancipation. When there was a surplus beyond the amount required by his contract, he allocated it to other slaves under such contracts. The donors had given the money for emancipation through such a contract; once the designated individual no longer needed it, he directed it to others in the same position.

The point is that Ahmad ibn Hanbal—may Allah have mercy on him—expressed differing views on selling a mosque when it could no longer be used, but his position did not differ regarding the sale of other endowed property when necessary.

In the report transmitted by his son ‘Abd Allāh, he said: “If a mosque falls into ruin, it is sold and its proceeds are spent on another mosque.”

Judge Abū Ya‘lā, in al-Mujarrad, Ibn ‘Aqīl, in al-Fusūl, and others stated—the wording is the judge’s:

“An endowment’s expenses are met from its income, for the aim is to benefit from it while preserving the property itself. This is possible only by spending on it; thus preserving it entails spending

---

## Page 15434

on it, with the remainder going to the beneficiary. If it produces no income—for example, if it is a slave who can no longer work or an animal that has become emaciated—the beneficiary may choose either to maintain it, since he is its owner, or to sell it and spend the proceeds on a replacement of the same kind. If it is endowed for the poor, its maintenance is paid from the public treasury, since no particular person owns it; it is therefore like a mosque. … (1)

If the ruler considers it appropriate to sell it and spend the proceeds on a replacement of the same kind, this is permissible. If the endowed property is a house that falls into ruin and can no longer be used, it is sold, and its proceeds are used to purchase another house, which is made an endowment in its place. Likewise, when an endowed horse becomes old and unserviceable, it is sold, and its proceeds are used to purchase a horse suitable for the purpose for which it was endowed.

He said in the report transmitted by Bakr ibn Muhammad:

‘If its proceeds suffice to purchase a horse, one is purchased and made an endowment; otherwise, the proceeds are put toward the purchase of an animal to be endowed.’

Likewise, if a mosque falls into ruin and stands in a location where no one prays, it may be relocated to an inhabited place, and its site may be sold. He explicitly stated this in the report transmitted by ‘Abd Allāh.

Abū Bakr said:

‘The ruler is responsible for having this witnessed.’

He continued: Abū Bakr also said in Kitāb al-Qawlayn:

‘‘Alī ibn Sa‘īd transmitted that mosques are not to be sold, but are to be relocated.’

Abū Bakr said:

‘I adopt the first view’—meaning the report transmitted by ‘Abd Allāh—because they unanimously agree that an endowed horse may be sold.

Ahmad also said, in the report transmitted by al-Hasan ibn Thawāb, concerning a slave belonging to a man in Mecca—meaning a slave held as an endowment—who refused to work:

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) A blank in the original.

---

## Page 15435

“He is to be sold and another slave substituted in his place.” Judge Abū Ya‘lā cited this report in al-Ta‘līq, in the discussion of emancipating a slave pledged as security.

Abū al-Barakāt said:

“He thus treated the slave’s refusal as equivalent to his becoming unserviceable.” That is, he must be compelled to perform all the work, just as a hirer is compelled, even though his refusal is unlawful. The impossibility of benefiting from him in this manner was treated as equivalent to his becoming unserviceable, in consideration of the interests of the endowment.

Section

As for replacing a mosque with another to serve a beneficial purpose, even though the original mosque remains usable, there are two views within Ahmad’s school. His followers differed on this matter, but permissibility is more clearly supported by his explicit statements and evidence. No explicit statement from him supports the other view; rather, his followers relied on the implications of his written words. He often rules that this is permissible when there is a need. This may mean that permission is restricted to cases of need; alternatively, he may have singled out such cases because that was what he had been asked about and needed to explain.

Abū Bakr ‘Abd al-‘Azīz discussed this at length in al-Shāfī, from which Zād al-Musāfir was abridged. He said: Al-Khallāl narrated to us: Sālih ibn Ahmad narrated to us: My father narrated to us: Yazīd ibn Hārūn narrated to us: Al-Mas‘ūdī narrated to us, from al-Qāsim, who said:

“When ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd—may Allah be pleased with him—arrived at the public treasury, Sa‘d ibn Mālik had already built

---

## Page 15436

the palace and established a mosque near the date merchants.”

He continued: “The treasury was then broken into, and the man who had broken into it was apprehended. He wrote to ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, who replied: ‘Do not cut off the man’s hand. Relocate the mosque and place the treasury on its qiblah side, for there will always be someone praying in the mosque.’ So ‘Abd Allāh relocated it and marked out this site for it.”

Sālih said: My father said:

“It is said that the treasury was broken into from the mosque of Kufa, so ‘Abd Allāh ibn Mas‘ūd relocated the mosque. The date merchants now occupy the site of the old mosque.”

He said: I asked my father about a man who built a mosque and then wished to move it elsewhere. He replied:

“If the man who built the mosque wishes to move it for fear of thieves, or because its site is filthy, there is no harm in moving it. It is said that the treasury was broken into while it was within the mosque, so Ibn Mas‘ūd relocated the mosque.”

Muhammad ibn ‘Alī narrated to us: Abū Yahyā narrated to us: Abū Tālib narrated to us:

Abū ‘Abd Allāh was asked whether a mosque could be relocated. He replied:

“If it is cramped and its congregation cannot hear, there is no harm in moving it to a more spacious site.”

Muhammad ibn ‘Alī narrated to us: ‘Abd Allāh ibn Ahmad narrated to us, saying:

I asked my father about a mosque that had fallen into ruin: “Do you think its land may be sold and the proceeds spent on another mosque that they have newly established?”

He replied:

“If it has no neighbours and no one keeps it in use, I see no harm in selling it and spending the proceeds on the other mosque.”

Muhammad ibn ‘Abd Allāh narrated to us: Abū Dāwūd narrated to us, saying: “I heard Ahmad being asked about a mosque containing two valuable timber beams

---

## Page 15437

which had deteriorated, and they feared the mosque might collapse. ‘May these two beams be sold, the proceeds spent on the mosque, and two palm trunks put in their place?’

He replied: ‘I see no harm in that.’ He cited as evidence endowed animals that can no longer be put to use: they are sold and their proceeds applied to the endowment.”

There is no doubt that his words establish the permissibility of replacing a mosque when this serves a beneficial purpose, even if the original mosque remains usable, because the benefit afforded by the second is greater. The original property then becomes free of the endowment restrictions.

Abū Bakr said in Zād al-Musāfir (Provisions for the Traveller): Ahmad said, as reported by Sālih:

“The treasury in Kufa was broken into while Ibn Masʿūd was in charge of it. He wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—and ʿUmar wrote back: ‘Relocate the mosque and place the treasury on its qiblah side, for there will always be someone praying in it.’ Saʿd then moved it to the site of today’s date merchants, and the date merchants’ market came to occupy its former site. The treasury was established on its qiblah side. Thus, there is no harm in relocating mosques when they fall into ruin.”

He said, as reported by Abū Tālib:

“If a mosque is too cramped for its congregation, there is no harm in moving it to a more spacious site.”

He permitted its relocation because the benefit afforded by the original mosque was diminished, not because using it had become impossible.

Al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā said: He said, as reported by Sālih:

“A mosque may be relocated for fear of thieves, or if its site is filthy.”

Al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā adopted the second view.

He also said, as reported by Abū Dāwūd, concerning a mosque whose congregation wished to raise it above ground level and establish a drinking-water facility and shops beneath it, while some of them objected:

“The view of the majority of them should be taken into account

---

## Page 15438

and there is no harm in it.”

He said: “The apparent meaning of this is that he permitted shops and a drinking-water facility to be established beneath the mosque.”

He said: “This must be understood as applying where a need arose for this because it would benefit the mosque.”

He said: “Our shaykh Abū ʿAbd Allāh—that is, Ibn Hāmid—used to prohibit this. He interpreted the case as concerning a disagreement at the outset of the mosque’s construction, before it was made an endowment.”

He said: “Our principles do not preclude permitting this when it serves a beneficial purpose, since we permit selling the mosque and relocating it elsewhere.”

He said: “Ahmad also said, as reported by Bakr ibn Muhammad, concerning a mosque that was not secure against dogs and other animals and had a minaret: he permitted the minaret to be dismantled and its materials used to build the mosque’s wall, because this served a beneficial purpose.”

Ibn ʿAqīl inclined towards Ibn Hāmid’s view in al-Fusūl, saying: “This must be understood as applying where a need arose for it. Just as, in his view, altering and relocating a mosque was permissible only when needed, this unqualified statement must be understood in that light, not as applying to an already established mosque.”

He said: “The more fitting interpretation is that this concerns a mosque whose construction is just beginning. On this interpretation, they disagreed over how it should be built. Once it has become a mosque, however, it may neither be sold nor have a drinking-water facility established beneath it.”

Abū Muhammad likewise preferred Ibn Hāmid’s view, saying: “It is more correct and more appropriate, even though it runs contrary to the apparent meaning.”

He said: “A mosque may not be relocated or replaced, nor may its courtyard be sold and converted into a drinking-water facility and shops, unless it can no longer be used. The need for a drinking-water facility and shops does not render the mosque unusable, so it may not be diverted to that purpose.”

He said: “If it were permissible to turn

---

## Page 15439

the area beneath the mosque into a drinking-water facility and shops to meet this need, it would also be permissible to demolish the mosque, turn its site into a drinking-water facility and shops, and build a replacement mosque elsewhere.”

This is plainly a strained interpretation that contradicts his explicit statement. His statement expressly concerns people wishing to raise a mosque above ground level and establish a drinking-water facility and shops beneath it, with some of them objecting. His answer was that the view of the majority should be considered. Had this concerned its initial construction, no one would have had grounds to dispute its construction if it was permissible, nor would the majority’s view have been considered. If they were jointly undertaking the construction, none of the partners could be compelled to accept what the others wanted unless it was obligatory, and it could only be built with their agreement.

Moreover, his words, “They wished to raise it above ground level and establish a drinking-water facility beneath it,” clearly indicate that it stood directly on the ground, and that they wished to raise it and establish a drinking-water facility underneath. Ahmad gave weight to the choice of the majority of those who prayed in the mosque because one must give preference to the better of the two options. What most of them chose would benefit the majority more, and would therefore deserve preference.

Furthermore, the wording of the case, as cited by Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, is as follows. He said: “He said, as reported by Sulaymān ibn al-Ashʿath: ‘If a man builds a mosque and someone else wishes to demolish it and rebuild it better than before, but the original builder objects, the matter is referred to the wishes and consent of the neighbours, if they favour demolishing and rebuilding it. If they wish to raise the mosque above ground level and establish a drinking-water facility beneath it, but frail elderly men oppose them, saying, “We cannot climb up,” it is nevertheless to be raised and a drinking-water facility established beneath it. I know of no

---

## Page 15440

harm in that, and the view of the majority is to be considered.’”

He thus expressly permitted replacing its structure with a better one, even if the original founder of the endowment objected. He also permitted raising it and establishing a drinking-water facility beneath it, even if frail elderly men opposed them, provided the neighbours chose this, giving weight to the majority’s view.

He said:

He also said, as reported by Ibn al-Hakam: “If the mosque has a minaret but is not securely enclosed, there is no harm in demolishing the minaret and using its materials in the mosque’s wall to secure it.”

Abū al-ʿAbbās said:

Even if the evidence they cited were sound, it would entail giving preference to a different opinion. There would then be two opinions on the matter, one of which they have preferred. How much more so, then, when these arguments are weak!

As for the assertion, “Relocation and replacement are permissible only when benefiting from the property becomes impossible,” this is not conceded. They have offered no evidence for it, either from revealed law or from the teachings of the school. This prohibition on which they rely comes neither from the Lawgiver nor from the founder of the school. Rather, the evidence of revealed law and the statements of the school’s founder indicate the contrary.

Ahmad said: “If the mosque becomes too small for its congregation, there is no harm in relocating it to a more spacious site.” Its being too small for its congregation has not rendered it useless; rather, its benefit remains as before, but the number of people has increased. Another mosque could be built for them, and it is not a requirement that a mosque accommodate everyone. Nevertheless, he permitted relocating it to another site, because gathering people in one mosque is better than dividing them between two mosques. The larger the congregation, the better, for the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A man’s prayer with another man is more meritorious than his prayer alone

---

## Page 15441

and his prayer with two men is more meritorious than his prayer with one; the greater the number, the more beloved it is to Allah Most High.” Narrated by Abū Dāwūd and others.

This is despite his permitting another mosque to be built when the population increases, even nearby, while prohibiting the construction of a mosque intended to cause harm.

Ahmad said, as reported by Sālih:

“A mosque must not be built with the intention of harming a neighbouring mosque. But if the population increases, there is no harm in building one, even nearby.”

Thus, although he permitted another mosque to be built when the population increased, even nearby, he also permitted relocating a mosque that had become too small for its congregation to a more spacious site, because this was better and more beneficial—not out of necessity.

Another reason is that the rightly guided caliphs, ʿUmar and ʿUthmān—may Allah be pleased with them both—altered the Prophet’s Mosque, and ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb ordered the mosque of Kufa to be relocated, its original site becoming the date-sellers’ market. This was done for an overriding benefit, not because those mosques had ceased to serve their purpose: their benefit had not ceased, but remained.

Likewise, subsequent Muslim caliphs, such as al-Walīd, al-Mansūr and al-Mahdī, did the same with the two Sacred Mosques. Al-Walīd also did so with the mosque of Damascus and elsewhere, consulting the scholars and receiving their approval. Indeed, Mālik and others issued rulings permitting endowed property adjoining a mosque to be purchased and its beneficiaries compensated. They thus permitted the sale of endowed property and compensation for it for the benefit of the mosque, not for the benefit of its beneficiaries. If it were sold and compensation provided for the benefit of its beneficiaries, this would be all the more deserving of neighbourly consideration.

As for the assertion:

“If it were permissible to turn the lower part of the mosque into a drinking-water facility and shops for this

---

## Page 15442

need, it would also be permissible to demolish the mosque, turn its site into a drinking-water facility and shops, and replace it with a mosque elsewhere.”

The reply is: We accept that conclusion. This is precisely what Ahmad mentioned and reported from ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—and upon which he based his position. For ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb demolished the original mosque—the congregational mosque of the people of Kufa—and replaced it with a mosque elsewhere in the city. The site of the original mosque became the date-sellers’ market.

Thus, the very case they presented as a counterexample in their objection and as the basis of their analogy is the case that Ahmad and others reported from the Companions, and which he and his followers cited as evidence against their opponents. Ahmad’s followers said that this implies the Companions’ consensus—may Allah be pleased with them—on this matter. They said—the following is Ibn ʿAqīl’s wording in al-Mufradāt, in the discussion of replacing a mosque:

“Furthermore, Yazīd ibn Hārūn narrated, saying: ‘Al-Masʿūdī narrated to us from al-Qāsim, who said: “When ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd came to the treasury, Saʿd ibn Mālik had already built the palace and established a mosque near the date sellers. The treasury was broken into, and the man who broke into it was apprehended. He wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—and ʿUmar wrote back: ‘Do not cut off the man’s hand. Relocate the mosque and place the treasury on the mosque’s qiblah side, for there will always be someone praying in the mosque.’ So ʿAbdullāh relocated it and marked out this site for it.”’”

Ahmad said:

“It is said that the treasury at the mosque of Kufa was broken into, so ʿAbdullāh relocated the mosque. The present location of the date sellers is the site of the old mosque.”

Ibn ʿAqīl said:

“This took place while many of the Companions were still present, so it is

---

## Page 15443

tantamount to consensus, since no one objected, even though they would not remain silent about what they considered wrong. They objected to Umar’s prohibition of excessive dowries, and a woman even challenged him. They also dissuaded him from inflicting the prescribed punishment on a pregnant woman, saying: ‘If Allah has given you authority over her back, He has given you no authority over what is in her womb.’ They objected to Uthmān’s performing the prayer in full during Hajj, whereupon he said: ‘I have entered a town where my family resides.’ They opposed Ali when he held that slave women who had borne their masters’ children could be sold. Had relocating the mosque been objectionable, it would have deserved denunciation all the more, since it was a conspicuous act of grave import.”

He also cited the report narrated by Abū Hafs in al-Manāsik from Ā’isha—may Allah be pleased with her—that she was asked:

“Mother of the Believers, the covering of the Kaaba is sometimes passed around?”

She replied:

“It should be sold and its proceeds devoted to charitable purposes.”

Thus, Ā’isha instructed that the covering of the Kaaba be sold, although it was an endowment, and that its proceeds be devoted to charitable purposes, because that was more beneficial to the Muslims.

Those who preferred Ibn Hāmid’s position concerning income-producing endowments, such as Abū Muhammad, likewise addressed this matter. He said:

“If an endowment’s benefit has not ceased entirely, but has diminished, or another property would be more beneficial and yield a greater return for its beneficiaries, it may not be sold. The basic rule is that selling it is forbidden; permission was granted only out of necessity, to safeguard the endowment’s intended purpose from being lost while that purpose can still be achieved and benefit derived from it. If we say so, the intended purpose is lost. The exception, of course, is when its benefit diminishes to the point where it no longer counts as a benefit, so that its existence is equivalent to its absence.”

The response is: What they have stated is not conceded. They have offered no evidence for it, either from Islamic law or from the teachings of their legal school.

---

## Page 15444

and even if they cite something implied or expressly stated in Ahmad’s words, at most it represents one reported opinion of his, contradicted by another that accords more closely with his explicit statements and principles. Once his statements and principles establish that a mosque may be replaced when doing so serves a greater benefit, this applies all the more to other endowments. He also explicitly permitted the sale of other endowments for the sake of benefit, not necessity, as we shall explain, if Allah Most High wills.

They may also be told: There is no necessity to sell the endowment. Rather, it is sold to secure a greater benefit and to meet the beneficiaries’ need to obtain its full benefit—not because of a necessity that makes forbidden things permissible. It may be sold to secure its full benefit even when they are not compelled by necessity. If its sale were impermissible because it was forbidden, neither necessity nor anything else would make it permissible, just as a free person who has been emancipated may not be sold, even if the former master who emancipated him desperately needs the proceeds. At worst, the endowment would cease to be useful, just as would happen if it were an animal and died.

They may further be told: In most cases, its sale has occurred only when its benefit has diminished, not when its benefit has ceased altogether. If its benefit had ceased altogether, no one could benefit from it—neither the purchaser nor anyone else. Selling something that has no benefit is also impermissible. At worst, it falls into ruin and becomes a vacant plot. Such a plot can still be put to use by leasing it to someone who will build on it. This is what people call “hikr” (ground leasing). It is also possible to borrow the funds needed for rebuilding and repay the loan from the endowment’s rental income.

This can take two forms:

The first is for someone to volunteer to provide a loan; however, this cannot be relied upon.

---

## Page 15445

The second is to lease it—not on the basis of a generically described obligation—and use the rent collected to rebuild it, so that the tenant receives the use corresponding to the rent paid.

These are two methods available to people when an endowment falls into ruin. Sometimes they lease the land, which remains under a ground lease. At other times, they obtain rent in advance to finance rebuilding; this rent is lower than it would have been had it not been paid in advance. This is possible with most endowments that fall into ruin. Nevertheless, they have permitted selling the property and purchasing a replacement with its proceeds because this better serves the endowment’s beneficiaries—not because of necessity or because all benefit has ceased. Such a property would scarcely be useful, and no one buys something that cannot be used. Yet it may be difficult for no tenant to be available while a purchaser is found. However, permission to sell an endowment when it falls into ruin is not conditional upon there being no tenant. Rather, it may be sold and replaced when that is more beneficial than leasing it, since bare land yields little rent.

Likewise, when rent is obtained in advance to finance rebuilding, the benefit diminishes. The beneficiaries receive no benefit from it during the period in which the tenant receives the use corresponding to the amount spent on rebuilding; they benefit only afterwards. Moreover, the rent paid in advance is low, so the endowment’s benefit is diminished in this case.

It is thus clear that what justifies selling and replacing the endowment is its diminished benefit, because the replacement is better and more beneficial. The justification is not the complete cessation of benefit. Even if complete cessation were assumed, let that be counted among the necessities that permit forbidden things. Whatever is permitted out of need rather than necessity—such as women adorning themselves with gold and silk, or medical treatment using gold and silk—has been permitted only to secure full

---

## Page 15446

benefit, not because of the necessity that permits eating carrion and the like. Rather, the need here is to obtain the full benefit, since diminished benefit leaves a shortfall that calls for its completion. This is what need means in such cases. As for necessity whose unmet demands result in death, illness, or inability to fulfil obligations—as with the necessity recognized as permitting the consumption of carrion—that degree of necessity is not required in cases such as this. And Allah knows best.

For this reason, a group of our school’s jurists permitted this in the case under discussion. They explained that when an endowment becomes entirely unusable, its endowment status ends; the discussion concerns cases in which something usable remains. Among these jurists is Abū ‘Abd Allāh ibn Taymiyyah, who said in Targhīb al-Qāsid (Encouragement for the Seeker):

“The fifth ruling: When an endowment becomes unusable, several situations may arise.

First: It ceases to exist altogether, as when an endowed horse dies. Its endowment status has then ended.

Second: Something of monetary value remains, as when a tree perishes, a horse becomes emaciated, or a mosque falls into ruin. It is then sold, and the proceeds are spent on acquiring something comparable, or a share in something comparable.

Third: When a mosque’s mats become worn out, or its beams break and splinter, they are sold and the proceeds spent on the mosque’s needs. Likewise, if its beams are about to break, or its building is about to collapse, and it is known that delaying would leave the property unusable, it may be sold.

---

## Page 15447

Ahmad—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said, as reported by Abū Dāwūd:

‘If a mosque contains timbers that retain monetary value but have deteriorated, they may be sold and the proceeds spent on the mosque.’

Fourth: If a mosque falls into ruin and its materials are suitable for another mosque that needs them, they are transferred to it, while the land is sold. This applies if the mosque cannot be rebuilt with the proceeds from selling some of its materials; otherwise, those materials are sold and the proceeds used to rebuild it. Ahmad expressly stated this.

Fifth: If a mosque becomes too small for its congregation, or people disperse from it because the neighbourhood has fallen into ruin, it is sold and the proceeds spent on building another mosque, or acquiring a share in a mosque.”

The jurist who stated this has thus made clear that it cannot be sold when its usefulness has ceased altogether, but only when something usable remains. The purpose, then, is to replace it with something more beneficial to the endowment’s beneficiaries. None of our school’s jurists stipulated that leasing the site must be impossible, although it is known that in most cases the site can be leased. Its beneficiaries, however, receive less from it than they would if it were built upon. If it is sold, the proceeds may purchase something whose rental income is more beneficial to them. This is because someone will buy the site to build upon it for his own use, benefiting from it as his property, and will therefore be interested in acquiring it. Its sale proceeds can then purchase something yielding a more beneficial income than the site itself.

This, then, is the case whose permissibility our school’s jurists have agreed upon. In essence, it amounts to their replacing the endowment for its beneficiaries with something more beneficial to them.

---

## Page 15448

If it is objected that al-Khiraqī’s wording is:

“When the endowed property falls into ruin and yields nothing, and something that yields income for the endowment’s beneficiaries is purchased with its sale proceeds.”

The answer is:

This wording may mean that it no longer yields any of the income it produced when its buildings were intact—as with houses and shops that have fallen into ruin. If they merely fell into disrepair, they would still yield some of the income they produced when fully maintained; if they were completely ruined, however, they would yield none of that income. Alternatively, “yields nothing” may mean that it has ceased to be useful in any way.

If he intended the first meaning—which is the apparent meaning and the appropriate way to understand his words—then it accords with what we have said. This is why he said, “It is sold.” If it had ceased to be useful in every respect, its sale would not be permissible.

If, however, he meant that the endowment’s beneficiaries could no longer benefit from it in any way because its site could not be leased, although others could still benefit from it—as Ahmad said concerning the slave—then the explicit meaning of his words accords with what has already been stated. Their implication, however, would be that if the beneficiaries could lease it for even the smallest rent, selling it would not be permissible. At most, this would constitute one opinion within the school. Ahmad’s explicit statements, however, contradict this regarding the sale of a mosque, a horse endowed for jihad, and other such things, as already mentioned concerning the mosque.

As for a horse endowed for jihad, if it becomes unfit for that purpose, its purchaser may buy it to ride or to drive a mill. Those engaged in jihad could also benefit from it in similar ways: carrying loads on it, using it to drive a mill, or hiring it out and benefiting from its rental income. Yet the benefit intended by endowing it—namely, riding it in jihad—has ceased, even though their ability to benefit from it has not ceased in every respect.

---

## Page 15449

Section

If, according to the apparent position of his school, an endowed mosque—which is endowed for direct use and whose premises are accorded sanctity under Islamic law—may be replaced with another for a beneficial purpose, because the replacement is more useful and suitable, even though the original has not become entirely unusable, and the original property is released from its endowment status, then replacing income-producing endowed property with something more suitable and useful is all the more permissible. This is so even though two positions are reported from him concerning whether a mosque may be sold when it has become entirely unusable. For he permits the sale of income-producing endowed property when needed, with no alternative position reported from him, whereas two positions are reported from him concerning the sale of a mosque when needed.

Thus, if the apparent position of his school permits the original mosque to be released from its endowment status and another mosque to be endowed in its place for a beneficial purpose, even though the original remains usable, then it is all the more permissible to release income-producing property from its endowment status and endow something more suitable in its place, even though the original remains useful.

Selling an income-producing endowment is more readily permissible than selling a mosque, and replacing it is more readily permissible than replacing a mosque. This is because the mosque’s premises themselves are accorded sanctity under Islamic law and are intended for direct use. It may therefore neither be leased nor may compensation be taken in exchange for its use. By contrast, an income-producing endowment may be leased, and compensation may be taken in exchange for its use. The intention is not that the beneficiary should personally make direct use of it, as is intended in the case of a mosque. Nor does it possess the sanctity established by Islamic law on account of the right of Allah Most High that a mosque possesses.

---

## Page 15450
