Moreover, an endowment made for Allah’s sake combines features of emancipation and the transfer of ownership. It more closely resembles the case of a slave woman who has borne her master’s child, according to those who prohibit transferring ownership of her.

Insofar as no one may sell endowed property and retain the proceeds as his own, give it away, or pass it on by inheritance, it resembles emancipation and manumission. Insofar as it can be exchanged for compensation, which is then used to purchase something to take its place, it resembles the transfer of ownership. For if endowed property is destroyed, compensation must be paid for it, and the proceeds used to purchase a replacement, as scholars generally hold. This differs from an emancipated slave, who has become free and can no longer be exchanged for compensation.

The kind of sale permitted for property under unrestricted ownership is never permitted for endowed property: namely, for its owner, his guardian, or his agent to sell it and take ownership of the proceeds without providing a substitute to take its place. This is the kind of sale mentioned alongside gifts and inheritance, as Umar ibn al-Khattāb said concerning his endowment: “It is not to be sold, given away, or inherited.”

A comparable case is that Umar ibn al-Khattāb and Uthmān ibn Affān—may Allah be pleased with them both—designated land conquered by force, such as the land of al-Sawād and elsewhere, as communal property for the Muslims. They did not distribute any land conquered by force. Because they prohibited purchasing such land, lest a Muslim submit to humiliation—for the land tax is akin to the jizya—or the Muslims’ rights be nullified, some scholars supposed that they prohibited its sale because it was an endowment and endowments cannot be sold. They claimed that this entailed that land in Mecca could not be sold, since Mecca had been conquered by force.

This is mistaken. Land subject to the land tax which was conquered by force and designated as communal property may be given away and inherited: it passes from its holder to his heir, and its holder may give it away. This is not possible with endowed property.

If such land is sold to someone who takes the seller’s place with respect to it and changes nothing, then this is a matter in which the rulings of Islamic law attach to the specific properties themselves, whereas in other matters they attach to the category rather than the individual item. Its land tax is therefore paid, so this causes no harm to its rightful beneficiaries, nor does it extinguish their right to it.

---

## Page 15451

And the sale of endowed property is prohibited only to prevent the rights of its beneficiaries from being extinguished. With such land, however, all agree that one holder may replace another, whether through an exchange for compensation or otherwise.

The point here is that an endowment combines features of emancipation and the transfer of ownership. Hence jurists hold two well-known views concerning an endowment for a specified individual: does it require his acceptance, like a gift, or does it require no acceptance, like emancipation? This differs from an endowment for a public purpose, such as mosques. An endowment for a specified individual more closely resembles a transfer of ownership than an endowment of mosques or other endowments for public purposes. Endowing mosques more closely resembles emancipation than do other endowments, for mosques are devoted exclusively to Allah, Mighty and Majestic. As He, the Exalted, says:

“And the mosques belong to Allah, so do not invoke anyone alongside Allah.”

He, the Exalted, also says:

“It is not for the polytheists to maintain the mosques of Allah while bearing witness against themselves to disbelief. Their deeds have come to nothing, and they will abide in the Fire forever.”

“The mosques of Allah are maintained only by those who believe in Allah and the Last Day, establish prayer, give zakah, and fear none but Allah. It is they who may be expected to be rightly guided.”

Mosques are the houses of Allah, just as an emancipated slave becomes free—that is, devoted exclusively to Allah. Tahrīr (emancipation) means release from bondage; the same root occurs in tīn hurr, meaning pure clay. Emancipation thus means freeing the slave exclusively for Allah.

For this reason, the Lawgiver prohibited making part of one’s slave free while leaving the rest enslaved, saying:

“Allah has no partner.”

Thus, if someone emancipates part of his slave, the slave becomes wholly free. In the two Sahīh collections, Ibn Umar reports that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever emancipates his share in a slave, and has sufficient wealth to cover the slave’s price, must have the slave fairly valued and pay his co-owners their shares; the slave is then freed in full at his expense.”

Likewise,

---

## Page 15452

a similar report from Abū Hurayrah appears in the two Sahīh collections. Thus, he required the slave’s emancipation to be completed, even though he was partly owned by someone else. The person freeing him was obliged to pay his co-owners their shares of the price so that the slave’s emancipation would be complete and Allah would have no partner. The four imams and the rest of the Muslim community hold that emancipation must be completed. The majority, however, say that if the person freeing the slave has sufficient means, he must pay compensation, as stated in the hadiths of Ibn Umar and Abū Hurayrah.

If he lacks the means, some hold that the slave must work to pay for his freedom. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him, the report preferred by Abū al-Khattāb. Others do not require this. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad according to the other report, which most of his followers preferred.

The point is that, of all endowed properties, mosques are most fully released from private ownership and most closely resemble emancipation. No one may receive compensation for their use, as is permissible with other endowments, just as someone who frees a slave no longer owns his services. Nevertheless, Ahmad followed the Companions in permitting a mosque to be replaced with another mosque and the original mosque to be converted into a marketplace.

He also permitted pilgrimage offerings and Eid sacrifices to be replaced with better ones, even though these too are dedicated to Allah, Exalted is He, and must be slaughtered for Him. It is therefore all the more appropriate to permit substitution in other endowments, which more closely resemble transfers of ownership. For human rights admit forms of exchange and substitution that the rights of Allah, Exalted is He, do not. An exchange involving a human right is prohibited only if it wrongs someone else, involves a right of Allah, or concerns one of Allah’s rights. Thus, the prayers and Hajj may not be replaced by another act, nor may the direction of prayer

---

## Page 15453

be replaced with another direction, nor the month of Ramadan with another month, nor the time and place of Hajj with another time and place. Indeed, when the people of the pre-Islamic age introduced postponement, which involved moving Hajj to another time, Allah, Exalted is He, said:

“Postponement is only an increase in disbelief, by which those who disbelieve are led astray. They permit it one year and forbid it another, to match the number of months Allah has made sacred, thereby permitting what Allah has forbidden.”

As for the three mosques built by the prophets—peace be upon them—to which journeys are prescribed and travel becomes obligatory through a vow, their sites may not be exchanged for other sites. They may, however, be expanded, and their layout and buildings may be changed, as established by the Sunnah and the consensus of the Companions. Other mosques, by contrast, do not become binding destinations through a vow, nor are they destinations for such journeys. They may therefore be replaced where this serves a beneficial purpose, as explained above. And Allah knows best.

This is further clarified by the scholars’ disagreement concerning an endowment designated for a particular beneficiary: does it belong to the beneficiary, remain the property of the donor, or belong to Allah, Exalted is He? These are three well-known views within the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others. Most of Ahmad’s followers, including al-Qādī and Ibn ʿAqīl, favour the view that it belongs to the beneficiary.

A mosque and similar endowments, however, do not belong to any particular individual, by agreement of the Muslims. Rather, they are said to belong to Allah. They may also be said to belong to the Muslim community, since its members are entitled to benefit from them. If such an endowment may be replaced with something better where this serves a beneficial purpose, then replacing the former kind is all the more permissible, either through a direct exchange or by selling it and purchasing a replacement with the proceeds. As explained above, the replacement is to be something of the same kind that is more beneficial to the beneficiary.

---

## Page 15454

Section

Ahmad explicitly allowed something that goes further than this: endowing property that can be used only by replacing the original asset. Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz stated in al-Shāfī:

Al-Maymūnī reported from Ahmad: “If dirhams are endowed for one’s household, zakat is due on them; if they are endowed for the needy, no zakat is due.”

I asked, “What if a man endows a thousand dirhams in Allah’s cause?”

He replied, “If they are for the needy, nothing is due on them.”

I asked, “What if he endows them for horses and weapons?”

He replied, “This is a matter of uncertainty and ambiguity.”

Abū al-Barakāt said: “The apparent implication of this is that money may be endowed for lending, or for investment with the profits given in charity, as we have related from Mālik and al-Ansārī.”

He said: “Mālik’s position is that endowing money for lending is valid. The author of al-Tahdhīb and others mentioned this in their discussion of zakat. They held that zakat is due on it, just as they held concerning livestock endowed for the poor.”

Muhammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ansārī said: “Dinars may be endowed, since they can be used only by expending the coins themselves. They are entrusted to someone for investment on a profit-sharing basis, and their profits are directed to the purpose designated by the endowment.”

It is well known that, in both lending and profit-sharing investment, the original property passes out of one’s possession and its replacement takes its place. The substitute was thus allowed to stand in its place in the interests of the endowment, even if this was not strictly necessary for the endowment.

This question is disputed within Ahmad’s school. Many of his followers prohibited endowing dirhams and dinars on the grounds mentioned by al-Khiraqī and those who followed him. They cited no explicit statement from Ahmad to this effect; al-Qādī and others transmitted this position only from al-Khiraqī and others.

---

## Page 15455

Al-Qādī, however, interpreted al-Maymūnī’s report as follows:

“Endowing dirhams and dinars is not valid, according to what al-Khiraqī reported. Ahmad said, in al-Maymūnī’s report: ‘If someone endows a thousand dirhams in Allah’s cause and for the needy, no zakat is due on them. But if he endows them for horses and weapons, this is a matter of uncertainty and ambiguity.’”

Al-Qādī said: “He did not mean endowing the dirhams themselves. Rather, he meant bequeathing a thousand dirhams to be spent on horses in Allah’s cause, and he withheld judgment on the validity of this bequest.”

Abū Bakr said: “This is because the maintenance of the horses and weapons is the responsibility of whoever endowed them. It appears that he was uncertain where these dirhams should be spent if maintaining the horses and weapons was the responsibility of their owners.”

The former interpretation is more sound, because the question explicitly states that he endowed the thousand dirhams, not that he bequeathed them to take effect after his death. Had he bequeathed money to be spent on horses endowed by someone else, that would have been permissible without dispute, just as it would be permissible to bequeath funds for the upkeep of a mosque built by someone else.

The assertion that maintaining the horses and weapons is the responsibility of whoever endowed them is not accepted as the apparent position of the school. Rather, if the donor has stipulated funds for their maintenance, those funds are used; otherwise, their maintenance is paid from the public treasury, as with other endowments serving public purposes, such as mosques. If no one can be found to provide for an endowed asset’s upkeep, it is sold; the donor is not obliged to maintain it.

Moreover, Ahmad withheld judgment on whether zakat was due, not on whether endowing the money was valid, for that was what he had been asked about. His position is that when an endowment benefits a specific group, such as the descendants of a particular person, zakat is due on the endowed assets themselves. Thus, if someone endows forty sheep for the descendants of a particular person, zakat is due on the flock itself, according to his explicit statement. This is also Mālik’s position.

In Muhannā’s report, he said concerning someone who endows land or sheep in Allah’s cause: “Neither zakat nor the agricultural tithe is due from him: this is devoted to Allah’s cause. Those dues apply only if he dedicates it to his relatives.”

---

## Page 15456

For this reason, his companions said: “This indicates that the beneficiary owns the endowed property itself.” They regarded this as one of the two positions reported from him.

Another view within his school holds that no zakat is due on the endowed property itself, because that ownership is incomplete. Al-Qādī adopted this view in al-Mujarrad, as did Ibn ʿAqīl. It is also the view of most of al-Shāfiʿī’s followers.

As for property endowed for a public purpose, such as jihad or support for the poor and needy, no zakat is due on it according to his school or that of al-Shāfiʿī. Mālik, however, holds that zakat is due on it. Ahmad therefore withheld judgment concerning funds endowed for horses and weapons, because the matter admits of uncertainty: horses and weapons may be assigned to particular people, whether the donor’s children or others, unlike a general endowment that does not subsequently become restricted to particular beneficiaries.

Section

In Bakr ibn Muhammad’s report, he said concerning someone who bequeathed a horse, a saddle, and a silver-mounted bridle to be held as a dedicated endowment in Allah’s cause:

“It should remain as he endowed and bequeathed it. But if the silver from the saddle and bridle is sold and the proceeds devoted to an endowment of the same kind, that is preferable to me, because the silver serves no useful purpose. Perhaps a saddle and bridle could be purchased with that silver, which would be more beneficial to the Muslims.”

He was asked: “May the silver be sold and the proceeds spent on maintaining the horse?” He replied: “No.”

This report is among those mentioned by al-Khallāl, his companion Abū Bakr ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, al-Qādī, Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, and others. Ahmad thus explicitly stated that the horse and silver-mounted bridle should remain as the donor endowed and bequeathed them, and that selling the silver from the saddle and bridle and devoting the proceeds to an endowment of the same kind was preferable to him.

He said: “Because the silver serves no useful purpose. Perhaps a saddle and bridle could be purchased with that silver, which would be more beneficial to the Muslims.” Thus, he allowed a choice between retaining the endowed ornamentation as an endowment and

---

## Page 15457

selling it and using its proceeds to purchase a saddle and bridle that are more beneficial to the Muslims. This makes clear which of the two options is preferable.

His statement, “Because the silver serves no useful purpose,” does not mean that it has no benefit whatsoever. Adornment is a permissible benefit, and it is permissible to hire someone to fashion lawful ornaments. If someone destroys such lawful workmanship, he must compensate for it; Ahmad explicitly stated this. Were there no benefit in it, hiring someone to produce it would not be valid, nor would its destruction entail liability. Rather, he meant that its benefit was incomplete, just as someone may say, “This is of no benefit,” meaning that it does not provide a full benefit.

This is supported by his statement, “Its proceeds may be used to purchase something more beneficial to the Muslims.” This indicates that both options are permissible, but the second is more beneficial. Moreover, if it had no benefit whatsoever, endowing it would not be valid, since it is impermissible to endow something from which no benefit can be derived. This makes clear that the preferable option is to sell the endowed property and replace it with something more beneficial to its beneficiary. Doing so is preferable to retaining it as an endowment because it better serves the beneficiary’s interests. Yet he did not make replacement obligatory.

His statement, “It should remain as he endowed and bequeathed it,” implies that this ruling applies both to what he endowed and to what he directed in his will to be endowed, even though the question put to him concerned someone who had directed in his will that it be endowed. It is well known that his conditions must be followed concerning what he directed to be endowed, just as they must be followed concerning what he himself endowed. Likewise, his instructions must be followed concerning a slave whom he directed to be emancipated, just as they must be followed concerning one whom he himself emancipated. It is impermissible to endow different property or emancipate a different slave from those specified in his will, just as it is impermissible to substitute different property or a different slave for what he himself endowed or whom he emancipated. Thus, the permissibility of replacement in one case is like its permissibility in the other. He based his recommendation of replacement solely on the replacement being more beneficial to the Muslims than the ornamentation.

---

## Page 15458

An analogous case is that of someone who endows property decorated with carvings, marble, woodwork, or other embellishments that increase its price. It may be sold and its proceeds used to purchase something more beneficial to the endowment’s beneficiaries. What matters, then, is what benefits them most. That silver may be more beneficial to its purchaser, since the benefit a private owner derives differs from that derived by the endowment’s beneficiaries. This is why an endowed property that has fallen into ruin may be sold when it no longer serves its purpose. It is well known that something wholly devoid of benefit cannot lawfully be sold. Here, however, its benefit has ceased for the endowment’s beneficiaries, but not for a private owner: the beneficiaries seek rental income or accommodation, neither of which is possible while the property lies in ruins, whereas a private owner may purchase it and restore it at his own expense.

There are two positions in Ahmad’s school concerning such ornamentation, including the ornamentation of helmets, body armour, sword belts, and similar military equipment. The equipment of horses used in jihad is treated like that of the fighters themselves.

This report from Ahmad indicates that it is permissible to adorn horse equipment, such as saddles and bridles, with silver. He permitted such equipment to be endowed, while considering it preferable to sell it and devote its proceeds to endowing similar items. Had he not regarded it as permissible, no choice between the two would have been offered.

Al-Qādī said in al-Mujarrad: “The apparent meaning of this is that he held the endowment of the silver on the bridle and saddle to be invalid, because using it is forbidden. This differs from jewellery whose use is permissible. He allowed its proceeds to be spent on items of the same kind as those endowed—saddles and bridles—but prohibited spending them on the horse’s upkeep, since that is not of the same kind as the endowed property.”

Al-Qādī based this on the view that such ornamentation is forbidden, and that if someone endows something whose use is forbidden, it should be sold and its proceeds used to purchase something whose use is permissible, which is then endowed for the same purpose. Clearly, were the endowment contract not understood to allow replacement in the interests of the endowment, this would not be permissible. Similarly, in the case of sale

---

## Page 15459

and marriage, since these contracts do not entail a right of replacement, it is invalid to sell something whose use is unlawful or to marry a woman with whom intercourse is forbidden. This resembles the case of someone who offers an animal that is ineligible as a sacrificial offering: its proceeds are used to purchase an eligible offering. The same applies to the Eid sacrifice. Ahmad’s words support the first approach, not the second.

This is the approach of Abū Muhammad and others among Ahmad’s followers. Abū Muhammad said:

“Ahmad permitted the silver from a saddle and bridle to be used to purchase a saddle and bridle, because this directs their value to equipment of the same kind as that which the silver had adorned, once it no longer serves a useful purpose there. This resembles a horse endowed for jihad: if it becomes unfit and can no longer be used in jihad, it may be sold and its proceeds spent on another like it.”

He said:

“It was not permissible to spend the silver on the horse’s upkeep, because that would divert it from its designated purpose.”

Abū Muhammad treated this as a case in which all benefit had ceased, like a horse becoming unfit or an endowed property falling into ruin. This accords with his principle that an endowment may be sold only when it has ceased to provide any benefit whatsoever, as discussed above. It also indicates that endowing jewellery is valid, which is the position of al-Khiraqī, al-Qādī and others. Al-Qādī presents its validity as the sole position of the school. Abū al-Khattāb and others, however, recognize disagreement on the issue; indeed, they cite explicit statements that it is invalid, according to the statements of Ahmad that reached them.

Al-Qādī said:

“What if someone endows jewellery to be lent out and worn? In the reports of al-Athram and Hanbal, he said, ‘It is not valid,’ and rejected the hadith narrated from Umm Salama concerning its endowment.”

Al-Qādī said:

“The apparent meaning of what al-Khiraqī transmitted is that endowing it is permissible, because it can be used while the property itself remains intact. His statement, ‘It is not valid,’ means that the hadith is not authentic

---

## Page 15460

on this subject; he did not mean that endowing it is invalid.”

Abū al-Khattāb said:

“As for endowing jewellery to be lent out and worn, this is permissible according to the apparent meaning of what al-Khiraqī transmitted. Al-Athram and Hanbal, however, reported from him that it is invalid.”

His permission to endow a silver-adorned saddle and bridle accords with what al-Khiraqī mentioned. Nevertheless, he prefers replacing it with something more beneficial to the endowment’s beneficiaries: purchasing a saddle and bridle with the proceeds of the silver adornment.

Section

Ahmad’s explicit statements in several places, and the position adopted by the majority of his followers, permit replacing pilgrimage offerings and Eid sacrifices with better ones. In Abū Tālib’s report, Ahmad said concerning a man who buys a sacrificial animal and fattens it for Eid al-Adhā:

“He may exchange it for a better one, but not for an inferior one.”

He was then asked, “If he replaces it with a better one, may he sell it?” He replied, “Yes.”

Al-Qādī said:

“In the reports of Sālih, Ibn Mansūr and ʿAbd Allāh, permission to replace a sacrificial animal with a better one is stated without qualification.”

He added:

“I saw in the questions recorded by al-Fadl ibn Ziyād: ‘Once he has designated it as a sacrifice, he may sell it only to someone who intends to offer it as a sacrifice.’”

These two reports from him parallel the two reports concerning a mosque: may it be sold, or its materials transferred to a better mosque? Likewise here: in one of the two reports, he prohibited taking anything in exchange for it

---

## Page 15461

unless it is itself to be sacrificed, because the sanctity of the sacrifice attaches to that particular animal.

Al-Khiraqī said:

“Once an animal has been designated as an Eid sacrifice, it may be exchanged for a better one.”

Al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā said in al-Taʿlīq:

“If a man designates a badanah (a camel or cow) as a sacrificial offering, he may sell it, but must provide another badanah in its place. If he does not designate its replacement until it has grown in body or hair, or has given birth, he must provide an animal equivalent to it, including that increase, and offspring equivalent to its offspring. But if he designates its replacement before that growth or birth, nothing is due from him for the increase.”

I know of no one among Ahmad’s followers who disagreed with this except Abū al-Khattāb, who adopted the position that replacing it is impermissible. He said:

“If a man vows to offer an Eid sacrifice and specifies the animal, his ownership of it ceases, and he may neither sell nor exchange it. The same applies if he vows to free a particular slave or makes a vow concerning specified dirhams.”

He said:

“In my view, this is what follows by analogy from the school’s principles, because specifying the object has the same force as an explicit stipulation in a vow, which cannot be annulled. For Ahmad expressly stated, in the reports of Sālih and Ibrāhīm ibn al-Hārith, concerning someone who vows to sacrifice a particular animal which subsequently loses the sight of one eye or develops a defect: ‘It fulfils his obligation.’ Had it remained his property, it would not have fulfilled his obligation, and he would have been required to provide a sound animal, just as if he had made an unrestricted vow to offer a sacrifice.”

He said:

“Likewise, in Hanbal’s report, he expressly stated concerning a pilgrimage offering that becomes incapacitated within the Sacred Precinct: ‘It fulfils his obligation.’ Had it remained his property, it would not have fulfilled his obligation, and a replacement would have been required. There are other such rulings as well. All this supports what I have said.”

Abū al-Khattāb based this on the premise that the man’s ownership of the animal had ceased, and that replacing it is therefore impermissible once ownership has ceased. This is also the position of the followers of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. Abū Hanīfah permits replacing it with a better one. His followers, together with al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā and those who agree with him, based this on the premise that the man’s ownership of the animal had not ceased. The dispute between the two groups concerns this underlying premise. Ahmad and the jurists among his followers

---

## Page 15462

do not need to base their position on this premise.

Abū al-Khattāb said:

“This is what analogy dictates concerning vows: if someone vows to pray in a particular mosque, he is bound to do so there. We depart from this only because of the revealed ruling expressed in the Prophet’s words, peace and blessings be upon him: ‘Journeys are not to be undertaken except to three mosques.’”

He was asked: “Then if someone vows to pray in al-Masjid al-Aqsā, may he pray in the Sacred Mosque instead?”

He replied:

“If the report to that effect is not authentic, we do not concede this according to this reported position.”

What Abū al-Khattāb said contradicts not only Ahmad’s explicit statements and the position of the majority of his followers, but also his other principles. According to Ahmad, the permissibility of substitution does not depend on the animal remaining in its owner’s ownership; indeed, ownership has no bearing on whether substitution is permissible. If someone vows to free a slave and designates a particular one, it is unquestionably impermissible to substitute another, even though the slave has not passed out of his ownership.

Indeed, he also holds that the Eid sacrifice has passed out of its owner’s ownership, yet it may be exchanged for a better one. We say the same concerning mosques, just as we permit substitution in things pledged by vow. Slaughtering a sacrifice is an act of worship devoted to Allah, and slaughtering a better animal is more beloved to Allah Most High. This is therefore like replacing something pledged by vow with something better, which is also better for the people of the Sacred Precinct. Emancipation is different: the slave himself is entitled to it, and substituting another would extinguish his right.

The disagreement over whether an Eid sacrifice designated by vow remains its owner’s property or passes out of his ownership and belongs to Allah resembles the disagreement concerning an endowment for a public purpose. The well-known position in Ahmad’s school, and that of the majority, is that such property belongs to Allah.

It may also be said that it belongs to the Muslim community. Those who may effect its transfer are the Muslims entitled to benefit from it; they act by virtue of custodial authority, not ownership.

---

## Page 15463

and likewise, if a sacrificial offering or an Eid sacrifice designated by vow is held to have passed out of its owner’s ownership, he still has authority to slaughter it and distribute its meat. He similarly has authority to exchange it for another. This is comparable to someone destroying it: he would receive its monetary value and purchase a replacement with it, even though he did not own it. Thus, its passing out of his ownership does not preclude his exercising authority over it under Islamic law.

Asking, in this and similar cases, “Does its owner own it or not?” is like asking: “Can a slave own property? Can those at war with the Muslims acquire ownership of Muslim property? Does the beneficiary of an endowment own the endowed property?” Disagreement over these questions arose only because ownership was assumed to be a single category whose various forms were all alike. This is not so. Rather, ownership is legally conferred power. The Lawgiver may permit a person one kind of disposal but not another, granting him authority to undertake one act but not another. He thus possesses a particular form of ownership, unlike that of an heir. Nor is an heir’s ownership identical in every respect to that of a purchaser; the two may differ. Likewise, ownership of plunder, war booty, and similar property may differ from ownership acquired through purchase or inheritance.

Thus, if someone says, “He owns the designated Eid sacrifice,” meaning that he owns it as he owns something he has purchased—so that he may sell it and keep its price, give it to whomever he wishes, and leave it to be inherited as property—this is not the case. Likewise, if saying that it has passed out of his ownership means that his authority to dispose of it has ceased, as authority to dispose of property ceases through enslavement or sale, this too is not the case. Rather, he has a particular form of ownership over it: the right to safeguard it, slaughter it, distribute its meat, give some as gifts and charity, and eat from it. These rights over his sacrifice belong to him and to no one else.

---

## Page 15464

Section

There are several lines of evidence for this.

First: It is established in the two Sahīh collections, on the authority of ʿĀʾishah, may Allah be pleased with her, that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Were it not that your people have only recently left the age of pre-Islamic ignorance, I would have dismantled the Kaaba, brought it down to ground level, and given it two doors: one through which people would enter and another through which they would leave.”

It is well known that the Kaaba is the most excellent endowment on the face of the earth. Had altering and replacing it in the manner he described, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, been obligatory, he would not have refrained from doing so. It is therefore clear that this was permissible and would have been more beneficial, were it not for the consideration he mentioned: that Quraysh had only recently embraced Islam. This would have involved replacing its structure with another. It is thus clear that this is permissible in principle. Replacing one structural arrangement with another is one form of replacement.

It is also established that ʿUmar and ʿUthmān altered the structure of the Prophet’s Mosque, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. ʿUmar rebuilt it using materials like those of its original construction: mud bricks and palm trunks. ʿUthmān, however, rebuilt it using superior materials, such as teak. In either case, the Rightly Guided Caliphs replaced the mud bricks and trunks that had formed part of the endowment with other materials. This was among the most widely known occurrences, yet no one objected to it.

There is no difference between replacing one building with another and replacing one site with another when the benefit to be gained calls for it. Thus, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb replaced the mosque of Kufa with another mosque: he replaced the site itself

---

## Page 15465

and the original site became a market for date sellers. Thus, the site became a market after having been a mosque. This is the clearest possible example of replacing an endowment for the sake of a greater benefit. Moreover, it is established that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted replacing what one had vowed to do with something better.

In Ahmad’s Musnad and the Sunan of Abū Dāwūd, Abū Dāwūd said:

Mūsā ibn Ismā‘īl narrated to us: Hammād—meaning Ibn Salamah—narrated to us: Habīb al-Mu‘allim informed us, from ‘Atā ibn Abī Rabāh, from Jābir ibn ‘Abd Allāh:

“A man stood up on the day of the conquest and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I vowed that if Allah, Mighty and Majestic, granted you the conquest of Mecca, I would pray in Jerusalem.’ Abū Salamah once specified: ‘Two units of prayer.’ He replied, ‘Pray here.’ The man repeated his request, and he replied, ‘Pray here.’ The man repeated his request again, so he said, ‘Then do as you wish.’”

Abū Dāwūd said: “Something similar was also narrated from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf, from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

There is a third chain for this report in the Sunan, transmitted by Ahmad and Abū Dāwūd from a group of the Prophet’s Companions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Abū Dāwūd said:

(1) ibn Khālid narrated to us: Abū ‘Āsim narrated to us. And ‘Abbās al-‘Anbarī narrated to us: Rawh narrated to us, from Ibn Jurayj: Yūsuf ibn al-Hakam ibn Abī Sufyān informed us that he heard Hafs ibn ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf narrate this report from men among the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, adding that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“By Him who sent Muhammad with the truth, had you prayed here, it would have sufficed you in place of prayer in Jerusalem.”

Abū Dāwūd said: “The Ansār narrated it from Ibn Jurayj.” Hafs ibn ‘Umar ibn Hinnah said; and ‘Umar said: “They both informed him, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf, from men among the Companions of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”

> 
> (For this report there is a third chain of transmission in the Sunan, transmitted by Ahmad and Abū Dāwūd from a group of the Prophet’s Companions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Abū Dāwūd said: Mukhallad ibn Khālid narrated to us: Abū ‘Āsim narrated to us; and (2) ‘Abbās al-‘Anbarī (3) narrated to us: Rawh narrated to us, from Ibn Jurayj: Yūsuf ibn al-Hakam ibn Abī Sufyān informed us that he heard Hafs ibn ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf [and ‘Amr—‘Abbās said, “ibn Hanna”—who both informed him, from ‘Umar ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf] (4), narrating this report from men among the Prophet’s Companions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He added: The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “By Him who sent Muhammad with the truth, if you prayed here, it would suffice you in place of a prayer in Jerusalem.”
> 
> Abū Dāwūd said: Al-Ansārī (5) transmitted it from Ibn Jurayh, saying: [“Ja‘far ibn ‘Umar”; and he said, “‘Amr ibn Hayya”; and he said:] (6) “They both informed him, from ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn ‘Awf, from men among the Prophet’s Companions, may Allah bless him and grant him peace.”)
> 
> (1) The correction is based on al-Musnad, 5:363; Sunan Abī Dāwūd, no. 3306; Tuhfat al-Ashrāf, 11:193; and Atrāf al-Musnad, 8:314.
> 
> (2) Abū Dāwūd has the chain-switch marker (h), which here functions as a conjunction.
> 
> (3) Al-Fatāwā reads “al-‘Ambarī,” a textual corruption. Abū Dāwūd adds “the meaning” after this, indicating that the wording of the report is Khālid ibn Mukhallad’s, whereas ‘Abbās al-‘Anbarī’s report conveys the same meaning.
> 
> (4) The words in square brackets are omitted from al-Fatāwā because the copyist’s eye skipped ahead.
> 
> (5) Al-Fatāwā reads “al-Ansār,” a textual corruption.
> 
> (6) The words in square brackets appear in a corrupted form in al-Fatāwā. They concern the transmitters’ differing versions of the names “Hafs ibn ‘Umar” and “‘Amr ibn Hanna.” Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15466

In the Musnad and Sahīh Muslim, Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with them both, reported:

“A woman fell ill and said, ‘If Allah heals me, I shall certainly set out and pray in Jerusalem.’ She recovered and then prepared to leave. Maymūnah came to greet her, and the woman told her about this. Maymūnah said, ‘Stay, eat what you have prepared, and pray in the mosque of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, for I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say: “One prayer in it is better than a thousand prayers in any other mosque, except the mosque of the Kaaba.”’”

This is the position of scholars generally, including al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, Abū Yūsuf—the companion of Abū Hanīfah—Ibn al-Mundhir, and others. They said: If someone vows to pray in Jerusalem, praying in the mosque of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, fulfils his vow. If he vows to pray in the mosque of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, praying in the Sacred Mosque fulfils his vow. If he vows to pray in the Sacred Mosque, however, praying elsewhere does not fulfil his vow according to the majority. This is the position of Ibn al-Musayyib, Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī according to the sounder of his two opinions, and Abū Yūsuf, the companion of Abū Hanīfah.

It is reported from Abū Hanīfah that no particular place becomes binding for prayer. This differs from vowing to visit the Sacred Mosque for Hajj or ‘umrah, which is indisputably binding. Abū Hanīfah based this on his principle that a vow makes an act obligatory only if acts of the same kind are obligatory under Islamic law. Mālik, Ahmad, and al-Shāfi‘ī, according to the apparent position of his school, hold that a vow makes any act of obedience obligatory, even if acts of that kind are not obligatory under Islamic law, as established in Sahīh al-Bukhārī on the authority of ‘Ā’ishah

---

## Page 15467

, may Allah be pleased with her, who reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah must obey Him, and whoever vows to disobey Allah must not disobey Him.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, also said, “Pray here,” and said:

“If you prayed here, it would suffice in place of a prayer—or every prayer—in Jerusalem.”

He specifically instructed him to pray in the Sacred Mosque; he did not say, “Pray wherever you wish.” He also said:

“If you prayed here, it would suffice in place of a prayer in Jerusalem.”

Thus, he made prayer in the superior mosque—not prayer just anywhere—the means of fulfilling the vow. This shows that he directed him to the alternative only because it was superior, not because the place of prayer had never become binding.

His statements establishing the superiority of his mosque and the Sacred Mosque over al-Aqsā Mosque are recorded in the collections of authentic hadith. The superiority of the Sacred Mosque over his mosque is recorded in the Sunan collections and the Musnad. It is also established in the two Sahīh collections, through the reports of Abū Hurayrah and Abū Sa‘īd, that he said:

“No journey should be undertaken except to three mosques: the Sacred Mosque, al-Aqsā Mosque, and this mosque of mine.”

In a wording recorded by Muslim:

“One travels only to three mosques.”

This indicates that travelling to these three mosques is an act of piety, a means of drawing near to Allah, and a righteous deed. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, permitted him to go to al-Aqsā, despite instructing him to pray in the Sacred Mosque and telling him that doing so would fulfil his vow. This shows that his instruction was a recommendation, and that the man had the choice of performing the very act he had vowed or performing something superior to it.

It is well known that a vow obliges a person to perform the act of obedience he has vowed to Allah Most High, because the Prophet said:

“Whoever vows to obey Allah must obey Him.”

This is an obligation he has imposed upon himself, not one originally imposed by Islamic law.

---

## Page 15468

Then the Lawgiver explained that a superior substitute fulfils this obligation. The obligation to sacrifice an animal designated for the Eid sacrifice or as an offering for the Sacred Sanctuary is of the same nature as the obligation arising from a vow concerning a specifically designated thing. This shows that replacing it with something better is preferable to slaughtering that particular animal.

The same applies to a liability imposed by Islamic law: for example, when someone owes a one-year-old she-camel but gives a two-year-old she-camel, or owes a two-year-old she-camel but gives a three-year-old she-camel. A hadith from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, recorded in the Sunan collections explains that giving something better than what is required suffices. It was narrated by Abū Dāwūd in his Sunan and by others.

Abū Dāwūd said:

Muhammad ibn Mansūr narrated to us; Ya‘qūb ibn Ibrāhīm narrated to us; my father narrated to us, from Ibn Ishāq, who said: ‘Abd Allāh ibn Abī Bakr narrated to me, from Yahyā ibn ‘Abd Allāh ibn ‘Abd al-Rahmān ibn Sa‘d ibn Zurārah, from ‘Umārah ibn ‘Amr ibn Hazm, from Ubayy ibn Ka‘b, who said:

“The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, sent me as a zakat collector. I came to a man, and when he gathered his livestock for me, I found that he owed only a one-year-old she-camel. I told him, ‘Give a one-year-old she-camel, for that is the zakat you owe.’ He replied, ‘That provides neither milk nor a mount. Here, however, is a young, large, fat she-camel; take her.’ I told him, ‘I will not take what I have not been instructed to take. The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is nearby. If you wish to go to him and offer him what you have offered me, then do so. If he accepts it from you, I will accept it; if he refuses it, I will refuse it.’ He said, ‘I shall do so.’ He went with me, bringing the she-camel he had offered me, until we came to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. He said, ‘O Prophet of Allah, your envoy came to collect the zakat on my livestock. By Allah, neither the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, nor any envoy of his had ever come to collect it before him. I gathered my livestock for him, and he stated that I owed only a one-year-old she-camel, and that is

---

## Page 15469

something that provides neither milk nor a mount. I offered him a large young she-camel to take, but he refused. Here she is; I have brought her to you, O Messenger of Allah. Take her.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to him, ‘That is what you owe, but if you voluntarily offer something better, Allah will reward you for it, and we will accept it from you.’ He replied, ‘Here she is, O Messenger of Allah. I have brought her to you, so take her.’ The Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, then ordered that she be accepted and prayed for blessing upon his wealth.”

The ruling indicated by this hadith—that an animal older than the required age suffices—is the position of the great majority of scholars, including the well-known jurists and others. It is thus established that replacing what is obligatory with something better is permissible, indeed recommended, whether the obligation is imposed by Islamic law or undertaken by the person himself. There is no distinction between an obligation owed as a general liability and one attached to a specifically designated thing. Although a general liability is unspecified in one respect, it is nevertheless defined and distinguished from other things. Hence, there is undoubtedly no entitlement to replace it with something inferior.

Accordingly, if someone vows to endow something but endows something better, that is preferable. Thus, if he vows to build for Allah a mosque of a specified description, or to establish an endowment of a specified description, then builds a better mosque and establishes a better endowment, that is preferable.

Even if he specifies the property, saying, ‘I undertake, for Allah’s sake, to make this house into a mosque, or to endow it for the poor and needy,’ then builds something better or endows something better, that is preferable. This is like someone who vows to pray in al-Aqsā Mosque but prays in the Sacred Mosque instead, or someone who owes a one-year-old she-camel but gives something better.

The jurists have disagreed about exceeding a prescribed obligatory amount—for example, giving more than one sā‘ for zakat al-fitr. Most permit it, and this is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad

---

## Page 15470

and others. Mālik is reported to have regarded this as disliked. As for giving something of superior quality, they agreed that this is permissible. The correct view is that both are permissible, because Allah Most High says:

“And upon those who are able to fast is a ransom: feeding a needy person. But whoever voluntarily does more good, it is better for him. And fasting is better for you, if only you knew.”

It is established by scholarly consensus—and recorded in the authentic hadith collections and other hadith works, as well as works of Quranic commentary and jurisprudence—that when Allah first made fasting Ramadan obligatory, a person who was not travelling could choose between fasting and feeding one needy person each day.

The obligatory amount was therefore to feed one needy person, but Allah, glorified be He, encouraged feeding more than that, saying:

“And upon those who are able to fast is a ransom: feeding a needy person. But whoever voluntarily does more good, it is better for him.”

Then He said:

“And fasting is better for you.”

Thus, while they had this choice, there were three levels: the highest was fasting; next was feeding more than one needy person each day; and the lowest was confining oneself to feeding one needy person. Then Allah made fasting compulsory and removed the choice between these three alternatives.

If it is objected:

In Sunan Abī Dāwūd, ‘Abd Allāh ibn Muhammad al-Nufaylī narrated to us: Muhammad ibn Salamah narrated to us, from Abū ‘Abd al-Rahīm, from Jahm ibn al-Jārūd, from Sālim ibn ‘Abd Allāh, from his father, who said:

“‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, designated a choice she-camel as a sacrificial offering and was offered three hundred dinars for her. He came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I have designated a choice she-camel as a sacrificial offering and have been offered three hundred dinars for her. May I sell her and use the proceeds to buy large sacrificial animals?’ He replied, ‘No. Sacrifice that very animal.’”

Did he not therefore forbid him to sell her and use the proceeds to buy large sacrificial animals?

---

## Page 15471

The reply is:

This incident—even assuming its authenticity—is a particular case. It contains no general wording that entails an unrestricted prohibition on substitution, nor have we permitted substitution without qualification. No scholar permits replacing an offering with something inferior to the original. Nothing in this hadith indicates that the proposed substitute was better than the original; rather, its apparent meaning is that the original was superior. It is established in an authentic hadith that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked:

“Which slaves are best to free?”

He replied:

“Those who command the highest price and are most prized by their owners.”

Allah Most High says:

“And whoever honours the symbols of Allah—indeed, that is from the piety of hearts.”

It has been said that honouring them includes choosing fine, well-fattened animals and paying high prices for them. This choice she-camel was precious, which is why such a high price was offered for her. Offering her to Allah was therefore better than offering several inferior animals bought with her price. A magnificent horse may be presented to a great king and be dearer to him than several horses bought for the same price. Superiority does not lie in numbers alone. Indeed, Allah Most High says:

“You will never attain righteousness until you spend from that which you love.”

Thus, when a person seeks to draw near to Allah Most High by offering something dearer to him, it is better for him than offering something else, even if the two are equal in value. For an offering and a sacrifice are acts of worship involving both bodily action and wealth; they are not merely charitable donations. Rather, when someone slaughters a prized animal from his own wealth for Allah Most High, that is dearer to Allah Most High.

One of the early Muslims said:

“Let none of you offer to Allah Most High something he would be ashamed to present to someone he holds in esteem.”

Allah Most High says:

“And do not deliberately choose inferior things to give, when you yourselves would not accept them unless you overlooked their defects.”

The two sons of Adam each presented an offering, and it was accepted from one but not from the other. It has been related that the reason for this was that one offered something precious from his wealth, while the other offered something inferior from his wealth. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15472

Shaykh al-Islām was asked:

If someone establishing an endowment or making a vow dedicates something, then considers something else more beneficial to the endowment’s beneficiary, may he replace it, as in the case of an Eid sacrifice?

He replied:

As for replacing something dedicated by vow or as an endowment with something better, as with replacing a sacrificial offering, this falls into two categories.

The first is replacement because of need: when something ceases to serve its purpose, it is sold and the proceeds are used to purchase a substitute. For example, if a horse endowed for military campaigns can no longer be used for that purpose, it is sold and the proceeds are used to purchase a substitute. Likewise, if the area surrounding a mosque falls into ruin, its materials and fittings may be transferred elsewhere, or it may be sold and the proceeds used to purchase a substitute. Or, if the endowment cannot benefit its designated recipient in accordance with the endower’s purpose, it is sold and the proceeds are used to purchase a substitute. If it falls into ruin and cannot be restored, its plot of land is sold and the proceeds are used to purchase a replacement.

All of this is permissible, for when the original property no longer fulfils its intended purpose, its substitute takes its place.

The second is replacement to secure a greater benefit. Examples include replacing a sacrificial offering with a better one, or building another mosque that better serves the townspeople in place of an existing mosque, and selling the original. This and similar replacements are permissible according to Ahmad and other scholars. Ahmad cited as evidence the fact that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—relocated the old mosque of Kūfah, and its former site became a market for date sellers. This constituted replacing the mosque’s site.

---

## Page 15473

As for replacing its building with another, Umar and Uthmān rebuilt the Prophet’s mosque—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—with a structure different from the original, and enlarged it. The same applies to the Sacred Mosque. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said to Āishah:

“Were it not that your people have only recently emerged from the age of ignorance, I would have demolished the Kaaba, rebuilt it at ground level, and given it two doors: one through which people would enter and another through which they would leave.”

Thus, were it not for an overriding consideration, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would have altered the Kaaba’s structure. It is therefore permissible to change the form of an endowed building to secure a greater benefit.

As for replacing one plot of land with another, Ahmad and other scholars explicitly permitted this, following the Companions of Allah’s Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Umar did this, and the matter became widely known without anyone objecting.

As for replacing property endowed to generate income with something better—for example, when someone endows a house, shop, orchard, or village that yields little income, then replaces it with something more beneficial to the endowment—Abū Thawr and other scholars permitted this. Among them was Abū Ubayd ibn Harbawayh, the judge of Egypt, who also ruled accordingly. This follows by analogy from Ahmad’s ruling permitting a mosque to be moved from one site to another for a beneficial purpose. Indeed, if it is permissible, for the sake of a benefit, to replace a mosque with something that is not a mosque, so that its former site becomes a market, then replacing one income-producing property with another is even more clearly permissible. This also follows by analogy from his ruling permitting a sacrificial offering to be replaced with a better one.

He also explicitly ruled that if a mosque stands at ground level and people wish to raise it and build a water-supply facility beneath it, and the neighbours prefer this, it should be done. Some of his followers, however, prohibited replacing a mosque, a sacrificial offering, or endowed land. This is also the position of al-Shāfiī and others. Nevertheless, the scriptural texts, transmitted reports, and analogical reasoning entail that replacement is permissible to secure a benefit. And Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

---

## Page 15474

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

“Someone endowed property for the poor. It is part of a group of vineyards whose owners suffer harm because of the endowment. May he revoke it and endow something else instead? If he does so, do both properties become endowments?”

He replied:

“If this causes harm to all the neighbours, it is permissible to exchange it for property that serves the same purpose. The original property then reverts to private ownership, and the replacement becomes an endowment. This is what Umar ibn al-Khattāb, may Allah be pleased with him, did with the mosque of Kufa: when he established another mosque in its place, the original site became a market for date sellers.”

He was also asked:

“A stable was endowed to support a water basin provided for public use. The administrator sold the stable but has purchased nothing with the proceeds for six years. Is this permissible?”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. There is no doubt that selling it without acquiring a replacement that serves the same purpose is impermissible. However, if he sold it because it had ceased to be useful and purchased a replacement with the proceeds, this is permissible according to the sounder of the scholars’ two opinions. If he replaces it with something better while it remains useful, the matter is disputed. And Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 15475

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A village has several mosques, some of which have fallen into ruin. Prayer is held in only one of them, and an endowment supports them all. Must the ruined mosques be restored and congregational prayer established in a second mosque? Is it permissible to close them?”

He replied:

“Yes, the mosque must be restored so that prayer can be held in it. Likewise, an imam must be appointed to another mosque when there is a benefit and a need for doing so. It is impermissible to close mosques to the purposes for which they were instituted. However, when the local population is small and one mosque suffices—for example, when they live around it—they are not required to divide their congregation among other mosques.”

He was also asked:

“Regarding the endowment of any group in which a member has died leaving a full brother and a child—scholars differ widely over cases of this kind—does his share go exclusively to the child or to the brother? Some people testified that, under the donor’s stipulation, it belongs exclusively to the child, not the brother, although they had not accurately ascertained the boundaries of the endowed property; indeed, they described some of its boundaries differently from what they actually are. Is it permissible for them to do this? And may the judge rule

---

## Page 15476

on the basis of this testimony without seeking further clarification? What is the ruling on all the matters raised in this question? Please give us a detailed ruling; may you be rewarded, if Allah Most High wills.”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. Testimony asserting entitlement under an endowment is inadmissible; the same applies to inheritance. These are matters requiring independent juristic judgment, like determining whether water is pure or impure. Rather, a witness testifies to the stipulations he knows, and the judge then rules on those stipulations according to his own juristic judgment. Allah knows best.”

He was asked:

“An endowment was established for a man and then for his children. The farmers divided it among themselves, after which some exchanged their portions for ones adjoining their partners’ portions. Are the division and exchanges thereby annulled?”

He replied:

“The underlying property of an endowment dedicated to a single class of beneficiaries cannot validly be partitioned. Its benefits, however, may validly be apportioned; this is called ‘apportionment of use.’ If such an arrangement is unrestricted, it is not binding, especially if the endowed property changes. A different arrangement for apportioning its use may therefore be adopted.”

He was asked:

“A church in a village has an endowment. The Christian community in that village has died out, and those who remained have embraced Islam. May it be made into a mosque?”

He replied:

“Yes. If none of the protected non-Muslims entitled to that church remain, it may be made into a mosque, especially if it is in the territory of Greater Syria, for that territory was conquered by force.”

---

## Page 15477

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A mosque stands beside a closed, dilapidated church. Some of its walls have fallen onto the mosque’s entrance and the open areas through which it is approached. Part of the wall that collapsed and fell against the mosque’s wall has shifted, and there is fear that its collapse may harm Muslims and those praying in the mosque. If the entire church falls into ruin, may it be demolished?”

He replied:

“Yes. If there is fear of damage to the mosque and harm to those praying in it, whatever threatens the mosque and its worshippers must be removed. If the danger can only be removed by demolition, the church is to be demolished. Indeed, it is established that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

‘Two qiblahs must not coexist in one land, nor may jizyah be imposed on a Muslim.’

“If this church stands in land conquered by force, it must be removed and not left standing beside the mosque. Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A mosque has no endowment, and beside it is an open space. May accommodation for the imam be built there? Please give us a ruling.”

He replied:

“That is permissible in these circumstances, since the open space is not part of the mosque, as stated in the question. Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 15478

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man at a mosque lives off its endowment but does not attend to its needs. The founder of the endowment has children who are in need. May they replace him with someone else and take whatever exceeds the mosque’s needs?”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. If he does not fulfil his duties, whoever has the authority to replace him should appoint someone who will fulfil them, unless the first man repents and commits himself to fulfilling his duties. Any surplus beyond the mosque’s needs may be spent on other mosques and on relatives of the endowment’s founder and neighbours of the mosque who are eligible for charity.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“Is it permissible to use the endowment’s income to build accommodation outside the mosque to house those who attend to its needs?”

He replied:

“Yes, they may use the endowment’s income to build accommodation outside the mosque that serves the interests of those entitled to that income who attend to the mosque’s needs.”

---

## Page 15479

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A mosque is an old building with an upper storey. No one has lived in or benefited from that storey because it is dilapidated. Its weight is damaging the mosque and causing it to deteriorate, and the mosque has no funds for repairs. Is it permissible to dismantle the upper storey, or should the mosque be closed?”

He replied:

“If dismantling the upper storey serves the mosque’s interests, it should be dismantled, and the salvaged materials should be used for the mosque’s benefit. If possible, they should be used to purchase property to be endowed for the mosque, or to fund repairs to the mosque or its endowed property.”

He was asked:

“A man leased endowed land and built upon it as he wished. He then endowed that building, stipulating that the rent for the endowed land be paid from the income of the endowment he had established upon it. The judge ruled that the endowment was valid under the conditions stated in its deed. Is it permissible to revoke it or not?

“If, after the endowment has been legally established, its founder wishes to revoke it in order to include others through a second endowment, is that permissible?”

He replied:

“Once the judge has ruled that the endowment is valid, it may not be altered, nor may its conditions be changed.”

---

## Page 15480

He was asked:

“An endowment for the poor and needy contains olive trees and other trees that bear little fruit in some years. If they were cut down and sold, their proceeds could purchase property yielding more income. May the administrator do this? If some beneficiaries ask him to cut down the trees, sell them and distribute the proceeds among them, are they entitled to this, or should property be purchased instead?

“If someone assumes responsibility for the endowment and finds that his predecessor has altered the founder’s stipulations, then strives to implement those stipulations, may he receive the customary stipend despite having been unable to fulfil them? This administrator is poor and has no wealth. May he receive something from the share allocated to the poor and administer the endowment without remuneration? Please clarify this for us.”

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. Yes, those trees may be sold and their proceeds used to purchase something yielding more income. Trees are like buildings: the administrator may change the form of an endowment to one that better serves its interests, just as the Rightly Guided Caliphs altered the form of the two mosques in the two Holy Sanctuaries, and as ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb moved the mosque of Kufa from one location to another, and in other similar cases.

“The proceeds from selling the trees must not be distributed among the current beneficiaries, because trees are like buildings: their sale proceeds do not belong exclusively to the current beneficiaries. This is not on the same footing as trees, crops and benefits from which each successive group of beneficiaries is exclusively entitled to whatever is obtained during its own time.

---

## Page 15481

As for the administrator, he must perform as much of his obligatory work as he can, and receive remuneration corresponding to that work. If the amount due is ten shares of the remuneration payable, he may take it; and if the work he performs entitles him to the full amount, he may take it all. He may also receive, on account of his poverty, what a poor person receives on that account. Allah knows best.”

He was asked about altering the form of an endowment.

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. Whatever extends beyond the boundaries of the endowment into the Muslims’ roadway or infringes upon neighbours’ rights must undoubtedly be removed. Whatever extends into a public thoroughfare must be removed. If it extends onto someone else’s property, it may remain with the owner’s permission; otherwise, it must be removed.

“As for altering the form of the building without encroaching upon anyone’s rights, the deciding consideration is the interest served. If its present form better serves the endowment and its beneficiaries, it should be retained. If restoring its former form would serve them better, it should be restored. If rebuilding it in a third form would better serve the endowment, it should be built accordingly. Thus, the form of the building should be determined by the interests of the endowment, adapting to those interests wherever they lie.

“It is established that the Rightly Guided Caliphs, such as ʿUmar and ʿUthmān, altered the form of endowments to serve their interests. Indeed, ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb went further than this: he relocated the old mosque of Kufa, whose former site became the date-sellers’ market, and built a mosque for the people elsewhere. Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 15482

He was asked:

Someone entered into a tree-planting arrangement on endowed land, stipulating that the endowment would receive two-thirds of the trees planted and the worker one-third. May succeeding administrators sell the endowment’s share of the trees?

He replied:

“He may not sell it unless a need requires this. Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman endowed shops and a house for her two children, stipulating that, after her sons and her children’s sons, the endowment should revert to the endowment of the school of Nūr al-Dīn al-Shahīd and other purposes sanctioned by Islamic law. Some of the woman’s relatives then wrongfully and fraudulently sold the endowed property. The heirs brought proceedings against the purchaser, and the judge entered an annotation concerning the witnesses to the deed, which was valid and duly established. The purchaser then made the property a charitable endowment to provide bread for the poor, with the chief appointed as its administrator. Is this valid? If the chief, a learned and devout man, knows that this person has wrongfully seized the property, is it lawful for him to serve as its administrator? What is the ruling?

He replied:

“It is not permissible to sell property belonging to a valid, binding endowment that continues to provide its intended benefit. Nor is the purchaser’s endowment of it valid. The administrator of the second endowment may not direct its benefits to anyone other than the original beneficiaries, or deal with it without a justification sanctioned by Islamic law, whether he acts under that invalid administrative appointment or otherwise. Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 15483

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man built a wall in a Muslim cemetery, intending to reserve the enclosed area for burying his own dead. Another man claimed that some of his dead lay beneath the wall and within the enclosure. Is he permitted to do this?

He replied:

“He has no right to build a wall in a Muslim cemetery or to reserve any part of it exclusively for himself, to the exclusion of others entitled to use it. Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Most of a bathhouse is endowed for the poor, the needy, and jurists. A man who owns nearby bathhouses contrived to purchase a share in it, took his share of the lead on hand, and put the bathhouse out of operation, causing harm. Is he obliged to contribute to its upkeep on the same basis as the endowment, or not?

He replied:

“Praise be to Allah. He has no right to deal with the jointly owned bathhouse without the co-owners’ permission, nor does the Lawgiver authorize him to do so. He may not take possession of any part of it without the co-owners’ permission, nor may he divide

---

## Page 15484

anything on his own and take his share of it, whether lead or anything else. He may not alter the structure of any part of it, nor may he alter the boiler or anything else. All of this is agreed upon by the Muslims. He has no right to close it; rather, it must be rented out on behalf of all the co-owners if any of them requests this, and the rent divided among them.

This is the position of the great majority of scholars, including Abū Hanīfa, Mālik, and Ahmad. If the bathhouse requires essential repairs, the co-owner must contribute to them alongside the others, according to the sounder of the two scholarly views. Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A public water conduit has an overflow that discharges into a wastewater channel. Nearby is a conduit carrying pure water, but its supply is small. Is it permissible to divert that overflow to the ablution facility? Will the person who does this be rewarded? Is it permissible to prevent him from doing so?

He replied:

“Yes, this is permissible with the ruler’s permission. It is not permissible to prevent it if preventing it serves no interest recognized by Islamic law. Whoever works to bring this about will be rewarded. Allah knows best.”

---

## Page 15485

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked: (*)

About an endowment for which compensation is used to purchase something to take its place. An example is an endowment destroyed by someone: compensation is collected from him and used to purchase its replacement. Scholars unanimously agree that anyone who destroys an endowment is liable to compensate for it. Liability also arises from possession. Thus, if someone unlawfully seizes it and it perishes while in his wrongful possession, he must compensate for it, by scholarly agreement. Some scholars have, however, disagreed over whether unlawful seizure entails liability for certain kinds of property, such as real estate, and whether certain kinds, such as movable property, may validly be endowed. Nevertheless, they have not disputed that compensation is due for its destruction at someone’s hands, as with other property.

This differs from the case of a slave woman who has borne her master a child. Although scholars agree that compensation is due for causing her death, they disagree over whether liability also arises from possession of her. Most—including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad—hold that it does. Abū Hanīfa, however, holds that it does not.

Liability arising from possession is contractual liability, such as the seller’s responsibility to deliver the item sold, to ensure that it is free of defects, and to ensure that it was rightfully sold. His liability for recourse arising from the sale follows from the contract, even if he has not expressly stipulated it.

One of the established grounds for purchasing a replacement with the proceeds of an endowment is that the endowment has ceased to provide any benefit. In that case, according to the school of Ahmad and others, it is sold and its price used to purchase something to take its place. May it be exchanged for something better while it is still producing income? There are two opinions within his school. Permitting this is the position of Abū Thawr and others.

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 230):
> This is not a question addressed to the shaykh, but rather a statement by him—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—as is evident. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15486

The point is this: where replacement is permissible, must the replacement be on the same street or in the same town as the original endowment? Or may it be elsewhere if that would better serve the endowment’s beneficiaries—for example, if they reside in a different town and purchasing the replacement there would benefit them more through greater income and easier access?

We say: I know of no one who has stipulated that the replacement must be in the town of the original endowment. Rather, Ahmad’s explicit statements and principles, together with the general and unrestricted wording of his statements and those of his followers, entail doing whatever best serves the endowment’s beneficiaries. His guiding principle in this matter is to safeguard the interests of the endowment. Indeed, his guiding principle regarding contracts generally is to consider people’s interests. Allah has commanded righteousness and forbidden corruption, and has sent His messengers to secure and perfect benefits and to eliminate and reduce harms.

“And Moses said to his brother Aaron, ‘Take my place among my people, put things right, and do not follow the way of those who spread corruption.’”

Shuayb said:

“I only intend reform as far as I am able.”

And the Most High said:

“Whoever fears Allah and sets things right—no fear will there be concerning them, nor will they grieve.”

And the Most High said:

“When it is said to them, ‘Do not spread corruption on earth,’ they say, ‘We are only reformers.’”

“Indeed, it is they who spread corruption.”

Ahmad ibn Hanbal permitted replacing one mosque with another when this served a beneficial purpose, just as he permitted alterations to a mosque for such a purpose. He cited as evidence that Umar ibn al-Khattāb—may Allah be pleased with him—replaced the old mosque of Kufa with another mosque, and the first mosque became a market for date sellers.

Ahmad also permitted relocating a mosque to another village if its locality fell into ruin. Indeed, according to the stronger of the two opinions reported from him, that mosque may be sold and its price used to build another mosque in a different village if it is no longer needed in the first village. Thus, he considered the benefit to mosques as a category, even if the mosque was in

---

## Page 15487

a village other than the original one. Since mosques are shared by all Muslims, there is even stronger justification for relocating an endowment dedicated to particular people to their town than there is for relocating a mosque. An endowment for specified beneficiaries is their exclusive right, in which no one else shares. At most, after their line has died out, it may pass to a general charitable cause, such as the poor and needy; it would then be like a mosque.

Thus, if having the endowment in their town would benefit them more, purchasing its replacement there is what its administrator ought to do. This is comparable to an endowed horse that is sold and replaced with something purchased for its value: if it was endowed for people at one frontier post who subsequently moved to another, purchasing the replacement at the frontier post where the horse is secured takes precedence over purchasing it at another post. If, however, the horse is endowed for all Muslims, it is like an endowment for a general charitable cause, such as mosques or endowments for the needy.

This is further illustrated by the following: if the endowed property were movable—such as lighting, weapons, or scholarly books—and were endowed for the specified descendants of a man, it could be kept wherever they lived. Indeed, that would be the required course, unlike an endowment dedicated to the inhabitants of a particular town.

But if compensation is obtained for it, should that compensation, when movable, be used to purchase something to take its place? Purchasing the replacement with this compensation in the town where the beneficiaries reside takes precedence over purchasing it at the location of the original real estate, if that would benefit them more; for specifying the location of the property

---

## Page 15488

at its original site serves no purpose recognized by Islamic law and offers no benefit to the endowment’s beneficiaries. Whatever the Lawgiver has not commanded and offers a person no benefit is neither obligatory nor recommended.

Thus, someone purchasing a replacement with the compensation is neither obliged nor recommended to retain the original location. Rather, choosing another location is permissible. It may be recommended, and may even be obligatory when the benefit can only be secured by doing so. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15489

Chapter on Gifts and Donations

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Which is better: charity or a gift?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. “Charity” is what is given for Allah’s sake as a pure act of worship, without any particular regard for a specific individual or seeking anything from him. Rather, it is directed to appropriate recipients of charity, such as those in need.

A “gift,” however, is intended to honor a particular person, whether out of love, friendship, or a desire to have a need fulfilled. For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, would accept gifts and reciprocate them, so that no one would have a favor to hold over him. He would not consume the impurities people shed to cleanse themselves of their sins—namely, charitable offerings. For this and other reasons, he would not partake of charity.

Once this distinction is clear, charity is better, unless a gift serves a purpose that makes it better than charity. Examples include giving a gift to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, during his lifetime out of love for him; giving a gift to a relative to maintain ties of kinship; and giving to a brother for Allah’s sake. Such giving may be better than charity.

---

## Page 15490

He was also asked:

If someone gives another man a gift of something whose particulars are unknown, or grants him permission to use it, is this valid? For example, what if he allows him to take the fruit of a tree next year? And if he wishes to revoke this, may he validly do so?

He replied:
