The scholars have differed over giving gifts whose particulars are unknown. Mālik permitted this, even allowing someone to give another whatever he had inherited from a certain person, without knowing its amount or whether it was a third or a quarter. The same applies if he gives him a share of a house without knowing how large that share is. He likewise permits giving something not yet in existence, such as the fruit of one’s trees for the current year or for ten years. Al-Shāfiʿī did not permit this. The recognized position in the schools of Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad is likewise to prohibit it. However, Ahmad and others permit forms of settlement concerning undetermined claims, and release from such claims, that al-Shāfiʿī does not permit. Abū Hanīfah likewise permits some such arrangements that al-Shāfiʿī does not.

Al-Shāfiʿī requires the amount of the subject matter to be known in most contracts, even the compensation for a wife’s release from marriage, the dower, and the terms stipulated for non-Muslims under Muslim protection. Most scholars allow greater latitude in this regard. This is discussed in its proper place. Mālik’s position on this issue is stronger.

This question is connected to another fundamental principle: contracts of exchange, such as sale, marriage, and compensated divorce, become binding before possession is taken. Thus, taking possession—which follows from and is entailed by the contract—

---

## Page 15491

is not a condition for the contract to become binding.

As for gratuitous arrangements, such as gifts and loans for use, Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī hold that they become binding only upon taking possession, whereas Mālik holds that they become binding upon conclusion of the contract.

There is disagreement within Ahmad’s school, as there is concerning specifically identified property: does the contract alone make it binding, or must possession be taken? Two opinions are reported from him. The same applies to certain forms of loans for use. The early generations regularly lent trees for their produce and livestock for their milk. Likewise, they gave gifts of fruit and milk not yet produced, regarding these arrangements as binding. This, however, resembles a loan for use, because the subject of the contract comes into being gradually, like the benefit derived from an asset. Hence such produce, like the benefits of an endowment, is among the things to which its beneficiary is entitled. Accordingly, arrangements involving remuneration with a share of such produce are valid, such as tending trees for a share of their fruit.

As for granting permission to use such things, the scholars do not dispute its permissibility, whether the thing concerned already exists or has yet to exist, and whether its particulars are known or unknown. Such permission, however, does not constitute a binding contract, just as a loan for use is not binding according to those who hold that view, such as Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfiʿī. Mālik, by contrast, regards it as binding when its limits are defined by stipulation or custom. Within Ahmad’s school there are differing views and further distinctions.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A woman gave her husband her document. She had no father, but did have brothers. Are they entitled to prevent her from doing this?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Her brothers have neither guardianship over her nor authority to restrict her disposal of her property. If she is legally competent to give away her property, her gift is valid whether they approve or not. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15492

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman had children by different fathers. She singled out one child and gave him a share of her property as a charitable gift, to the exclusion of his siblings. She then died while still residing in the property she had given away. Is the charitable gift valid or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he had not taken possession of it before she died, the gift became void according to the well-known position in the schools of the four imams. If she had delivered it into his possession, then, according to the correct view, the recipient may not retain it exclusively; rather, it must be shared between him and his siblings. Allah knows best.

He also said:

Section

As for contracts whose binding and settled status depends on taking possession—such as charitable gifts, gifts, pledges, and endowments, according to those who hold that taking possession is a condition for the contract to become binding—these too are valid for undivided shares according to the majority of scholars, including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad. Abū Hanīfah did not permit them, arguing that taking possession is a condition of these contracts, yet possession cannot be taken before partition.

The majority, however, maintain that possession is taken under these contracts just as it is in a sale, even though taking possession is a consequence of a sale, not a condition of its validity or binding force. Possession can also be taken of property that cannot be divided, for they unanimously agree that an undivided share of such property may be given as a gift, since partition is impossible.

---

## Page 15493

Moreover, when someone gives an undivided share whose gift is unanimously held valid—such as a share in indivisible property—or a share whose gift is disputed, according to those who permit it, such as Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad, and it is duly taken into possession and held by the recipient of the gift or charitable donation, it thereby becomes binding by the consensus of the Muslims. The recipient may then exercise all rights of disposal permitted in jointly owned property. He may sell it or give it away; alternatively, the donor and the recipient may agree to divide its use by allocating separate areas or periods of time. They may also rent it out together, as co-owners do with the owner of the share that was not given away. If the donor exercises his rights by residing alongside the recipient, whether under an arrangement dividing their use or otherwise, this neither diminishes nor invalidates the gift. Whoever says otherwise has contravened the consensus of the Muslims.

As for the matters set out by the jurists of Mālik’s school in their books—stipulating an option; holding that the property’s remaining in the donor’s possession, whether through rental, borrowing, or otherwise, invalidates the taking of possession; holding that the recipient’s taking possession is invalidated if the property returns to the donor shortly afterwards, while there is disagreement if it returns after a longer period, such as a year; and holding that the gift becomes void if the donor falls ill or becomes insolvent before possession is taken, and similar matters—these all concern the donated property itself.

The same applies to their disagreement over whether the donor may be compelled to deliver possession. According to Abū Hanīfah and al-Shāfi‘ī, he may not be compelled, whereas according to Mālik, he may. Two positions are reported from Ahmad concerning unfair pricing.

All these and similar questions concern the donated property itself, whether separately owned or an undivided share. As for the half that remains in the donor’s ownership, they agree—with an agreement necessarily known among scholars of Islamic law as part of the religion of Islam—that the owner’s exercise of his rights over it does not invalidate the gift or the possession previously taken of the donated share. They also agree that these two co-owners may exercise their rights just as other co-owners do. Any student of jurisprudence who imagines that

---

## Page 15494

after possession of the undivided share has been delivered, the two parties’ living in the house—one occupying the half that remains his and the other occupying the other half, whether under an arrangement dividing their use or otherwise—invalidates the gift, as Mālik holds in the case of residence in the donated property itself, has contravened the consensus of the Muslims. Such a person is far removed from any sound grasp of jurisprudence or knowledge of Islamic law. For if this were correct, someone who gave away an undivided share would be prevented from benefiting from what remained his, and would be in the same position as someone who had given away the whole property.

Moreover, the jurists discussed this only in relation to the donated property, because the donor’s continued control over it, or its return to him shortly afterwards, ordinarily precludes the recipient’s taking possession. This might also become a means of making a gift without transferring possession: the donor could portray his residence there as a loan for use, as a stratagem. Hence it is reported that ‘Uthmān, may Allah be pleased with him, said:

“What is the matter with people who give their children gifts, but if a man’s child dies, he says, ‘It is my property and remains in my possession,’ yet in the event of his own death, his claim is, ‘I had given it to him’? Only what the child has taken into possession from his father’s property is established as a gift.”

They then asked him about a young child, and he said, “His father’s possession counts as possession on his behalf.” Mālik and others adopted this position. This is clear in itself, whether the donated property is separately owned or an undivided share.

As for the other share, which was not given away, it is like a separate item of property that was not given away. Its owner exercises the rights of a co-owner: if the property requires repairs, he is compelled to undertake them according to Ahmad and according to the apparent position of Mālik’s school. The right of pre-emption also applies to it. Where physical division of the property is possible—that is, if it is divisible—it may be divided.

If it is indivisible, may he be compelled to sell when the other co-owner requests this so that they can divide the proceeds? Scholars hold two views on this. Compelling the sale is the position of Mālik, Abū Hanīfah, and Ahmad. The contrary position is that of

---

## Page 15495

al-Shāfiʿī. This is clear to anyone with even a slight acquaintance with Islamic jurisprudence, provided he understands the jurists’ reasoning. But someone who fails to distinguish between these matters, and imagines that the rulings they mention concerning the donated property apply both to it and to the other share, shows a profound lack of discernment in legal rulings and questions of jurisprudence. This issue admits of no dispute or disagreement whatsoever.

It is astonishing that they seek a transmitted ruling on this issue in the “Book of Gifts and Charity” and similar sections. That is not where it belongs; rather, it belongs in the “Book of Co-ownership and Partition” and similar sections. The question concerns dealings with the remaining share, not with the donated share.

If someone imagines that their tacit acquiescence establishes each party’s possession of the whole, the answer is: in that case, each co-owner would possess the entirety of the jointly owned property. If this argument were sound, a co-owner’s possession could never be valid under any circumstances. Abū Hanīfah stated this only concerning property capable of division.

Moreover, even supposing that a co-owner’s possession extends to the whole, this does not preclude the taking of possession required for an undivided share. When someone gives away a share in a particular property, he need only deliver possession of what he has given, while retaining possession of what he has not given. This holds whether his continued possession of his own share is said to extend to the whole or not. Thus, by the consensus of the Muslims, the donor’s control over his own share and his dealings with it neither prevent possession from being taken initially nor invalidate its continuation.

He was asked:

“Someone gave away a quarter of a property, but it turned out to be less than that. Is the gift invalid?”

He answered:

“It is not invalid.”

---

## Page 15496

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man had two daughters and a pregnant former wife. He assigned two thousand dinars and four properties to his daughters in writing. His former wife subsequently gave birth to a son, but he assigned nothing to him. The father later died, leaving assets in addition to what he had assigned to his daughters. These remaining assets were divided among them according to the prescribed rules of Islamic inheritance. Should what he assigned to the daughters be revoked or not?”

He answered:

“Scholars disagree on this matter if he had fully transferred ownership to the daughters and possession had been taken.

“But if he merely recorded two thousand dinars as a liability owed by him to them, without delivering possession, or gave them something without taking possession of it on their behalf, then this transaction is revoked, and everything is divided among the son and the two daughters.

“If possession was taken, however, there is disagreement. It is reported that Saʿd ibn ʿUbādah divided his wealth among his children. After his death, a child conceived during his lifetime was born to him, and Abū Bakr and ʿUmar ordered that the child be given his share of the inheritance. The same should therefore be done in this case, for the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

‘Fear Allah and treat your children fairly.’

“He also said to someone who wished to single out some of his children for a gift:

‘I will not bear witness to injustice.’

“The daughters must fear Allah and give the son his due.”

---

## Page 15497

The Prophet’s words—peace and blessings be upon him—to the man who singled out some of his children, “Have someone other than me witness this,” were a warning to him, for he also said:

“Take it back.”

That man did indeed take it back. If, however, he made a bequest to the daughters to take effect after his death, it is not binding, by scholarly consensus. The correct scholarly view is that a man who singles out his daughters for gifts while excluding his unborn child must revoke those gifts during his lifetime, as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, commanded. If he dies without revoking them, they must be revoked after his death as well, according to the sounder of the two views, in obedience to Allah and His Messenger, in observance of the justice commanded, and in following the example of Abū Bakr and ʿUmar, may Allah be pleased with them both. The favoured recipient may not lawfully take the excess; rather, that recipient must share all the property with their siblings according to the justice Allah has commanded. Allah, glorified and exalted is He, knows best.

He was asked:

“A man owns a slave woman and permits his son to enjoy intimacy and have intercourse with her, without transferring ownership of her to him, giving her to him as a gift, or doing anything else of that kind. The slave woman then bears a child to her owner’s son. Does permission to enjoy intimacy and have intercourse with her constitute a transfer of ownership to the son? Is the child free, and does the slave woman acquire the status of umm walad (a slave woman who has borne her master a child) in relation to her owner’s son, so that her owner—the young man’s father, who permitted his son to enjoy intimacy and have intercourse with her—is forbidden to sell her?”

The answer:

Praise be to Allah. This question rests on two principles. The first concerns the form of contracts. The position of Mālik, of Ahmad according to the well-known view in his school, and of others is that sales, gifts, and leases do not require a prescribed verbal formula; rather, they are concluded

---

## Page 15498

through conduct: whatever people regard as a sale, gift, or lease is treated accordingly. The well-known position of al-Shāfiʿī requires a verbal formula, except in certain specified cases. Where a verbal formula is used, however, the majority hold that Islamic law prescribes no fixed wording; rather, whether the wording conveys the transaction is determined by customary usage. This is the position of the majority.

Accordingly, they recognize a gift as valid when expressed in such words as: “I grant you this house for your lifetime,” “I give you this food to eat,” or “I provide you with this animal to ride,” and similar expressions that those addressed understand as conveying a gift. Likewise, providing a woman with her bridal furnishings when she goes to her husband’s home constitutes a transfer of ownership to her, as the jurists of the schools of Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad, and others have ruled. This is because Allah mentions sales, leases, and gifts in His Book in general terms, without a fixed definition in either language or Islamic law; their meaning is therefore determined by custom. The purpose of speech is to convey meanings, so any wording that conveys the intended transaction suffices to conclude the contract.

Accordingly, under people’s customary practice, if someone buys a slave woman for his son and says, “Take her for yourself and enjoy intimacy with her,” or something similar, they regard this as a transfer of ownership.

Furthermore, if someone knows that intercourse with a slave woman is permissible only by virtue of ownership, then, in permitting his son to enjoy intimacy with her, he can only intend to transfer ownership of her to him. Thus, if something has occurred that signifies a transfer of ownership according to the majority of scholars—and theirs is the sounder of the two views—the son has had intercourse with a woman he owns. His child is free, with paternity established as his, and the slave woman becomes his umm walad; she may not be sold, given away, or inherited.

If, however, it is assumed that the father did nothing whatsoever to transfer ownership, but the son believed that he had become her owner, his child is likewise free, with paternity established as his, and he is not liable to the prescribed punishment. And if

---

## Page 15499

the son also believed that he did not own her, but had intercourse with her with permission, then this depends on the “second principle.” Scholars differ concerning someone who has intercourse with another person’s slave woman with that person’s permission. Mālik holds that he acquires ownership of her upon payment of her value, whether or not she becomes pregnant. The other three imams hold that he does not thereby acquire ownership of her.

According to Mālik’s view, therefore, she likewise belongs to the son and becomes his umm walad, and his child is free. According to the other three imams, the slave woman does not become his umm walad. But is the child free—as in the case of someone who has intercourse with his wife’s slave woman with her permission? Two positions are reported from Ahmad. The first is that the child is not free; this is also the position of Abū Hanīfah, even if the man believed that she was lawful for him. The second is that the child is free. This is the correct view when the man believed that she was lawful for him. It is the expressly stated position of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad concerning a pledgee: if he has intercourse with a slave woman pledged to him, with the pledgor’s permission, believing that this is permissible, his child is free from conception because of the legal doubt involved. Doubt arising from belief or ownership precludes the prescribed punishment by agreement of the imams. It likewise affects the child’s freedom and paternity, just as when someone has intercourse with her under an invalid marriage or invalid ownership: the child is free by agreement of the imams.

Abū Hanīfah disagrees with the two of them in this case and holds that the child is a slave. Mālik, however, holds that the man has acquired ownership of the slave woman through the intercourse for which permission was given.

Must the man who had intercourse with permission pay the child’s value? Al-Shāfi'ī has two views on this. The first, which is also Ahmad’s expressly stated position, is that he is not obliged to pay the child’s value, because he had intercourse with the owner’s permission. This is therefore like destroying someone’s property with that person’s permission. The second is that he must pay the child’s value, and this is the view of some

---

## Page 15500

of Ahmad’s followers. Some of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers claim that this is his sole position on the matter. As for the dower, he is not obliged to pay it according to the schools of Ahmad, Mālik and others. Al-Shāfi‘ī has two views on this, one being that he must pay it, which is also Abū Hanīfah’s position. In every case where the slave woman does not become an umm walad, she may be sold.

He was asked:

A woman, while in sound health, gave her son a charitable gift consisting of a share of everything capable of division, over a period increasing every ten years. The donor then died. During his own lifetime, the recipient subsequently gave his son everything his mother had given him. After the first donor’s death, all of this was established before a judge, who issued a ruling accordingly. May the remaining heirs have this invalidated on the grounds of his continued residence there after it had been handed over to her son, the recipient, or not?

He replied—may Allah have mercy on him:

If this charitable gift remained in the donor’s possession until the donor died, it became void according to the well-known positions of all the imams. If the judge established that the transaction had taken place, his establishing that fact does not entail its validity. As for ruling that it is valid under these circumstances when the donor has heirs, no knowledgeable judge would do so—unless the case is not as described, in which event he would not be ruling on this matter. But if the donor had transferred the charitable gift

---

## Page 15501

out of his possession to the recipient and handed it over in accordance with Islamic law, then this is a matter well known to the scholars. If the donor had not given his other children the same, he was obliged to take back that gift or give the others the same, as established in an authentic report from al-Nu‘mān ibn Bashīr, who said:

“My father gave me a slave boy. My mother, ‘Amrah bint Rawāhah, said, ‘I will not be satisfied until you have Allah’s Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, witness this.’ I then went to the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and said, ‘I have given my son a slave boy, but his mother said she would not be satisfied until I had Allah’s Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, witness this.’ He asked, ‘Do you have any other children?’ I replied, ‘Yes.’ He asked, ‘Have you given each of them the same as you gave him?’ I replied, ‘No.’ He said, ‘Have someone other than me witness this.’”

In another version:

“Do not ask me to witness this, for I do not bear witness to injustice. Fear Allah and treat your children fairly. Take it back.”

So he took it back. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man owned a house and gave one-half and one-quarter of it as a charitable gift to his own son, while giving the remaining quarter to his full sister. The son to whom he had given the half and the quarter subsequently died. The donor then gave the entire house as a charitable gift to his daughter. Is this final gift valid, rendering the earlier gifts void, or not?

He replied:

If he had transferred ownership of the quarter to his sister and she had taken possession of it, and had transferred ownership of the three-quarters to his son, then the sister’s property passes to her heirs, not to the daughter. The owner has no right to transfer it to his daughter. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15502

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman of sound judgment died, leaving a father, a mother, and a husband. Her father took her household effects and gave the heirs nothing. What is the ruling?

He replied:

This cannot be accepted from him. Rather, whatever property was in her possession belonged to her and passes to her heirs. Even if he bought it himself and provided it for her as her bridal trousseau in the customary manner, this constitutes a transfer of ownership to her. He therefore has no right to take it back after her death.

He was asked:

If someone is given a hunting dog and gives the donor a gift in return, may the donor benefit from that gift?

He replied:

If he gave the trained dog without intending to receive anything in exchange or seeking a return for his gift, but simply to honour the recipient, and the recipient subsequently gave him something, there is nothing wrong with that.

---

## Page 15503

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

If someone gives another person a gift and then takes it back, is that permissible?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. It is reported in the Sunan collections that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A donor may not take back his gift, except a father regarding what he has given his child.”

This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī, Mālik, Ahmad and others. An exception applies when the gift is intended to secure something in return: for example, someone gives a man a gift so that he will reciprocate or attend to a need of his. If the recipient does not fulfil the condition established explicitly or by custom, the donor may recover his gift or its equivalent value. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man gives another man something, either as a new gift or by forgiving a debt owed to him. Then hostility arises between them, and he takes back his gift. Is he entitled to do so? If the donor denies having made the gift, and the recipient swears that he owes the donor nothing, does he thereby swear falsely?

---

## Page 15504

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. A donor may not take back his gift, except a father, unless the gift was intended to secure something in return, whether expressly stipulated or understood by custom. If it was given in return for something that was not received, the donor may take it back. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A gift was given to a man’s son, after which the man disposed of it and claimed that it belonged to him. Does this constitute taking back the gift?

He replied:

Yes, this constitutes taking it back. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man gave someone a horse. Some time later, the donor demanded payment for its use. The recipient told him, “I cannot afford to pay anything. If that is not acceptable, take back your horse.” The donor said, “I will not take it back unless you pay me for its use.” Is this permissible, and is he entitled to payment for its use?

He replied:

If the recipient returns the gifted property itself, the donor is entitled to nothing beyond that. He may neither demand payment for its use nor claim compensation from the recipient, for the recipient bore liability for it and fed it in return for the benefit he derived from it.

---

## Page 15505

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man presented a slave to an emir in expectation of recompense, as is customary among people, without concluding a sale. The slave remained with the emir, serving him for a year, and then the emir died. Does the man who gave the slave have any claim against the emir’s heirs, whether for the slave’s price, payment for his service, or on any other grounds?

He replied:

Yes. If he gave him as a gift on condition of receiving recompense, whether expressly stipulated or understood by custom, he may take back the gift so long as he has not received the recompense to which he is entitled, provided the gifted property still exists. If it has perished, he is entitled to its value or the recompense. Here, “recompense” means the consideration stipulated in return for the gift.

He was asked:

A man gives an emir a gift to have a request fulfilled, to gain his favour, to obtain employment in his service, or for some similar purpose. Is it permissible to accept the gift under these circumstances?

If accepting the gift makes him inclined to attend to the matter, whereas without accepting it he feels no such inclination, is it permissible for him to accept it and attend to the giver’s business, or should he neither accept it nor attend to it?

---

## Page 15506

And if a man whose word carries weight with his master is given food or a gift, not in return for having a request fulfilled, may he accept it? If returning it would hurt the giver’s feelings, is it lawful for him to accept it or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. It is reported in Sunan Abī Dāwūd and elsewhere that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Whoever intercedes for his brother, then accepts a gift that his brother gives him, has entered a great gateway to usury.”

Ibn Masʿūd was asked about unlawful gain. He replied, “It is when you intercede for your brother, and he gives you a gift which you accept.”

The questioner then asked him, “What if the gift is for something wrongful?” He replied, “That is disbelief:

‘And whoever does not judge by what Allah has revealed—then it is those who are the disbelievers.’”

For this reason, the scholars have said that if someone gives a person in authority a gift so that he will do something unlawful for him, it is forbidden for both the giver and the recipient. This is a form of bribery, concerning which the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“May Allah curse the bribe-giver and the bribe-taker.”

A bribe is also called “al-birtīl.” In its lexical sense, “al-birtīl” means a stone with an elongated mouth.

If, however, someone gives him a gift to make him cease his oppression of him, or to obtain a right that he is obliged to grant him, the gift is forbidden for the recipient, but the giver may give it to him. Thus, the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, used to say:

“I give one of them a gift, and he goes away with it, tucking fire under his arm.” He was asked, “O Messenger of Allah, why do you give to them?” He replied, “They insist on asking me, and Allah does not allow me to be miserly.”

The same applies to giving something to someone who has freed a slave but concealed the manumission, or has kept some information secret, or oppresses people. Giving to such people is permissible for the giver, but accepting it is forbidden for them.

As for a gift given in return for intercession:

---

## Page 15507

such as when someone intercedes with a ruler on a man’s behalf to have an injustice against him removed, secure his rights, obtain an office he deserves, enlist him among the fighting troops—when he is entitled to this—or obtain for him a share of funds endowed for the poor, jurists, Quran reciters, devout worshippers or others—when he is an eligible beneficiary. In these and similar cases of intercession that help fulfil an obligation or put an end to something forbidden, accepting a gift is likewise impermissible. The giver, however, may offer what will enable him to secure his rights or ward off injustice. This is what has been transmitted from the early generations and the great imams.

Some later jurists permitted this, classifying it under “reward-based contracts.” This contradicts the Sunnah and the statements of the Companions and the imams, and is therefore mistaken. Such work serves the public interest, and undertaking it is either an individual or a collective obligation. If accepting a reward for such work were sanctioned, it would follow that appointments to office and distributions of revenues acquired without fighting, charitable funds and other resources would go to those who paid for them. Likewise, protection from injustice would be afforded to those who paid for it, while those who did not pay would receive neither appointments nor funds nor protection from injustice—even if they were more deserving and more beneficial to the Muslims than those who paid.

The benefit here is not a private benefit to the person making the payment that would justify taking a reward from him, as for recovering a runaway slave or a stray animal. Rather, the benefit is for the public at large—by which I mean the Muslims. Every position must be entrusted to the most suitable person available who is capable of fulfilling its duties. The stipends allotted to fighting men, prayer leaders, callers to prayer and scholars must be given to those most deserving and most beneficial to the Muslims. This is obligatory upon the ruler, and the Muslim community must assist him in it. Thus, taking a reward from a particular individual for this leads to these

---

## Page 15508

matters being sought in return for payment. Seeking positions of authority is itself forbidden, so how much more so when payment is involved! It would follow that whoever was in a position to pay would be appointed and given funds, even if someone else were more deserving and better suited. Indeed, this would entail appointing the ignorant, the sinful and the wicked, while passing over the knowledgeable, just and capable; and granting military stipends to the sinful and to cowards incapable of fighting, while passing over upright, courageous men who benefit the Muslims. Such practices cause much corruption. If someone takes payment and intercedes for an undeserving person when another is more deserving, he may neither take the payment nor intercede; refraining from both is better. If, however, he takes payment and intercedes for the person most deserving and best suited, while passing over the undeserving, then withholding intercession while taking payment is more harmful than interceding for someone undeserving.

The intercessor whose request secures acceptance for his intercession should be told:

You must act with sincerity towards Allah, His Messenger, the leaders of the Muslims and the Muslims at large. This would be your duty even without such influence and wealth; how much more so when you possess them! You must give sincere counsel to the person with whom you intercede, explaining who deserves appointment, employment and financial support, and who does not. By doing so, you act sincerely towards the Muslims, and through obedience you act sincerely towards Allah and His Messenger, for this is among the greatest acts of obedience. You must benefit the deserving person by helping him obtain these things, just as you must pray, fast and strive in Allah’s cause.

As for a man whose word carries weight, if he partakes of food beyond the hospitality sanctioned by Islamic law, he must repay his host with something equivalent, or else refrain from partaking of the excess. Otherwise, his acceptance of this additional hospitality is like accepting a gift. He is then like a witness who gives testimony, or an intercessor who intercedes, in return for hospitality or payment. This is among the causes of corruption. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15509

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man presented a slave to a dignitary. The established custom was that anyone making such a presentation would receive the slave’s price or its equivalent, but he received nothing. The slave married, had children, and died. Are his children free or not? Are the original owner’s children entitled to inherit from the person concerned under the guarantee?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah.

If the established custom was to give compensation, and he gave the slave on that condition, he is entitled to one of two things: either compensation or the recovery of his gift. As for the slave, if the recipient has not emancipated him, he remains his property. His children follow their mother’s status: if she is free, they are free; if she is enslaved, they belong to her owner, not their father’s owner. According to the four schools of law and others, children follow their mother in freedom or slavery, and their father in lineage and the bond of patronage arising from emancipation.

If the donor did not reclaim the gift before its recovery became impossible, he may demand compensation from the recipient if he is alive, or claim it against his estate if he is dead, like any other debt. If the slave had been emancipated and had children by a free woman, they are free.

---

## Page 15510

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man bought a slave and gave him property until the slave became wealthy. It then emerged that the slave had actually been free. May he recover what he gave him in the belief that he was a slave?

He replied:

Yes, he may recover it.

He was also asked:

A man divorced his wife, asked her to reach a settlement, and settled with her. He recorded two dinars as due to her, then said to her, “Give me one of the dinars as a gift.” She did so, but he then divorced her. May she revoke the gift in these circumstances?

He replied:

Yes, she may revoke what she gave him in these circumstances, for he asked her for the gift and nevertheless divorced her. She had not wanted him to take her money at his request and then divorce her. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15511

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man gave his wife a gift of one thousand dirhams and had a deed drawn up acknowledging his liability for that sum, but he did not deliver any of it to her. She died, and her heirs demanded the amount from him. May he revoke the gift?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If he previously owed her nothing—neither this amount nor anything for which this amount could serve as compensation, such as having taken some of her trousseau and agreed with her to pay this amount in settlement of its value, or something similar—then her heirs are not, in reality, entitled to any of this debt. If there is an acknowledgment of debt, he may require them to swear that they do not know that the reality behind this acknowledgment differs from its outward purport. If evidence establishes against both the person making the acknowledgment and its beneficiary that the acknowledgment was a sham, then it has no basis in reality.

If the property of hers to which his acknowledgment related was worth less than this amount, but he agreed to a settlement with her for more than its value, scholars differ over whether this excess is binding. Some scholars among the followers of al-Shāfi'ī and Ahmad hold it invalid, whereas Abū Hanīfah holds it valid. The latter also follows by analogy from the position of Ahmad and others, and it is the correct view. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15512

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had debts that exhausted all his property and still exceeded its value. During his final illness, he gave some of that property to a freed slave. Are the creditors entitled to recover it?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Yes. If his debts exhaust all his property, he may not, during his final illness, make any gratuitous disposition in anyone’s favour—whether a gift, a preferential financial concession, or a release from debt—without the creditors’ consent. Indeed, the heirs have no entitlement until the debts have been paid. This is agreed upon by the Muslims, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, ruled that debts take precedence over bequests. Gratuitous dispositions made during one’s final illness are treated as bequests, by agreement of the four imams.

He was also asked:

A man died, leaving two sons, a daughter, and a wife, and the estate was divided among them. The children also had a sister in the East. When she arrived to claim her inheritance, she found that both sons and the wife had died, and that what remained was in her sister’s possession. She brought a claim against her sister and demanded payment, but then feared that this would sever their relationship. She therefore had witnesses attest that she had released her sister from liability

---

## Page 15513

and once she had granted this release, her husband swore on pain of divorce that her sister would not visit her and that she would not visit her sister. The woman had given her sister the money only to foster family ties and affection between them, but her purpose had not been achieved. May she therefore revoke the gift? Does the release prevent her from bringing a claim for it and seeking payment?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

If, when making the gift, she said, “I am giving this to my sister so that she will help me with my affairs and we will support one another while living away from home”; or if her sister said to her, “Give me this inheritance,” and she replied, “I am giving it to you only so that you will attend to my needs while I am away from home,” and then gave it to her; or if they reached a similar agreement, whereby she made the gift in return for some benefit she would receive from her sister—then, if her purpose is not achieved, she may rescind the gift and reclaim it.

There are two opinions within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere concerning the return to be provided for a gift of this kind. One view is that the benefit provided should equal the value of the gift. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A woman’s husband owed her her dower. When she was nearing death, she summoned an upright witness and a group of women and had them witness her declaration that she had released him from the dower debt. Is this release valid or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If the dower remained a debt he owed her until she entered her final illness, this release is not valid without the consent of the other heirs. But if she had released him while in good health

---

## Page 15514

then the release is valid and may be established by one witness and an oath according to Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Ahmad. It may also be established by the testimony of two women and an oath according to Mālik and one opinion within Ahmad’s school. If, during her illness, she acknowledges that she released him while in good health, this acknowledgment is not accepted according to Abū Hanīfah, Ahmad and others, but it is accepted according to al-Shāfiʿī. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Allah has given everyone entitled to a share his share, so no bequest may be made to an heir.”

A person in his final illness may not single out an heir for more than Allah has allotted him.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who favored some of his children over others.

He replied:

During his final illness, he may not single out any of them for more than their allotted inheritance, by the consensus of the Muslims. If he does so, the other heirs may reject this disposition and recover their rightful shares. Indeed, even if he does so while in good health, it is impermissible according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions. Rather, he must revoke the gift, as the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, commanded. It must be returned whether the giver is alive or dead; after his death, the favored recipient must return it.

---

## Page 15515

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man gave something to one of his children but gave nothing to the other because the first was obedient to him. May he favor the one who obeys him and deprive the one who disobeys him? The son who received nothing swore an oath invoking divorce that he would not speak to his father unless his father treated him equally. Is there a way out for him? Does an oath invoking divorce have the same status as other oaths, or not?

He replied:

A man must treat his children justly, as Allah and His Messenger have commanded. It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, addressed Bashīr ibn Saʿd when he gave his son al-Nuʿmān a gift and came to the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, to ask him to witness it. He said to him:

“Fear Allah and treat your children justly.”

He also said:

“Do not ask me to witness this, for I do not bear witness to injustice.”

And he told him, “Take it back.” So Bashīr took it back. He also told him, by way of warning:

“Do not call anyone other than me to witness this.”

However, he may single out one of them on grounds recognized by Islamic law: for example, if one is needy and obedient to Allah, while the other is wealthy and disobedient, using his wealth to facilitate sin. If he gives to the one Allah has commanded him to give to, and withholds from the one Allah has commanded him to withhold from, he has done well.

---

## Page 15516

As for the man who swore that he would not speak to his father, whatever oath he swears of the kinds sworn by Muslims, he must offer the expiation for an oath if he breaks it.

Whatever oath he has sworn, if he considers breaking it better than keeping it, he should offer expiation for his oath and break it, as the Quran and Sunnah indicate. This applies whether he swears by Allah’s name or invokes a vow, divorce, emancipation, zihār (declaring his wife forbidden to him like his mother), or a declaration that something is forbidden to him. For example, he might say: “If I do such-and-such, my wealth shall be given in charity, I shall be bound to perform ten pilgrimages and fast for a year, my wives shall be divorced, and my slaves shall be free,” or something similar. Expiation suffices for any oath of the kinds sworn by Muslims.

As for oaths that are not of these kinds—such as swearing by the Kaaba, shaykhs, kings, or forefathers—they are unlawful, nonbinding oaths and have no sanctity.

In the law prescribed by Allah and His Messenger, there are only two kinds of oath: a binding oath, which requires expiation, and a nonbinding oath, which entails nothing if it is broken.

Any scholar who affirms the existence of a binding oath that cannot be expiated holds a weak position, contrary to the Quran, the Sunnah, the statements of the Companions, and clear analogical reasoning. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

A man had children, both sons and daughters, and before his death he gave gifts to the daughters but not to the sons. Does any liability remain upon him, or not?

---

## Page 15517

He answered:

It is not lawful for him to give gifts to some of his children to the exclusion of others. Rather, he must treat them fairly, as the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) commanded when he said:

“Fear Allah and treat your children fairly.”

A man had given a gift to one of his children and asked the Prophet to witness it. He said, “I will not bear witness to injustice,” and instructed him to take it back.

If this was merely a verbal declaration, and he did not hand over to the daughters what he had given them before he died or entered his terminal illness, then this gift is to be set aside by agreement of the imams, notwithstanding an isolated dissenting opinion. If, however, he had delivered it into their possession while in good health, the scholars hold two views on whether it must be returned. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man left children, both sons and daughters. The daughters married before their father’s death and received substantial wedding trousseaus. Then, when the man died, the sons inherited only a small amount. Must the daughters share with the sons both the inheritance and what they already hold, or not?

He answered:

A man must treat his children equitably when giving gifts; he may not favor some over others. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) commanded this when he prohibited unjust favoritism and ordered that it be reversed. If a man does this and dies before restoring fairness, those who received preferential treatment must establish fairness between themselves and their siblings. They must therefore divide all the property—both the earlier gifts and the remaining estate—according to the Book of Allah Most High:

“The male shall have the share of two females.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15518

He was asked—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

A man gave slaves to his children, then wished to free them. Is it better for him to take them back and free them, or to leave them in his children’s possession?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If his children need the slaves, leaving them with his children is better than taking them back and freeing them. Indeed, helping a needy relative is better than freeing a slave, as recorded in the authentic hadith collection:

“Maymūnah, the wife of the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace), freed a slave woman of hers and mentioned this to the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace). He said, ‘Had you given her to your maternal uncles, that would have been better for you.’”

If the Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) considered giving a slave to a maternal uncle better than freeing that slave, how much more so when the recipients are one’s own needy children?

If, however, the children have no need of some of the slaves, freeing such a slave is a good deed. He may revoke this gift according to al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad, and others, but he may not revoke it according to Abū Hanīfah. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15519

He was also asked—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

A man’s wife died, leaving children and assets in his care, and he cannot afford to marry. May he use the children’s assets to purchase a slave woman who will serve them and with whom he may have intercourse? May he also marry using their wealth?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If this does not harm his children, he may take ownership of enough of their wealth to purchase a slave woman with whom he may have intercourse and who will serve them. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A woman’s husband gave her what was due to her during his lifetime. She has children by him, and he also gave her a sum beyond her dower for her own benefit and that of her children. If someone brings a claim against her and seeks to have her swear an oath, may she swear to protect herself from injustice?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If he gave his children by her a gift and took possession of it, and this involved no injustice to anyone, then it was a valid gift, and no one has the right to take it away

---

## Page 15520

from her. If he entrusted her with the children’s share, both during his lifetime and after his death, and she is qualified to administer it, no one has the right to take it from her.

If she swears an oath, she is to swear that she has something belonging to the deceased. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man gave his son a charitable gift and recorded it in his wife’s marriage contract. The father has since fallen on hard times, and his son has shunned him. May he revoke his gift or not?

He answered:

If it had been given to the woman as part of the wife’s dower, a person may not reclaim it, by scholarly consensus.

He was asked:

A man gave his older children something, then gave his younger children an equivalent amount. After they had taken possession of what he had given them, he said, “Buy property with the income and establish it as an endowment for everyone.” Does this constitute revocation of the gift or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. What has been mentioned does not terminate the ownership of the two children to whom the property was given, for it does not constitute revocation of the gift. Even if it did constitute revocation, he would not be permitted to revoke a gift of this kind. For if he has given the other two children enough to establish equality between them and

---

## Page 15521

the rest, he has no right to depart from the justice that Allah and His Messenger have commanded. How could he, when the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“Fear Allah and treat your children justly.”

He also said:

“I will not bear witness to injustice.”

Regarding a gift that favored one child over another, he said, “Take it back.” And, as a warning to the one who showed favoritism, he said:

“Have someone other than me witness this.”

And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man transferred ownership of some property to his daughter. She then died, leaving her father and her child. May the man revoke the transfer of what he had assigned to his daughter in writing or not?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Whatever the daughter fully owned and had taken possession of passed to her heirs upon her death. Her father receives one-sixth, and the remainder goes to her son, provided she has no heir “other than the two of them.” By scholarly agreement, he has no right, after his daughter’s death, to revoke his transfer of ownership to her.

He was asked:

A man gave his daughter some jewelry to which no one else had any claim, and swore an oath, invoking divorce, that he would not take any of it from her. He then needed to take some of it from her. May he revoke his gift or not? If she willingly gives him some of it, does he break his oath or not?

---

## Page 15522

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. He may revoke what he gave her. However, if he does what he swore not to do, he breaks his oath. If his intention was not to take anything without her willing consent or permission, then he does not break his oath if she willingly agrees or gives permission.

He was asked:

A man gave his wealth to his children, and one of them gave his share to his own child. The original father then revoked his gifts to his children, and they returned them, except for the son who had given his share to his child: he refused. Must he take it back from his child and hand it over to his father?

He answered:

Praise be to Allah. If he has given something to his child and no third party’s rights have become attached to it—for example, through the child having incurred a debt or having been married on the strength of that gift—he may revoke it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

A man’s mother died, leaving him, his father, and his daughter. His daughter then died. His father wished to arrange a marriage for him, but said, “I will not arrange your marriage until you transfer to me ownership of what you inherited from your mother.” He transferred ownership of it to his father, who then gave him one-quarter as a gift in the presence of witnesses. After that, illness overtook

---

## Page 15523

his father and deprived him of his reason. He then revoked what he had given his son and made it an endowment for his wife, his son, and his daughter, but did not name the son in question. He copied out the endowment deed twice. May he favor his children and exclude this son from everything he inherited from his mother?

He answered:

If the father gave his son something as compensation for what he had taken from him, he may not revoke it; there is no disagreement among the scholars on this. If, however, he gave it to him as charity for Allah’s sake, the scholars hold two views concerning its revocation. The first is that he may not revoke it. The second is that he may revoke it; this is the view of Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and Ahmad.

Whenever he revokes a gift or establishes an endowment while deprived of his reason, or enters into a contract in that state, neither his revocation nor his endowment is valid if illness has deprived him of his reason. There is no disagreement among the scholars on this.

He was asked:

A sum of money was stolen from a man, and he suspected that one of his sons had taken it. He then began supplicating against him and shunned him, although the son was innocent and had taken nothing. Will the son be rewarded on account of his father’s supplications against him?

He answered:

Yes. If the son has been wronged, Allah expiates his sins through the wrong he suffers and rewards him for his patience. Whoever wrongfully supplicates against another incurs sin.

---

## Page 15524

He was also asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man died leaving some wealth, which his children divided among themselves, giving their mother her settlement and her one-eighth share. Shortly afterward, the children found that their mother had wealth amounting to one-third of the estate. They asked, “Where did you get this money?”

She replied, “When your father was ill, I asked him for something, and he gave me one-third of his wealth.”

They took the money from their mother, saying, “Our father gave you nothing.” Must the money be returned to her?

He answered:

Whatever a person gives to an heir during his final illness does not take effect without the other heirs’ approval. Thus, whatever the sick man gave his wife is treated like the rest of his estate, unless the remaining heirs approve the gift. The children ought to let their mother keep it and approve the gift to her, but they cannot be compelled to do so. Rather, the entire estate is to be divided. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“No bequest is to be made to an heir.”

---

## Page 15525

Book of Bequests

He was asked—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

Concerning someone who said during his final illness, “This money is to be paid to the orphaned children of so-and-so,” when it was unclear whether this was an acknowledgment of their entitlement or a bequest.

Answer

He replied:

If there is contextual evidence clarifying whether he intended an acknowledgment or a bequest, it should be acted upon. If his intention remains unknown, property deemed to belong to him cannot be removed from his ownership on the strength of ambiguous wording. Rather, the statement is to be treated as a bequest.

He was also asked:

A man holding money for safekeeping, whose depositor fell ill, asked him, “Does your son not know about this deposit?”

The depositor replied, “So-and-so, the captive, will return with no means of supporting himself,” intending thereby that the money should be set aside for him, but saying nothing further. If the amount falls within one-third of his estate, may the custodian spend it on that captive’s needs?

---

## Page 15526

He replied:

A bequest is validly made by any wording that indicates it, as when the testator’s intended meaning is understood from his words. Recognition of the bequest’s [legal effect] (1) regarding disposal of the property remains contingent on the beneficiary’s acceptance, whether expressed verbally or indicated by customary practice, and on his permission to dispose of it or authorization under Islamic law. A captive’s money may be spent on securing his release without his permission. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who wrote his will and stated in it that he owed his wife one hundred dirhams, although she was unaware that he owed her anything: may his executor, after his death, pay the dirhams to his wife without requiring her to swear an oath, if he acknowledged her entitlement when she had no actual claim?

He replied:

It is not lawful for her to take any of it, for this would constitute a bequest to an heir. By the consensus of the Muslims, a bequest to an heir is impermissible unless the remaining heirs approve it.

As for the judicial ruling, she is to receive nothing until she affirms the truth of his acknowledgment made during his final illness; otherwise, it is invalid according to most scholars. If she affirms his acknowledgment in full, but the executor or one of the heirs alleges that it was made without any actual entitlement—as some people customarily cast the creation of a new entitlement in the form of an acknowledgment of an existing one—this is comparable to alleging that an acknowledgment was made before possession was taken. The scholars have differed over requiring an oath in such a case. The correct view is that he must swear an oath. The correct view is that she is to receive nothing until she swears an oath.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I have not found it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Protecting the Collected Fatwas from Omissions and Textual Corruption).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for the Shāmila Encyclopedia.

---

## Page 15527

He was asked:

Concerning a woman who freed a slave girl who had not yet reached puberty and assigned her property to her in writing, but the property remained in her possession until her death—that is, the death of the mistress who had freed her—and she left heirs: is her transfer of ownership to the girl valid, or may the heirs reclaim the property, or part of it?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Merely granting ownership, without possession being taken in the manner prescribed by Islamic law, does not make a gift binding. Rather, the heir may reclaim it. The same applies if it was a sham gift: an outward gift accompanied by taking possession, but with an agreement between the donor and recipient that the donor may reclaim it whenever she wishes, or a similar device used to deprive an heir or creditor of their rights. If that is the case, the gift is likewise invalid. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning someone who had witnesses attest that his father held three hundred for a Hajj on behalf of a certain woman. Her heirs said, “He may take only one-third of it for the journey.” The man whose holding of the money was attested said, “My mother donated it.” What is the ruling?

He replied:

This attestation alone does not establish that the money forms part of an estate of which the heirs are entitled to two-thirds. The money may not have belonged to the woman, and the Hajj may have been the obligatory Hajj, whose expenses are paid from the estate itself. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15528

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a man who gave property to his own daughter and appointed another man as his executor. The executor leased the property for thirty years and subsequently died. The girl under his guardianship attained legal capacity, but did not consent to the lease he had granted. Moreover, he had leased the property at a rate other than its fair rental value. Is the lease to be rescinded, allowing her to dispose of her property as owners ordinarily do?

He replied:

She is entitled to rescind this lease, without any disagreement among the scholars. The only disagreement is whether it is void from the outset or whether the lessor bears liability for it. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who bequeathed unequal shares to his children and had witnesses attest to this as he was dying: is this bequest to be implemented or not?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. A sick person may not single out some of his children for an immediately effective gift or a bequest taking effect after death, nor may he acknowledge owing any of them anything. If he does so, it may not be implemented without the consent of the remaining heirs. All of this is agreed upon by

---

## Page 15529

the Muslims. None of the witnesses may give testimony that assists in this injustice. Such favoritism is among the major sins that incur punishment in the Fire. Indeed, the compilers of the Sunan have transmitted reports containing a severe warning against anyone who does this, for he sows resentment and disunity among his descendants, particularly in their dealings with him, causing them to be undutiful towards him and fail to treat him kindly.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who owned crops and date palms and said to his family as he was dying: “Spend from my two-thirds share on the poor and needy until my child has offspring, at which point it shall be theirs.” Is this bequest valid or not?

He replied:

Yes, this bequest is valid. A bequest to grandchildren who are not heirs is permissible, just as al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām made a bequest to the children of ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr. A bequest may validly be made to a beneficiary not yet in existence and may comprise property not yet in existence. Thus, the revenue is to go to the poor until the grandchildren are born, at which point it shall be theirs. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who bequeathed a particular property exclusively to his sons, excluding his daughters, and had this formally registered before the judge before his death: is this permissible?

He replied:

It is not permissible for him to favor some of his children over others in his bequest or during his final illness, by agreement of the scholars. Nor, according to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions, may he favor some of them with gifts

---

## Page 15530

while in good health either. Rather, he must treat them equitably and take back the excess, just as the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, instructed Bashīr ibn Saʿīd, saying: “Take it back,” so he took it back. He also said: “I will not witness injustice,” and told him, by way of warning: “Have someone else witness this.” The child who has been favored may not take the excess; rather, he must return it during the lifetime of the unjust, oppressive giver. According to the sounder of the two scholarly opinions, he must also return it after the giver’s death, just as he must during his lifetime.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a woman who bequeathed a sum from one-third of her estate to a little girl under her father’s guardianship. The woman died, and the girl’s father accepted the bequest on her behalf after the woman’s death. He then brought a claim before the judge on the girl’s behalf for what the woman had bequeathed. Evidence established the woman’s death, her having made the stated bequest, and the father’s acceptance of it on his daughter’s behalf. However, the judge withheld judgment awarding the girl what had been established before him by evidence, because she was too young to swear an oath. Should her father swear the oath? Or should the judge postpone judgment until she reaches puberty and then require her to swear? Or is neither course required?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Her father does not swear the oath, because he is not the person entitled to the bequest. Nor should the judge postpone judgment until she reaches puberty and swears an oath. Rather, he should award it to her, without disagreement among the scholars, unless contrary evidence is established.

Indeed, this extends further: suppose that a minor or a person of unsound mind has an established claim against an absent party, whether for a debt arising from a sale, repayment of a loan, compensation for an injury, or some other claim for which an adult claimant of sound mind would swear that he had neither waived the claim nor received payment, according to one of the two opinions of

---

## Page 15531

the scholars; judgment is nevertheless awarded to the minor or the person of unsound mind, and his guardian does not swear an oath, as the scholars have expressly stated. Thus, if someone brings a claim against a minor or a person of unsound mind concerning an injury or an entitlement, judgment is not awarded to the claimant, nor does the minor or person of unsound mind swear an oath, even though an adult of sound mind would make his statement only under oath. There are analogous cases.

This concerns matters in which an oath is prescribed, either by scholarly agreement or according to one of the two scholarly opinions. How much more so, then, in the case of a bequest, for which the scholars have not mentioned requiring the beneficiary to swear an oath? Only some people have adopted this requirement.

The scholars agree that a bequest may be made to an unborn child, who becomes entitled to it upon being born alive. No Muslim has said that it must be withheld until the child reaches puberty. Nor does the child swear an oath. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a woman who made several bequests during her illness, including something for her husband and her brother. A long time later, she gave birth to a son, and subsequently died. Does this invalidate the will?

He replied:

Whatever exceeds one-third of the estate belongs to the heir, and no part of the orphaned child’s property may be given away gratuitously. As for the husband, who is an heir, the bequest to him is invalid because he is an heir. The bequest to the brother, however, is valid, because the presence of the son excludes him from inheritance, even though he was an heir when the will was made.

The bequests she made to her brother and the other beneficiaries must therefore be assessed. If one-third of the estate covers them, they are to be fulfilled; otherwise, that third is divided among them in proportion to their respective bequests.

---

## Page 15532

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Concerning a woman who died leaving no heir except the son of her maternal half-sister, having bequeathed more than one-third of her estate to charity: may the executor carry out this bequest and give the remainder to her sister’s son?

He replied:

The beneficiary is to be given one-third. Any excess is valid if the heir approves it; otherwise, it is invalid. According to those who recognize the inheritance rights of distant kindred, the sister’s son inherits the entire estate and is the heir in this case. This is the position of the majority of the early generations, Abū Hanīfa, Ahmad, and groups of scholars within the school of al-Shāfi‘ī. It is also a position within Mālik’s school when the public treasury is corruptly administered. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who died leaving six sons, a son’s son, and a son’s daughter. He bequeathed to his son’s son a share equal to that of one of his sons, and to his son’s daughter one-third of what remained of the estate’s third after his son’s son had been given his share. What would each of his sons receive?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The apparent position of the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and Abū Hanīfa is that this estate is divided into sixty shares, with eight for each son. The beneficiary of the bequest receives one-third of what remains after the third: twelve, one-third of which is four. There are methods by which this calculation is worked out. The answer to this question is well known in scholarly works.

---

## Page 15533

He was asked—may Allah Most High have mercy on him:

Concerning a man who died leaving considerable wealth and a young child. During his illness, he stipulated in his will that a particular horse of his be sold and its entire price given to someone who would perform the obligatory Hajj on his behalf. The horse was sold for nine hundred dirhams.

The judge intended to hire someone unrelated to the deceased to perform Hajj for this amount. Another man then came and said, “I will perform Hajj for four hundred.” Is this permissible, or must the bequest be carried out as stipulated?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. Rather, the entire amount he bequeathed must be paid, provided it falls within one-third of his estate. If it exceeds one-third, the heirs are not obliged to pay the excess, unless Hajj was obligatory upon him and the obligatory Hajj cannot be performed [without that amount] (1). And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who left children and bequeathed one dirham a day to his sister. She was paid this until the funds were exhausted, leaving nothing of the estate except a property yielding six hundred dirhams annually. Should she be given that amount, or one dirham as he bequeathed to her?

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I could not locate it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-al-Tashīf (Protecting Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā from Omissions and Scribal Errors).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila (the Comprehensive Encyclopedia).

---

## Page 15534

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. If what remains is insufficient to give her one dirham each day while leaving two dirhams for the heirs, she may receive only an amount that leaves two-thirds for the heirs. Nothing beyond one-third may be given without the consent of the entitled heirs, provided that whoever gives consent is an adult of sound judgment and legally competent to make a gratuitous disposition. If the person giving consent does not meet these conditions, or does not give consent, she is to be given nothing.

Even if the deceased left only the property, she is to receive from its income whichever is less: the dirham bequeathed to her or one-third of the income. If the income is less than three dirhams a day, she receives only one-third of it. Thus, if it is one dirham, she receives only one-third of a dirham. If she has taken more than one-third of the income, the excess is to be recovered from her. There is no disagreement among the scholars concerning this. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a woman who died leaving her father, her paternal uncle—her father’s full brother—and her grandmother. Her father had declared her to be of sound judgment before marrying her off. Then, during her final illness, she bequeathed half to her husband and the other half to her paternal uncle, leaving nothing by bequest to her father or grandmother. Is this bequest valid?

He replied:

The bequest to the paternal uncle is valid, but any amount exceeding one-third takes effect only with consent. No part of the bequest to the husband takes effect without the heirs’ consent. If the heirs do not consent to what exceeds one-third, the husband receives half of what remains after this bequest of one-third, the grandmother receives one-sixth, and the father receives the remainder, which is one-third.

---

## Page 15535

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

Concerning a woman who, five days before her death, made bequests for several purposes, including Hajj, Quran recitation, and charity: must her will be carried out?

He replied:

If she directed that money be taken from one-third of her estate and spent on an act of devotion and obedience to Allah, her will must be carried out, even if she made it during her final illness. If, however, the amount bequeathed exceeds one-third, the excess is subject to the heirs’ approval: if they approve it, it is valid; otherwise, it is void. If she made a bequest for a purpose other than obedience to Allah, that bequest is not to be carried out.

He was asked:

Concerning a man who, at the time of his death, instructed his wife not to give any worldly possessions to someone who would recite the Quran and dedicate its reward to him. He claimed that he knew enough of the Quran by heart to suffice him, although his wife had not known that he had memorized the Quran. Was he right to give these instructions?

The wife, to whom he entrusted these instructions, intends to give something as a gift to a deserving person to assist him, and for him to recite a portion of the Quran and dedicate its reward to her deceased husband. May she be permitted to do this?

---

## Page 15536

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. His instructions must be carried out, for paying someone to recite the Quran and dedicate its reward to the deceased is an innovation not reported from any of the early generations. The scholars discussed only the case of someone who recites for Allah’s sake and dedicates the reward to the deceased. There are differing scholarly views concerning payment for teaching the Quran.

As for hiring someone to recite and dedicate the reward, this has not been reported from any of the imams, nor did they permit it. When recitation is performed for payment, it becomes an exchange for compensation: there is no spiritual reward in it, and nothing reaches the deceased. Only righteous deeds reach him. None of the imams endorsed hiring someone merely to recite; their discussion concerned hiring someone to teach.

If this woman wishes to benefit her husband, however, she should give in charity on his behalf whatever she intended to spend on hiring someone. By agreement of the imams, charity reaches the deceased, and Allah benefits him through it. If she gives this money in charity to poor Quran reciters so that they need not depend on their recitation for their livelihood, a reward is earned in proportion to the assistance they receive in reciting, and Allah benefits the deceased through that. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

Concerning a man’s mosque supported by an endowment, whose property was subject to ground rent: before his death, he directed that money be taken from one-third of his estate to purchase the land for which the endowment paid ground rent. Its purchase proved impossible because that land was itself an endowment. He had heirs of limited means, and the executor agreed with them to spend part of the third on repairing the mosque. If some of the third is left to the orphans, is the executor personally liable for it?

He replied:

Rather, the executor must disburse the entire third as the deceased instructed him, leaving none of it to the heirs. Then, if it is possible to purchase the land specified by the testator, he must purchase it and establish it as an endowment

---

## Page 15537

and if that is not possible, he must purchase another property and endow it for the purpose stipulated by the testator. This accords with what the scholars have stated concerning someone who says, “Sell my slave to Zayd and give the proceeds in charity.” If that person refuses to purchase him, the slave is sold to someone else and the proceeds are given in charity.

A bequest directing the purchase of a particular property and its charitable dedication as an endowment is like a bequest directing the sale of a particular property and the charitable distribution of its proceeds. Here, the beneficiary of the bequest is the charitable or endowment purpose, which remains in place. If the particular property specified is unavailable, its substitute takes its place. Similarly, if someone destroys endowed property or property bequeathed in a will, the compensation for each takes its place in this respect.

A distinction must therefore be drawn between the property bequeathed or endowed and substituting someone else for the beneficiary of the bequest or endowment. If someone made a bequest to Zayd, no one else would be entitled to it. Likewise, if he directed that a particular slave of his be emancipated, or vowed to emancipate a particular slave, and that slave died, another slave would not take his place.

The jurists disagreed concerning someone who directed that a particular person perform Hajj on his behalf for a specified sum, but that person declined, and the Hajj was voluntary: should Hajj still be performed on his behalf or not? There are two views, constituting two juristic positions within the school of Ahmad and other schools. This is because Hajj is an objective in its own right, while the particular individual designated is also an intended consideration. Some jurists therefore gave precedence to the designation of that individual.

Others said: Rather, Hajj is also an intended objective, just as charity and an endowment are intended objectives. Specifying the pilgrimage is like specifying the property to be endowed or given in charity. If what was specified cannot be secured, a substitute is provided, just as it is in charity and endowments.

---

## Page 15538

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was also asked about a man who, during the illness that ended in his death, directed in his will that a beverage kept in a perfumer’s shop, worth one hundred and fifty dirhams, be sold.
