Its proceeds were to be added to three hundred dirhams from his wealth, and the total used to purchase a property to be endowed for the needs of a mosque: its imam, its muezzin and its lamp oil. He had recorded these instructions in writing before his illness.

He answered:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds.

If someone directs in his will that a particular item of his property, whether immovable or movable, be sold, that a further sum he has specified from his wealth be added to its proceeds, and that the total be devoted to a lawful endowment, this is permissible.

If this can be covered by one-third of his estate, it must be set aside, even without the heirs’ consent. As for what he gives his heirs during his final illness, if he gives any of them more than their inheritance share, this is not permissible without the heirs’ approval. But if he gives each person a particular item equal in value to their entitlement, or part of it, the scholars have two views within the school of Ahmad and elsewhere:

“The first”: He may do so. This is the position of al-Shāfiʿī.

“The second”: He may not do so. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa.

If the view is adopted that he may do so in accordance with each heir’s inheritance share, then a gift made by a sick person during a life-threatening final illness is equivalent, in such a matter, to a bequest taking effect after his death, by agreement of the imams.

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15539

Chapter: The Appointed Executor

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About a guardian duly authorized to act for orphans: the orphans owned a house, which the guardian’s agent sold before viewing it. He received the price, but a higher offer was subsequently made for it. May he accept the higher offer or not?

He answered:

If the agent sold it at its fair market value and it had been viewed on his behalf, the sale is valid. If it had not been viewed on his behalf, there is a difference of opinion. If he sold it below its fair market value, he has been negligent in discharging his trust. He may be held liable for the resulting loss, or the sale may be rescinded if the full fair market value is not offered to him. And Allah knows best.

He was also asked:

About a man of high standing who had extensive dealings with people and left testamentary instructions concerning various matters. During the testator’s lifetime, a man came to his executor and said:

“So-and-so, I have come to you while so-and-so is still alive

---

## Page 15540

—the testator—concerning some money: he owes me such-and-such an amount.”

The executor mentioned this to the testator, who said: “If anyone makes a claim against me after my death, have him swear an oath and pay him without requiring evidence.” Is the executor permitted—or obliged—to do this upon the claimant’s oath?

He answered:

Yes. The executor must pay this claimant what he claims if he swears to it, whether or not it falls within one-third of the estate. If it falls within the one-third, then, at worst, the testator would be making a voluntary gift by authorizing this payment.

If someone makes a bequest to a particular person conditional upon his performing some act, or to an unspecified person meeting a given description, both bequests are valid by agreement of the imams. They do not dispute the validity of a bequest involving something unspecified, nor have they disputed the validity of an acknowledgment involving something unspecified. Thus, no one should doubt that the payment must be made if it falls within the one-third. Doubt might arise only where it exceeds the one-third.

The unquestionably correct view is that this payment must be made from the estate as a whole, because debts take precedence over bequests. These words signify that the oath is to be transferred to the claimant and that whatever he swears to is to be paid. But is transferring the oath equivalent to an acknowledgment or to evidence? Scholars hold two views on this.

If it is regarded as equivalent to an acknowledgment, then this constitutes an acknowledgment in the claimant’s favor. At most, it is an acknowledgment by description or with an unspecified element; both forms of acknowledgment are valid by scholarly agreement. Moreover, an acknowledgment in favor of this particular person is not an acknowledgment in favor of someone unspecified, for his case prompted the general wording, and the case that prompts general wording is certainly included within its intended scope. It is as though the testator had said: “If this particular person swears to what he claims, give it to him.” A condition of this kind is permissible by scholarly agreement and must be carried out. And if

---

## Page 15541

it is held that transferring the oath is equivalent to evidence, the claimant’s oath, coupled with the defendant’s refusal to swear, constitutes evidence. The claimant has thus produced evidence supporting his claim, and there is no doubt that what he claims must be handed over to him. This follows the principle of those who do not adjudicate by transferring the oath to the claimant, such as Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī, and one of the two positions within the school of Imam Ahmad.

For those who adjudicate on the basis of refusal to swear, such as Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad, according to the better-known of the two reports from him, the case is stronger still. If both litigants consent and the claimant swears, they regard this as permissible; it also comes under refusal to swear. Thus, when a man knows that he has had numerous dealings with others, some unsupported by evidence, and that he owes obligations whose holders and amounts he may not know, such a stipulation on his part is not a voluntary gift. Rather, it is a testamentary instruction to discharge an obligation. By Muslim agreement, a testamentary instruction to discharge an obligation owed to another person is payable from the estate as a whole.

For if someone knows that he owes an obligation but doubts whether he has discharged it, he may not swear an oath denying it. Rather, if the defendant swears an oath and pays him, he has fulfilled his duty. If he owes an obligation but does not know precisely to whom, he must do what will discharge his liability, for whatever is indispensable to fulfilling an obligation is itself obligatory. This is like someone who has missed one of a day’s prayers but does not know which, or someone who owes a debt to one of two men but does not know which is entitled to it. If he says, “Whichever of you swears an oath shall have it,” or something similar, he has fulfilled his duty.

Furthermore, if a claim is made against someone and he does not know whether it is true or false, he may not categorically deny it under oath, for that would be swearing to something he does not know. Rather, he must act according to what he considers most likely. If someone whom he believes informs him of something, he should act upon it. And if he transfers the oath to the claimant

---

## Page 15542

when the facts are unclear to him, he has done what is required of him. For if he forbade them to pay the claimant, he might be unjustly withholding payment from someone entitled to it. Yet if he ordered that every claimant be paid, this could lead someone to claim what he is not entitled to, and such payment would be a voluntary gift. If, however, he orders that the claimant be required to swear an oath and then be paid, he has done what is required of him, basing his decision on what he considers most likely to discharge his liability. If he has fulfilled his obligation, that amount is paid from the estate as a whole.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

A man was guardian of his brother’s children, then died leaving children of his own. They took possession of their father’s property. Must the deceased guardian’s children relinquish that property, and may a claim be brought against them?

He replied:

If it is known that the orphans’ wealth was mixed with the man’s wealth, one must ascertain how much he spent from the orphans’ wealth on maintenance and other expenses, and claim the remainder. Similar matters are dealt with accordingly, with reference to established custom.

He was asked:

A man entrusted with an orphan’s wealth has used it in profit-sharing business for three years and earned a profit through lawful means. Is it permissible for the guardian to take any of the profit, or does it belong exclusively to the orphan?

---

## Page 15543

He replied:

All the profit belongs to the orphan. However, if the guardian is poor and has worked with the wealth, he may take the lesser of the customary wage for such work and what suffices for his needs. He must not take more than the wage for his work; and if that wage exceeds his needs, he must take no more than he needs.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A guardian has young orphans in his care, and their mother is pregnant. Should he provide maintenance for the children and for the person who cares for the children and their mother, once she has received her dower? May the children, their mother, and their caregiver consume all the wealth?

He replied:

As for the wife, she is to be given her share before she gives birth. As for the remaining heirs, if the division of the estate is postponed until the birth, the orphans should be maintained according to what is reasonable. There is no harm in mixing their wealth with their mother’s wealth, having their bread prepared together, and cooking their meals together, if this serves the orphans’ interests. The Companions asked the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about this, and Allah Most High revealed:

“They ask you about orphans. Say, ‘Improving their condition is best. If you mix your affairs with theirs, they are your brothers. Allah knows the corruptor from the reformer.’”

As for the unborn child, if you postpone the division, no issue arises. If you proceed with it immediately, the share of a male heir should be reserved for the child as a precaution. Is the wife entitled to maintenance and accommodation on account of the child in her womb? The scholars hold three opinions on this.

---

## Page 15544

The first opinion is that she is entitled to neither maintenance nor accommodation. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah, of Ahmad according to one of the two reports from him, and of al-Shāfiʿī according to one opinion.

The second is that she is entitled to both maintenance and accommodation. This is one of the two reports from Ahmad and the opinion of a group of scholars.

The third is that she is entitled to accommodation but not maintenance, as reported from Mālik and from al-Shāfiʿī according to one opinion.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An orphan girl who owns property has received a marriage proposal. May her guardian sell some of her real estate and spend the proceeds on a bridal trousseau, clothing, and jewellery appropriate for someone of her standing?

He replied:

Yes, her guardian may sell enough of her real estate to provide her with a trousseau, supplying the customary trousseau and jewellery.

He was asked:

A man is guardian over his two sisters, who have grown up [waladayhimā], and he has found them to be of sound judgment. Does he need to establish this before a judge, or to have witnesses?

---

## Page 15545

He replied:

If the guardian finds them to be of sound judgment, their property is to be handed over to them. He does not need witnesses; rather, he acknowledges their sound judgment and hands their property over to them. This is permissible without the judge’s permission, although he may have it formally established before the judge. And Allah knows best.

He was asked:

An executor paid a debt on behalf of the testator without having it established before a judge, and made a settlement on behalf of an absent person at less than fair value. May the heirs rescind this?

He replied:

The executor may not pay an alleged debt without legally valid evidence, nor merely on the claimant’s assertion; otherwise, he is liable for the payment. Nor may he transfer property in settlement except at fair value. If he transfers it for less than its value by a margin beyond what people ordinarily tolerate in transactions, he must either make good the shortfall in the heirs’ entitlement or rescind the settlement and pay the creditor his due.

Legally valid evidence takes several forms: an acknowledgment by the deceased, or by someone whose acknowledgment is binding upon him, such as his agent acknowledging something within the scope of his authorization. This includes the ruler’s administration and his household steward. Other forms include a witness whose testimony is accompanied by the claimant’s oath, the deceased’s handwriting when it is known to be his, and other such evidence.

He was asked:

A Christian died, leaving an estate and a will. Debts owed by him came to light, some recorded in documents and others not. May the executor pay the creditors without having their claims established before a judge?

---

## Page 15546

He replied:

If the deceased customarily recorded what he owed people in a ledger or similar record, and had a scribe who recorded his liabilities and the like with his permission, reference should be made to the records in his own handwriting or that of his agent. Any recorded debt bearing no indication of payment is equivalent to an acknowledgment by the deceased. In such matters, writing has the same standing as speech. An agent’s acknowledgment concerning matters within his authority is admissible, whether made orally or in his authenticated handwriting. However, the creditor must swear to his entitlement or that the debtor’s liability has not been discharged, just as he would if the debt were established by an oral acknowledgment. As for giving a claimant what he claims merely on his own assertion, when nothing distinguishes his claim from anyone else’s, this is not permissible. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning an executor or someone in a similar position: some of the executor’s property is held jointly by him and an executor appointed over him, and the ward has a share in it. The co-owners sell their shares, or rent the property for the executor, and the guardian needs to sell the orphan’s share or lease it out along with theirs. May the executor purchase it?

He replied:

He may purchase it, because the co-owners are not suspected of improper dealing when selling their own shares. Moreover, when the co-owners designate the executor, he rather than anyone else becomes the designated party in respect of the orphan’s share, which is necessarily included. The underlying rationale supports this. Allah Most High says:

“If you mix your affairs with theirs, they are your brothers. Allah knows the one who causes corruption from the one who puts things right.”

---

## Page 15547

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning an orphan’s executor who traded for the orphan and for himself using his money: he bought a type of merchandise for the orphan, then sold it and made another purchase for him with the proceeds. Later, he purchased the aforementioned merchandise and died without specifying whether it belonged to one of them or to them both. Does the merchandise belong to the executor’s heirs or to the orphan?

He replied:

If it is known that he purchased it entirely with his own money or entirely with the orphan’s money, then it belongs to one of them. The matter should be investigated to determine whether its ownership can be established by ascertaining how much money belonged to the orphan and how much belonged to the executor, and by examining the account books, his handwritten records, and similar evidence. If the orphan’s property was distinguishable—for example, through his recording what he purchased for him, or something similar—it would be among the items he had not recorded as his own.

If it proves impossible by any means to determine who is entitled to it, the jurists have three opinions:

The first is that it should be divided between them, as Abū Hanīfah held, just as when two people each claim ownership of an item in their joint possession.

The second is that the matter should be left pending until they reach a settlement, as al-Shāfiʿī held, because one of them is entitled to it, but it is not known which.

The third, which is Ahmad’s position, is that lots should be drawn between them; whoever wins the draw must swear an oath and then take it. This is based on the report in the Sunan collections:

Abū Hurayrah—may Allah be pleased with him—reported that two men brought a dispute over some goods before the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and neither had any evidence. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, “Draw lots over it.”

It was narrated by Abū Dāwūd and al-Nasāʾī. In another wording narrated by Abū Dāwūd:

“If both are unwilling to swear the oath, or both wish to swear it, let them draw lots over it.”

Al-Bukhārī also narrated it, with the wording:

“The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—offered a group of people the opportunity to swear an oath. They hastened forward, so he ordered that lots be drawn between them to determine which of them would swear.”

And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15548

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning orphans under legal guardianship who have an executor and a caregiver, and whose mother has married an unrelated man: does he have any authority over them?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. Their mother’s husband has no authority over their persons or their property. Indeed, a mother married to an unrelated man has no right to custody, lest that man become their custodian. Since a wife is subject to her husband’s authority, the Lawgiver has removed her right to custody so that they will not be in the custody of an unrelated man. Rather, custody belongs to their maternal grandmother or another relative. Their property is entrusted to the executor, while guardianship in marriage belongs to their male agnatic relatives.

He was also asked:

Concerning a man who, as death approached him, made the following bequest in his presence: “Half of this house is for the Noble Sanctuary, and half is for my slave, ‘Sanuqirr,’ who has been manumitted and is free.” He had no heir other than his master’s son. The executor told his master’s son, “Muslims may not withhold this.” Yet he disregarded the executor’s words, sold it, and dealt with the house as its owner. Is his sale valid?

He replied:

If the bequest falls within one-third of the estate, it must be carried out, and the heirs have no right to invalidate it. If they deny it, the beneficiary may require them to swear an oath. Whenever a witness testifies in his favour to acceptance by the executor or someone else, he may supplement his witness’s testimony with his own oath and take his share.

---

## Page 15549

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a man lawfully placed under legal guardianship, whose executor passed away to the mercy of Allah Most High, leaving a son. The son took possession of what his father had left, including the ward’s property that had been in his father’s possession. The orphan requested an accounting from the executor’s son. Is he entitled to this?

The son claims that his father handed over some of his ward’s property to Zayd, who was not legally entitled to receive it, and that witnesses were called to attest to the transaction between them. The recipient to whom the executor handed over the property then claimed that he had delivered it to the orphan. Is this testimony admissible against the orphan under legal guardianship in the circumstances described, or not? May the orphan seek recovery from the executor’s estate for the property he handed over to someone not legally entitled to receive it? May the executor’s son recover what his father handed over without lawful grounds? What is the ruling on this?

He—may Allah have mercy on him—replied:

If the executor dies and it is not known that the orphan’s property was lost without negligence, it is legally presumed to remain and must therefore be accounted for in the deceased’s estate. But is it treated as a debt, sharing proportionately with the claims of other creditors, or as property held in trust, to be recovered from the estate itself? There is disagreement on this. If the heir claims that he returned it to the orphan, his unsupported statement is not accepted.

If, however, the executor handed it over to someone else, who then delivered it to the orphan, and this is established and the transfer was permissible, then the executor is absolved of liability in this matter—for example, if

---

## Page 15550

the orphan has attained sound judgment and his property is handed over to him after his sound judgment has been ascertained, even if this has not been established before a judge. The lifting of his legal restriction requires neither judicial verification nor a ruling. Rather, whenever the executor ascertains that he has attained sound judgment, he must hand over his property to him, as Allah Most High says:

“If you find them to be of sound judgment, hand over their property to them. Do not consume it wastefully and hastily before they grow up. Whoever is wealthy should abstain, and whoever is poor may take a reasonable amount. When you hand over their property to them, call witnesses over them. Sufficient is Allah as a Reckoner.”

If, however, the executor handed the property over to someone to whom it was not permissible to entrust it, he is liable for it. If the property subsequently reached the orphan, whose sound judgment was evident, the executor is absolved of liability, just as anyone who wrongfully seizes property is absolved when it reaches its rightful owner—even if its return involves no action or transgression on his part. For example, the owner might recover it by force, someone else might retrieve it for him, or the wind might carry it to him.

If, after his sound judgment has been ascertained, the orphan denies having received the property from that recipient, who was not the executor’s agent, his statement is accepted upon his oath. But if he denies that the executor or his agent handed it over to anyone, is his statement accepted, or that of the executor? There is a well-known disagreement among the scholars concerning this.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning an executor who holds property belonging to orphans: may he set aside a share from his own property and a share from theirs, and spend it on them and himself?

---

## Page 15551

He replied:

He should provide for the orphan in a reasonable manner. If mixing the orphan’s food with his own is better for the orphan, he should do so, as Allah Most High says:

“They ask you about orphans. Say, ‘Setting their affairs right is best. If you mix your affairs with theirs, they are your brothers.’ Allah knows those who cause corruption from those who set things right.”

When Allah threatened those who consume an orphan’s property with severe punishment, the Companions would keep the orphan’s food separate from their own, and it would spoil. They therefore asked the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, about this, and Allah revealed this verse.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning orphans under the care of an executor who have a maternal half-brother: the executor sold their brother’s share to them and stated [that] (1) the property was in ruins. The orphans were not informed of the sale whereby the executor sold their brother’s share to them. Is this sale permissible or not?

He replied:

An executor may not sell real estate except to meet a need or secure a clear, overriding benefit. If he states that he sold it because of its dilapidation, he may not purchase it for the other orphan, since this would harm the other orphan if he is telling the truth, and harm the first if he is lying.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I could not find it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-fatāwā min al-saqt wa-l-tashīf (Safeguarding Majmū‘ al-fatāwā against Omissions and Textual Corruption).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — coordinator of the book for al-Maktaba al-Shāmila.

---

## Page 15552

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a man who had a slave woman by whom he had five children: he deposited some dirhams with someone and told him, “If I die, give her the dirhams.” He then died, and some of the dirhams were taken from the executor. Her children then summoned her before the judge and demanded the dirhams from her. She handed them over and acknowledged that she had received them from the testator. They then demanded the entire sum from the executor, claiming that the money she had acknowledged as part of those dirhams had not come from him, and that her acknowledgment had been made only after they had coerced her into it. Is the woman’s statement that it formed part of the sum to be accepted or not?

He replied:

The statement of the depositary appointed as executor concerning the amount of the money is to be accepted upon his oath. His statement that he paid the woman what he paid her is also to be accepted if she confirms his account. The statement of each of them that they hold no more than that is to be accepted upon their respective oaths.

A bequest to a slave woman who has borne her master a child is valid if it can be covered by one-third of the estate. This woman is entitled to take what was bequeathed to her if it amounts to less than one-third. If the heir denies the bequest, she is entitled to require him to swear an oath. If an upright witness testifies in her favor and she supplements his testimony with her oath, judgment shall be given in her favor. Once the money has passed out of the executor’s hands, his testimony in her favor is admissible.

---

## Page 15553

And if she initially concealed what the executor held in order to obtain from him what had been bequeathed to her, that would excuse her before Allah, even if she had no evidence to establish it. For someone who knows that he is in fact entitled to money and takes it is acting on a legal interpretation, although scholars differ concerning the issues in this area. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning an executor who relinquished his appointment before the judge, handed the money over to him, and requested his authorization for a formal record to be handed over: must the judge grant this request?

He replied:

If he needs this to protect himself from harm, the judge must grant his request. The purpose of adjudication is to ensure that rights reach those entitled to them and to prevent wrongdoing. This comes under enjoining what is right, forbidding what is wrong, and enforcing compliance. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a man who appointed two men as executors to look after his child, and they then exerted themselves to establish their appointment: may they recover from the orphan’s property the expenses they incurred in establishing it?

He replied:

If they undertook the executorship without remuneration, whatever they reasonably spent on establishing their appointment is chargeable to the orphan’s property. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15554

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

Concerning a man whose companion died while engaged in jihad: he gathered his companion’s estate over a period of three years, with considerable effort. Is he entitled to remuneration for this?

He replied:

If he is an executor, he is entitled to whichever is less: the customary remuneration for someone like him or what suffices for his needs. If he was compelled to undertake this work, he is entitled to the customary remuneration for someone like him. If he undertook it voluntarily without remuneration, he is not entitled to any payment; rather, his reward is with Allah. If he performed work that had to be done, without intending to do so gratuitously, there is disagreement over whether he must be remunerated. The stronger view is that he must.

---

## Page 15555

Book of Inheritance

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a woman whose husband died, leaving children.

He replied:

The wife is entitled to her dowry. Regarding any portion still owed by him, she has the same standing as the other creditors. After the debts and any enforceable bequest, if there is one, have been discharged, she receives one-eighth of the remainder, alongside the children.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

About a woman who died, leaving a husband and both parents. Her father had taken charge of the estate and stated that she lacked sound financial judgment. Is her husband entitled to inherit from her?

He replied:

Of what this woman left, her husband receives one-half, her father one-third, and her mother the remainder, which is one-sixth. This is the position of the four imams, whether or not she possessed sound financial judgment.

---

## Page 15556

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About a woman who died, leaving both parents, a husband, four sons and a daughter. The husband said to a group of witnesses: “Bear witness that my share—namely, six shares—is for my wife’s parents and her aforementioned children, according to their prescribed shares under Islamic law.” What portion does each of them receive?

He replied:

If he has transferred ownership of his portion, consisting of six shares, to the other heirs according to their prescribed shares under Islamic law, the remaining eighteen shares being allocated as eight shares to the parents and ten to the children, then those six shares are redistributed proportionately over these eighteen shares. The whole is thus divided among them into eighteen shares, just as the surplus remaining after the prescribed shares is redistributed among their recipients, according to those who uphold such redistribution. The heir’s portion that he has assigned to them is treated like a surplus redistributed among them.

He was asked:

About a woman who died, leaving a husband, a grandmother, full siblings and a son: what is each entitled to inherit?

He replied:

The husband receives one-quarter, the grandmother one-sixth, and the son the remainder. The siblings receive nothing, by agreement of the imams.

---

## Page 15557

He was asked:

About a woman who died, leaving a husband, two daughters, her mother and two full sisters: do the sisters inherit?

He replied:

The husband’s prescribed share is one-quarter, the mother’s one-sixth, and the two daughters’ two-thirds. The original denominator is twelve, increased to thirteen through a proportional reduction of the prescribed shares. The sisters receive nothing alongside the daughters, because sisters in the presence of daughters are residuary heirs, and nothing remains for the residuary heirs. This is the position of the four imams.

He was asked:

About a woman who died, leaving a husband, a mother, a full sister, a paternal half-sister, and a maternal half-brother and half-sister.

He replied:

The estate is divided into ten shares. The original denominator is six, increased to ten through a proportional reduction of the prescribed shares. This case is called “Dhāt al-Furūkh” because of the extent of that adjustment. The prescribed allocations are: one-half for the husband; one-sixth, or one share, for the mother; three shares for the full sister; one-sixth for the paternal half-sister, completing the two-thirds; and one-third, or two shares, for the two maternal half-siblings. The total is thus ten shares. This is agreed upon by the four imams.

---

## Page 15558

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him:

About a woman who died, leaving a husband, a daughter, her mother and a maternal half-sister: what is each of them entitled to?

He replied:

The estate is divided into eleven shares: six for the daughter, three for the husband and two for the mother. The maternal half-sister receives nothing, since she is excluded from inheritance by the daughter, by agreement of all the imams. This follows the position of those who uphold redistribution of the surplus, such as Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad.

According to those who do not uphold redistribution of the surplus, such as Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, the estate is divided into twelve shares: six for the daughter, three for the husband and two for the mother, with the twelfth share going to the public treasury (*).

Section

The point here is that the scriptural texts encompass all legal rulings. We shall demonstrate this through some of the most difficult issues, thereby drawing attention to the rest. Inheritance law is among the most difficult of these.

We therefore say:

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 231):
> This is how the answer appears in the Fatāwā. However, its attribution to the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—should be checked and authenticated. The solution given here is mistaken because it includes the husband in the redistribution of the surplus (radd), whereas, according to the majority, the husband does not receive any such redistribution. Some have even reported a consensus on this (1). This majority includes the imams mentioned here: Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad.
> 
> Had the statement here not been attributed to the imams Abū Hanīfa and Ahmad, we would have said that it was one of the Shaykh’s own positions in which he differed from them. However, he states this position and attributes it to them, which gives greater reason to question its attribution to him (2). The correct division is into sixteen shares: four for the husband, three for the mother, and nine for the daughter. And Allah Most High knows best.
> 
> (1) See the chapter on radd in the inheritance section (Kitāb al-Farā’id or al-Mawārīth) of all works of jurisprudence. See, for example, al-Mughnī, 9:48.
> 
> (2) We are not claiming here that the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was immune to mistakes and errors. Allah forbid! Rather, he was a human being, subject to these things like anyone else. However, the issue before us is clear, and its ruling is among the best-known matters in inheritance law. It is therefore unlikely that the Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—would have made such a mistake. The attribution of this fatwa should thus be verified. And Allah Most High knows best.

---

## Page 15559

The scriptural text and analogical reasoning—which are the Book and the Balance—both indicate that the one-third share belongs exclusively to maternal half-siblings. This is the position of ʿAlī and those who agree with him, and the position of Abū Hanīfah and Ahmad according to the well-known report from him. Harb transmitted the view that full siblings share in it, which is the position of Zayd and those who agree with him, as well as Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī. Conflicting positions on this matter have been reported from ʿUmar, ʿUthmān and other Companions. Indeed, it has been said that conflicting reports exist from every Companion except ʿAlī and Zayd: the reports from ʿAlī consistently state that he did not allow such sharing, while those from Zayd consistently state that he did.

Al-ʿAnbarī said: “Analogical reasoning supports ʿAlī’s position, while juristic preference supports Zayd’s.”

Al-ʿAnbarī said: “This is an elegant reconciliation and an accurate formulation.”

The response is that both the scriptural text and analogical reasoning support ʿAlī’s position. As for the scriptural text, Allah Most High says:

“But if there are more than that [i.e., two or more], they share a third.”

This refers to maternal half-siblings. If we include full siblings among them, the maternal half-siblings no longer share the third among themselves; rather, others compete with them for it.

If it is argued that full siblings belong to this group because they too are children of the same mother, this is mistaken. Allah Most High says:

“If a man or woman leaves neither a father nor a child, but has a brother or sister, each of them receives a sixth”—and the rest of the verse.

In the reading of Saʿd and Ibn Masʿūd, the words (on the mother’s side) occur. By consensus, maternal half-siblings are intended. This is also indicated by His words:

“Each of them receives a sixth.”

Full siblings and paternal half-siblings are addressed in another verse, where He says: “They ask you for a ruling. Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning one who leaves neither a father nor a child. If a man

---

## Page 15560

dies leaving no child but a sister, she shall have half of what he leaves; and he inherits from her if she has no child.’” Thus, He assigned her half the estate and him the entire estate. This is the ruling concerning full siblings.

Then He says:

“If there are siblings, both men and women, the male shall have the share of two females.”

By agreement of the Muslims, this is the ruling concerning full siblings, not maternal half-siblings. Allah Most High’s statement of this ruling in this verse, and of the other ruling in the earlier verse, shows that the two categories are distinct. Since the scriptural text grants maternal half-siblings one-third, anyone who gives them less than that has wronged them. Full siblings belong to a different category. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Give the prescribed shares to those entitled to them; whatever remains goes to the nearest male relative.”

This entails that when the prescribed shares leave nothing, the residuary heirs receive nothing. Here, the prescribed shares have left nothing.

As for the claim, “If their father were a donkey, they would still share the same mother,” it is invalid both in terms of observable reality and in terms of revealed law.

As for observable reality, if their father were a donkey, their mother would have been a she-donkey, and they would not have been descendants of Adam.

If it is said, “What he means is that the father’s existence is equivalent to his nonexistence,” the response is: This is false, for what exists cannot be nonexistent.

As for revealed law, Allah has laid down a ruling for full siblings different from His ruling for maternal half-siblings.

---

## Page 15561

And if it is said, “If the father does not benefit them, should he not at least cause them no harm?”

The reply is: On the contrary, he may harm them just as he may benefit them. Consider a case in which there is one maternal half-sibling and numerous full siblings: the maternal half-sibling alone receives one-sixth, while all the remainder goes to the full siblings. Were it not for the father, they would share one-third with that single sibling. If the father’s presence can benefit them, it can also deprive them of inheritance. Thus, it is clear that it can harm them.

Furthermore, the principles of inheritance rest on the rule that a combined kinship through a male and a female is not treated as two separate ties. A full brother is not treated as either a paternal half-brother or a maternal half-brother. He does not inherit solely through his maternal relationship, just as he does not inherit solely through his paternal relationship; rather, he inherits through the combined relationship through both parents.

The maternal relationship is treated separately only when it constitutes an independent tie. An example is that of two paternal cousins, one of whom is also a maternal half-brother. Here, the majority hold that the maternal half-brother receives one-sixth, and the two share the remainder. This is reported from ʿAlī. It is reported from Shurayh that he awarded the entire estate to the maternal half-brother, treating him as though he were a cousin through a full paternal uncle. The majority, however, say: Both stand equally as paternal cousins, whether through a full paternal uncle or a paternal half-uncle. The maternal sibling relationship is independent, not combined with the other tie in a way that would make him equivalent to a cousin through a full paternal uncle.

Another illustration clarifying the ruling in the “shared-inheritance case” is that, if the heirs included paternal half-sisters, they would be assigned two-thirds, and the prescribed shares would be proportionately reduced. Yet if their brother were present with them, they would receive nothing. He is therefore called the “ill-fated brother.” Because his presence makes them residuary heirs, he sometimes benefits them and sometimes harms them. His presence is not treated as absence when it harms them. Likewise, since the paternal tie

---

## Page 15562

makes the siblings residuary heirs, it sometimes benefits them and sometimes harms them. This is how residuary inheritance operates: a residuary heir sometimes receives the entire estate, sometimes most of it, sometimes a smaller portion, and sometimes nothing at all—when the prescribed shares exhaust the estate. Whoever awards residuary heirs a share when the prescribed shares exhaust the estate has departed from the explicitly established principles of inheritance.

If someone says, “This is juristic preference,” the reply is: This is a preference that contradicts the Book and the balance of justice, for it wrongs the maternal half-siblings by taking their entitlement and giving it to others. Those who dispute this issue have no proof beyond its being Zayd’s opinion. It has also been reported that ‘Umar ruled accordingly, and some people in Medina and elsewhere followed that ruling, just as they followed similar rulings concerning inheritance involving a grandfather and siblings. They also followed Zayd’s opinions on other inheritance questions out of adherence to his authority, even when the textual evidence and analogy supported those who disagreed with him.

Some argue for this on the basis of the saying:

“The most knowledgeable of you in inheritance law is Zayd.”

This, however, is a weak hadith with no established basis. Zayd was not known for expertise in inheritance law during the Prophet’s lifetime, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Even concerning Abū ‘Ubaydah, the only authentically established statement is:

“Every nation has a trustworthy man, and the trustworthy man of this nation is Abū ‘Ubaydah ibn al-Jarrāh.”

Likewise, they follow Zayd regarding the grandfather, although the majority of the Companions held otherwise. The majority agreed with al-Siddīq that the grandfather, like the father, excludes siblings from inheritance. This is reported from more than ten Companions. It is also the position of Abū Hanīfah and one of the two views within the schools of al-Shāfi‘ī and Ahmad. Abū Hafs al-Barmakī, one of Ahmad’s followers, adopted it, and it was reported by

---

## Page 15563

some of them as a view transmitted from Ahmad. Those who allow siblings to inherit alongside the grandfather are ‘Alī, Ibn Mas‘ūd and Zayd, each of whom held a distinct position of his own. ‘Umar ibn al-Khattāb remained undecided on the matter. The correct position is undoubtedly that of al-Siddīq, for numerous reasons we have set out elsewhere.

As for “the two ‘Umarī inheritance cases,” nothing in the Quran indicates that the mother receives one-third when inheriting alongside the father and the husband. Rather, Allah assigned her one-third when she and the father inherit the estate. Thus, the Quran indicates that, of whatever she and the father inherit together, she receives one-third and he receives two-thirds. Leading Companions—including ‘Umar, ‘Uthmān, ‘Alī, Ibn Mas‘ūd and Zayd—and the majority of scholars relied on this to establish that the parents divide what remains after the prescribed share of either spouse in the same one-to-two ratio. This is by analogy with their division of the entire estate when they inherit it together, just as they divide what remains after debts and bequests.

The Quran’s implicit meaning rules out the mother receiving one-third unconditionally. Whoever grants her one-third unconditionally, even when there is a wife, has therefore contradicted the Quran’s implicit meaning. The majority, however, have acted upon that implication: they have not treated her inheritance when the deceased’s father inherits from him as equivalent to her inheritance when his father does not inherit. Rather, if his father inherits from him, his mother receives one-third unconditionally. If, however, his father does not inherit from him, but a relative ranking below the father does—such as the grandfather, paternal uncle or brother—then she is all the more entitled to one-third. For if she receives one-third alongside the father, she is all the more entitled to it alongside any other residuary heir.

---

## Page 15564

Thus, the Quran indicates that if the deceased’s only heirs are his mother and father, or his mother and a residuary heir other than the father or a son, his mother receives one-third. This is an inference from the weaker case to the stronger. The son, however, has a stronger claim than the father, so she receives one-sixth alongside him.

When an heir entitled to a prescribed share inherits alongside the residuary heirs, the mother has been allotted one-sixth in the presence of daughters or sisters. As for a single sister inheriting with the mother, the mother receives one-third alongside a male sibling, so she is all the more entitled to it alongside a female sibling. Her share is reduced from one-third to one-sixth only by the presence of siblings; one sibling does not constitute a plurality of siblings. Since she receives one-third alongside a single brother, she is all the more entitled to it alongside a paternal uncle or another such heir.

There is disagreement concerning the grandfather. A view is reported from Ibn Mas‘ūd, while the majority hold that she receives one-third of the estate alongside the grandfather. This is correct, because the grandfather is more distantly related to the deceased than she is, and the father excludes him from inheritance. He therefore cannot deprive her of any part of her entitlement. Strict analogy places the father in relation to the mother as the daughter in relation to the son, and the sister in relation to the brother: each pair consists of a male and a female of the same class, both being residuary heirs. Likewise, the wife has been allotted half the share allotted to the husband, because they are a male and a female of the same class.

As for what the Book indicates concerning the mother’s inheritance, Allah says:

“Each parent receives one-sixth of what he leaves if he has children. If he has no children and his parents inherit from him, his mother receives one-third.”

Allah Most High prescribed this share for her on two conditions: that the deceased have no children, and that his father inherit from him. This indicates that she is not invariably given one-third whenever there are no children.

---

## Page 15565

and this further demonstrates the correctness of the position held by the leading Companions and the majority, who maintain that she is not given one-third of the entire estate in the two ‘Umariyya cases: a husband and both parents, or a wife and both parents.

Ibn ‘Abbās and those who agreed with him said: “For if she were given one-third here, she would receive it whenever there were no children, without qualification, which is contrary to what the Quran indicates.” It is also reported that he said to Zayd: “Does the Book of Allah mention one-third of the remainder?” That is, the Book of Allah mentions only one-sixth and one-third.

The response is that the Book of Allah does not grant her one-third unconditionally. How, then, can he give her one-third when either spouse is present? Rather, the Book of Allah contains an indication that precludes giving her one-third alongside the father and either spouse. Were that the case, it would have said: “If he has no children, his mother receives one-third.” On that supposition, she would be entitled to one-third without qualification. Since, however, one-third was assigned only in certain circumstances, we know that she is not entitled to it unconditionally. This inference by contrary implication, also called the argument from the wording, demonstrates the invalidity of the view of those who give her one-third except in the two ‘Umariyya cases. There is no basis for giving her one-third, quite apart from its being contrary to consensus.

Later he said:

His words, “And his parents inherit from him, his mother receives one-third,” indicate that she receives one-third and the father receives the remainder, through the words “his parents inherit from him.” Having assigned the inheritance to the two of them and then specified her share, He thereby indicated that the remainder is his share. Giving the father the remainder alongside her does not entail that anyone else must receive what he receives.

We grant the other residuary heirs their shares only on the basis of His words:

“Blood relatives have a stronger claim upon one another in the Book of Allah,”

and His words:

“For everyone We have appointed heirs to what parents and close relatives leave,”

---

## Page 15566

and by the Prophet’s words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace):

“Give the prescribed shares to those entitled to them; whatever remains goes to the nearest male relative.”

Section

As for sisters inheriting alongside daughters as residuary heirs—as He says, “and he has a sister”—this position, held by the majority of the Companions and scholars, is likewise supported by the Quran and Sunnah. Allah Most High says:

“They ask you for a ruling. Say, ‘Allah gives you a ruling concerning collateral inheritance: If a man dies leaving no child but a sister, she shall have half of what he leaves. He inherits from her if she leaves no child.’”

This indicates that the sister inherits half when there is no child, and that the brother inherits the entire estate when she leaves no child. It follows that, when there is a child, the sister does not receive half of what the deceased leaves. Otherwise, she would receive half whether or not he had a child, making the mention of a child misleading, pointless and harmful. Allah’s speech is exalted above such things. Among the passages illustrating this are His words:

“He leaves no child but has a sister; she shall have half of what he leaves,”

and His words:

“If he has no child and his parents inherit from him, his mother receives one-third.”

Once it is established that she does not inherit half when there is a child, that child must be either male or female.

---

## Page 15567

A male child excludes her from inheritance, all the more so since he also excludes the brother, as indicated by His words:

“He inherits from her if she leaves no child.”

Thus, unrestricted inheritance is established for him only when she leaves no child. Unrestricted inheritance means receiving the entire estate. This indicates that, if she leaves a child, he does not receive the entire estate: he is either excluded or receives only part of it.

If she leaves a child, that child must be either a son or a daughter. The Quran has explained that a daughter receives only half. This indicates that she does not deprive him of the other half when the only heirs are a daughter and a brother.

Since Allah’s ruling concerns collateral inheritance, which applies to someone who leaves neither father nor child, it is known that this is not the ruling for someone who has neither child nor father. Since Allah Most High has explained that the brother receives the estate—the sister’s estate—and is therefore her residuary heir, the father has an even stronger claim to be a residuary heir. If the father and brother are residuary heirs, the son has a stronger claim still.

Allah Most High says:

“For everyone We have appointed heirs to what parents and close relatives leave behind.”

The Prophet’s words (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) also indicate this:

“Give the prescribed shares to those entitled to them; whatever remains goes to the nearest male relative.”

This shows that only residuary heirs inherit what remains after the prescribed shares have been distributed. It is known that the son is the nearest relative, followed by the father, then the grandfather, then the brothers. The Prophet (may Allah bless him and grant him peace) ruled that the descendants of brothers sharing a mother inherit from one another, to the exclusion of those descended from brothers with different mothers. Thus, a full brother has precedence over a paternal half-brother. A son’s son takes the place of the son. Likewise, the descendants of a nearer forefather are more closely related than the descendants of a more distant forefather; and whichever of them is closest to the more distant forefather

---

## Page 15568

is closer to the deceased. When two relatives are equally close in degree, the one related through both parents takes precedence over the one related through the father alone.

Since the Quran establishes that the sister receives half when there is no child, and that, where there is a male child, the son is a residuary heir who excludes the sister just as he excludes her brother, there remains the question of the sister inheriting alongside female children. Nothing in the Quran rules out her inheritance in this situation.

When she inherits alongside a daughter, there are three possibilities: she is excluded, she receives half, or she inherits as a residuary heir. There is no basis for excluding her, since she does not encroach upon the daughter’s share. Her brother would not be excluded, so neither should she. Were she excluded, it would be in favour of a more distant relative; yet a more distant relative does not exclude a closer one.

Moreover, assigning her half would place her on an equal footing with the daughter when they inherit together. The daughter, however, has the stronger claim, so the sister cannot be treated as her equal. Indeed, prescribing half for the sister could reduce the daughter’s share below half. For example, where there are a husband and a daughter, assigning the sister half would make the prescribed shares exceed the estate, requiring a proportional reduction that would bring the daughter’s share below half. Siblings do not encroach upon children’s shares, whether through a prescribed share or through residuary inheritance, for children have the stronger claim. Allah has only granted the sister half when the deceased leaves neither a father nor a child.

Once both her exclusion and her entitlement to a prescribed share have been ruled out, the only remaining possibility is that she inherits as a residuary heir, taking precedence over more distant relatives such as a paternal uncle or a paternal uncle’s son. This is the view of the majority.

This is also supported by the hadith reported by al-Bukhārī: “When Ibn Masʿūd was told that Abū Mūsā and Salmān ibn Rabīʿa had said, concerning a daughter, a son’s daughter, and a sister: ‘The daughter receives half

---

## Page 15569

and the sister half. Go to Ibn Masʿūd, for he will agree with us,’ he said: ‘Then I would indeed have gone astray and would not be among the rightly guided! I shall rule in this case according to the ruling of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: the daughter receives half; the son’s daughter receives one-sixth, completing two-thirds; and the remainder goes to the sister.’”

This establishes that sisters inherit as residuary heirs alongside daughters. A sister can become a residuary heir through another heir—her brother—so there is no impediment to her becoming a residuary heir alongside a daughter. As for the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “Give the prescribed shares to those entitled to them,” and so on, its wording is general, but exceptions have been made for a woman who frees a slave, a woman who undergoes mutual imprecation, and a woman who takes in a foundling. This is based on his statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“A woman receives three inheritances: from the person she freed, her foundling, and her child concerning whom she underwent mutual imprecation.”

Since this general statement already admits exceptions, the case under discussion is likewise excepted on the strength of the evidence already mentioned.

Section: The Inheritance of Two Daughters

Allah Most High says:

“Allah instructs you concerning your children: a male is entitled to the share of two females. If there are more than two daughters, they receive two-thirds of what he leaves; but if there is only one, she receives half.”

Thus, the Quran establishes that a daughter receives one-third alongside her brother, half when she is alone, and that more than two daughters receive two-thirds. There remains the daughter’s share in the remaining case: if she receives one-third, not one-quarter, alongside a male, then she is all the more entitled to one-third, not one-quarter, alongside a female.

For He also says: “And if there is

---

## Page 15570

only one, she receives half.” He thus makes her entitlement to half conditional upon her being the only daughter, implying that she receives it only when this condition is met.

This differs from His words, “If they are daughters.” He uses the pronoun (kunna, “they”) and (wa-nisā’an, “and women”), both plural. It would not have been possible to say “two,” because the plural pronoun is not confined to two, nor is the ruling confined to two. It was therefore necessary to say “more than two,” since the ruling for two was already known, as was the ruling for one.

If a single daughter receives half and more than two receive two-thirds, two daughters cannot receive more than two-thirds. They therefore cannot receive the entire estate, with half going to each. Three daughters receive only two-thirds—so how could three receive more? Nor would half suffice for her, since she is entitled to it only on condition that she is the sole daughter; she therefore does not receive it when she is not the sole daughter.

This implication is apparent in the accusative reading of “And if there is only one.” Here, “one” is the predicate of kāna (“to be”); the understood wording is: “If she is a single daughter,” meaning that she is alone, with no other daughter alongside her, “she receives half.” She therefore does not receive that share when another daughter is alongside her. Half is thus ruled out, as is the entire estate, leaving only two-thirds. This is one indication furnished by the verse.

Furthermore, Allah’s statement concerning sisters—“If there are two, they receive two-thirds of what he leaves”—indicates that two daughters have a stronger claim to two-thirds than two sisters do.

There is also the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “when he gave the two daughters of Sa'd ibn al-Rabī' two-thirds, their mother one-eighth, and their paternal uncle the remainder.”

This is a matter of consensus; no dissent from Ibn 'Abbās concerning it is authentically established.

---

## Page 15571

The verse concerning (children) indicates that the ruling for more than two daughters is the same as that for two. Likewise, concerning sisters, He says, “If there are two, they receive two-thirds of what he leaves,” without mentioning more than two. For if two sisters are entitled to two-thirds, then a greater number are all the more entitled to that share. This differs from the verse concerning daughters: His words, “A male receives the share of two females,” indicate only that a daughter receives one-third alongside her brother. When there are two daughters, she is no longer entitled to one-third. Thus, the explanation in each verse is most eloquent.

Since the initial wording indicated the inheritance of two daughters, but not of more than two, He subsequently explained the inheritance of more than two daughters. In the “summer verse,” however, the wording indicated the inheritance of two sisters, which, by stronger implication, also indicated the inheritance of three, four, or more. There was therefore no need to mention more than two sisters.

Thus, in the former passage He mentioned more than two daughters without mentioning two daughters; in the other verse He mentioned two daughters without mentioning more than two, as clarity of expression required in each context. Having explained the ruling for one sister and one brother, and for two or more sisters, it remained to explain the case of two or more daughters from both categories, so that the explanation would encompass every division.

The word ikhwah (“siblings”), like all other plural expressions, may denote a category without specifying its number, thus including two or more. It may also mean three or more. In this verse, however, it signifies plurality without further restriction, because He had already explained the case of one. Moreover, in the inheritance rulings He mentioned, He distinguished between one and more than one, while treating the different numbers—two and three—alike. He stated this explicitly in His words:

“If a man or woman leaves an estate, having neither a father nor offspring,”

through to His words:

“They share in one-third.”

Thus, His expression (kānū, “they are”)

---

## Page 15572

is a plural pronoun. His words, “more than that,” mean more than one brother or one sister. Then He says, “They share in one-third,” referring to them through both a plural pronoun—“they”—and an explicit plural noun—“partners.” This indicates that plural forms in the inheritance verses encompass numbers without restriction, from two upwards, as in His words:

“Allah instructs you concerning your children,”

“If he has siblings, his mother receives one-sixth,”

and:

“If they are siblings, both men and women.”

Section

As for the grandmother, as al-Siddīq said, no share is prescribed for her in Allah’s Book. The mother mentioned in Allah’s Book is described with qualifications that restrict the ruling to one’s immediate mother. Thus, although a grandmother may be called a mother, she is not included in the term “mother” used in the inheritance rulings. She is, however, included among the “mothers” in His words:

“Your mothers are forbidden to you in marriage.”

But the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, “gave her one-sixth.” Her inheritance was therefore established by the Sunnah of the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. No general statement concerning grandmothers has been transmitted from him; rather, he awarded an inheritance to the grandmother who asked him. When the second grandmother came to Abū Bakr, he allowed her to share the one-sixth with the first.

People have differed concerning grandmothers. One view is that the two—the mother’s mother and the father’s mother—do not inherit, as held by Mālik and Abū Thawr. Another view is that only three inherit: these two and the grandfather’s mother, based on the report of Ibrāhīm al-Nakha'ī: “The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,

---

## Page 15573

awarded inheritance to three grandmothers: your two grandmothers on your father’s side and your grandmother on your mother’s side.” This is a fair report transmitted by a Successor directly from the Prophet, for Ibrāhīm’s reports of this kind are among the best. Ahmad therefore adopted it. The text mentions inheritance only for these grandmothers.

Another view is that all grandmothers whose relationship to the deceased is traced through an heir inherit. This is the view of the majority, including Abū Hanīfah, al-Shāfi‘ī, and others, and is also a position within Ahmad’s school. This view is stronger. Although the wording of the text does not encompass every grandmother, when the second grandmother came to al-Siddīq, he told her: “The sixth that was awarded was only for someone other than you; but if she were its sole claimant, it would be hers.” Thus he awarded inheritance to the second, although the text concerned someone other than her.

Moreover, there is no disagreement that grandmothers farther up the maternal line inherit. Thus, the father’s mother’s mother and the mother’s mother’s mother inherit by agreement. This leaves the mother of the grandfather’s father: what distinguishes her from the grandfather’s mother, even if a distinction is drawn between the father’s mother and the grandfather’s mother? It is well known that the grandfather’s father takes the grandfather’s place; indeed, he is simply a more distant grandfather. Likewise, the grandfather stands in the father’s place. On what grounds, then, can one distinguish between the father’s mother’s mother and the mother of the grandfather’s father?

This is clarified by the fact that the deceased’s mother’s mother and his father’s mother stand in an equal relationship to him. Likewise, his father’s mother’s mother and his father’s father’s mother stand in an equal relationship to his father. They must therefore share in the inheritance.

Furthermore, these scholars allow the mother’s mother’s mother to inherit, however much farther the maternal line extends, while denying inheritance to the father’s father’s mother. They thus give preference to the grandmother on the mother’s side over the grandmother on the father’s side. This is a weak view, for the mother’s mother has no greater claim than the father’s mother, and maternal relatives have not

---

## Page 15574

been given precedence in any legal ruling. Rather, paternal relatives have precedence in all legal rulings, and the same applies to custody. The correct view is that she is not excluded from inheritance by her son—that is, the father—as stated in the stronger of the two reports from Ahmad, on account of the hadith of Ibn Mas‘ūd. Moreover, although her relationship is traced through him, she does not inherit in his capacity. Rather, her position alongside him resembles that of maternal half-siblings alongside the mother: they are not excluded by her.

The rule asserted by some, “Anyone whose relationship is traced through a person is excluded by that person,” is invalid both as stated and in its converse. As stated, it is disproved by maternal half-siblings inheriting alongside the mother. Its converse is disproved by a son’s children being excluded by their paternal uncle, and a brother’s children being excluded by their paternal uncle, and similar cases in which someone is excluded by a person through whom their relationship is not traced. The effective reason is that one inherits in the same capacity as the other. Thus, anyone who inherits in another person’s capacity is excluded by that person if the latter is more closely related to the deceased. Grandmothers take the mother’s place and are therefore excluded by her, even when their relationship is not traced through her.

As for “a son’s daughters alongside a daughter” receiving one-sixth to complete two-thirds, and likewise paternal half-sisters alongside a full sister, this is because Allah says:

“Allah instructs you concerning your children: for the male, a share equal to that of two females. But if there are only females, more than two, their share is two-thirds of what he leaves.”

It is known that this address includes sons’ children, but not daughters’ children, and that His words “your children” encompass those whose lineage is traced to the deceased—namely, his children and his daughter’s children—and that they are included in order of priority. Sons’ children are included when there are no immediate children, in accordance with the established rule that whatever remains after the prescribed shares goes to the nearest male relative, and a son

---

## Page 15575

is more closely related than a son’s son. Thus, when there is only one daughter, she receives one-half, leaving one-sixth of the share allotted to daughters. If there are sons’ daughters, they would have been entitled to that entire share were it not for the daughter; once she takes one-half, the remainder is theirs.

The same applies to a full sister together with a paternal half-sister:

“Ibn Mas‘ūd reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, awarded the daughter one-half and the son’s daughter one-sixth, completing two-thirds.”

But when the daughters receive the full two-thirds, no prescribed share remains. If there is a male residuary heir among the descendants through sons, the remaining estate goes to him, because he is the nearest male relative. If she is of his generation or an earlier generation, he makes her a residuary heir, according to the majority of the Companions and scholars, including the four imams and others. Ibn Mas‘ūd, however, excludes her because she would not inherit on her own.

The disagreement also concerns a paternal half-sister together with her brother when the daughters have received the full two-thirds. The majority make daughters residuary heirs alongside their brothers, dividing the remainder so that each male receives the share of two females, whether inheriting as residuaries increases or decreases their shares. The case for their inheriting here is stronger, and Ibn Mas‘ūd’s position concerning a reduction in their shares is well known.

---

## Page 15576

Section

Concerning those “whose order of death is unknown,” so that it cannot be determined which of them died first, the disagreement is well known. The view more consistent with the principles of the Sharia is that they do not inherit from one another; rather, each is inherited from by his living heirs. This is the majority view and also one position within Ahmad’s school, though not its well-known position.

This is because, under the fundamental legal principles, what is unknown is treated as nonexistent. Consider someone who finds lost property: since the owner’s circumstances are unknown, the unknown is treated as nonexistent, and the finder becomes the owner of what he has found, because its ownership is not known.

Likewise, concerning a “missing person,” Ahmad adopted the opinions of the Companions who treated the unknown as nonexistent. They therefore regarded the woman as the second husband’s wife so long as the first husband’s situation remained unknown, both in fact and outwardly, just as with found property. Once his situation becomes known, the marriage becomes subject to his approval or rejection, and he is given the choice between his wife and the dowry. If he chooses his wife, she is his wife, the second marriage is invalidated, and no divorce from the second husband is required.

The point here is that Ahmad followed the Companions who treated the unknown as nonexistent. In the present case, if one of the two died before the other, that fact is unknown, and the unknown is treated as nonexistent. Thus, one person’s death preceding the other’s is treated as nonexistent, and neither inherits from the other. Furthermore, inheritance was assigned to the living so that the heir might succeed the deceased and benefit from his wealth.

---

## Page 15577

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who died leaving a paternal uncle (his father’s full brother) and a paternal half-sister: How should the estate be divided?

He replied:
“The sister receives half, and the paternal uncle receives the remainder. This is agreed upon by the Muslims.”

He was asked about a woman who died leaving a daughter, a maternal half-brother, and a paternal uncle’s son: What does each receive?

He replied:
“The daughter receives half, and the paternal uncle’s son receives the remainder. The maternal half-brother receives nothing. However, if he is present when the estate is divided, he ought to be given something. A daughter excludes a maternal half-brother from inheritance according to the schools of all four imams. Allah knows best.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a woman who died leaving a husband, a father, a mother, and two children—a daughter and a son. After her death, her father also died, leaving his father, sister, grandfather, and grandmother.

---

## Page 15578

He replied:
The husband receives one-quarter, and each parent receives one-sixth, together making one-third. The remainder is divided into three shares between the two children: two for the son and one for the daughter.

As for the father’s estate, his grandmother receives one-sixth and his father receives the remainder. His sister and grandfather receive nothing, as both are excluded by his father.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a man who had children and acquired a slave woman, by whom he fathered a son. He then freed her, and she married and had children. The man died, having assigned a house specifically to his son by the slave woman; that son has also died. Are his maternal half-siblings entitled to any share alongside his paternal half-siblings?

He replied:
The mother receives one-sixth, his maternal half-siblings receive one-third, and the remainder goes to his paternal half-siblings, with each male receiving the share of two females. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked about a woman who died leaving a husband and a sister’s son.

He replied:
The husband receives half. As for the sister’s son, according to one view he receives the remainder. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and his followers, of Ahmad according to the well-known view reported from him, and of a group of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers.

---

## Page 15579

According to the second view, the remainder goes to the public treasury. This is the position of many of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers and of Ahmad according to one reported view.

Underlying this issue is the scholars’ disagreement concerning “distant kindred”—blood relatives who are neither entitled to a prescribed share nor residuary heirs. The position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad according to one reported view is that the estate of someone who leaves neither an heir entitled to a prescribed share nor a residuary heir goes to the Muslim public treasury. The position of most of the early generations, Abū Hanīfa, al-Thawrī, Ishāq, and Ahmad according to the well-known view reported from him is that the remainder goes to these blood relatives:

“They have a greater claim upon one another in the Book of Allah.”

And because the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“The maternal uncle is the heir of one who has no heir: he inherits his wealth and secures his release from captivity.”

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked about a man who died leaving a wife, a full sister, and three daughters of a full brother: Are the brother’s daughters entitled to anything alongside them? What share does each woman receive?

He replied:

The wife receives one-quarter, and the full sister receives one-half. The brother’s daughters receive nothing. The remaining quarter goes to any residuary heirs, if there are any. Otherwise, it reverts to the sister according to one of the two scholarly views; according to the other, it goes to the public treasury.

---

## Page 15580

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man died leaving a daughter, the young children of a paternal half-brother, a grown male paternal cousin, a female paternal cousin, and a maternal half-brother who is not also one of his paternal cousins. Who inherits the estate, and who becomes the daughter’s guardian?

He replied:

As for the inheritance, half goes to the daughter and half to the brother’s sons. As for the girl’s custody, it belongs to the female paternal cousin, rather than to her father’s maternal half-brother or to the male paternal cousin, who is not a mahram (a relative permanently forbidden to her in marriage). He has guardianship over the orphan girl’s property; that guardianship belongs to an executor or his deputies.

He was asked:

What is the ruling concerning someone who leaves two daughters and a paternal uncle who is his father’s maternal half-brother?

He replied:

If the deceased leaves his two daughters and his maternal half-brother, his maternal half-brother receives nothing, by agreement of the imams. Rather, the two daughters receive two-thirds, and the remainder goes to the residuary heirs, if he has any. Otherwise, it reverts to the two daughters or goes to the public treasury.

---

## Page 15581

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man died leaving a full brother, a full sister, two daughters, a wife, and an estate. The brother was absent. How should the estate be divided?

He replied:

The wife receives one-eighth, and the two daughters receive two-thirds. The siblings receive five of the estate’s twenty-four shares, divided between the brother and sister in a ratio of two to one. Thus, the wife receives three shares, each daughter eight shares, the brother three and one-third shares, and the sister one and two-thirds shares.

He was asked:

A man’s maternal aunt died leaving an estate but no heir. Does her sister’s son inherit from her?

He replied:

According to one of the two scholarly opinions, he is the heir; according to the other, her estate goes to the public treasury administered according to Islamic law.

He was asked:

A man had a female paternal cousin and a male paternal cousin. The female cousin died leaving a daughter. Then the male cousin died leaving two sons. Thus, the two sons remained, along with the daughter of

---

## Page 15582

the deceased female paternal cousin; the daughter then died, leaving children of a paternal uncle. Who is entitled to inherit: the children of the male paternal cousin on her mother’s side, or the children of her own paternal uncle?

The answer:

The position of Imam Ahmad and others who uphold inheritance by representation—a similar view having been transmitted from the Companions and Successors, and this being the majority position—is that each of the distant kindred takes the place of the relative through whom they are connected, whether closely or distantly related. Proximity to the heir is not taken into account. Then, with the line of kinship being the same, the paternal uncle’s children receive two-thirds of the estate, and the children of the mother’s male paternal cousin receive one-third, since the latter’s connection ultimately runs through the mother. When a mother inherits alongside a father or grandfather, the mother receives one-third and he receives the remainder. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man died leaving a wife and three sons by her. One son then died, leaving his mother and his two brothers. Another then died, leaving his mother and his brother. The third then died, leaving his mother and a son of his own. What share of his estate does the mother receive?

He replied:

The wife receives one-eighth of the first deceased man’s estate, and the remainder goes to the brothers, who are his sons.

As for the first brother to die, his mother receives one-sixth of his estate, and the remainder goes to his two brothers.

As for the second brother, his mother receives one-third of his estate, and the remainder goes to his brother.

As for the third brother, his mother receives one-sixth of the estate, and the remainder goes to his son.

---

## Page 15583

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:
