Two men were paternal half-brothers. The mother of one of them was a slave woman who had borne her master a child. She married a man and had two children by him. This woman’s son married and had a son, then died, leaving his son, who inherited from him. That son subsequently died. His paternal half-brother had already died during his lifetime, leaving a son. When the son died, his brother had left two—his father’s maternal half-brothers—and he also left a paternal cousin on his father’s side. What share belongs to his father’s brothers, and what share belongs to his paternal cousin?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. The entire inheritance belongs to his paternal cousin on his father’s side. His father’s two maternal half-brothers receive no inheritance. This is agreed upon by the Muslims. However, a person should make a bequest to relatives who will not inherit from him. If he has made no bequest and they are present when the estate is divided, they should be given something from it, as Allah Most High says:

“And when relatives, orphans and the needy are present at the division, give them something from it and speak to them kindly.”

He was asked:

A man died, leaving two sons, two daughters, a wife and a brother’s son. The two sons subsequently died. The wife took her share and married an unrelated man, while the shares of the two sons remained undivided. The wife became pregnant by her new husband, and the remaining heirs wished

---

## Page 15584

to divide the available property, but the others were prevented from doing so until the wife gives birth. If she gives birth, will she have a share in the property?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. As for the first deceased man, his wife receives one-eighth, and the remainder goes to his sons and daughters, each male receiving the share of two females. The brother’s son receives nothing. Thus, the wife receives three twenty-fourths, each son seven twenty-fourths, and the two daughters together seven twenty-fourths.

When the first son died, he left his brother, his two sisters and his mother. The second brother left his two sisters, his mother and his paternal cousin. If the unborn child had already been conceived when either of them died, the child inherits from him, being his maternal half-sibling.

The woman’s husband should refrain from intercourse with her from the time of this son’s death, as ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib—may Allah be pleased with him—directed in such a case. If he does not have intercourse with her and she gives birth, it will be known that the child had already been conceived at the time of death. If he has intercourse with her and the pregnancy is delayed, the matter becomes uncertain. Nevertheless, any heir who wishes to receive his entitlement may be given two-thirds, while a share of one-third is held back for the unborn child. Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

An orphan had property held by a court-appointed trustee. His paternal uncle deliberately killed him out of envy, and this was established against him. What penalty does Islamic law require for him? What is Allah’s ruling concerning the division of the victim’s estate, including endowed property and other assets, when his heirs include his father, a maternal half-brother, a maternal grandfather and the killer’s children?

---

## Page 15585

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. The deceased’s estate belongs to his heirs. The killer inherits nothing, by agreement of the imams. The mother receives one-third, the maternal half-brother one-sixth, and the remainder goes to the paternal cousin. The maternal grandfather receives nothing. As for the “endowment,” its disposition is governed by the donor’s stipulations that accord with Islamic law.

As for “the victim’s blood,” the right to seek retaliation belongs to his heirs: his mother, his brother, and the son of the paternal uncle who committed the killing, according to the schools of al-Shāfiʿī, Ahmad and others.

According to Mālik, if they disagree—the mother seeking one course and the paternal cousin another—the paternal cousin’s wishes take precedence, since he is the agnatic relative. This is one of the positions transmitted from Mālik, adopted by many of his followers. In the “second,” transmitted by Ibn al-Qāsim and followed in practice by the scholars of the Maghrib, the decision rests with whoever demands retaliation, whether the male agnatic heir or the female heir entitled to a prescribed share. The “third transmitted position” agrees with the school of al-Shāfiʿī: a pardon granted by any heir is valid, and the remaining heirs’ entitlement becomes a financial liability owed by the killer.

But may the paternal cousin put his own father to death? There are also two views on this. According to “the first,” he may not, as held by al-Shāfiʿī and by Ahmad in the well-known position transmitted from him. According to “the second,” he may, as Mālik held; this is also a position within Ahmad’s school. However, the right of retaliation belonged initially to the victim and then passed to the heir. Mālik nevertheless considered it reprehensible for him to put his father to death.

Anyone entitled to retaliation may pardon the killer or take blood money. If any of those entitled to retaliation grants a pardon, retaliation is waived, and the remaining heirs retain their entitlement to blood money. An entitled heir may take blood money without the killer’s consent according to the school of al-Shāfiʿī

---

## Page 15586

and Ahmad, according to the well-known position transmitted from him. According to another report, blood money may be taken only with the killer’s consent; this is also the position of Abū Hanīfah and Mālik. If retaliation is waived for an intentional killer, he is given one hundred lashes and imprisoned for a year according to Mālik and a group of scholars, though the other scholars do not hold this view.

He was asked about the following verses:

My grandmother is his mother, and my father his grandfather; … I am his paternal aunt, and he my maternal uncle.
Give us your ruling, O Imam—may Allah protect you … and shield you from the misfortunes the nights may bring.

He replied—may Allah have mercy on him:

A man married his son to his daughter’s mother, … and took the daughter in lawful wedlock.
She bore him a daughter, so the poets tell, … who called that woman’s son, “My maternal uncle.”

A man married a woman, and his son married her mother. The man had a daughter, and his son had a son. His daughter is the one addressed in the poem. Her grandmother—her mother’s mother—is the mother of the grandson and the wife of the son. Her father is the grandfather of his son’s son. She is the boy’s paternal aunt, being his father’s half-sister through their father; and he is her maternal uncle, being her mother’s half-brother through their mother. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15587

He was asked—may Allah have mercy on him—about the following verses:

“What of a people who, when one of their number had died, … began dividing the wealth and robes?
A woman, not of their kin, said: … ‘Shall I tell you of a wondrous case?
Within my womb is a child who will ever thank you; … delay the division until you know what I bear.
If it is a boy, he receives not a mustard seed; … but if instead a girl, she is entitled
To half, truly and certainly—a right denied by none … who knows Allah’s prescribed shares without error.
I have told you my case without falsehood; … I speak neither in ignorance nor merely in parables.’”

The Answer

He replied:

There are a husband, a mother, two maternal half-siblings, and an unborn child of the deceased’s father. The pregnant woman is not the deceased’s mother, but her father’s wife. The husband receives one-half, the mother one-sixth, and the maternal half-siblings one-third. If the unborn child is male, he is a paternal half-brother and receives nothing, by scholarly consensus. If the unborn child is female, she is a paternal half-sister and is allotted one-half, in addition to the other prescribed shares. The calculation therefore has an original denominator of six, increased to nine.

If, however, the unborn child is being carried by the deceased’s mother, the answer remains the same according to one of the two scholarly opinions held by the Companions and those who followed them. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and the well-known position transmitted from Ahmad. According to the other opinion, if the unborn child is male, he shares with the maternal half-siblings as one of them and is not excluded from inheritance. This is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, and one report from Ahmad.

---

## Page 15588

The Shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A woman’s husband had been suffering from a chronic illness for three months. He asked her for a drink, but she was slow to bring it, so he became displeased with her and said, “You are divorced three times.” She remained with him, caring for him, and twenty days later he died. Does the divorce take effect? If he swore an oath in a case of this kind, would he have broken it? May an heir prevent her from inheriting?

He replied:

The divorce takes effect if he was of sound mind and acting of his own free will. However, she inherits from him according to the majority of the leading scholars of Islam. This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, and al-Shāfiʿī in his earlier opinion. It accords with the ruling of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān concerning the wife of ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf: he divorced her during his final illness, but ʿUthmān ruled that she should inherit from him.

She must observe whichever waiting period ends later: the waiting period following divorce or that following her husband’s death. If, however, he had lost his reason, his divorce has no effect.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was also asked:

A man divorced his wife once, before consummating the marriage, during the illness from which he died. Does this count as a divorce intended to deprive her of her inheritance? Should his purpose be counteracted, so that his wife inherits from him and receives her full dower? Or, in these circumstances, does she receive no inheritance and only half the dower?

---

## Page 15589

He replied:

Praise be to Allah, Lord of all worlds. This question rests on the ruling concerning a man who divorces his wife during his final illness after consummating the marriage. The majority of earlier and later scholars hold that she inherits from him. This accords with the ruling of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān—may Allah be pleased with him—in favour of Tamādur bint al-Asbagh, the wife of ʿAbd al-Rahmān ibn ʿAwf, who had divorced her during his illness. This is the position of Mālik, Ahmad, Abū Hanīfah, and al-Shāfiʿī in his earlier opinion.

On this basis, does she inherit after her waiting period has ended? And does a woman divorced before consummation also inherit? Scholars hold two opinions. The sounder opinion is that she inherits in these circumstances as well. This is the position of Mālik, the well-known position reported from Ahmad, and one opinion of al-Shāfiʿī. It is reported that ʿUthmān awarded her an inheritance after her waiting period had ended. Furthermore, she inherits because her entitlement to the estate became established when her husband entered his final illness. His freedom to dispose of his property then became restricted in order to protect her rights and those of the other heirs: he could make no gratuitous transfer to an heir, nor give more than one-third to a non-heir, just as he could not do so through dispositions taking effect after death.

Since, in relation to the heirs, his dispositions during his final illness are treated like those taking effect after his death—and he has no power to deprive her of her inheritance after death—he likewise has no such power once his final illness has begun. This is the case commonly known among scholars as “divorce intended to deprive a wife of her inheritance.” The view just stated is the correct one, on which the fatwa was based.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married off his daughter and recorded the dower as payable by her husband. The husband subsequently fell ill. When his illness became severe, three days before his death, he divorced his wife to prevent her from inheriting. Does this divorce take effect, and what is she entitled to receive from his estate?

---

## Page 15590

He replied:

If this woman’s divorce was revocable and her husband died while she was still in her waiting period, she inherits from him by the consensus of the Muslims. If the divorce was irrevocable, as in the case of a threefold divorce, she also inherits from him according to the great majority of the leading scholars of Islam. This was the ruling given by the Commander of the Faithful, Uthmān ibn Affān—may Allah be pleased with him—when Abd al-Rahmān ibn Awf divorced his wife, Bint al-Asbagh al-Kalbiyyah, three times during his final illness. Uthmān consulted the Companions, who advised that she should inherit from him. No disagreement on this matter is known from any of the Companions.

Disagreement emerged only during the caliphate of Ibn al-Zubayr, who said: “Had I been in his place, I would not have awarded her an inheritance.” Yet consensus had already been established before Ibn al-Zubayr became qualified to exercise independent juristic reasoning. This ruling was adopted by the leading scholars among the Successors and those who came after them. It is the position of the scholars of Iraq, such as al-Thawrī, Abū Hanīfah and his followers; the scholars of Medina, such as Mālik and his followers; and the hadith jurists, such as Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others like him.

It is also al-Shāfi‘ī’s earlier opinion. In his later opinion, however, he agreed with Ibn al-Zubayr, on the grounds that the divorce takes effect: if she were to die, he would not inherit from her, by agreement, and likewise she does not inherit from him. Moreover, the divorce has made her unlawful to him: neither intercourse nor other sexual enjoyment with her is lawful for him. She is therefore no longer his wife and does not inherit.

The majority said:

The heirs’ rights attach to the property of a person in his final illness from the onset of that illness, and his freedom to dispose of it becomes restricted in order to protect their rights. During his final illness, he may therefore make only such gratuitous transfers as he could make through dispositions taking effect after his death. He has no right during his final illness to deprive some heirs of their inheritance and reserve it for others, just as he has no right to do so through dispositions taking effect after death. Nor may he give more than one-third of his property gratuitously to a non-heir during his final illness, just as he has no power to do so through dispositions taking effect after death.

---

## Page 15591

And a hadith states:

“Whoever deprives someone of an inheritance, Allah will deprive him of his inheritance in Paradise.”

Accordingly, once his illness has begun, he has no right to deprive her of her inheritance, whether through divorce or otherwise. This remains so even if the divorce takes effect as far as he is concerned, for he may sever his own ties to her, but cannot extinguish her rights against him. According to this view, there is disagreement over the waiting period she must observe: the waiting period for divorce, the waiting period following a husband’s death, or whichever of the two is longer. These are three opinions, the strongest being that she must observe whichever waiting period ends later. Likewise, there are two opinions on whether she becomes entitled to her full dower. The stronger view is that she does, since this is among her rights that become firmly established, just as she becomes entitled to inherit.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man married two women, one Muslim and the other a woman of the People of the Book. He then said, “One of you is divorced,” and died before clarifying whom he meant. Who inherits his estate after him, and which woman must observe the waiting period for divorce?

He replied:

This question requires some distinctions, and scholars disagree about it. Some distinguish between divorcing a particular wife and then forgetting or not knowing her identity, and divorcing an unspecified wife and dying before singling her out by designation or identification.

Some hold that the divorce takes effect for all the wives, as Mālik maintains. Others hold that it takes effect for only one, as the other three imams maintain. If it is assumed that a particular wife was designated, but she has not been identified, should a division be made

---

## Page 15592

of the estate between the divorced wife and the other, as Abū Hanīfa maintains? Or should the matter be suspended until the two reach a settlement, as al-Shāfiʿī maintains? Or should lots be drawn between the divorced wife and the other, as Ahmad and other hadith jurists maintain? These are three opinions. Drawing lots after death concerns property; hence, it is accepted even by some who do not accept drawing lots to identify a divorced wife.

The correct view in this case—whether the divorced wife was unspecified or her identity was unknown—is that lots should be drawn between the two wives. If the lot falls to the Muslim wife, neither she nor the protected non-Muslim wife inherits anything: the former because she is divorced, and the latter because a non-Muslim does not inherit from a Muslim. If the lot falls to the non-Muslim wife, the Muslim wife receives a wife’s full share of the inheritance. This applies when the divorce precludes inheritance, as when he divorces her irrevocably while in good health.

If, however, the divorce was revocable, whether pronounced during health or illness, and he dies before the waiting period ends, she remains his wife and inherits from him. By agreement of the imams, she must observe the waiting period following a husband’s death. According to the majority of them—including Mālik, al-Shāfiʿī and Abū Hanīfa—her waiting period ends upon its completion. This is also one of the two opinions reported from Ahmad. The well-known opinion reported from him is that she must observe whichever of the two waiting periods, for bereavement or divorce, ends later.

If the divorce was irrevocable and pronounced during his final illness, the majority of scholars hold that a woman so divorced inherits if he divorced her with the intention of depriving her of her inheritance. This is Mālik’s view: he holds that she inherits even if her waiting period has ended and she has remarried. It is also the position of Abū Hanīfa, who holds that she inherits as long as she remains in her waiting period. The well-known view reported from him is that she inherits so long as she has not remarried. Al-Shāfiʿī likewise has three opinions, but his later position is that she does not inherit.

---

## Page 15593

As for the case where he is not suspected of intending to deprive her of her inheritance, the majority hold that she does not inherit. On this basis, the woman in question does not inherit, because a divorce of this kind, in which he did not specify which wife he was divorcing, does not indicate an intention to deprive her of her inheritance. For those who hold that she inherits unconditionally—as Ahmad does according to one of the two reports from him—the ruling is likewise as stated.

Where an irrevocably divorced woman inherits, one view is that she must observe whichever of the two waiting periods ends later. This is the apparent position of Ahmad’s school and the view of Abū Hanīfa and Muhammad.

Another view is that she observes only the waiting period for divorce. This is Mālik’s view and the well-known view of al-Shāfiʿī; it is also one report from Ahmad and one opinion of al-Shāfiʿī.

As for the case where the identity of the divorced wife remains unknown, one of the two women must observe the waiting period following a husband’s death, and the other the waiting period for divorce. Each is therefore obliged to observe one of the two waiting periods, but it is unclear which obligation applies to which woman. Thus, the stronger view here is that each must observe both waiting periods, since only in this way can each be certain of having discharged her obligation.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man died, leaving a slave woman who had borne him a child. She subsequently died, leaving a son and two daughters. Do the daughters share the right of patronage with the son? And do they inherit anything alongside him?”

---

## Page 15594

He replied:

There are two reports from Ahmad on this matter. According to the first—which is also the view of Abū Hanīfa, Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī—the right of patronage belongs exclusively to males. According to the second, sons and daughters share the right of patronage, with a male receiving the share of two females. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man owns a slave woman and has a son. The son had unlawful intercourse with her, and she also had unlawful intercourse with others. She bore a child and attributed him to the man’s son, who acknowledged him as his own, with the master’s consent. Does the child inherit when the man who acknowledged him dies, or not?”

He replied:

If the son acknowledged the child as his own during his lifetime, saying, “This is my son,” the child’s lineage is established through him, and he is counted among his children, provided he has no other known father. The same applies if it is known that the slave woman belonged to the son, for:

“The child belongs to the lawful bed, and the adulterer has no claim.”

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man’s mother owns a slave woman. He had intercourse with her without his mother’s permission, and she became pregnant by him and bore a boy. He then acquired ownership of both of them and wishes to sell his son born of unlawful intercourse.”

He replied:

He should free him, by agreement of the scholars. Indeed, the scholars have differed over whether the boy becomes free automatically upon coming into his ownership, without an act of manumission. There are two views:

---

## Page 15595

The first is that he becomes free automatically upon coming into his ownership. This is the position of Abū Hanīfa and the view of al-Qādī Abū Yaʿlā among Ahmad’s followers. Nevertheless, neither inherits from the other.

The second is that he does not become free automatically upon coming into his ownership. This is the position of Mālik and al-Shāfiʿī, and the position explicitly reported from Ahmad. And Allah knows best.

He—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man gave his wife a slave woman as part of her dowry, and she freed her. Some time later, he had intercourse with the freed woman, who bore a son, while his wife bore a daughter. He then died. Does the son born to the freed woman inherit alongside the daughter born to his wife?”

He replied:

If he had intercourse with the freed woman without marrying her, knowing that intercourse with her was forbidden, then his child is a child born of unlawful intercourse. According to the four imams, the child does not inherit from the man who had intercourse with his mother, nor does that man inherit from the child. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15596

Chapter on Manumission

He was asked about freeing a child born of unlawful intercourse.

He replied:

It is permissible to free a child born of unlawful intercourse, and one is rewarded for freeing him. And Allah knows best.

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

“A man from Quraysh married a slave woman, and she bore him a child. Is the child free, or is he a slave?”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of the worlds. If a man marries a woman knowing that she is a slave, then, by agreement of the imams, his child by her belongs to her master. A child follows his father in lineage and patronage, and his mother in freedom and slavery.

If the child belongs to a people whom all agree may be enslaved, then all agree that he is a slave. If he belongs to a people whose enslavement the jurists dispute, such as the Arabs, then his status as a slave is likewise disputed. The correct view is that “enslaving Arabs and non-Arabs” is permissible, as established in the two Sahīh collections, where Abū Hurayra—may Allah be pleased with him—said:

“I have continued to love Banū Tamīm ever since three things

---

## Page 15597

I heard the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—say about them. I heard the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—say, ‘They are the fiercest of my community against men.’ Their zakat contributions arrived, and the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘These are the zakat contributions of our people.’ He said: A captive woman from among them was with ʿĀʾisha, and the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Free her, for she is a descendant of Ishmael.’”

In another wording recorded by Muslim:

“There are three qualities I heard the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—mention concerning Banū Tamīm, and I have loved them ever since. ʿĀʾisha was under an obligation to free a slave, so the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Free someone from among these people.’ Their zakat contributions arrived, and he said, ‘These are the zakat contributions of my people.’ He also said, ‘They are the deadliest of people in great battles.’”

In the two Sahīh collections, with the wording given here being Muslim’s, Abū Ayyūb al-Ansārī narrated that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“Whoever says ten times, ‘There is no god but Allah alone, with no partner. His is the dominion, and His is all praise, and He has power over all things,’ is like someone who has freed four persons from the descendants of Ishmael.”

This hadith states that descendants of Ishmael may be freed, thus establishing that they may be held in slavery. Likewise, he “instructed ʿĀʾisha to free someone from the descendants of Ishmael in fulfillment of her obligation to free a slave.” The report says, “from Banū Tamīm,” because they are descendants of Ishmael.

In Sahīh al-Bukhārī, Marwān ibn al-Hakam and al-Miswar ibn Makhrama narrated:

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—stood when the delegation of Hawāzin came to him as Muslims and asked him to return their property and captives. The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said to them, ‘Those whom you see are here with me

---

## Page 15598

, and the words I love most are those that are truest. So choose one of the two: either the property or the captives. I had waited for you.’ The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had waited for them for ten-odd nights after returning from al-Tā’if. When it became clear to them that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—would return only one of the two, they said, ‘Then we choose our captives.’

The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—stood among the Muslims and praised Allah as He deserves, then said, ‘To proceed: your brothers have come to us in repentance, and I have decided to return their captives to them. Whoever among you wishes to give them up willingly may do so. Whoever wishes to retain his claim to his share until we compensate him from the first spoils Allah grants us may do so.’

The people said, ‘We willingly agree to this, O Messenger of Allah.’ The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘We cannot tell which of you has consented and which has not. Go back until your representatives report your decision to us.’ The people went back, and their representatives spoke to them. Then the representatives returned to the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and informed him that they had willingly agreed and given their consent.”

This authentic hadith establishes that he took the women of Hawāzin captive—although they were Arabs—and distributed them among those entitled to the spoils, so that they became their slaves. Afterwards, he sought to obtain them back from their owners, either as a voluntary donation or in exchange for compensation.

A hadith also reports that he freed them, as in the report of ‘Umar: while he was observing a devotional retreat, he learned that the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had freed the captives, so he freed a slave woman in his possession.

The Muslims used to have intercourse with these captive women by right of ownership, as in the case of the captives taken at Awtās, who were among the captives of Hawāzin. Concerning them, the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said:

“A pregnant woman must not be approached sexually until she gives birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until she has undergone one menstrual period to establish that she is not pregnant.”

---

## Page 15599

Imam Ahmad relates in his Musnad that ‘Ā’ishah—may Allah be pleased with her—said:

“The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—distributed the captives of Banū al-Mustaliq. Juwayriyah bint al-Hārith fell to the share of Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās, or to a cousin of his, and she entered into an agreement with him to purchase her freedom. She was a charming, beautiful woman. She came to the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—and said, ‘O Messenger of Allah, I am Juwayriyah, daughter of al-Hārith ibn Abī Dirār, the fortunate one among his people. You are aware of the affliction that has befallen me, and I have come to seek your help in paying for my freedom.’ The Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—said, ‘Would you like something better than that?’ She asked, ‘What is it, O Messenger of Allah?’ He replied, ‘I will pay the sum due under your agreement and marry you.’ She said, ‘Yes, O Messenger of Allah.’ He said, ‘I have done so.’ News spread among the people that the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—had married Juwayriyah bint al-Hārith, so they released the captives in their possession. Through his marriage to her, a hundred households of Banū al-Mustaliq were freed. I know of no woman who brought greater blessings to her people than she did.”

These and similar hadiths are well known—indeed, mass-transmitted—establishing that “the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—used to take Arabs captive.” His successors did likewise after him. As the imams and others have stated: “The Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—took Arabs captive.” Abū Bakr took Banū Nājiyah captive and pursued the Arabs in this way, taking them into slavery. Allah had said to them:

“And married women are forbidden to you, except those whom your right hands possess. This is Allah’s decree for you.”

The hadith of Abū Sa‘īd and other reports state that this verse was revealed concerning captive women: Allah permitted them to have intercourse with these women by right of ownership.

---

## Page 15600

And if she is taken captive and enslaved without her husband, intercourse with her is undoubtedly permissible. There is only an anomalous dissenting view within Ahmad’s school, and disagreement has also been reported within Mālik’s school.

Ibn al-Mundhir said:

“All the scholars whose views have been recorded agree that if a woman becomes someone’s property while her husband remains in enemy territory, her marriage is dissolved, and her owner may have intercourse with her after the waiting period to ascertain that she is not pregnant.”

If, however, she is taken captive together with her husband, the scholars disagree concerning this.

It is well known that most of those whom the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—took captive were captured in “war.” He also fought the People of the Book: he set out to fight the Christians in the year of Tabūk, although no fighting took place between them. He also sent against them the expedition commanded by Zayd, then Ja‘far, then ‘Abd Allāh ibn Rawāhah. These Christians included both Arabs and Romans. Likewise, he fought the Jews at Khaybar, as well as Banū al-Nadīr and Banū Qaynuqā‘. The Jews of Arabia included Israelites. Similarly, the Jews of Yemen included both Arabs and Israelites.

Furthermore, the grounds for enslavement are “unbelief,” subject to the condition of “war.” A free Muslim may never be enslaved, nor may someone protected by a treaty. Unbelief combined with belligerence is present in every unbeliever; thus, he may be enslaved just as he may be fought. Whatever permits killing the combatants also permits taking their dependants captive. This ruling applies generally to Arabs and non-Arabs alike. This is the position of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī in the later of his two opinions, and Ahmad.

Abū Hanīfah, however, does not permit enslaving Arabs, just as he does not permit imposing the jizyah upon them, because the Arabs were distinguished by noble lineage, since the Prophet—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was one of them

---

## Page 15601

and their unbelievers were distinguished by their extreme hostility towards him. This therefore barred the acceptance of jizyah from them, just as jizyah is not accepted from an apostate, both to treat him more severely and because of the honour he had acquired through his previous adherence to Islam.

He also cited the report that ‘Umar said:

“No Arab may be held in slavery.”

Those who disagreed with him held two views concerning the permissibility of enslaving those from whom jizyah is not accepted. Both are reported from Ahmad.

The first is that enslavement is comparable to accepting jizyah: anyone from whom jizyah is not accepted may not be enslaved. This is the position of Abū Hanīfah and others, and the view preferred by al-Khiraqī, al-Qādī and other followers of Ahmad. It is also the view of al-Istakhrī, one of al-Shāfi‘ī’s followers.

According to Abū Hanīfah, jizyah is accepted from every unbeliever except Arab polytheists. This is also one position reported from Ahmad. On this basis, enslaving Arab polytheists is not permitted, since jizyah is not accepted from them, whereas enslaving non-Arab polytheists is permitted. This is also al-Shāfi‘ī’s view, based on his position that Arabs may not be enslaved.

The other position reported from Ahmad is that jizyah is accepted only from the People of the Book and the Magians, as in al-Shāfi‘ī’s school. According to this position within Ahmad’s school, no polytheist may be enslaved, whether Arab or non-Arab. This is the view preferred by al-Khiraqī, al-Qādī and others.

Under these two positions within Ahmad’s school, enslavement is prohibited on grounds of religion, not lineage. Thus, if someone takes an Arab woman captive and she embraces Islam

---

## Page 15602

he enslaves her; if she does not embrace Islam, he compels her to do so. This is how they interpret the practice of the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, and the Companions in enslaving Arabs. As for an idol-worshipping slave, they do not permit him to retain his religion under slavery, as they permit under jizyah. Similarly, the Companions took Arab and idol-worshipping women captive and had intercourse with them. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“A pregnant woman must not be approached sexually until she gives birth, nor a woman who is not pregnant until one menstrual period has established that her womb is free of pregnancy.”

The four imams agree that intercourse took place only after the women had embraced Islam, and that intercourse with an idol-worshipping woman is impermissible, just as marrying her is impermissible.

“The second view” is that idol worshippers from whom jizyah is not accepted may be enslaved. This is the position of al-Shāfi‘ī and of Ahmad according to the other report from him. It rests on the fact that the Companions enslaved them, and we do not know that they compelled them to embrace Islam. Moreover, since they may not be killed, they must be enslaved; and slavery entails a degree of bondage absent from the collection of jizyah.

What we have explained makes clear that the correct view is that enslaving Arabs is permissible. As for the “report” mentioned from ‘Umar, if it is authentic and explicit concerning the point in dispute, Abū Bakr and ‘Alī nevertheless differed from him, for they took Arabs captive. It is also possible to understand ‘Umar’s statement as referring to the fact that the Arabs embraced Islam before their men were enslaved, and so slavery was not imposed upon them. Likewise, all of Quraysh embraced Islam, and slavery was therefore not imposed upon them because of their Islam, not because of their lineage. The Companions did not have the opportunity to take the women of Quraysh captive as they were able to

---

## Page 15603

take the women of other Arab groups captive. For this reason, none of them were enslaved, and no prohibition against taking them captive has been transmitted from the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him.

As for an Arab man marrying a slave woman, a free man may marry a slave woman only under two conditions: fear of falling into sin and lack of the means to marry a free woman, according to the schools of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, and Ahmad. They explain this by saying that such a marriage results in his child being enslaved. Thus, a free man, whether Arab or non-Arab, may marry a slave woman only out of necessity. If he marries her out of necessity, his child is a slave. According to Abū Hanīfah, however, the impediment is his already having a free woman as his wife; he distinguishes between Arabs and others regarding enslavement. If a man has unlawful intercourse with a slave woman, her child belongs to her master as a slave, by agreement, even if the father is an Arab, because paternal lineage is not legally established.

If, however, he has intercourse with her within marriage, believing her to be free, or waits until her womb has been established to be free of pregnancy and then has intercourse with her believing her to be his own slave woman, his child is free, whether Arab or non-Arab. Such a man is called “the deceived party.” The child of a man deceived in a marriage or purchase is free because he believed that he was having intercourse with a free wife or his own slave woman. He must pay a ransom to the slave woman’s master, as the Companions ruled, because he deprived her master of his ownership rights and was therefore liable for compensation. There are further subsidiary rulings and disagreements on this matter, but this is not the place to discuss them. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15604

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:

A man had a slave who ran away and then returned. Upon returning, the slave concealed his knife and killed himself. Does his master bear any sin? Is it permissible to offer the funeral prayer for him?

He replied:

Praise be to Allah. He had no right to kill himself, even if his master had wronged and mistreated him. Rather, if he could not protect himself from that injustice, he was obliged to endure patiently until Allah granted relief. If his master had wronged him to the point that he did this—for example, by providing inadequately for his upkeep, overworking him, beating him unjustly, seeking to commit an indecent act with him, or the like—then his master bears a burden of sin proportionate to the wrongdoing attributable to him.

“The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, did not offer the funeral prayer for someone who had killed himself, but told his Companions, ‘Offer the prayer for him.’”

Thus, it is permissible for people generally to offer the funeral prayer for him. As for religious leaders whose example is followed, if they refrain from offering the prayer for him to deter others, following the Prophet’s example, peace and blessings be upon him, then this is right. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 15605

He—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—was asked:

About some slaves who had stood surety for a man. They belonged to a vile Muslim living in Tatar lands. They are united in obeying Allah and His Messenger: they wish to perform Hajj, offer their prayers, pay zakat, and give charity. Their master, however, is dominated by disobedience and prevents them from obeying Allah and His Messenger. They could not bring themselves to disobey Allah and His Messenger. He is a highway robber, drinks wine, commits unlawful sexual intercourse, abandons prayer, and kills people whose lives Allah has made inviolable. They ask him to sell them, but he refuses; they ask him to free them, but he refuses. Whenever they mention any of this to him, he beats and imprisons them, leaving them to starve. So they agreed among themselves and fled to Egypt, seeking to obey Allah and His Messenger. Some of them have now performed Hajj. Is there any explicit text concerning their obedience to Allah and His Messenger, given that they have run away?

He replied:

If their circumstances are as they describe—that this man prevents them from doing what Allah and His Messenger have commanded and forces them to do what Allah and His Messenger have forbidden—then leaving his control was permissible; indeed, it was obligatory. They have done well in what they did, for someone who behaves in this way has no claim to inviolability. This would be so even if he were under Muslim authority; how much more so if he were under Tatar authority? It is obligatory to fight him, even if he is a Muslim.

These emigrants who fled to save themselves have done well in doing so. When a slave emigrates from territory at war with the Muslims, he becomes free, and no one has authority over him.

---

## Page 15606

He was asked:

About an agent who took a sum of his master’s money and bought slaves with it. He was asked, “Why are you taking your master’s money and buying slaves with it?” He replied, “I am buying them for him, and they remain his property; then I shall free them all.” When emancipating them, he claimed that they were his own slaves. He is now unable to repay their purchase price. Is the emancipation valid?

He replied:

If he bought slaves for the man with his permission, then they belong to that man. If he emancipated them without their owner’s permission, his emancipation of them is invalid. If he bought them with the man’s money without his permission, the owner of the money may take possession of them, or he may require the man who misappropriated his money to repay it. If this purchaser emancipates them, the owner of the money may take possession of them, and the emancipation is void. And Allah knows best.

> End of Volume Thirty-One.
